While he spoke to us on the way Texts taken from the preaching of the founder of Opus Dei

ROME 2000 Vol. VIII (English Translation 2002)

# PREFACE {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.presentacion}

It gives me great pleasure to write this introduction to the latest volume in the “Bonus Pastor” series. These notes from our Father’s oral preaching consist of twenty-six meditations, covering the period from 1945, before he first went to Rome, to March 1974, little more than a year before he went to heaven. I know that you will welcome this book with a holy zeal to follow more closely the clearly-marked footsteps our Founder left us with his teaching and his holy life. The title of the book immediately brings to mind a Gospel scene our Father meditated on countless times: where Jesus meets the disciples who were leaving Jerusalem, disheartened by the events of his Passion and Death. For the reredos of the first oratory in the Ferraz residence, in 1935, a time of great poverty, our Founder commissioned a sculptor to make a statue of our Lady. While it was being done, he put in its place a picture of the scene of the disciples of Emmaus at the moment they recognise Jesus in fractione panis – in the breaking of the bread. On 31 March 1935 the Blessed Sacrament was reserved in a Centre of the Work for the first time. St Luke tells us that, when the Master vanished from their sight, the disciples “said to one another: ‘Did not our hearts burn within us while he talked to us on the road, while he opened to us the scriptures?’ And they rose that same hour and returned to Jerusalem; and they found the eleven gathered together and those

who were with them, who said, ‘The Lord has risen indeed, and has appeared to Simon!’ Then they told what had happened on the road, and how he was known to them in the breaking of the bread.”1 Our earthly existence is a pilgrimage toward heaven in which we never lack Jesus’ company: “I am with you always, to the close of the age”,2 he promises us in St Matthew. And he has kept his promise: we meet him, alive and real, in the Blessed Eucharist. We hear his voice in Sacred Scripture, especially in the Gospels, where he speaks to every man and woman of all times. He impels us toward holiness by means of the Holy Spirit, who dwells in the Church and in our souls in grace. He reveals himself to us, as he did to the disciples at Emmaus, in the faces of all those people who offer us the witness of a Christian life, especially Blessed Josemaría, who incarnated the spirit of Opus Dei perfectly. The title of this book of meditations mirrors this same idea. As we read these texts and use them for our own prayer, we once again experience Jesus himself walking beside us and “opening to us the Scriptures”, as he did for Cleophas and his companion on the road to Emmaus, making use of our Father’s own prayer. Yes, in the considerations Blessed Josemaría offers us, behind the attractive metaphors he uses, we find our Lord’s own words, his teaching, which is always so endearing, explained with the familiar features of the spirit of Opus Dei. Behind our Father’s very personal way of praying and preaching, we discover the voice, the gestures, the beloved figure of our Jesus. These texts cannot be regarded as writings of our Founder, since he never checked them himself after they had been collected together. Nevertheless, they express his spirit accurately: you will recognise many words or phrases which you have very often meditated on before. We can now read them in context, thanks to the

## 1 Lk 24:32-35 2 Mt 28:20 9 PREFACE

notes we always used to make of his get-togethers, plus the tapes we also recorded in the last fifteen years of his life. As I said, you will feel great joy, as I do, on finally seeing this book in print. Our joy is enhanced by the thought that we are celebrating the second millennium of Christ’s birth. Although the special commemoration which the Church is celebrating will finish in a few weeks’ time, the fruits of the Holy Year need to continue alive and active in our souls, because every day, as our Founder used to say, is a good and holy occasion for each of us to be converted anew to God. Moreover, we are about to begin the year leading up to the centenary of Blessed Josemaría’s birth, 9 January 2002, and we have to prepare carefully to obtain all the spiritual benefits our Lord has in store for us. It can help us a lot to read these pages calmly and reflectively, because they provide us with a magnificent opportunity to become more and more identified with our holy Founder’s spirit. In the different passages we hear the voice of Christ speaking to us in our Father’s attractive and yet demanding tones, and leading us to put his teaching into practice. May God grant that these meditations impel us more and more strongly to travel joyfully along the path of our Christian vocation.3 If we really try to do so, the words Blessed Josemaría wrote in The Way will be true of each of us: “Nonne cor nostrum ardens erat in nobis, dum loqueretur in via? – Did our hearts not burn within us as he talked to us on the road?” If you are an apostle, these words of the disciples of Emmaus should rise spontaneously to the lips of your professional companions when they meet you along the ways of their lives.4 Don’t be surprised to find a number of repetitions here and there. They are indeed the same phrases or the same stories, but

3 Cf. Mass of Saint Josemaría, Prayer after Communion 4 The Way, 917

always with a different nuance and fresh emphasis. I am reminded now of something said by Don Alvaro, who was such a faithful son to our Father, referring precisely to these repetitions, that in the thirty-five years he lived with Blessed Josemaría, his words possessed the vitality of a soul completely in love who deliberately says the same things with different nuances, or different applications. I have chosen to date these lines on the day the Church celebrates the two thousandth anniversary of the birth of Christ in Bethlehem. I implore the Blessed Trinity to enable us to progress ever further along the path of fulfilment of God’s holy will. I ask this though the intercession of the Blessed Virgin Mary, Mother of God and our Mother too; of the Patriarch St Joseph; of all the angels and saints; and with the special backing of our beloved Father, Blessed Josemaría. “To the blessed and only Sovereign, the King of kings and Lord of lords, who alone has immortality and dwells in unapproachable light, whom no man has ever seen or can see. To him be honour and 5 eternal dominion. Amen.”

Rome, 25 December 2000, two thousandth anniversary of the birth of our Lord Jesus Christ.

5 1 Tim 6:15-16

# LITTLE THINGS * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.1}

A spiritual author tells the story – and I’ve often talked to you about it – of how the robber bands of his time used to take with them on their raids a skinny little boy whom they found extremely useful. In aristocratic mansions people were very careful to bar the front door, and the rooms on the main floor with their fine windows and balconies. But sometimes a tiny little window would be carelessly left open because it was very high up on one of the top floors and not considered important. Whereupon the gang formed a human tower, the little fellow climbed up to the top, slipped through the window that had been left ajar by mistake, and gained entry to the house. He then crept silently down the stairs until he got to the front door, and opened it, so that all the bandits could come in to plunder, murder and destroy. My children, this is what often happens to souls who seek Christian perfection. “What, me, commit a grave sin? No, never!” But… maybe they don’t give any importance to little things. They habitually leave a little window open, high up, and through it the devil gets in, and then stealthily tries to open the front door and let in the band of grave sins.

* Notes of a meditation given during a recollection in Madrid, in 1945: the exact date is unknown

## The importance of little things {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.1.1}

What I’m going to tell you now is something you’ve heard a hundred times before! I’ll say it in words from Sacred Scripture: Qui spernit modica paulatim decidet,1 he who despises small things will fail little by little. You and I cannot neglect little things: they form the whole framework of our lives. If we are really trying to follow Christ, if we are indeed seeking holiness through the sanctification of ordinary work, we have no option but to be very faithful in little things. We serve the Church, we become holy, if we fulfil the little duty of every moment perfectly. And if not, we don’t. You and I carry out a professional job and have the same social obligations as the rest of our fellow-citizens. We live in the same normal, everyday environment as many of our colleagues. For them as well as for us, the prime matter, so to speak, of our existence, is work, ordinary circumstances. But I would like to offer you the following consideration. Have you ever seen a potter modelling clay? He takes a lump of clay, he puts it on the wheel, he sets it rotating, and fashions a pot, and then another, and a hundred – all identical, soulless products. Then along comes a man who is an artist. He takes a similar lump of clay to the other fellow’s, and creates something totally different. What is it about that man’s hands, his soul, his movements, and his work, that enables him to produce, not pots in series, but a work of art? Something similar happens in the spiritual life. None of us can settle for producing objects which may or may not have a bit of artistic merit. What we each have to do at every moment is imitate the artist who actually creates beauty. For that to happen, you just have to sanctify the mundane little things which are within your reach, day by day and hour by hour. In his Gospel, St Matthew has recorded some words of Christ which sum up everything I would like to remind you about in this

1 Sir 19:1

period of prayer: Qui ergo solverit unum de mandatis istis minimis et docuerit sic homines minimus vocabitur in regno caelorum,2 whoever relaxes one of these small commandments and teaches men to do likewise, shall be called least in the kingdom of heaven. On the other hand, qui autem fecerit et docuerit, hic magnus vocabitur in regno caelorum,3 he who looks after these small commandments and teaches that way to others, shall be called great in the kingdom of heaven. And you, by divine vocation, have to be great in the kingdom of heaven, on the basis of fulfilling the little duties of every moment well!

## Little things done for love {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.1.2}

You already know, my son, because you have heard me saying so from the beginning, that for someone in love there are no unimportant details. Love gives such greatness to our actions that the smallest thing can achieve the level of heroism. How pleasing in God’s eyes are our faithfulness in those points, and our small constant mortifications! How they transform the will! How they enlarge your soul! And how much you contribute by your faithfulness in those tiny duties, to making life pleasanter for other people! Scripture tells us how on a certain occasion, as our Lord was walking in one of the Temple courts, vidit eos, qui mittebant munera sua in gazophylacium, divites,4 he saw some rich men putting money into the Treasury – large sums of money, offered perhaps with little love of God; money which they could renounce without much sacrifice because they were contributing from what they could easily afford. “And he saw a poor widow put in two small copper coins”:5 a tiny offering, but made with deepest love. Nobody

2 Mt 5:19 3 Ibid. 4 Lk 21:1 5 Lk 21:2

noticed it; only God, who sees into man’s inmost depths, saw her generosity. And Jesus, turning at once to his disciples, said to them: “Truly I tell you, this poor widow has put in more than all of them; for they all contributed out of their abundance, but she out of her poverty has put in all she had to live on.”6 How our Lord’s eyes must have lit up on seeing the widow’s generosity, shown in that small offering! That’s how his eyes light up with love every day, as he sees the love you put into the little details of your ordinary life: the norms of piety, the careful finishing off of your ordinary work right down to the smallest details; and as he sees your mortification, done for love, in something that contributes to your neighbour’s well-being. You close a door gently and quietly, so as not to disturb other people’s rest; you put a piece of furniture back in its place, concerned about the tidiness of your home; you put away the books or things you had been working with in their proper place; you take care of your clothes and other objects so that they don’t get spoilt, because you want to practise Christian poverty… So many details, which, when undertaken for love, show the sensitivity of your soul. It makes me very sad to see people always chasing after great and extraordinary things, not realising that sanctification is within their grasp in ordinary things, things which are commonplace only in appearance. I feel sorry for people like Tartarin of Tarascon who want to hunt lions in the corridors of their houses.† They abandon the attempt to acquire Christian perfection in everyday life, and distract their imagination with absurd desires for deeds of great heroism, which they don’t have, and never will have, the opportunity to carry out. I feel very sorry for them: in order to perform great deeds, the important thing is to kill the little foxes which destroy the Lord’s vine: little faults against sincerity, love, or sacrifice.

6 Lk 21:3-4 † Tartarin of Tarascon: a character from a novel by the French writer Alphonse Daudet (1840–97) (Tr.)

Examine your life carefully. You will see that you are full of faults which harm you and perhaps those around you as well. Remember that microbes are just as dangerous as wild beasts. You are cultivating those faults and errors as microbes are cultivated in a laboratory, through your lack of humility, your lack of prayer and your lack of self-knowledge; and then those bacteria infect your surroundings as well. “Catch us the foxes, the little foxes, that spoil the vineyards”,7 we read in Holy Scripture. The little fox who can destroy your soul may be hidden in a hundred daily details; little deceptions that play the role of that scrawny boy whom the robber band brought with them to get in through the window. You don’t take any notice… and they end up eating the grapes of your vineyard. Look, my children: great buildings are built little by little, thanks to little things. One spell of work today, and another tomorrow, and then another; laying bricks or stone blocks all day long; hauling sand and cement bag by bag, steel girders one by one… That’s how the Egyptian pyramids were built, and the great cathedrals, and modern skyscrapers. During the Spanish Civil War we often used to climb up to the top of the great cathedral of Burgos, and there, high up on one of the towers, I often used to make the same observation to those who came along with me. We drew so many conclusions from it! From the top of the tower the whole tracery of stone can be clearly seen: figures of saints, heraldic devices and marvellous pinnacles, none of which can be seen from below. The men who built it were a wonderful example of perseverance and perfection in little things: they spent days, months and years working on something which would very rarely be seen close up, because they were doing their duty in God’s presence.

7 Song 2:15

What you have to do, my son, is build a cathedral in your interior life, by polishing and carving away with a chisel… Now you understand the need and the beneficial effects of being very faithful to our plan of life, taking loving care of the norms of piety which leave us a wonderful autonomy, at the same time as they spur us onward. Caritas enim Christi urget nos! 8 – the love of Christ urges us on. Nobody can become a saint overnight. Holiness is the result of God’s grace and a constant effort to improve your interior life, toiling away as you have been trying to do for years now.

## The donkey at the water wheel {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.1.3}

In one of the Psalms there are some verses which always move me deeply: ut iumentum factus sum apud te, et ego semper tecum. I am like a little donkey before you, I tell our Lord, and you are always with me: that’s presence of God. Tenuisti manum dexteram meam. Translating it somewhat freely, I normally say to him: you have taken me by the bridle; et in voluntate tua deduxisti me, and you have helped me fulfil your will, that is, you have kept me faithful to my vocation. Et cum gloria suscepisti me, and afterwards you will give me a loving embrace.9 I have often watched a donkey at a water wheel, going round and round at an unchanging pace. It’s normally blinkered so it can only see the path in front of it. And so it proceeds along sturdily and humbly, always on the same trajectory, requiring very little. It seems to be totally unaware that the flowers in the garden, the aromas, all the produce, are the result of its apparently self-effacing, monotonous and unexciting work. That’s the way I want you to be, my son, just like that: constantly going round the water wheel of your life, producing sweet-smelling flowers and fruit, on the basis of little things, and always joyfully.

8 2 Cor 5:14 9 Cf. Ps 73:22-24

Don’t be afraid of boredom. Where there’s love, there’s no room for boredom. Do you remember your mother’s caresses when you were small? I’m sure you didn’t find them irksome or boring. When you love God as he should be loved, as you know he should be loved, and as you want to love him, then the fulfilment of your plan of life, your constant effort to conquer yourself, the humility of our way, which avoids all ostentation and anything strange or startling, acquires an ever new flavour. I think that the sort of “Lives of Saints” which speak only of extraordinary events and spectacular miracles do Christians a lot of harm. They tell us nothing about their interior life. But just like you and me, the saints had lots of defects and shortcomings. Those stories tell us nothing about their struggles, nor their defeats or victories. They don’t tell us that sometimes the saints trembled with fear; that they were men or women of flesh and blood. Such books make them seem like beings from another planet, and that’s not the case. The lives of the saints, as proper biographies should demonstrate, have been like yours and mine. They were God’s donkeys, they struggled, they toiled away, they suffered, they conquered… and were conquered themselves, but afterwards they got up quickly and went on fighting. They were people who paid loving attention to details. That’s the way to achieve holiness, and there isn’t any other. The perfect fulfilment of little things is of capital importance, because little infidelities are paralleled by big infidelities.

## A sporting attitude {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.1.4}

When someone wants to enter an athletics competition he first has to train, and he has to spend a long and arduous period repeating the same exercises continually. He practises self-denial, he abstains from certain drinks and from anything else which could affect his performance. He keeps to a very strict timetable. He goes back to the same exercises again and again. All these little things, these

gymnastic exercises, help him to get fit. Only if he trains like that can he hope to do well in the athletics competition.10 Well, the same happens in the spiritual life. It is impossible to win the prize in the great supernatural competition except by means of training. To take part in the competition without having trained is to be virtually certain of losing. Moreover, we shouldn’t discount the well-known military tactic of engaging the enemy on the terrain that suits us best. I remember when I went to Teruel, during the Spanish Civil War, to spend some time with a son of mine who had been stationed there. It was shortly after it had been recaptured by the troops who had previously lost it. From the ruins of the old seminary, along with the divisional commanders, we could see the whole scene. They were analysing the previous military operations, and they concluded that perhaps the mistake had been to concentrate all the troops inside the town. If they had occupied there, and there, and over there – they pointed out some strategic strong-points outside the town itself – perhaps they would have been beaten, but the defeats would not have left the town at the mercy of the enemy. It would have given time to send up reinforcements, and Teruel wouldn’t have fallen… It’s an elementary tactic. If you give battle to the enemy of your soul in the heroic minute of the morning, getting up on time and being punctual to the morning prayer; if you take him on when engaging in your professional work, when perhaps you don’t feel like studying, but you know you must because it is your duty, and you say, “Help me, Lord! Mother, help me!” and you set your elbows down on the table without making any concessions to yourself; if you try hard to have a kind word for someone you find irksome, or to overcome that youthful impetuosity which causes you to rush around and run up and down the stairs… then, even though

10 Cf. 1 Cor 9:24-27

you may often be beaten, the defeats will not be of any great importance, because they will have to do with issues which have no great moral significance. On the other hand, there will be many other occasions when you will conquer, and will conquer through love. These victories are very important, because the enemy will find it more difficult to pitch the battle in front of the main towers of the fortress of your soul. If he ever gets that far, since you have trained yourself to respond to God’s grace, and your will has been strengthened, you will repel him easily and effectively.

## The means to conquer {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.1.5}

When there is no struggle in little things the interior life grows feeble, and lukewarmness makes its appearance. While I am doing my prayer aloud, you too are meditating on your own. Let your heart break out in affections, and examine your present life to see if you are really struggling in little things. Because it would be a great pity if you weren’t zealous, if you didn’t stir up in your soul a genuine struggle for holiness. It would be a terrible sign of lukewarmness, and as you know, Scripture says that God will vomit the lukewarm out of his mouth.11 My children, do you want me to tell you how to acquire zeal for holiness in little things? Well, in the first place, through devotion to our Lady. Be pious. We children of God have to become very pious, sincerely devout. You have to foster a totally filial love for our Lady. Then you’ll be able to count on her wonderful help. Have you ever watched a little child trying to climb some stairs? After a lot of effort he manages to get up one step, and then another – like you – and then a third… and when he is just about to get to the landing, his legs buckle under him, he loses his balance, and rolls all the way down again. And what does he do then? He cries out at once for his mummy. And like a good mother, she answers: “My little love!” She takes him in her arms, kisses him… and seeing that he can’t do

11 Cf. Rev 3:16

any more, that he’s already done enough, it is she who carries him to the top. That’s what your heavenly Mother does for you. If you do what you can and have recourse to her, I am certain you will benefit even from your falls to make it to the top in the arms of our good Mother. Another important element is perseverance. Once, and again, and twenty, and a hundred times… Keep trying, optimistically. Today you couldn’t? Humble yourself, and tomorrow you will! Ask God for his grace, have recourse to your heavenly Mother, and persevere humbly. By yourself you’re nothing, you can’t do anything, you don’t possess anything, you don’t know anything… but with God’s grace you are omnipotent: omnia possum in eo qui me confortat!12 – you’ll be able to do it all. Another means to conquer is to have an apostolic motive for everything you do. Before each struggle, decide on an intention to offer it for, out of charity. I guarantee you it will bear fruit. In the morning, when it’s time to get up to do the prayer and go to work, and perhaps you’re feeling tired, have this supernatural thought: My God, this is for the person who needs it most, for the Pope, for the Father – I promise you, I need it a lot – for your brothers in the Work, for the holy souls in Purgatory, for your parents, brothers and sisters, for your friends… Be certain, my children, that there’s no such thing as an insignificant action. I told you so a moment ago. There is nothing which can be regarded as trivial or unimportant. All seeds tend to be equally small, but some produce annuals, plants that only last one year, whereas others produce trees that live for centuries. The seeds you sow along the pathway of your life will only be annuals if you want it that way, if you aren’t generous. On the other hand, if you are determined, if you really give yourself to God our Lord, if you conquer yourself for love of him, ready to adore him, to serve the Church, then those seeds will become leafy trees laden with fruit.

12 I can do all things in him who strengthens me: Phil 4:13

It’s time to finish our prayer. Without being tragic about it, we have to bear in mind that your life, and mine too, will end some day. We will present ourselves before God after having struggled a lot. May it be the case that at that moment Christ’s eyes will light up once more, and he will point to us and say: “This one has been loyal, he has worked well. He never denied me his love in little things. He sought holiness in ordinary work, and found it.” Euge, serve bone et fidelis, quia super pauca fuisti fidelis, super multa te constituam: intra in gaudium domini tui.13 The master’s words of praise for the faithful servant are the “formula of canonisation” used by our Lord: because you have been faithful over a little, I will set you over much; enter into the joy of your master.

13 Mt 25:21

# DIALOGUE WITH CHRIST * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.2}

Emitte lucem tuam et veritatem tuam.1 Send out your light and your truth: words of Psalm 43, verse three, which we recite every day.† What a contrast between the unreflective attitude of all those people out there, in this world of ours, and the recollection you are going to have today! We are no better than anybody else: it is just that God has chosen us. But see how human beings chase after earthly things, how much they long for fame, wealth, or sensual enjoyment. And this is true of both rich and poor, men and women, young, middle-aged and elderly: all of them! Well, we have to seek the things of the spirit with as much single-mindedness as others put into pursuing earthly goals, or even more. I repeat: we have to make at least that same effort in God’s service, in what has to do with the welfare of our own souls and the salvation of the world. I insist, my son: you and I have to resolve seriously not to overlook the least thing, either supernatural or human, which we can use in the Church’s service and our own sanctification. And for that to be so, the first thing we need is a life of faith, the conviction that Christ is alive: Iesus Christus heri et hodie, ipse et in saecula! 2

* Notes of a meditation given during a day of recollection in Madrid, 12 October 1947 (feast of Our Lady of the Pillar) 1 Ps 43:3 † The Psalm was formerly recited by the priest at the foot of the altar before he began to say Mass (Tr.) 2 Heb 13:8

Jesus Christ is the same yesterday and today and for ever. He is alive! He is for ever! With the same powers. What does it take, my son, for God to work wonders in your soul? Living faith! Operative faith, operative!

## Intimacy with God {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.2.1}

Do you recall how on more than one occasion I have reminded you of something we read in Holy Scripture? In summing up our Lord’s life, St Luke says: coepit Iesus facere et docere,3 Jesus began to do and teach. First he did, so that you and I might learn. Years ago I came across an old Japanese engraving, showing two amusing scenes. One showed a family, all gathered around a low table: father, mother and children, all wearing kimonos, almond-eyed and with fringes. Beside the table there was a pole twenty metres high, and on top of it a lighted lantern. The caption underneath read: “The presumptuous man.” From afar the light attracted attention, and those who saw it could have thought that it signalled the presence of a family atmosphere, whereas in fact they were in the dark. The other scene showed another family, also with their kimonos, fringes and almond eyes. On the table, close up, was a lantern. And the caption underneath read: “The discreet man.” From afar the light probably didn’t attract attention, but anyone who approached found there an atmosphere of intimacy and family warmth. What I want is for you to be supernaturally discreet, sincere with God and with yourself, without attracting attention: first of all seek Christ’s light for yourself, for your own life. Your faith should be a faith with deeds. And what are we going to do during this recollection? We are going to store up, as it were, the flowing waters, and make a reservoir which we can then have recourse to in times of drought,

3 Acts 1:1

when the bad days come along. That requires us to get to know Jesus more intimately. He is the model, and you have to be, not only alter Christus, but ipse Christus, Christ himself. Whether we like it or not, we human beings have an inclination to mimicry, to follow the examples we see and admire in others. There are people, for example, who do their best to look like a particular film star. It’s very common: they imitate their behaviour, their mannerisms, the way they dress… Something similar happens in intellectual circles. I recall now a certain institution which was particularly active in Spain in the twenties and thirties, which inspired and continues to inspire a group of individuals who cultivated a very friendly demeanour and a seemingly very attractive way of treating people, but who were driven by a rabid anticlericalism which has rarely been equalled. In all sectors that God has left to man’s free discussion – science, literature, the arts – people behave the same way. There are few really outstanding intellects, and equally few at the opposite extreme: most of us are somewhere in the middle. We cluster around the more illustrious figures who stand out from the rest; and as you know, it’s common to hear people say: “I’m for so-and-so on that issue;” and someone else will say: “Well, I’m for that other person.” This gives rise to schools of thought in scientific, artistic and literary circles. There’s always mimicry. Well, look, my son, given that you are going to be drawn to some individual who impresses you because of his stature, intellect, honesty, goodness, or attractiveness, be drawn to Christ, who is your King, your God, and your Love! Get to know him! Speak to him! Listen to him! Tell him you want to imitate him. And to do that, take up the Gospel every day, as is established in your plan of life, wanting to get to know him better. Become one of the people there, in that divine story, and react accordingly. Contemplate Christ’s miracles, take in the ebb and flow of the multitude around him, exchange words of friendship with the first Twelve… Look our

Lord in the eyes and fall in love with him, so that you become another Christ. Do you see, my children? We have to get to know Christ. Very often God places the most staggering truths on the lips of children! I remember Don Manuel González, the bishop of Palencia, who died with a reputation for holiness, a good friend of mine. He once told me a story he published in one of his newsletters. Once upon a time, while teaching catechism to a group of children during a parish visitation, he asked them: “Let’s see, why should we go to Holy Communion every day?” One little fellow jumps up, waving his dirty hand in the air. “Tell me,” says the bishop. And the child gives some hopelessly silly answer. “No, it’s not that.” Then another one gets up, with a runny nose, and suggests something equally wide of the mark. “No, not that either.” Finally, in a corner, a little gypsy boy cries out: “Bishop, Bishop, I know, I know!” “Go on then, tell us.” And in his Andalusian accent, the little fellow replies: “We have to go to Holy Communion every day, because to love him we have to ‘feel’ him.” What a wonderful answer from a little gypsy boy, a whole lesson in ascetical and mystical theology! To love Christ we have to “feel” him, we have to relate to him, talk and listen to him. I remind you of that now, in this first recollection of the academic year. My son, to fall more deeply in love with Christ, to love him truly, with all the affection of your heart – don’t be afraid to love him like that – you have to talk to him and listen to him. And you do that in the Bread and in the Word: the Sacred Host, at Holy Communion, and your constant daily prayer. But we’ll keep that topic for another day.

## Making an effort {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.2.2}

Whenever we do the prayer together, whenever someone reads from a book or the priest preaches to you, you can’t remain passive,

like a sack of potatoes or one of the benches in the oratory. You have to put something into it yourself. Do you remember the man St Luke tells us about, the one with the withered hand? He was paralysed, but our Lord commanded him: extende manum tuam!4 stretch out your hand. Et extendit, et restituta est manus eius,5 he held out his hand, and it was restored to life. But he had to make an effort! If he hadn’t, he wouldn’t have been healed. You, my son, so as not to be inactive in everyday life, so as not to be supernaturally sterile, so as to work for Christ, for his holy Church, you need to have your arms unencumbered, free, full of vigour. Jesus says to you now something he has said many times before: extende manum tuam! But you have to want to; you have to make the effort which, with God’s grace, enables you to overcome the concupiscence of the flesh, to vanquish pride, to overcome your paralysing laziness which, as soon as you take your eye off it, pulls you down to a bourgeois existence. God is so good that he understands how at times someone isn’t able to make even that much effort. That is why he has wished to record another of his miracles in the holy Gospel, so we can imitate it. I am referring to the sick woman St Luke mentions in chapter thirteen of his Gospel. This unfortunate woman was crippled, unable to lift her eyes from the ground. But she placed herself where our Lord’s gaze could fall upon her. Let’s do the same: let’s place ourselves in front of Jesus, who is presiding over us in the tabernacle, and say: “Lord, here I am before you! Lord, look upon me!” Scripture tells us that the woman habebat spiritum infirmitatis annis decem et octo,6 she had had a spirit of infirmity for eighteen years. Poor lady! And you and I, how long have we been ill, with

4 Lk 6:10 5 Ibid. 6 Lk 13:11

the loathsome rash of our defects, of our bad habits, which we are unable to shake off? How many years have we been that way, my son? Isn’t it now time to get rid of it all, to really start to improve? Erat inclinata.7 That poor crippled woman was unable to look at the stars. In her bent-over condition, all she could see was the filthy mud of this world. Doesn’t something similar happen to you and me? At times don’t we see things from a temporal point of view instead of from the viewpoint of eternity, the viewpoint of an apostle? Are not we too bent over? In her case, nec omnino poterat sursum respicere:8 there was no way she could raise her head up to heaven. Quam cum videret Iesus, when Jesus saw her, vocavit eam ad se, he called her and said to her, mulier, dimissa es ab infirmitate tua, woman, you are freed from your infirmity. Et imposuit illi manus, et confestim erecta est et glorificabat Deum,9 he laid his hands upon her, and immediately she was made straight, and she praised God. My children, here we are in the presence of our God, before our good Jesus. O Jesus, hidden in the tabernacle, look at us! Turn your eyes toward us, because we want to live fully upright lives, looking up at the sky, at heaven, following your commandments and your Will, seeing everything with the eyes of eternity. My children, glorificate Deo, glorify God and bear him with you always, sharing in the Church’s desire to bring him souls.

## A rose for our Lady {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.2.3}

I don’t know why, but I am reminded of something I heard years ago. In a certain seminary there was a rector who was a very austere man and whom everybody had great respect for. In springtime, on feast days of our Lady, he used to go to the garden and pick a big

7 She was bent over: ibid. 8 Ibid. 9 Lk 13:12-13

bouquet of roses, and would then go around the rooms knocking on each door. When the seminarian opened the door he would hand him a rose, saying: “Here, offer this rose to our Lady.” The young man would be deeply moved. But other times, when one of them, through lack of discipline or study, hadn’t been a good son to our Lady, the rector would knock on the door but when it opened he would hug the roses to his breast and say: “Today you can’t give our Lady a rose.” This shouldn’t happen to you, my son. Even in the middle of winter, when here in Madrid the snow falls heavily, even then you have to offer our Lady a rose. Do you understand? Although your soul may feel like ground covered with the ice of winter, if you persevere lovingly in the fulfilment of your practices of Christian piety, be certain that in due course you will gather a lot of fruit. But it is important to make use of the moments when we feel more fervent. When we see clearly that it is worth while denying ourselves one day after another, one month after another, one year after another, all our life long, because Love is awaiting us afterwards in heaven, what insights we receive! All that has to be stored up, my dearest children. What we have to do is to create a reservoir in our souls to collect together all these graces from God: brightness, light, the sweetness of self-giving. And when you meet darkness, night, bitterness, that’s the moment to throw yourself into those clear waters of God’s grace. And then, even though at that moment you are blind, you will see; although you are dry, you will feel refreshed by the waters that rise from the Heart of Christ unto eternal life. If you are habitually recollected, if you normally try to refer everything to God our Lord, making your life a continuous prayer; if you are careful to perceive the light God sends in the intimacy of your soul, then on days of recollection you should try to do so even more. To do this, try to keep a strict silence. Don’t forget that

“silence is the door-keeper of the interior life”,10 and apply your senses and faculties to the topics covered in the talks and meditations. At the time for the examination of conscience, call to mind your own experience. Let your mind draw conclusions and let your will make practical resolutions. Look for mortifications in little things, and from now on do what you can so that even your senses – the point of entry for the things which distract you and draw you away from God – may provide you with an opportunity and a stimulus to persevere in your life of prayer. What else? Have recourse to our Mother the Blessed Virgin, especially today, bringing her not just a single rose, but many. Invoke her, implore her protection with lots of faith. Have recourse too to St Joseph, our Father and Lord, who is the patron of the interior life and principal patron of Opus Dei. Try to be on good terms with your holy Guardian Angel. In 1939 the Archbishop of Valencia, the predecessor of the present one, invited me to give some retreats in his diocese. One of them, the first I think, took place in Burjasot, for quite a large group of young university students. In every corner of the building there was evidence of its previous occupation by the Republican troops. When I arrived they were trying to tidy the place up and get it ready. They were about to take down a large placard which had been placed over a door, reading: “Let each wayfarer follow his own path.” I stopped them, and said: “Leave it – I like it. Learn from the enemy, as the saying goes.” After all, it was a good piece of advice. You, in the company of your Guardian Angel, follow your own path. You’re starting a new academic year in your studies; you’re starting a new year as sons of God in his Work, here in this Centre of Studies. Follow your path! Your path as a student and an apostle, your very own. Each wayfarer to his own path; you follow yours!

10 The Way, 281

The Archangel Raphael is the special patron of the young people who are in contact with the Work. The Book of Tobit is enchanting. When Tobias the younger is about to begin his journey to the house of Gabael, in order to recover the money they were owed, he kneels down before his father. And the patriarch places his hands on him and blesses him: Dominus sit in itinere tuo, et angelus eius comitetur tecum,11 may the Lord be on your path, and may his Angel go with you. This is the formula I chose as the blessing for a journey, adding to it an invocation to our Lady: Beata Maria intercedente, bene ambules: et Dominus sit in itinere tuo, et Angelus eius comitetur tecum. [Through our Lady’s intercession, may you have a good journey: and may the Lord be on your path, and may his Angel go with you.] On this journey which you are now beginning, may you always take God with you, and may his angel accompany you.

11 Cf. Tob 5:21

# THE ANNUNCIATION * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.3}

“The angel Gabriel was sent from God to a city of Galilee named Nazareth, to a virgin betrothed to a man whose name was Joseph.”1 The Blessed Virgin dwelt in Nazareth, and it was there that God sent his angel. Enter into the scene and reflect on our Mother’s greatness. When a head of State wants to deliver an important message to the leader of another country, he sends a representative, an ambassador. When God wants to send a message to the most excellent of created beings, who was to become the Mother of Jesus, he doesn’t entrust the mission to any angel at all, but selects the Archangel Gabriel.

## The greatness of ordinary life {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.3.1}

From the first pages of the Gospel, Mary gives us some wonderful and very specific lessons. In the feast the Church celebrates today, we see her filled with humility, leading a normal everyday life. Her soul was all-beautiful: greater than she, only God. Yet she remains hidden, unnoticed. Hidden not because she is concealing some secret, but rather in the sense that she doesn’t do anything strange or startling: her life is completely ordinary, like any normal person. She lives just like any other woman in the village of Nazareth, although she is in fact completely exceptional, the only human being ever to be conceived without original sin, the

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 25 March 1954, feast of the Annunciation 1 Lk 1:26-27

only one never to be subject to the power of the devil: tota pulchra, Immaculate. And in spite of being so exalted, she avoids any sort of ostentation and tries not to display to strangers the great gifts she has received from God. Isn’t this a wonderful example for Christian women and men of all time, and in particular for the daughters and sons of God in Opus Dei, who live in the middle of the world among our fellow human beings, as one of them, without parading the calling we have received, our vocation to holiness? What I mean is that we have to avoid any silly childish exhibition of our virtues; while on the other hand, our condition as committed Christians, disciples of Christ, ought to be immediately evident wherever we happen to be. St Luke tells us that our Lady was espoused to a man named Joseph of the royal house of David. He was a young man, a just man.2 When our Lord became incarnate and was made man, he needed to be cared for, to be raised in a family environment, like all children. He needed not only a mother, but also a man who would fulfil the role of father to him, and would show him all the affection and kindness that only fathers can give. That is why God chose St Joseph to be, as many prayers describe him, the guardian of his Mother’s virginity and foster father of Jesus. It makes me so happy to address the Holy Patriarch as my Father and Lord, and to foster devotion to him in my children and in all souls!

## Full of grace {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.3.2}

“The virgin’s name was Mary”,3 a name which forever fills the mouth with the taste of honey from the comb. “In Hebrew it means ‘star of the sea’ and in Syriac ‘lady’. And rightly so, because she was worthy to bear in her womb the Lord of the world and the everlasting light of eternity.”4

2 Cf. Mt 1:19 3 Lk 1:27 4 St Bede the Venerable, Commentary on St Luke’s Gospel, 1 (PL 92, 316)

We read in the Gospel: “He came to her and said, ‘Hail, O full of grace, the Lord is with you.’”5 He could have greeted her in many different ways, but he did so by reminding her of what for a Christian soul is the most important thing: the fact that she is gratia plena, the person who enjoys the closest friendship with God out of all created beings, because she is full of his grace. God is constantly granting you his gifts. Besides redeeming you and making you his daughter or son by baptism, besides providing you with the other sacraments, channels of grace, he has bestowed on you a very great treasure, the treasure of your vocation, the divine call to Opus Dei; and if you correspond faithfully to those graces, he constantly gives you yet more help. My daughter, my son, let me ask you: Do you love God’s grace? You will say yes, that you are doing everything you can to never lose it. I know you are, but are you trying to make sure that it actually grows in your soul every day? Do you try to respond to his inspirations with complete generosity? There are some words of Christ in the Gospel which are very clear but which sometimes trouble me: “For to him who has will more be given; and from him who has not, even what he has will be taken away.”6 If you and I respond to grace, like our Mother, God will enable us to respond even more; if we try to be faithful to our vocation, he will give us more help at every moment and make us holier every day. Let us make a firm resolution then, to thank God for all the gifts he has given us. This will lead us never to allow our souls to be stained even slightly, and to hate venial sin with all our strength. One small blemish on your face doesn’t really matter, perhaps, but two hundred, five hundred, a thousand… would look dreadful! You and I have to foster the good disposition of never wanting to offend our Lord deliberately by venial sin. We must hate deliberate venial sin

5 Lk 1:28 6 Mk 4:25

so much that we are always determined to reject even the slightest thing that could be an obstacle to his love. This is what is meant by really loving God’s grace, the sovereign treasure that comes to us from heaven. Full of grace! Our Lady gives us a lesson in how to love, how to respond. Dominus tecum! The Lord is with you. With God’s help we too will always be close to him and never wander away, since we rely on our heavenly Mother’s protection.

## Praising Mary {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.3.3}

Benedicta tu in mulieribus…7 Blessed are you among women, the Archangel greets her. Every day we address our Mother frequently like that, and we are greatly comforted by the thought that she is there with her Son in heaven, giving a supernatural meaning to our lives and watching over each of us: we thank her for it and implore her always to look after us. At the same time we realise that many people are quite unaware of our heavenly Mother’s existence. Many people who regard themselves as Christians, and who even seem to be seeking God’s glory, don’t realise that they are children of our Blessed Lady! What a pity! Do you want to cast an eye over your own behaviour? In Opus Dei, along with divine filiation, we are deeply conscious of our filiation to Mary. How well do you look after the Marian Norms and Customs for me? How well do you try to fulfil the delightful details of love that each one enshrines? How hard do you try, how much filial affection do you put into them? How much do you try to please her who is the channel of all the graces coming from her Son to succour your brothers and sisters, the Work, the Holy Church, and the entire human race?

7 Lk 1:28 (Vg); cf. 1:42

“But she was greatly troubled at the saying, and considered in her mind what sort of greeting this might be.”8 Faced with these great happenings from heaven, Mary in her humility is troubled. How humble she is, the Immaculate, the treasure of graces: gratia plena! She is so humble that at first sight she cannot understand why God should send her such an important messenger. Consider the way you react when some trivial event upsets you, not out of humility but out of pride. You think you are being overlooked, or humiliated, or treated with suspicion, or your qualities are not being taken into account. All this upsets you and takes away your peace of mind. You are children of our Blessed Lady, and you ought to be humble, because children should resemble their mothers. Haven’t you noticed how children resemble their mothers or fathers? Very often they have the same facial features, the same figure, the same way of speaking, of looking, of thinking and even of dressing. It is important for you to resemble our Lady; to be such good children that you make friends with our Lady. Then you will be able to ask her to give you the family spirit of Opus Dei: a great love for Mary, Mother of Fair Love, Mother of God.

## Humility and purity {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.3.4}

While I speak to you I am doing my own meditation aloud, and I’m telling her: O Mother of ours, may all your children in Opus Dei learn to live in humility. Teach us to avoid the turmoil that proceeds from pride. If only we were humble, how happy we would be! I have deliberately harped a lot on the idea, including in this oratory, that ninety-five per cent of the problems that assail men and women are the product of our imagination or our pride. If we are humble, if you are humble, you will be the happiest people on earth, each of you.

8 Lk 1:29

Ask our Lady, as I do for myself, to give you light, insight, so that throughout your life you will never be upset by your wretched pride, so that at least you will recognise right away that such upsets don’t come from God. Whatever robs us of peace, whatever upsets us, doesn’t come from God. So if you ever lose your peace, run to the feet of your Mother, the Blessed Virgin Mary. Go to your confessor, to spiritual guidance, and speak sincerely and humbly. You will then experience a great wave of peace and happiness. It will also be a sure sign that you love God’s grace and are on the path to holiness, because anyone who is struggling to achieve holiness is by that same token happy. As I have reminded you repeatedly, if you want to be happy, be holy; if you want to be even happier, be even holier; and if you want to be very happy, be very holy; that is, seek God and forget about yourself. When the Angel sees that our Lady is troubled, he reassures her: “Do not be afraid, Mary, for you have found favour with God.”9 I am deeply moved by these expressions of God’s thoughtfulness. In the pages of Holy Scripture we often find words of divine encouragement, uttered by God himself and by heavenly beings to raise man up from his wretchedness and prepare him for a dialogue of enlightenment and love, total confidence, when faced with situations that seem difficult or impossible, for which human strength is insufficient. Having put our Lady at her ease, the angel Gabriel continues: “Behold, you will conceive in your womb and bear a son, and you shall call his name Jesus. He will be great, and will be called the Son of the Most High; and the Lord God will give to him the throne of his father David, and he will reign over the house of Jacob for ever; and of his kingdom there will be no end.”10 She is going to be the Mother of God, of Christ the King!

9 Lk 1:30 10 Lk 1:31-33

Mother of God! How often in life, in meditation, spiritual reading, study, or your revision of the catechism of Christian doctrine, you have been struck by the beauty of the love for holy purity which shines forth from this passage of our Lady’s life. The angel assures her that the fruit of her womb will be the Son of God, and she asks: “How shall this be, since I have no husband?” And the angel said to her, “The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; therefore the child to be born will be called holy, the Son of God.”11 It is the great miracle of our Lady’s divine Motherhood: while remaining a virgin, she becomes the Mother of the Son of God. My children, rather than just relying on the help I provide for you with my words, you should meditate on this indescribable scene for yourselves. Contemplate it in every detail, with your eyes closed. If you’ve ever been in the oratory of Molinoviejo, you will recall the painting I placed there of our Mother tota pulchra, all pure, as she receives the message the Archangel brings her. United to Mary’s prayer, tell her: Make us Christians, your children, your children in Opus Dei, really love holy purity, each in our own state, and make our hearts free from the slightest attachment that is at variance with your purity.

## Mary’s response {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.3.5}

Gabriel has delivered his message. God himself seems to be on tenterhooks awaiting the consent which is to issue from the will and lips of that pure and humble Virgin. Those who are indeed on tenterhooks are you and I, the whole human race. St Bernard expresses it very well when he writes: “This the whole world is waiting for, kneeling at your feet. And rightly so, for on your lips is hanging the consolation of the wretched, the redemption of the

11 Lk 1:34-35

captive, the speedy deliverance of all who otherwise are lost; in a word, the salvation of all Adam’s children, of all your race.”12 The Holy Trinity has disposed, in its all-wise plan, that Christ will not come down to earth until our Lady gives her consent; the Son of God awaits that moment to become incarnate and redeem the world. The Archangel tells her that it will be caused by an extraordinary intervention from heaven, a wonderful divine work. The Incarnation of the Word will take place in a miraculous way, because, as we learned in the catechism when we were small, the power of God will pass through her as light passes through glass, without breaking or staining it. Then Mary replies: ecce ancilla Domini; fiat mihi secundum verbum tuum,13 behold, I am the handmaid of the Lord; let it be done to me according to your word. “Behold her humility, behold her devotion”, says St Ambrose. “She calls herself handmaid of the Lord, who has been chosen to be his Mother.”14 And at the enchantment of those virginal words, “the Word became flesh and dwelt among us”:15 heaven’s greatest miracle was worked for the sake of this earth. My children, we are descendants of Adam and Eve and, like all human beings, as a result of the sin of our first parents, we are attracted to the things of here below. But we are also dearly beloved children of our holy Mother Mary. If you make up your minds to say “fiat!” every day, what great feats, what wonderful things you will accomplish throughout the world, with God’s grace! When I consecrated the Work to the Most Sweet Heart of Mary, in the little house of Nazareth venerated in Loreto, I felt myself to be very much your Father. I went there in the name of all my daughters and sons, in difficult times in the history of our Opus Dei, in proof of devotion, love and confidence in our Mother the Blessed

12 St Bernard, Homilies in praise of the Virgin Mother, 4, 8 13 Lk 1:38 14 St Ambrose, Commentary on St Luke’s Gospel, 2, 16 15 Jn 1:14

Virgin. There, when they say the Angelus, they say: hic Verbum caro factum est:16 here the Word was made flesh. You can see it written on the altar. And Christian souls are deeply moved by the thought that in those lowly surroundings, in that house built by human hands, there took place the great mystery of the Incarnation, which is the greatest treasure of our holy faith and the beginning of our Redemption. Ecce ancilla! For love of her and to recall the power of our heavenly Mother, who made the Redemption possible with a few words, I have placed on my daughters’ lips the constant invocation: Sancta Maria, Spes nostra, Ancilla Domini. Because if you see yourselves as servants and handmaids of God, you will have our heavenly Mother’s spirit, you will love God’s grace, and you will have a holy zeal to carry out great deeds on earth by means of little things. In a word, you will feel the need to be very faithful to our Lord and to work in his name. This fidelity is only attained by fulfilling your plan of life. If you always do that, you will acquire a happiness – genuine fidelity – which, in the middle of your daily trials and struggles, will help you love our Christian vocation and travel gracefully along the path on which God has placed you.

## Fruits of holiness {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.3.6}

I know well, my children, that if you consider carefully what I’m telling you, if you meditate on it on your own, if you take these scenes from the Gospel to heart, set them before your mind’s eye, and talk to our Mother about them, you will feel the need to open your soul wide to her and reveal to her the depths of your heart, the motives for your actions. You will discover things in your life that need to be set right, and others that are going well but could go better. If you deepen your relationship with our Lady you will

16 Cf. ibid.

receive new lights, and learn many good things. You will acquire greater supernatural vision for your journey along this path of Opus Dei. And you will ask our heavenly Mother to grant the light and grace that pass from her Son’s Heart through her hands, to all who form part of this great family. And then, since you will be better, and I will try to be so too, the Work will be more fruitful every day. There will be more vocations, and more zeal for holiness in all the sons and daughters of God in Opus Dei, more joy in our daily work, more extensive apostolates: new facets, new colours, for this divine, radiant impulse that Opus Dei is in the world. Think of how many great things depend on you, on whether you want to be God’s instrument and not an obstacle; on whether you are in constant conversation with your heavenly Mother, on whether you contemplate her and ardently desire to learn from her, because Mary is Mother and Teacher. The Archangel Gabriel greeted her: benedicta tu in mulieribus,17 blessed are you among women. We Christians often say this, when we say the Hail Mary. I would really like you to acclaim her by your deeds, your daily conduct, in such a way that she feels blessed by all Christian men and women, and especially by those who have given themselves to God in a holocaust of love. God’s grace is abundant, and our Lady is omnipotent in her intercession. When she asks God for something her prayer is always heard; therefore she is called “all-powerful suppliant.” Well, our great Lady and our Mother, the Virgin Mary, who has always been the protector of Opus Dei, will be so until the end. If we don’t leave her, she won’t leave us. Therefore, my daughter, my son, fight to correct your shortcomings, and continue forward on your way. Love our Lady madly, and everything will go better. And your apostolic work, and

17 Lk 1:28 (Vg); cf. 1:42

that of all my daughters and sons in Opus Dei all over the world in the service of the Church, will be more beautiful every day.

# PRAYER OF THE SENSES * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.4}

I have often told you that the prescriptions of our particular law are holy, perpetual and inviolable. We love them with all our heart because they are the path, the way willed by God for his children in Opus Dei to become holy. In them, both the juridical structure of our Work and also the spirit God gave me can be clearly seen: a path which is as old and as new as the Gospel. Although to many it may appear quite novel in the life of the Church, and in a certain sense it is, I assure you that everything in Opus Dei is evangelical: it is all rooted in the perennial youth of the Gospel. How often have I told you that if you are not contemplatives you cannot persevere in Opus Dei! We are called to be contemplative souls in the middle of earthly activities. And truly contemplative people have to mortify their senses, to resemble Jesus, fixed to the wood of the Cross with three nails. In this meditation we will examine our consciences on this point, to improve our contemplative spirit.

## Need for mortification {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.4.1}

Amen, amen, dico vobis, nisi granum frumenti cadens in terram mortuum fuerit, ipsum solum manet,1 truly, truly, I say to you, unless a grain of wheat, once cast to the earth, dies, it remains

* Notes of a meditation given during a retreat in Rome, 13 April 1954 1 Jn 12:24

unfruitful. It remains alone! Si autem mortuum fuerit, multum fructum affert, but if it dies, it bears much fruit. “Unless a grain of wheat dies…” If we live for God, as I was saying, we ought to mortify our senses and keep them under control, because we also have to reach God in that way, to the point of the total self-giving which is death. Without fear! We won’t be afraid of death if we die to ourselves little by little at every moment of our lives: cotidie morior,2 says St Paul. Death is the common destiny of mankind. I won’t try to dwell on the idea – which is a truism – that one year more means one year less. But it is certainly true that some people ignore the fact of death, while others live in fear of it. In our case death is not something we should be anxious about, we don’t see it as a lurking threat. As I wrote very early on, and I remind you again now, “If you are an apostle, death for you will be a good friend who helps you on your way.”3 For others it may perhaps be the end; for us, please God, it will mean the beginning of true Life. In other words, all it means is moving house, making a graceful leap into heaven. I don’t want to speak to you now about death, but about mortification. What we’re more interested in is dying to ourselves little by little by means of the continual mortification of our disordered appetites. It may appear paradoxical, but it is absolutely essential: if we really want to live, we have to die. We have to die if we want to live a supernatural life, a life of prayer. In the last analysis, mortification is another form of prayer. Mortification is the prayer of the senses: given that they so often cause us to offend God, let’s use them to serve him too! The purpose of mortification – internal and external, active and passive – is to help us be detached from anything that doesn’t relate to God, and be united to him by obeying his holy Will, so that it is

2 I die every day: 1 Cor 15:31 3 The Way, 735

not we who live but God who lives in us.4 There are many things which it is lawful for us to enjoy. But if we renounce them at least from time to time, we are beginning to detach ourselves from the things of this world and to strengthen our poor human weakness. We thereby begin to acquire a will-power which, with God’s grace, will enable us to carry out as perfectly as possible the things he asks of us, acquiring essential practice in our ability to say no. This exercise, this dominion over our passions, will teach us to accept God’s will more readily. Mortification of the will has many aspects and many consequences. One is to strengthen our weak human will, so that Christ really does live in us. Another is purification, so completely necessary because of our faults and evil inclinations, and because on this earthly journey we are proni ad peccatum, inclined to evil. Another aspect of mortification, or another consequence if you wish, is the desire to make atonement to God our Lord for our own sins and for the sins of all mankind, past, present and future. God loves the human race, he has shed his blood to save us, and in spite of this extraordinary divine reality, how few people think about him, how few friends he has! We, who have received a special divine calling, together with a very special grace, have to increase our zeal for reparation every day, and direct to God all the joys and honours we can, with all the strength of our poor human hearts, in a loving spirit of expiation. The fruit of this self-mastery is spiritual progress, our own and other people’s, because by our voluntary acts of renunciation, by mortifying our flesh, our senses, and our curiosity, we show our love for Christ, desiring to be always centred on him qui dilexit me et tradidit semetipsum pro me,5 who loved us and gave himself up to death on the Cross for us, for love of us.

4 Cf. Gal 2:19-20 5 Gal 2:20

## Habitual mortifications {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.4.2}

Mortification is so important for us, so essential a part of our effectiveness, that many specific details and norms regarding its practice are set out in our Ius particulare. On our day on guard, a day when we are particularly vigilant and attentive to the holiness of others, we should offer God, among other things, an extraordinary mortification. Then there is a daily mortification for the Father, which is an expression of love, a response to the Father’s own love for his children. It should never be done out of routine or without feeling! Don’t omit it. I find it hard to imagine that you could ever forget it, because, although I myself am no example, I never forget about you, and I think the same will be true of those who come after me. But if you ever do forget it, say a prayer for the Father before going to bed. And the mortification offered to our Blessed Lady on Saturdays. And those who are sick are asked to offer their sufferings with love. We also stress the importance of mortification for safeguarding holy purity and for apostolate… My children, the Work has forged ahead on the basis of prayer and mortification. At the beginning we had no resources at all. There was nothing but youth, cheerfulness, determination, and the total conviction (insofar as human weakness allows) that we were doing God’s will; and all that was anointed with lots of prayer and penance. I remember that whenever I was going to talk to someone about their possible vocation to Opus Dei, since I really wanted God to grant them that grace, and I really wanted my words to find a response in that soul, I used to apply the discipline energetically, until the blood flowed. It wouldn’t be right to do things like that any more: times have changed and there are other mortifications. But if I had to found the Work again, if I had to begin once more, I would do it all over again. And you for your part, in your own apostolate, in order to win abundant grace from God, ought to mortify yourselves too, with the local Director’s permission. As I wrote for

you years ago: “A member of the Work who proposes to win a new vocation, offers extraordinary mortifications, and asks others to do likewise, convinced that of himself he can do nothing if he doesn’t obtain abundant grace from heaven by means of prayer and sacrifices.”6 We can’t ever lose this style of ours of acting, which is so deeply rooted in the Gospel and so effective! Mortification, my children, mortification. I can’t conceive of holiness without mortification. We have to lose our fear of penance. But you shouldn’t practise corporal mortifications imprudently, because it could damage your health. If you were all alone in the desert, like one of those old-time anchorites, perhaps it would be all right to act like that and allow your body to waste away little by little, as a holocaust offered to God. But that’s not the way we do things. In our case God has called us to serve him in the middle of the world, through our work; and so we have to keep our bodies in a fit state to work a lot for God. I don’t like penances that involve eating less, especially at your age. You give God a lot of glory by eating well, as much as you need, but including among the ingredients of the meal – as I spelled out for you years ago – the salt of mortification, and not being afraid of hard work. The body needs to be treated prudently. To do otherwise, I repeat, to the point of becoming weak or even ill, goes against the spirit God gave me and which is expressed in many aspects of our particular law. You can’t imagine how much I had to fight to defend it when I came to Rome in 1946! When Don Alvaro came to negotiate the papal approval of the Work, he was told we had come a century too soon. Eventually, when I presented our Ius particulare in the Holy See, some people commented on the fact that mention was made of penance: they said that that sort of thing wasn’t done any more. But I insisted, and it was approved, because I want it to be clear to my children throughout the centuries that the foundation of our life is prayer and mortification.

6 Instruction, 1 April 1934, 12

Mortification has to be looked for in small, everyday things: in work that is intense, constant and methodical. Little things that won’t harm our health but will keep our love of God alight. Mortifications at meals. Heroic minutes throughout the day. Punctuality. Tidiness. Guarding our sight on the street, without doing anything odd, which isn’t part of the spirit of the Work. We have to walk naturally; and when there is something harmful, or which could cause our love to grow cold, then we have to “see” without “looking.” Dozens and dozens of features and opportunities to take advantage of! I want to emphasise that we have to learn to sacrifice ourselves, not in spectacular outward demonstrations, but in the humble little things of every day; by eating a little less of something we like more, and a little more of what we like less, including among the ingredients of the meal the particularly tasty one of temperance; sitting or standing in a less comfortable posture, without doing anything peculiar… And don’t forget that a mortification which is very pleasing to God, very typical of the path created by Opus Dei, and which is always within our reach, is to be found in professional work carried out as perfectly as possible, including humanly. All this, for the love of God, is a very pleasing prayer to our heavenly Father.

## Like the beating of the heart {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.4.3}

Don’t forget either that among our habitual mortifications the first priority has to be given to passive mortifications, the ones we meet unexpectedly. Very often difficulties are far too subjective. We each find as many difficulties as we want. People who dwell in God have few, because whenever they encounter something objectively hard, they surrender to God’s will, ask for light to do the right thing, and that’s that. So when there seem to be lots of difficulties, we have to ask ourselves whether we are short on mortifications of our imagination, short on supernatural outlook. If you unite the

mortification in your work which I talked about before, with passive mortification, and offer it all happily to our Lord, you will live the life of a true and faithful Christian. You will learn to offer the active and the passive penances to God humbly and devoutly, without silly sentimentalism. One particularly good mortification is interior mortification: ensuring that our conversations don’t always centre on ourselves, always being ready to greet anything unpleasant with a smile, so as to make life pleasant for other people. Remember the Apostle’s advice: “Bear one another’s burdens, and so fulfil the law of Christ.”7 You must always try particularly hard to make life pleasant for others and avoid mortifying one another. You should say to yourself: I will willingly put myself out a little, to make other people’s path to God more attractive. My children, we have to die little by little, through continuous mortification in a thousand little things. And this shouldn’t in any way frighten us, because it should be as natural as our heartbeat. I don’t notice my heart beating right now, but it is. God help me the day it stops! I’ll say the same to you: in your spiritual life take care of the life of the heart, its constant beating, which means an effort to mortify yourself at every instant, and to maintain a loving conversation with our Lord, our Lady, St Joseph, and the Guardian Angels. Love, my son! Self-sacrificing love! Because human love too, when it is genuine, means sacrifice, self-denial, and joyful mortification. And after the ordinary mortifications come the extraordinary ones, with the Director’s permission, at those times, which are so easily recognised, when God asks us for more. You should always ask your Director for permission, because he is the one to moderate them. But moderating them doesn’t always mean lessening them. It also means increasing them if he thinks it appropriate. Your

7 Gal 6:2

discipline can be either like stroking yourself with cotton wool, or good and tough; you can wear the cilice, the chain, more tightly or less, but the corporal mortifications should never be omitted, except for medical reasons. Here I am, Lord, at your service!

## The root of effectiveness {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.4.4}

Our particular law explains how apostolic effectiveness is based on prayer and penance. The children of God in Opus Dei cultivate a spirit of mortification and penance, with a smiling asceticism, as a means of purification and of true and solid spiritual progress; they are a practical expression of love for Jesus Christ, qui dilexit me et tradidit semetipsum pro me, 8 who loved me and gave himself up for me; and finally, they are a preparation and a means for the perfect carrying out of all apostolate. Because, to be apostles, we have to carry Christ crucified within us, as St Paul describes.9 Our mortification has to be real and concrete, not imaginary. I don’t believe at all in the spirit of penance of someone who is ready to suffer martyrdom in his mind, but who gets indignant and upset at the first setback, at some little humiliation. How many people have I known like that! That is why I want to stress, my children, that the spirit of penance is not something one just talks about, nor is it present in someone who does great penances some days and forgets about it altogether on others. A true spirit of penance is seen in the ability to conquer oneself every day, offering things for love without showing off. That indeed is a love full of sacrifice, a pure love that does not look for applause. Love mortification and penance, for love’s sake, without seeking human consolations. And I come back to where we started: nisi granum frumenti cadens in terram mortuum fuerit, ipsum solum manet…10 Like the grain of wheat, my children, we need to die to be

8 Gal 2:20 9 Cf. 1 Cor 2:2 10 Unless a grain of wheat falls into the earth and dies, it remains alone…: Jn 12:24

fruitful. You and I don’t want to remain alone. We want to multiply our family and leave behind a furrow that is bright and deep. We must leave the animal man behind and launch out into the fields of the spirit, giving a supernatural meaning to all human affairs and, at the same time, to the people engaged in them. We are not alone in this task. Consider that you are – and I am – a child of God. God loves me more than all the mothers in the world together can love their children. And he is omnipotent and allknowing. Therefore everything that happens to me is for the best. Omnia in bonum!11 This is the way contemplative souls behave. If ever you encounter great trials, if you have the impression that your head is about to explode and your heart about to break, when you feel totally misunderstood and when God seems to have forgotten you, say slowly, calmly, savouring it, the prayer I taught you: Fiat, adimpleatur, laudetur atque in aeternum superexaltetur iustissima atque amabilissima Voluntas Dei super omnia. Amen. Amen. [May the most just and lovable Will of God be done, be fulfilled, be praised and exalted above all things forever. Amen. Amen.] I assure you that you will find God’s company in the midst of the greatest desolation. Faith, my children, faith in our Father God! I hope that nobody in Opus Dei ever experiences the bitterness of being alone, because you, my beloved children, have a Father and you have brothers and sisters. In my case – it was very good for my soul – our Lord allowed me to taste that loneliness so that I could pass on better, more strongly, the spirit of charity that imbues the Work. But I don’t want it to happen to you: nobody should ever feel alone! We are children of God and we are committed to an undertaking of holiness and apostolate in which we all feel very united to one another.

11 Cf. Rom 8:28

## Cheerful self-giving {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.4.5}

Mortification doesn’t mean more or less putting up with things we find difficult, and neither does it mean undertaking sacrifices bitterly, with no inner joy – such sacrifices would very often be sterile. What really matters is to offer our little daily difficulties or acts of self-denial with Love. Let us do everything for Love, my dearest children, and then our lives, however humble they are, will expand in every direction. My son, I’m sure you’ve seen what a mother’s love is like. You’ve witnessed their sacrifices, their self-denial, their anxiety and sleepless nights beside the cot of a sick child. Do you think that the children, with all their self-centredness and forgetfulness, really deserve such care? Then think of Christ’s love for us in bringing us to Opus Dei. Do you think we deserve it? Do you try to do at least as much for him as human beings do when they truly love someone? I call him Jesus, uninhibitedly, when I’m alone. Here before the tabernacle I’m not ashamed to call him by his name. My son, tell him that you love him too, that you’ll always love him. More and more all the time! Tell him that you love him now, while you are young, with all the strength of your heart, with all your passions, which are alive and sometimes a little savage! Tell him so especially now, and afterwards too, and express this affection with deeds just when you find it hard, because it’s always the right time to love him.
