Tell him: “Jesus, I love you, I love you with all my heart!” Don’t be ashamed to tell him so face to face, and out loud; I do it too. You have seen a child when his father asks him for some of the sweet he is eating. At first he resists. But then he gives it to him, and in response to his father’s smile, he puts his hand in his pocket and offers him everything he has, as if it were a treasure: some shiny round pebbles, an empty cotton-reel, a jackstone… And his father hugs him delightedly. We can only give very little things to our

Lord. Little things, like that child. Give them to him, my son! Give them to him, because when you do, your Father God will give you a big hug! And you will be happy, because mortification is happiness: gaudium etsi laboriosum [joyous, even though hard]. Our Lord listens especially to mortified and penitent souls; that is why he listens to my children in Opus Dei, who know that contemplative life is based on prayer and penance. If we offer our prayer and penance to God through our Lady’s hands, then they will indeed become truly effective.

# WHILE FISHING * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.5}

You know, my children, that our vocation – Christ’s call – leads us to become one with him, and he came into the world because omnes homines vult salvos fieri,1 he wants to redeem all mankind. There isn’t a single soul he doesn’t care for. Every one of them has cost him the price of all his Blood.2 Even though there are thousands of us, there are still very few of us in comparison with the vastness of the human race. We must therefore see ourselves as a tiny measure of leaven, ready to do good to the whole of mankind, the whole mass, mindful of the Apostle’s words: modicum fermentum totam massam corrumpit,3 a little leaven leavens the whole batch. We have to become like leaven, so we can influence and improve the dough.

## Like leaven {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.5.1}

You know how leaven acts, although perhaps those of you who are city-dwellers haven’t ever seen it in practice. When I was a child, during the summer holidays, I used to really enjoy seeing bread being made. At the time I didn’t try to draw any supernatural conclusions from it. I wasn’t like one of those people – I’m sure it’s totally exaggerated – who were supposed never to have eaten

* Notes of a meditation given during a retreat in Rome, 16 April 1954 1 1 Tim 2:4 2 Cf. 1 Pet 1:18-19 3 Gal 5:9

anything on Fridays, and to have been born chanting Kyrie eleison. And you are the same: we are ordinary Christians. What I liked about it was that the servants used to give me a cock made from the dough. I now recall the whole ceremony fondly: there was a real ritual involved in it. I don’t suppose it’s done like that any more. The first thing was to prepare the leaven – a portion of dough from the previous batch – and add it to the water and the well-sieved flour. I remember how the dough was carefully covered and left to rest until it rose and rose incredibly. It was then put in the oven, and out came that tasty bread, light and spongy, wonderful. This was because the leaven was good quality, had been properly prepared, and had let itself be dissolved and disappear into the dough, the “multitude”, which owed its goodness and quality to the leaven. We have no choice but to feel responsible for the dough, the multitude, and to do everything possible to be a Christian influence on all souls right from the start. If we really want to be saints, my children, we have to aim to become the leaven that makes the dough rise. Our lives are not self-centred: we are called to fight in the front line, to dissolve into the bloodstream of society, going unnoticed, and reach all hearts, carrying out in them the good task of turning them into good bread, wonderful food: bread which can be the peace – joy and peace – of all families, of all peoples. The aim of our apostolate is to make, out of all races, bread for the table and hosts for the altar, both the divine and the human; and to offer them all the light, the fire, the warmth and the joy of Christ. This is evident the moment you open our particular Law. In it you can read about the means we use to achieve this end: first, the supernatural means; after that, our friendship and our work, our personal achievements, our professional training, and the fact that if we are appointed to public office we carry out our duties faithfully and honestly…; then the corporate apostolates; and always, the personal apostolate of trust and good example that we each carry out, quite naturally, on a one-to-one basis.

## In the sea of the world {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.5.2}

Our Lord often used parables in his preaching: sine parabolis non loquebatur eis,4 he said nothing to them without a parable, St Matthew tells us. In this too I like to imitate the Master, making use above all of those fishing metaphors – boats and nets – which have such an evangelical flavour. We are like fish caught in a net. Our Lord has caught us in the net of his love, amidst the waves of this turbulent world of ours; but not in order to separate us from the world – from our surroundings, from our ordinary work – but so that, while belonging to the world, we may be totally his at the same time. Non rogo ut tollas eos de mundo, sed ut serves eos a malo,5 I do not pray that you take them out of the world, but that you keep them from evil. In this sea of the world many, many souls are being tossed about in turbulent waters... But listen to the words of Jeremiah which I made a note of many years ago: ecce ego mittam piscatores multos … et piscabuntur eos.6 Behold, says the Lord, I am sending many fishers – among them, you and me – and I will catch those fish, with a desire for the salvation of all souls, with divine zeal. Hey, it’s a great undertaking: launch out and fish! Human beings are swimming in some very hostile waters, among huge waves. Their lives, even when they appear cheerful and boisterous, are storm-tossed and sad; they are bereft of charity and understanding, and their shouts of laughter are just an attempt to drown out their sadness and bitterness. Men, like fish, devour each other. Our job is to draw them into the divine net and get them to love one another in the charity of Christ. I will never tire of telling you that I am interested in all souls. Nobody who meets Opus Dei should go away disappointed. If they

4 Mt 13:34 5 Jn 17:15 6 Jer 16:16

can’t become Numeraries they can become Supernumeraries or Associates: the vocation is the same. And in any event they can become Cooperators, or at least friends. Nobody should be denied this supernatural warmth, this divine fire which consumes us, which consumes me: I don’t mind putting it like that, even though I’m just a poor human being. From time to time my brother Santiago reminds me that when he was small he used to hear me singing the verse from the Gospel which says: ignem veni mittere in terram, et quid volo nisi ut accendatur?7 I came to cast fire upon the earth; and would that it were already kindled! And the response is: ecce ego quia vocasti me,8 here I am, for you have called me. We have to set the world on fire, my dearest children! We have to bring people to the point where the whole earth is set ablaze with the divine light of grace brought to us by Christ. Because, from the moment you and I heard our Lord’s command, “henceforth you will be catching men”,9 we too have become the fishermen Jeremiah spoke about.

## Fishers of men {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.5.3}

Do you want to accompany Christ on his divine fishing expedition? Let’s open the Gospel at St Luke, chapter five. Jesus was surrounded by the multitude, who were anxious “to hear the word of God.”10 He got into Simon Peter’s boat and told him: Duc in altum, et laxate retia vestra in capturam,11 put out into the deep water and let down your nets for a catch. And Peter replied: “‘Master, we toiled all night and took nothing. But at your word I

7 Lk 12:49 8 1 Sam 3:6,9 9 Lk 5:10 10 Lk 5:1 11 Lk 5:4

will let down the nets.’ And when they had done this, they enclosed a great shoal of fish.”12 The fish were so plentiful that rumpebatur autem rete eorum,13 their net was breaking under the strain. Et annuerunt sociis, qui erant in alia navi, ut venirent et adiuvarent eos,14 they had to call their partners in the other boat to come and help them. Our apostolate in the service of the Church will produce such a multitude of fish that we will have to call the fishermen from the other boats to come too and help with the great catch of Jesus Christ. Jesus tells each of us: “Look, the multitudes are yours because they are mine. Go out fishing!” “But, Lord,” we could tell him, “there are so many dangers...! People don’t respond, they don’t listen, they don’t come, they don’t move…!” Venite post me, et faciam vos fieri piscatores hominum,15 follow me, and I will make you fishers of men. My children, you have been called, like the Apostles, to fish in the ocean of the world, to continue doing your job, to remain where you were before you were called. Don’t forget that “whatever you do, in word or deed, do everything in the name of the Lord Jesus.”16 You have to be contemplatives and carry out everything for God: everything for Love. In Opus Dei we each seek holiness in our own state of life, in the exercise of our own profession, in everyday life. But I will dare to say that God has given us the gift of working miracles, and big ones too. We give light to the blind… Who could not relate thousands of cases? How a person who was blind almost from birth, recovered his sight and received all the splendour of Christ’s light. And

12 Lk 5:5-6 13 Lk 5:6 14 Lk 5:7 15 Mt 4:19 16 Col 3:17

another was deaf, and another dumb, unable to utter a single word befitting a child of God... And his tongue has been purified, and he now speaks like a man, not an animal. In nomine Iesu,17 in the name of Jesus we have restored the power of speech to the dumb. And that maimed creature, unable to do a single good deed, and that comfort-lover, who saw things but did not do them. You told him: surge et ambula!18 – arise and walk. And that other person, dead and rotten, so rotten that he stank like a corpse, who heard, as in the miracle of the widow’s son at Naim, the words: adolescens, tibi dico, surge!19 – young man, I say to you, arise. My son, miracles like Christ’s, miracles like those of the first Christians. You will perform them if you are alter Christus, another Christ, or rather ipse Christus, Christ himself; if you attain the full identification with our Lord that contemplative life entails. Then you will go to all peoples, to everyone – they all have souls to be saved! – and the nets will start to break because of the weight of the catch, so much so that you will have to call others to come to catch them too, in abundance, as Simon Peter did with his companions.20 And those who do come to us, as a result of God’s grace and your work, will in turn bring others; and these yet others; and so on until the end of time. We feel moved to cry out: “All the ends of the earth have seen the victory of our God. Make a joyful noise to the Lord, all the earth; break forth into joyous song and sing praises!”21

## On the right side of the boat {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.5.4}

But let’s take a look at that other catch, the one after the Resurrection. It was Simon Peter who exclaimed: vado piscari,22 I am going fishing. Peter was a fisherman before becoming an

17 Acts 3:6 18 Mt 9:5 19 Lk 7:14 20 Cf. Lk 5:7 21 Ps 98:3-4 22 Jn 21:3

Apostle, before becoming a priest, before becoming the head; and he continued being one afterwards. Before becoming an Apostle he was a fisherman; after becoming an Apostle he was still a fisherman: afterwards he had the same profession as before. It’s the same with us. Even our priests, insofar as possible – though they have very little time for it, if any – also do the same; and in any event they retain their professional outlook. Hearing him say this, Thomas Didymus, Nathanael, the sons of Zebedee, and two others, replied to Peter: venimus et nos tecum, we will also go with you. “They went out and got into the boat; but that night they caught nothing.” Mane autem facto… “Just as day was breaking, Jesus stood on the beach; yet the disciples did not know that it was Jesus.”23 He appeared to his Apostles, to those souls who had given themselves to him, and they didn’t realise it. How often Christ is not just close by, but actually within us, and we live in such a human fashion! How often he’s standing right beside us and we don’t recognise him! He doesn’t get a single affectionate glance, a single loving word, a single fruitful act of service to express his children’s generous desires! They didn’t recognise him. “Jesus said to them, ‘Children, have you any fish?’”24 This homely picture of Christ overjoys me. For Jesus, God himself, to say that! He who already possessed a glorious body! And when they said they had nothing to give him our Lord commanded them: mittite in dexteram navigii rete et invenietis,25 cast the net on the right side of the boat, and you will find some. They cast the net out to starboard. It was a specific task, so we can learn from it. It is no longer a matter of being leaven for the

23 Jn 21:3-4 24 Jn 21:5 25 Jn 21:6

dough of the multitude. Now it is a matter of being fishermen who fish for fishermen. They are fish of a different kind. They are other instruments who, later on, must become fishers of men. “So they cast it, and now they were not able to haul it in, for the quantity of fish. The disciple whom Jesus loved said to Peter, Dominus est! ‘It is the Lord!’”26 Out of love: love is always farseeing. It is love that first perceives signs of affection like that. The adolescent Apostle, because of his love for Jesus, because he loved Christ with all the purity of a heart which had never been corrupted, said: Dominus est! “When Simon Peter heard that it was the Lord, he put on some clothes … and sprang into the sea.”27 Peter represents faith. He threw himself into the water with wonderful recklessness. With love and faith, what can we not achieve? Alii autem discipuli navigio venerunt.28 The other disciples, in the boat, came to land, towing the net behind them. They would bring it as we have seen fishermen do when working close inshore. They hauled in the net with the fish, they landed it on the shore and laid it at Christ’s feet. That is what we want to do: all the fish are for our Lord, but there are some which we lay especially at his feet, so they may be raised to his heart. That is the work of proselytism, the continuation of our apostolate. “Simon Peter went aboard and hauled the net ashore, full of large fish, a hundred and fifty-three of them.”29 One hundred and fifty-three fish, all counted. They were all caught to starboard. And this time the net did not break, nor did they have to ask others for help, for these fish were for them. Another fruitful work of apostolate, which is the apostolate of apostolates. It is the work of winning new apostles. If a son of mine does not work to win new

26 Jn 21:6-7 27 Jn 21:7 28 Jn 21:8 29 Jn 21:11

apostles... he is doing badly. There is something seriously the matter with him. If he truly loves his way then he should want others to share his happiness, because good always spreads outwards. Alas for any son of mine who does not desire to attract other souls!... Souls are to be sought in very specific places, depending on their human qualities, their intelligence, abilities and sense of loyalty. There are also supernatural qualities which perhaps they lacked previously, but which now they acquire through their friendship with you. There is an exact number of fish. In comparison to the crowd there are few of them. They don’t disrupt our normal arrangements, nor is there any need to ask other fishermen who are also out fishing to come and help us. Do you understand?

## Loving all mankind {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.5.5}

My children, I would like you to have a great sense of enthusiasm about all this, and also to pass it on to all your friends, relatives, professional colleagues and neighbours. Bring that friend of yours, make him a fisherman, for he may be on his way to being fished. I have always tried to have this holy shrewdness, and have taught you to do the same, in attracting other souls to our way. Invite your friends to work at the apostolate with you. Get them involved in some particular undertaking, some noble task of service, and let them put their hearts into it. That way, little by little, they will get more and more involved in the Work, more infected with this spirit of ours, and perhaps they may obtain from our Lord the call to our own Christian vocation. We are like red-hot coals, and these coals burn whatever they touch, or at least they raise the temperature around them; otherwise they die out. Can you imagine the heart of any child of God in his Opus Dei being extinguished, cold, supernaturally lifeless and bereft of that working effectiveness which burns by its very presence?

There is nothing more satisfying than to lead many souls to the light and warmth of Christ. People whom nobody has shown the value of ordinary life, for whom ordinary things seem empty and without meaning, who fail to understand and wonder at this great truth, that Jesus cares for us, for even the smallest and most insignificant. To everybody you have to say: “Christ is seeking you too, as he sought the first Twelve, as he sought the Samaritan woman, as he sought Zacchaeus, as he said to the paralytic: surge et ambula,30 get up and walk, because the Lord wants you; or to the widow’s son at Naim: tibi dico, surge!31 I say to you, arise from your self-centredness, from your mediocrity, from your death.” There are so many good things waiting to be done, so many wonderful apostolic initiatives of the Work... They are all attractive, effective and very numerous... Some seem small, but they will do a great deal of good to very many people. Besides, they are an opportunity for a second catch of fish to be laid at Christ’s feet. These are the hundred and fifty-three fish. Let us finish up with some words of St Mark, from chapter eleven of his Gospel. See how Jesus replies to John. Take careful note, because it is something that has been very present, right from the start, in Opus Dei. They say to him: “‘Teacher, we saw a man casting out demons in your name, and we forbade him, because he was not following us.’ But Jesus said, ‘Do not forbid him; for no one who does a mighty work in my name will be able soon after to speak evil of me. For he that is not against us is for us.’”32 My children, don’t ever try to hinder the apostolate of anybody who works for Christ. I don’t mind letting you know that people have often tried to hinder us: they didn’t want us to do anything. You can’t act like that: rejoice that there are many labourers in the Lord’s vineyard.

30 Mt 9:5 31 Lk 7:14 32 Mk 9:37-40

We are not upset by anyone. We are happy to see everyone else going about their work. The world of souls is like an enormous ocean. You should love the apostolate other people are doing! We are not upset by anyone. We keep to our own waters. With the passage of the years you will learn about many details of the history of the Work. You will learn that many people have tried to copy our spirit, our way of doing things… but all they had to show for it was a fine corpse, a dead body. None of that bothers us. We couldn’t care less about it, although it is unpleasant when people imitate you down to the way you walk… Nevertheless, I repeat, no-one bothers us, not even those who employ such disreputable methods. My children should never be concerned with the apostolate done by others, except to praise it. As for us, we shall always go on fishing, with our own spirit! Our Blessed Lady was present in the tidal wave of apostles that her divine Son sent across all the seas of the world to sanctify the ordinary activities of mankind. Ask her, Queen of Apostles, Queen of Opus Dei, to help you fulfil the resolutions of this time of prayer. Through her intercession you will come to be alter Christus, ipse Christus – another Christ, Christ himself! – to your fellow Christians and to all souls.

# IN JOSEPH’S SCHOOL * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.6}

With great love, from the start of the Work, we have called St Joseph our Father and Lord. I have to say that he is even more than that: he is the Teacher of the interior life. And you and I need this interior life in order to be faithful in our external activities. What does interior life mean, but to dwell in constant dialogue with God and with the Mother of God? And who had a closer relationship with God and with our Blessed Lady than St Joseph? Every day I am deeply moved by a prayer I say before Mass, one of the preparatory prayers the Church recommends priests to say: O felicem virum, beatum Ioseph… O Joseph, happy and fortunate man, to whom was granted not only to see and to hear that God whom many kings longed to see and did not see, longed to hear and did not hear; but to carry him in his arms, to kiss, and clothe, and care for him.1 And you know that in the vast sea of God’s Trinity and Unity, wherever one Person is to be found the others are necessarily present too. Therefore St Joseph, who was on such familiar terms with God the Son, had an equally familiar relationship with God the Father and God the Holy Spirit.

## St Joseph’s mission {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.6.1}

The Holy Patriarch was not an old man, but young, energetic and hardy, a great lover of loyalty, a man who was strong. Holy

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 19 March 1958, feast of St Joseph 1 Roman Missal, Prayer before Mass

Scripture describes him with a single word: “just”.2 Joseph was a just man, filled with all virtues, as was proper for the man who was to be God’s protector on earth. At first he is taken aback to discover that his Immaculate Spouse is with child. He sees the hand of God in what has happened, but he doesn’t know what to do. And being upright, so as not to put her to shame, he decides to send her away quietly. But an angel of the Lord speaks to him in a dream, saying: “Joseph, son of David, do not fear to take Mary your wife, for that which is conceived in her is of the Holy Spirit; she will bear a son, and you shall call his name Jesus.”3 Just imagine how relieved Joseph must have been to hear the angel’s words! How happily he would have obeyed! Immediately, “when Joseph woke from sleep, he did as the angel of the Lord commanded him; he took his wife.”4 How beautiful! Joseph, spouse of Mary, a human being, is chosen to take the place of the father of the Son of God. Commenting on this passage, a Father of the Church places these words on the angel’s lips: “Although he is of the Holy Spirit, do not imagine that you are left outside the working of this divine plan. Although you have had no part in his begetting, since the Virgin has retained her virginity intact, nevertheless, all that pertains to the name of father, while safeguarding the honour of virginity, that do I give you, such as giving the child a name. It is you who will name him. For though this is not your offspring, yet shall you show a father’s care toward him. Thus, beginning with the giving of the name, I unite you closely with him who is to be born.”5 Let us fix our eyes on the Holy Patriarch St Joseph in order to understand his mission with regard to Christ more fully. In a way, Joseph is hidden – Sacred Scripture hardly mentions him. His

2 Cf. Mt 1:19 3 Mt 1:20-21 4 Mt 1:24 5 St John Chrysostom, Homilies on St Matthew’s Gospel, 4, 12

mission is divine: he is completely dedicated, completely committed to looking after Christ. And is this not the path, my child, that God has marked out for you and me? Like Joseph, we too are dedicated souls, completely committed to God’s service, and seeking to identify with Christ in everything. Our only aim is to resemble Christ, to become completely identified with him. Mihi enim vivere Christus est,6 for me, to live is Christ. St Joseph could say that too. We should all be able to say it. Take a look at how you behave, at how you are leading your ordinary, hidden life. Perhaps you will ask me: “Father, how can I tell if I’m following Christ, if I’m really becoming identified with him?” And I will answer: “If you seek him every day, if you strive hard to find him, if you love him, then indeed you are living by him.” And I seem to hear your immediate reply: “Father – it was love that brought me to the Work!” It’s true, my son. The love of Christ has brought us to Opus Dei. But don’t forget that man’s will is weak and easily inclined toward complacency. True love, on the other hand, has a very clear characteristic: it is a gift of self and does not seek selfish satisfaction; it is founded on sacrifice and expressed in deeds of service. We have to love Jesus non verbo neque lingua, sed opere et veritate,7 not just with words, but above all with deeds.

## Loving with deeds {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.6.2}

I could tell you lots of stories of things which have affected souls deeply. I remember, for example, a priest I knew many, many years ago. I never really got to know him properly. He was just a poor man, but he loved Jesus Christ. One day he was giving Holy Communion through the grille to some enclosed nuns. That priest had a contemplative soul, and he was reciting the words prescribed for distributing Holy Communion: Corpus Domini nostri Iesu

6 Phil 1:21 7 1 Jn 3:18

Christi custodiat animam tuam in vitam aeternam.8 At the same time he was speaking with his heart to our Jesus, hidden under the sacramental species, saying to him: “Lord, I love you more than these do.” And our Lord replied to him, without any external words: “Love means deeds, not sweet words.” At that divine reproach the poor man was at first frightened, but it was followed by a sense of peace: a great joy and peace after that loving “complaint” from Christ. My very dearest child, you are a soul dedicated to God, to Jesus. What are you up to? Do you love with deeds, deeds that are habitually small? Because only very infrequently do we have the opportunity to serve him in great things. Normally great deeds only happen in our imagination. You will perform great deeds too, when the opportunity comes, if you are faithful every day in the little things, if you become like a child before God. I have often seen a little toddler going with his mother to the fields and picking one little wildflower after another. He gathers flowers that you and I don’t even notice. He steps into puddles and searches under bushes to seek out little humble violets, flowers full of fragrance, which he gathers until he makes a posy, and offers it to his mother, who gives him such a loving look! My son, these things I am telling you are not silly sentimentalism, but upright and manly. If I offer you this comparison, it’s because in the presence of God our Father we can’t behave in any other way. Before the Divine Majesty you and I are like tiny tots. Our everyday lives are seemingly always the same; but if we see ourselves as little children before God, we will see that our day is filled with wonders, always different and always enchanting, because they are beautified by love. This is what God is asking of you: that hidden violet, your smile, your charity toward your brother, your hidden and fruitful sacrifice.

8 May the body of our Lord Jesus Christ keep your soul unto life everlasting: Roman Missal of St Pius V, Ordinary of the Mass

## Self-giving to others {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.6.3}

How often have I spoken to you about proselytism…! Because the desire for souls, the zeal to bring others to Christ, is absolutely essential to our vocation, and is a necessary consequence of our love for God and for the Work. And I’ve very, very often said that I don’t dispense any of my children from this sweet obligation. Well, listen: the best proselytism is to make sure that none of your brothers gets lost, that we all make it to the end of the road marked out for us by God. It’s so painful to lose, not just one’s clothing, but a portion of one’s heart, in a puddle along the way! When one of my children loses his way it’s possible that a good portion of the blame lies with those who lived with him and didn’t come to his aid. When a group go for a trip in the country, or in the snow, if one of the party gets lost the others look for him frantically and make every sacrifice to find him. They take a thousand risks, but they come to his rescue and lead him back to the right path. Otherwise they would be a total disgrace. Well, you have to do just the same for your brothers, and afterwards for other people too. This is a matter of justice: firstly, I insist, toward our own brothers. Help them, encourage them. Forward! To do this, we have fraternal correction, just like the first of the Church’s faithful, and our prayer for one another, and our individual efforts to overcome our own defects, our desire to be faithful and send the others wholesome, healthy blood, full of oxygen. We have the same resources as the Apostles and the early Christians: faith, given by God, with hope and charity: supernatural virtues. Then, doctrine and the sacraments. And as a clear expression, the sacrament of Penance, spiritual direction and sincerity when speaking. When I was younger I had a great devotion to the forty martyrs of Sebaste. Those soldiers, because they were Christians, were condemned to be submerged in a pool of icy water. When they were

about to be put to death they prayed: “Forty of us entered into battle: forty crowns we ask of you.” But one of them got out of the pool, whereupon the Holy Spirit moved one of their persecutors to get in and take his place, and become a witness to Christ. Well, we have to do the same for our brothers. We have to give ourselves to others. We have to give our lives for the people God has placed at our side, in the first place for those who have received the same divine call. The aspiration which comes straight from the Apostle’s very soul, mihi enim vivere Christus est,9 for to me to live is Christ, is joined with that other, et mori lucrum,10 and to die is gain. It is not a matter of physically losing one’s life, although for you and me death is not a matter of great importance. To die would be too comfortable, when there is so much to be done here on earth, when there are so few workers toiling for the Lord here below! Instead, think about dying a little each day; think about how we can each deny ourselves, put ourselves out in one matter after another, cheerfully, to make life more pleasant for your brother apostles. My son, don’t ever forget this motivation, if you want to be victorious in the struggle against your defects. Like that you will help others to be victorious too, and you’ll be contributing to their effectiveness.

## Responsibility {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.6.4}

Today thousands of people, daughters and sons of God in Opus Dei, are renewing the gift of themselves which, one day, they made to the Work. They come from all walks of life: university professors, politicians, factory workers, farm hands, domestic employees… Some are household names, while others, the majority, are like Joseph, completely unknown. They will be celebrities in heaven!

9 Phil 1:21 10 Ibid.

They are all equally important, because they are all crazily in love with God, and they know that another expression of dedication to God is a sense of responsibility. We should each feel the weight of our mission, being faithful wherever we find ourselves, as docile instruments of God, so that this family of ours, this army that is becoming universal, may respond with a single heartbeat in pursuing the Church’s goals, ut castrorum acies ordinata,11 like an army of peace in battle array. How happy it makes me whenever the Church asks us to take part in some apostolic project, because I know that my children will carry it out with a lot of love for God and a sincere sense of responsibility, cheerfully and humbly, conscious of being instruments of unity and effectiveness. Do you realise that you can help your brothers by sending them arterial blood; or on the contrary be a hindrance, like a landslide falling across their path? It would be bad enough if you yourself were to get lost; but it would be far worse to think that you could be a brake on the work of the others. What is there in your path that causes you not to be able to say truly: mihi vivere Christus est,12 for me, to live is Christ? Root out the obstacles which sometimes hinder your response! Today is a wonderful day to do so. Today God’s heart is ready to grant great graces. Ask Joseph to remove the obstacles. Tell him. Tell him that you can’t do it on your own, that he has to help you. Shout it aloud like the man stretched beside the pool whom we read about in the Gospel: hominem non habeo,13 I have no-one to help me. Tell St Joseph that. Today could be the day for a new conversion, a deeper conversion that is final and permanent. Hodie, si vocem eius audieritis, nolite obdurare corda vestra,14 if you hear his voice, harden not your hearts. You are listening to

11 Song 6:4 12 Phil 1:21 13 Jn 5:7 14 Heb 3:7-8; Ps 95:8

your Father, to the Founder of Opus Dei, and although that’s a lot (God will call you to account for having lived with the Founder, even though I’m a very poor specimen), it’s not just I who am speaking to you… God himself, in the depths of your heart, is asking you to open the doors wide so his grace can enter into the deepest recesses of your soul, to heal and purify and enlighten it. You and I cannot have a hard heart, like stony ground that the drops of water can’t penetrate. No. Let us ask our Lord to enter into us like life-giving rain, that makes the ground on which it falls bear rich fruit.

## Humble sincerity {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.6.5}

I would like to remind you once more that we have come to the Work to become saints. That is the purpose of our divine calling. We shouldn’t be surprised, therefore, on seeing that we still have a long way to go. And so we should acknowledge our deficiencies in all simplicity, without trying to dress them up as virtue. We should avoid pride, which blinds us terribly and makes us see everything the wrong way around. My children, be sincere with yourselves, be objective. Like that we will be effective in our dedication. It’s difficult: we have to be humble, to open our heart wide in spiritual direction so as to ventilate all the recesses of the soul. My children, flee from the “dumb devil”! The devil takes our sense of shame away in order to cause us to stumble, and then he gives us back an even greater sense of shame, to induce us not to mention our mistakes – perhaps the same mistakes as other people are prepared to brag about, and exaggerate, over a cup of coffee. You shouldn’t behave like that. Reveal everything you have within that bothers you, both small things and great, and like that you will always be victorious. Don’t forget that the more we are in the wrong, the more arguments the devil will supply us with for keeping quiet and not being sincere.

I know that you are doing your own prayer, while my mind, my heart and my lips are giving expression to the prayer of us all. Let us have recourse to our Father and Lord. For us Joseph, apart from these titles of Father and Lord, is also Teacher, because he teaches us, he instructs us and sets us an example; because, with his patronage, he gets us grace from heaven, helps us and makes us effective. Ask him for a refined sense of obedience. Ask him for a responsible attitude to work, and a love that is expressed in deeds. Ask him for the holiness we seek. Our meditation is coming to an end. We have the good custom of turning to our Mother, to whom, every day, we take the little flowers of our resolutions, those humble and hidden violets we pick up during the day. Now, on your own, you should invoke Mary and invoke Joseph. See how our meditation today has given us a lot to think about, a lot to ask for, and a lot to put into practice.

# LESSONS OF ORDINARY LIFE * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.7}

Our Lord came to teach us. We are all familiar with St Luke’s prologue to the Acts of the Apostles: primum quidem sermonem feci de omnibus, o Theophile, quae coepit Iesus facere et docere,1 in the first book, O Theophilus, I dealt with all that Jesus began to do and teach. Jesus came to teach, but by doing; he came to teach, to instruct us, but by being a model, by being at one and the same time both the teacher and the text. Stop and consider this first idea I wanted to outline to you. My children, are you and I determined to live a life that can be a model and lesson for others? Are we determined to be other Christs, to live like children of God? It’s not enough just to say so; we have to show it by our deeds. The fact that we are starting a new year invites us to consider slowly how we’ve been doing, both over the past months and throughout our whole life. Let’s see: are you happy with how you have been doing up to now? You who are another Christ, who are a child of God, can it be said of you that you have come facere et docere,2 to teach others by your behaviour all those good and noble things our Lord has obtained for us through his Redemption?

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 9 January 1959, our Founder’s birthday 1 Acts 1:1 2 To do and teach: ibid.

## Going unnoticed {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.7.1}

I’ve brought with me the notes of an old meditation which I’m not sure if I’ll use or not; they’ve been partly destroyed by the passage of time. Let’s meditate on the Gospel passage that features in the liturgy of these days: chapter two of St Matthew. But first I would like to make an observation based on the last words of St John’s Gospel: “But there are also many other things which Jesus did; were every one of them to be written, I suppose that the world itself could not contain the books that would be written.”3 Our Lord performed many miracles and uttered many sayings which the evangelists have not mentioned explicitly. Perhaps he may want us to learn something new today, as we contemplate him through the recollections which St Matthew selected and wrote down in his Gospel. “Now when Jesus was born in Bethlehem of Judea in the days of Herod the king, behold, wise men from the East came to Jerusalem, saying, ‘Where is he who has been born king of the Jews? For we have seen his star in the East, and have come to worship him.’”4 The wise men have made a long journey following a star. On arriving in Jerusalem they lose sight of it, and when this happens they do the obvious thing: they approach the inhabitants and ask them for news of the new-born king of the Jews, but nobody knows anything about him. A King who goes unnoticed; a King who is God, and yet no one realises it. What a good lesson for you and me, who so often want to be the garnish on every dish. Our Lord invites us to be the salt of the earth and the light of the world,5 that our good works should shine before men so that they give glory to our heavenly Father.6 But how

3 Jn 21:25 4 Mt 2:1-2 5 Cf. Mt 5:13-14 6 Cf. Mt 5:16

are we supposed to shine? Should it be something extraordinary that attracts attention, something unusual that dazzles and blinds people? Do you think this is the path Christ teaches us? Do you think this is the path God’s children in his Opus Dei should walk? Isn’t Christ teaching us to live alongside our peers, our professional colleagues, as one of themselves, unnoticed? Not unnoticed because of your reputation or work, nor because of your talents. Unnoticed rather because there is no need for others to know you are dedicated to God, striving to imitate Jesus Christ; that you are the salt of the earth, other Christs. Nevertheless, they should experience it; they should feel helped to be clean and honest when they see your behaviour, for example, how you respect other people’s legitimate freedom; and when they hear doctrine from your lips, backed up by the consistency of your example. But your dedication to God’s service should go unnoticed, just as Jesus’ life went unnoticed for the first thirty years. During these years I have very often witnessed my children’s way of life. How well they have done things! They spread peace and joy all around them, and those who live with them hardly even realise it. Only when time has passed and your brother is no longer around do people say: “What a good man he was!” But while they are there, they go unnoticed.

## The strangeness of not being strange {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.7.2}

Let us continue with the Holy Gospel. When asked by the Magi where the new-born king of the Jews was to be found, King Herod “was troubled, and all Jerusalem with him.”7 This sense of unease is something many people experience when they discover they are living with someone who is fighting to function on a higher level than the things of the flesh, family ties, and personal ambition, because he has been crucified with Christ,8 without his dedication to

7 Mt 2:3 8 Cf. Rom 6:6

God leading to a change of state or being different from his fellow citizens. They find it disquieting, surprising even, and sometimes they remark: “How strange!” I have had to reply: “How strange that there’s nothing strange about them? Strange because they dress like everybody else, because they have the same healthy interests, because they cultivate identical pursuits, because they are passionately interested in the honest affairs of this world?” I will never tire of repeating that, in trying to respond fully to the requirements of the faith, the members of Opus Dei dedicate themselves seriously to seeking Christian perfection with all its consequences; but they seek it precisely in their own state – single, married, widowed or priests – in the exercise of their own professional work. They don’t change the normal situation of their life or work, nor their hopes or aspirations. It is typical of Opus Dei to seek the strangeness of not being strange: being Catholic citizens like so many others, not wishing to be in any way different from others, because there is no reason why they should. It is natural for Catholics who follow Christ like this, imitating his thirty years of work and normality, not to attract attention. Perhaps their colleagues and friends, and even their relatives, may express surprise when they discover they have been living with them without being different in any way: like Jesus, they have passed quasi in occulto.9 And there is no reason to be surprised. If a son of God in Opus Dei were to act strangely and attract attention, if he didn’t behave like his colleagues, friends, and relatives in ordinary everyday things, he would show that he hadn’t understood the lesson our Lord teaches us with his birth. Behaving like that, getting involved in unusual things, would mean we had lost sight of the very essence of our divine calling, because then we would no longer be normal people: we would have become separated from the dough, we would have lost the character of leaven. The only thing we ought to stand out for is not standing out at all.

9 Not publicly but in private: Jn 7:10

## An invitation to everybody {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.7.3}

But let us continue. My children, I’m sure that every time you’ve read these pages of the Gospel you’ve come to many wonderful conclusions, from the moment you began, like the Wise Men, to seek Christ. Many of you have had the good fortune, as I did, of being called by Jesus while still young. On recalling how the vocation of John the Evangelist took place, I’ve sometimes commented to you that he was a young man with a hunger for the truth: the same hunger that clean-living adolescents and young adults have felt in all ages on first opening their eyes to the world’s realities. Do you remember the scene? Shortly after his baptism our Lord passed along once more by the spot where John the Baptist and some of his disciples were gathered. On seeing him, the Baptist pointed to him and said to his companions: ecce Agnus Dei,10 behold, the Lamb of God, inviting them to follow Jesus. Our Lord caught John’s eye, and Andrew’s, whereupon they immediately began to follow him. John was an adolescent. This may have happened in your case, and it certainly did in mine: I too was an adolescent when Christ caught my eye, and his grace moved my soul and made me follow him. John and Andrew started following Jesus. “Jesus turned, and saw them following, and said to them, ‘What do you seek?’”11 Jesus asked the obvious question, and divine grace drew another question from the Baptist’s disciples, less obvious but nevertheless an enchanting one: “And they said to him, ‘Rabbi (which means Teacher), where are you staying?’ He said to them, ‘Come and see.’ They came and saw where he was staying; and they stayed with him that day.”12

10 Jn 1:36 11 Jn 1:38 12 Jn 1:38-39

You yourselves recall the scene. Their conversation must have been lengthy, and love must have penetrated deeply into John’s adolescent heart, because when many years later he came to describe the whole divine adventure, that part of his Gospel has the freshness and aroma of a lovingly-kept diary: hora autem erat quasi decima,13 it was four in the afternoon, he tells us, recalling the precise moment when videns eos sequentes se,14 Jesus saw them following and invited them to accompany him. My children, my children, there are many human beings in the world, and not a single one of them is left uncalled by the Master. He issues a general call to all men of every race and tongue, from every latitude, from the moment he brings them into existence. He calls them with the statement that he has come to save all: omnes homines vult salvos fieri.15 And he calls them with that other wonderful statement that he also addresses to all without exception: “Be holy as my heavenly Father is holy.”16 He calls them to a Christian life, a holy life, a chosen life.

## Excuses {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.7.4}

Do you remember the parable of the rich man who held a banquet, and how the guests kept finding excuses to avoid attending? Just imagine the Heart of Jesus Christ as he described it! It is St Luke who tells the story: “A man once gave a great banquet, and invited many; and at the time for the banquet he sent his servant to say to those who had been invited, ‘Come; for all is now ready.’ But they all alike began to make excuses.”17 One said he had just bought a field, another that he needed to try out a yoke of oxen, yet another that he had just got married and therefore could not come… Many had been invited, “but few came, because sometimes”, as St

13 Jn 1:39 14 Jn 1:38 15 1 Tim 2:4 16 Mt 5:48 17 Lk 14:16-18

Gregory the Great says, “those who are subject to him by faith refuse, through their wayward lives, to participate in the eternal banquet.”18 Haven’t we too often looked for excuses and said no to Jesus? I have to confess with shame that my own conscience gnaws at me – I don’t know about you – for all the times I have failed to respond to his heartfelt invitation – all those inspirations of the Holy Spirit, all the gifts and graces, and all the insights which God our Lord gently implants in our hearts and minds. But see what the master of the household ordered his servants to do: go out to the highways! Compelle intrare,19 compel them to come in! Bring them here at all costs, force them to come! Very often you will have recalled this Gospel passage when trying to bring your own friends to Jesus. And when faced by the refusal of your companion, your relative, or your friend, and all their constant excuses, then Christ’s command, the “compelle intrare”, caused you to persist in prayer, mortification, petition, trying to enlighten their minds and move their wills, leading them to a more complete vocation to work with God, to co-redeem; to make them see that Christ had looked at them and was attracting them to himself like iron to a magnet. In short: you have tried to reproduce in your own life, and in the life of that other person, the scene of Christ and St John. You have done well, my son. If we all try to lead the souls around us to God like that, nobody can respond as the labourers in that other parable did to the question of the owner of the vineyard. “‘Why do you stand here idle all day?’ They said to him, ‘quia nemo nos conduxit, because no one has hired us.’”20 If we are truly faithful to our Christian vocation, that excuse should never be heard on the lips of anybody who crosses our path at any stage of our

18 St Gregory the Great, Homilies on the Gospels, 36, 2 19 Lk 14:23 20 Mt 20:6-7

lives. We should certainly go unnoticed, but at the same time teaching others by our behaviour, unnoticed yet effective, dissolved like salt, present at all cross-roads and thoroughfares and purifying everything. We ought to invite, propel and attract people to Christ. Because if in the Father’s house there are many mansions,21 in Opus Dei too, in our supernatural family, there are many different ways to serve our Lord. Allow me to make a digression. How often have I seen the eyes of young men light up when, having thought that full dedication to God was incompatible with a pure, upright love, they heard me telling them that marriage is also one of the divine paths of the earth! And how often have the eyes of their souls been opened to receive the light of God and a vocation to Opus Dei! My children, you have to approach your friends, you have to go out to all the thoroughfares and look for them in God’s name. If nobody had ever called you, you wouldn’t be here serving God with a divine vocation. Go out to the cross-roads; and, with your own work, wherever you are, you should feel the need to bring many people, so that the banquet hall will be filled. Apostolate and winning vocations are not forms of pressure. Never that. I would translate the phrase “compelle intrare” fairly freely as follows: be totally convinced that that person whom you regard as being very far away will end up cooperating in one way or another, through God’s grace, if you play your part. That’s what “compelle intrare” means. Allow me to give you a silly example. Have you ever seen a woman trying to get a size forty foot into a size thirty-five shoe? And she actually manages to do it! I repeat: you oblige them to enter the banquet hall, not by forcing them, because they have to remain free, but with the example of your pure and joyous lifestyle, with your prayer, trying to walk in God’s presence, being mortified and

21 Cf. Jn 14:2

obedient, taking loving care of material things, both those of your own personal use and those of the Centre. Like that, at the crossroads of the world, we participate, and make others participate, in this divine calling.

## Herod’s reaction {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.7.5}

Time is going by, my son. Very often our prayer doesn’t have to be particularly structured: the important thing is for it to flow like an intimate conversation, overflowing with love: gazing at God and being contemplated by him in return. Like Jesus’ glance at John, which decided the course of the disciple’s life forever. But let us return to the theme of our meditation. On hearing the question of the Magi, King Herod “was troubled, and all Jerusalem with him.”22 I have had the same experience, and maybe you have too. There are some excellent people who, on realising that Jesus is concealed very close by, that he is revealing himself to his chosen ones, become startled and upset, and very often react badly. Then our Lord’s words are fulfilled in them: inimici hominis domestici eius, 23 a man’s foes will be the people of his own household. It really hurts me to say this, but… how often it is a person’s family, friends, or relatives who are unreasonably opposed to their vocation! They don’t understand, they don’t want to understand, they reject God’s light. And they oppose all the noble things involved in dedicating one’s life to God. They dare to “test” the vocation of their children, their brothers and sisters, their friends, their relatives, and they act in a mean, underhand way. I tell you this, not to scandalise you but to forewarn you. They even justify this attitude by calling theirs a Christian family. What a shame!

22 Mt 2:3 23 Mt 10:36

On other occasions, especially when the seed of vocation to the Work takes root in our parents’ souls, what wonderful instruments they are! How marvellously they help us! King Herod, “assembling all the chief priests and scribes of the people, inquired of them where the Christ was to be born.”24 Sometimes the poor parents, God-fearing people, lose control of themselves completely and don’t let up until they find some priest – a good and even zealous man, but badly informed – who assures them they’re doing the right thing when in fact they’re causing harm, by telling them that there’s no such thing as their son’s vocation, that it’s something totally crazy… It’s very sad, but there is no shortage of priests who are ready to approve this underhand form of behaviour, which good people like our parents would be unable to credit. I’m speaking, my children, to put you on your guard against what could conceivably happen. Because there have even been families which have convoked meetings of priests to get their approval for something they will later be ashamed of. Years pass, and these poor people realise what they have done and greatly regret it. You can’t imagine how much it has pained me when, sometimes, a father and mother have knelt before me and said: “Please forgive us, Father!” I have always made them get up at once, telling them: “You don’t have to ask for forgiveness. What you did, you did with the best of intentions. Those who perhaps should say sorry are the people who deceived you, depending on the state of their conscience. But in any event the person to ask forgiveness from is God, not me.” Anyway, I had forgiven them from the start, because God gave me the grace to do so.

24 Mt 2:4

## Supernatural outlook {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.7.6}

See in the Gospel how thwarted and angry Herod became when the Magi failed to return to tell him where he could find the King of the Jews, the Baby wrapped in swaddling clothes. He became enraged and ordered a massacre. My children, today too you find the Gospel scene re-enacted. There are those who, lacking Herod’s power, use their tongues to destroy not just one person, but hundreds. They poison young people, reacting diabolically, more or less as Herod did when he heard about a Baby who was God. When in the course of your life you run up against this obstacle, this gathering of falsifiers, who harass and brainwash, who attempt

– such is the drift of their teaching – to darken God’s light, and try to make souls close their eyes, to avoid Christ’s gaze, what will you do then? What will we do? We will pray. Pray, and with supernatural outlook foster the vocation of that soul and of other souls we meet along life’s way. And remember, omnia in bonum! 25 God uses all these obstacles to purify, improve and ennoble vocations from the very outset. The learned men of Israel replied clearly to Herod’s question: according to the Scriptures, the Christ was to be born in Bethlehem of Judea, in the city of David.26 With his characteristic astuteness king Herod informed the Magi, asking them to give him a detailed report on their return: “He sent them to Bethlehem, saying, ‘Go and search diligently for the child, and when you have found him bring me word, that I too may come and worship him.’” “When they had heard the king they went their way; and lo, the star which they had seen in the East went before them, till it came to rest over the place where the child was. When they saw the star, they rejoiced exceedingly with great joy.”27 They rejoiced – note

25 Cf. Rom 8:28 26 Cf. Mt 2:4-6 27 Mt 2:8-10

how the sacred text admirably duplicates the idea – with great joy: gavisi sunt gaudio magno valde. We are growing impatient, my children, because time is slipping by and we haven’t yet reached the Child. But Jesus is there, in Bethlehem, waiting for us, with the same expression that we see in this crib scene: with his eyes wide open, his face eager, his arms outstretched to welcome us with love. “Going into the house [the Magi] saw the child with Mary his mother, and they fell down and worshipped him.”28 My children, you and I have reached Bethlehem and have drawn close to the Infant. Like St John of the Cross, who did human things in a divine way, we have danced him in our arms and said many ardent things to him. We have pressed him close to our hearts… You are manly individuals. I’m not speaking of childish things. I’m speaking of love. And love is expressed in deeds. Embrace him tightly in the intimacy of your soul. Seek God hidden in the depths of your pure heart, in the depths of your being when you are faithful, and never lose that intimacy. If you ever find you don’t know how to speak or what to say, and you dare not seek the Child again within your own hearts, turn to Mary, who is tota pulchra, so marvellous. O Lady, our Mother: God our Lord chose yours to be the hands that looked after him. Teach me how to treat your Son! Joseph, my Father and Lord, so humble and pure, worthy to carry Baby Jesus in your arms, to care for and embrace him: teach us how to approach our God, how to be humble and pure, how to become other Christs, how to live and teach – as he did – the ways of God, both hidden and bright, telling all men and women that they can attain marvellous holiness, extraordinary fruitfulness, in their everyday lives here on earth.

28 Mt 2:11

# PERSEVERANCE AND FRUITFULNESS * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.8}

In former times the last talk given on retreats and parish missions used to be called “meditation on perseverance.” And you, my children, have to ask yourselves: perseverance in what? What resolution should we persevere in, at the end of this retreat? I have to say that, apart from the specific resolutions each of you may have made in the presence of God, we all have to ask our Lord for one thing in particular and strive for it with all our might: perseverance in our way, fidelity to our divine vocation to the Work. A few minutes ago I was going through some old notes of mine. And although I have brought two of them with me, it occurred to me afterwards that it would be much better if, instead of speaking myself, I let Jesus do the talking. Our Lord makes his presence felt in many ways in the intimacy of your hearts. He speaks when you withdraw to pray alone, and also when you join your brothers to pray together, listening to one of them reading aloud from a book or to a priest preaching. And obviously he speaks most especially when in this little portion of the Church – the Work – it is the Father who is giving the meditation.

## Look at the sky {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.8.1}

Let us open St Luke’s Gospel at chapter thirteen, where the evangelist tells us how one Sabbath day our Lord met a woman who

* Notes of a meditation given at the end of a retreat in Rome, 15 January 1959

habebat spiritum infirmitatis annis decem et octo,1 who had had a spirit of infirmity for eighteen years. The same thing can happen to all of us, even to people who are trying to serve God, namely, that we can suffer from some spiritual illness. It doesn’t matter too much, because in the spirit of Opus Dei

– which is as old and as new as the Gospel – we have the medicine to cure every illness. The great pity is that sometimes we don’t realise it, and so we don’t go to the doctor and ask him for a prescription. Consider what happens in the life of the body. A person begins to feel unwell. “What’s the matter?” “I don’t know. I feel a bit funny…” So off he goes to see the doctor: the doctor examines the patient, does a series of tests, carries out a thorough check-up, and finally concludes: “That’s what it is, you’ve got a liver problem, or something in the stomach, or high blood pressure…”, and he prescribes the appropriate treatment. Well, a retreat has a similar function in the spiritual life as a medical check-up in the life of the body. We come here to carry out a deep examination of conscience, to find out the cause of some lack of zest in our struggle, the slackness we sometimes feel. If you often have a sense of unease, if difficulties often take away your peace of mind, if you have had, recently or in the past, a more serious fall than normal, you have to go to the divine doctor, you have to carry out a deep, sincere examination to identify the problem and its roots. Because the same could happen to you as to that poor woman: habebat spiritum infirmitatis,2 and, note carefully, erat inclinata:3 she couldn’t look up and see the stars shining. She was in the same situation as certain animals, incapable of looking upwards. I have some in mind, rather unattractive as animals go, who could be styled “the gentlemen of the lowly gaze”,

1 Lk 13:11 2 She had had a spirit of infirmity: ibid. 3 She was bent over: ibid.

because they go snuffling around the place with their heads held low, always with their eyes on the ground. And it very often happens that souls can be suffering from a disease without actually realising it. They are bent toward the things of the earth, and therefore can’t appreciate their vocation or the things of heaven. They spoil the atmosphere and create a thousand little upsets around themselves every day. They make life unpleasant, almost impossible, for others. Terrible, isn’t it? But let us return to Jesus, who has before him the woman who has been bent over for eighteen years and unable to look upwards. I know that it is quite possible that none of this applies to you, although for some particular reason it could happen. But if it doesn’t apply to you at the moment, at least it can help you when it happens to others whom it is your job to illuminate with the light of Christ. My children, this woman was aware of her illness and wanted to be cured. Although she was unable to look up at the sky, she certainly wanted to. Therefore when she went to pray in the synagogue, she positioned herself where our Lord would see her. Let me continue reading for you the exact words of the Gospel: “When Jesus saw her, he called her and said to her, ‘Woman, you are freed from your infirmity.’ And he laid his hands upon her, and immediately she was made straight, and she praised God.”4 Consider that our Lord is saying it to you now affectionately. From now on you can see the sky. From now on your soul will be well again and your life full of supernatural vibrancy. The woman looked up and gave glory to God. Lift up your heart, as I try to do too, in thanksgiving, seeking to glorify God who has freed us from the slavery of earthly things and has given us this light, this grace, this grandeur of a vocation to the Work. That way we will persevere.

4 Lk 13:12-13

I don’t want to have children who are bent over just because they refuse to stand up straight. Be humble. Acknowledge your mistakes. Lift up your eyes to the sky. Don’t look at things only from your own selfish viewpoint. Have faith, have hope, be reassured by the love Christ has for you. And if we ever have the impression that the horizon is closing in, that the earth is merging with the sky, then look at the sky. Like that you will do a lot of good on earth: by looking at the sky, looking up to heaven.

## The lost sheep {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.8.2}

Let us continue. Once again it is St Luke, this time in chapter fifteen, who speaks to us of shepherds and flocks. Remember the good shepherd, how lovingly he follows the stray sheep and goes out searching for it? Let’s read the Gospel passage: “What man of you, having a hundred sheep, if he has lost one of them, does not leave the ninety-nine in the wilderness, and go after the one which is lost, until he finds it? And when he has found it, he lays it on his shoulders, rejoicing.”5 Our Lord’s perseverance is wonderful! My daughter, my son, the devil would strip you, if he could, of all the gifts God has given you, especially faith, purity and vocation. So if the thought should ever cross your mind that you have strayed so far from God that there’s no hope of salvation – it would be a diabolical temptation to take your peace away – remember how Christ always goes to look for the soul that has gone astray. He never denies you the grace to begin again. In the Work, we see Jesus seeking out the lost sheep in every word of affection and support, in every act of clear guidance from the Directors, in the form of a correction which is full of supernatural and human sense, in some act of kindness, or some helpful piece of reading… We see his action too in all those

5 Lk 15:4-5

memorials to God’s great mercy which are dotted throughout the history of our Work. Then, if you are humble and acknowledge your mistakes, you will persevere. Try to raise your gaze to heaven; don’t just look at things from a purely selfish point of view. I repeat: have faith and hope, and trust in the love Jesus has for you. He is the Good Shepherd, and for your sake he will scale precipices and search through ravines, if need be. He will take you in his arms, hug you to his wounded breast and urge you to be faithful. It is only if you reject him with a flat ‘No’, or if you are foolishly determined to throw yourself over the precipice, that he will be unable to bring you back to the right path. Lord, knowing that you are the Good Shepherd and that we are sheep of your flock, how confident we can be and how easy it is to persevere! Quia ipse est Dominus Deus noster, et nos populus pascuae eius et oves manus eius.6 Jesus has us in his hands! Are you not deeply impressed by this way of acting, and by what our Lord adds afterwards? “When he comes home, he calls together his friends and his neighbours, saying to them, ‘Rejoice with me, for I have found my sheep which was lost.’”7 How beautiful! My daughter, my son, if you should ever go astray, a lot or a little, don’t deprive our Lord of the joy of finding you again, and of being able to say: “Here is a sheep who wants to be faithful, who wants to persevere in my flock.”

## Damming the waters {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.8.3}

Some people, even very bright ones, can be so foolish that as soon as they meet any of life’s difficulties, they quite illogically transfer it all to the terrain of vocation, as if that mistake or

6 For he is the Lord our God, and we are the people of his pasture, and the sheep of his hand: Ps 95:7 7 Lk 15:6

difficulty were an insurmountable obstacle on the path of fidelity. Don’t let yourselves be deceived! If I’ve warned you about it so often it’s because I’ve so often found it to be true: it is a source of peace, joy, reassurance and victory to know that, if I do anything stupid – something really serious – I will say so, I will talk about it at once, I will ask for help, and everything will be fine. I will do whatever I’m told, and I’ll keep going, because I have a vocation. As long as we fight tenaciously, as I have written, we will make progress and sanctify ourselves, even though we have lots of defects. There hasn’t been a single saint who hasn’t had to battle resolutely. Our mistakes shouldn’t cause us to get depressed and discouraged. Either can arise from pride or tiredness, but in both cases, anyone who goes to the Good Shepherd and speaks out clearly will find the right remedy. Things can always be put right, even when we’ve done something really serious! All it takes is to open our heart sincerely to the person we chat with, as well as to the priest who represents Christ for us, to be able to return to the fray once more with renewed energies. Nevertheless, some people see things from such an earthly point of view – like that crippled woman – that when they are faced with obstacles, instead of thinking of God’s goodness, omnipotence and fidelity, all they can think about is their own concupiscence and inadequacy. And even when they do pray, they pray in such a way that they seem to be saying: “Lord, don’t grant it to me!” As if they lacked faith in God’s power and mercy. That’s stupid! Anyone who acted like that would be like a man or woman who possessed a unique work of art, one that would enhance any of the world’s leading museums, but instead of thinking about how best to safeguard it and store it where it wouldn’t get damaged, thought: “As soon as I see a gang of stonethrowing urchins, I’ll take it out to them…” Be humble. Humility also entails a readiness to lose elegantly, to accept our shortcomings, and to experience within ourselves the

truth of our Lord’s words: sine me nihil potestis facere,8 without me you can do nothing. God never denies us his grace: he holds out his hand, and all we need to do is to try to reach that divine help. Consequently, it would be absurd, after a fall, to think: “I’ve been defeated, therefore I don’t have a vocation!” On the contrary, we have to reason as follows: “Precisely because I have been called with this Christian vocation in the Work, I will be humble, I will begin again and, with God’s grace, I have every guarantee: ubi autem abundavit delictum, superabundavit gratia,9 where sin increased, grace abounded all the more. I will be faithful in future.” Take advantage of these insights. Have you seen how they store water in countries where it’s not very plentiful in summer, but where it rains a lot in winter? It is stored in reservoirs and released during the dry season, and that way it can be used to generate electricity and irrigate the land. What I’m telling you is that during a retreat the soul receives a flood of graces; and when the person who addresses you is the Founder, even though he is only a poor human being, which is what I am, then the water is plenteous and divine. You should remember all these things, save them up in a great reservoir, and release them when needed, as a force that produces light and irrigates the parched land, so that later on you’ll be able to work away very happily and avoid draining your reserves of strength. Create a good reservoir of divine grace, of divine confidence in your vocation to Opus Dei now, and use the waters whenever you need them throughout your life; or whenever, through charity or justice, you have to help someone who is wavering. Teach them not to create stupid problems of vocation for themselves, either because of difficulties or interior falls, and to reason instead as follows: “precisely because I have a vocation, and because I don’t lack the grace of God and the help of

8 Jn 15:5 9 Rom 5:20

the Directors and of all my brothers or sisters, if I make the effort, from now on I will be victorious.” I can’t understand how people who have clearly received God’s call and have walked in its light for years can then waver. I just don’t understand it. My dearest children, you have seen the light of the star, like the Magi.10 So what if it is eclipsed occasionally? It will come back! All you have to do is to obey, be humble, be sincere, and the brightness of the star reappears. But keep looking upwards! Lift your eyes heavenwards. Trust in Jesus the Good Shepherd, who never abandons us. That way perseverance is assured.

## Everything for God {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.8.4}

Let us continue reading St Luke, once again in chapter fifteen. “What woman, having ten silver coins, if she loses one coin, does not light a lamp and sweep the house and seek diligently until she finds it?”11 And if you want to persevere you have to do the same. Our Lord likes to speak in parables because he knows what human nature is like. He knows we are like children and that we like using our imaginations when we are working something out. If some day you lose your peace of mind or are in danger of losing it – don’t forget that inner peace is worth more than a silver coin – if you ever lose control of your senses or notice that your heart wants to alter the order of charity – you understand what I mean, don’t you? – react at once. In our supernatural family there has never been any instance of the type of attachment which in some places is called “particular friendship”, nor will there ever be, because it is a dreadful thing. But if you notice that something like that is happening to you, or anything else either, turn on the light at once!

10 Cf. Mt 2:2 11 Lk 15:8

And how does one do that? By going post-haste to spiritual direction, to the confidential chat with the person who has the task of caring for your soul, and to confession, where you will get abundant light. With the help of that insight try to discover what was perhaps hidden in a forgotten corner of your soul, sweep out your heart until it is spotlessly clean, worthy of God, to whom you have dedicated your entire life. Your thoughts, your desires, your work, your gaze, ought to be heavenwards: Christ doesn’t want to share your heart, he wants it all. Reflecting on the joy of the woman who found her little treasure, our Lord continues: “Just so, I tell you, there is joy before the angels of God over one sinner who repents.”12 If there is such great joy in heaven – and Jesus says so, just so we can appreciate how true it is

– that the angels celebrate the conversion of a sinner, think how much more they must celebrate when a soul, with God’s help, does not stray from the path, does not lose the treasure, this marvellous pearl, this priceless jewel. Let us continue our meditation on chapter fifteen of St Luke’s Gospel, this time the parable of the prodigal son. This certainly gives us lots of material all by itself for the end of a retreat! You know the scene well. A man had two sons. Remember how the younger one asked him: “Father, give me the share of property that falls to me.”13 He is typical of lots of young fellows or brash young women nowadays, incapable of doing a decent day’s work, of looking after a family, of carrying out the great enterprise of getting married or of giving themselves to God… They make lots of sacrifices to keep fit or to keep their weight down but are incapable of the least act of self-denial to please God. That’s why my daughters and sons are so much needed in the world, in the middle of society.

12 Lk 15:10 13 Lk 15:12

Let us return to that poor fellow who squanders his inheritance so shamefully and ends up looking after a herd of unclean animals. He gets to the point of envying the poor beasts and wanting to eat what they ate. Wouldn’t it be a great pity if one of us were to feel that way, envying the “food” of worldly-minded people, those who haven’t given themselves completely to God? When I say “food” I mean materialistic forms of entertainment and behaviour; in a word, the worldly, dissolute lifestyle that is so prevalent among your relatives, friends and colleagues, and which we cannot share because, although we are present in the world, we cannot be worldly. Our Lord says so clearly when he prays for his disciples: de mundo non sunt.14 And he says the same about each of us. Having prayed, “I do not pray that you should take them out of the world, but that you should keep them from the evil one,” he adds: “They are not of the world, even as I am not of the world.”15 It would be a great pity if you were to hanker after the acorns the pigs ate. You understand what I mean, don’t you? Well, let us continue.

## Fruitfulness {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.8.5}

St Luke offers us another of our Lord’s parables, once again in chapter thirteen: “A man had a fig tree planted in his vineyard; and he came seeking fruit on it and found none.”16 Consider how God has taken you and me from the soil of this world and planted us in his house, in his vineyard – in the Holy Church, and within the Church, in Opus Dei. He comes to us every day, every moment, to see if we are producing fruit, because he has a right to find it. But see how the parable unfolds. When the landowner saw that the tree wasn’t producing any fruit, “he said to the vinedresser, ‘Lo, these three years I have come seeking fruit on this fig tree, and I

14 They are not of the world: Jn 17:14 15 Jn 17:15-16 16 Lk 13:6

find none. Cut it down; why should it use up the ground?’ And he answered him, ‘Let it alone, sir, this year also, till I dig about it and put on manure. And if it bears fruit next year, well and good; but if not, you can cut it down.’”17 When you think about your perseverance, think about the goodness of this God who comes to look for fruit: and when he doesn’t find it he doesn’t get impatient with us, but rather he fills us with his grace, he helps us to bear fruit. Fruit, my children: fruit! From that fig tree we pass to the other, the one Jesus cursed.18 The mouth which always uttered words of charity now pronounces words which are harsh and strong: “May no fruit ever come from you again!”19 Never, never more! Totally condemned, completely lost. From being full of life, with the possibility of producing sweet juicy fruit, it becomes a dead trunk, a lump of wood only fit for the fire. So you’re for the fire, my son? No, not at all! Your role is to produce fruit! All you need to do is to fulfil well, deeply, the norms of piety, because in these practices of Christian life are to be found all the virtues proper to a soul dedicated to God. That way, if you fulfil them habitually, with love, you will persevere in bearing fruit. My son, I would like you to reflect on two more parables: the grain of mustard seed, and the leaven. Do you remember that tiny little seed which ends up becoming such a leafy tree that the birds of the air come to seek shelter in its branches?20 You have come at a very good time. As long as I am alive, each of you is in a certain sense a co-founder. There are many apostolic undertakings waiting for us all over the world: little ones, like a mustard seed, in which we can love, and suffer, and work. If you are faithful you will see the seed grow.

17 Lk 13:7-9 18 Cf. Mt 21:18-19 19 Mt 21:19 20 Cf. Mt 13:31-32

And how will you persevere? Very simply, by carrying out the role of the leaven. Do you remember the parable?21 Perhaps you’ve never seen it done, because you’re too young. Nowadays everything is so automated, but in former times, when I was a child, bread was baked at home. I remember how the flour used to be prepared and the dough kneaded. But what I particularly liked was how a small portion of dough was kept from the previous batch to act as leaven for the next. I used to marvel at how the leaven was mixed into the new batch, without attracting any attention, giving taste and quality to the whole “multitude.” The dough swelled and swelled and was transformed into crunchy bread, good to taste and nourishing to eat. Your role is to be the leaven in this human race that has to become bread for God’s palate. Your desire to serve our Lord should lead you to spread out everywhere, merging into the multitude, but imbuing it with Christian spirit.
