There shouldn’t be a single environment where the influence of this divine leaven doesn’t make itself felt. Your hearts should fill with happiness thinking about being leaven in the dough. Our lives are not selfish: we are in the front line, in the blood-stream of society, noiselessly, without stupidly trying to attract attention; reaching out to all hearts, transforming them into wholesome bread, which is the peace – the joy and peace

– of all families and all nations. But to become leaven one condition is necessary: to go unnoticed. The leaven cannot have any effect unless it is mixed into the dough and becomes part of it. I will never tire of reminding you, my children, that you shouldn’t be distinguishable in any way from your colleagues, your equals. What we have to seek is to remain where we were hitherto, being what we are: ordinary Christians with a simple, ordinary lifestyle.

21 Cf. Mt 13:33

We have considered how you ought to be present everywhere in society, but like Jesus during his first thirty years. We have to be present everywhere, like our Lord, quasi in occulto,22 without parading our self-giving. Because if we want to be leaven in all the circumstances of society it is essential for us to be saints. The task is easy, if we let ourselves be guided by our Lord. We will then indeed be abundantly fruitful, we will become a leaven of Christian life in society. Not just you, but also the Associates, both men and women; and the Supernumeraries, who have received our same calling; and the Cooperators, who will be extremely effective. There shouldn’t be any honest environment in the world where you don’t make the wonderful power of our Christian vocation present. In the Upper Room in Jerusalem our Lady kept the Apostles and the holy women united awaiting the coming of the Holy Spirit.23 Afterwards, the first Twelve scattered throughout the whole world, taking Christ’s light, salt and leaven with them to all nations.24 That is how we children of God will behave if at every moment we are very united to the Blessed Virgin.

22 Not publicly but in private: Jn 7:10 23 Cf. Acts 1:14 24 Cf. Mt 28:19-20

# CHRIST IS RISEN! * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.9}

“My Lord and my God, I firmly believe that you are here.” Make a good act of faith! God is present here among us; and he is also present in your souls in grace, and in mine. “That you see me, that you hear me. I adore you with profound reverence.” It is a greeting full of praise. And immediately we make our apologies for the offences we have committed, like when we have treated a friend badly. “I ask you for pardon for my sins.” And since it is certainly true that without God’s help we can do nothing pleasing in his eyes, we add: “and grace to make this time of prayer fruitful.” Afterwards we seek recommendations, as people do everywhere. Personally I don’t like making recommendations; what I do instead is make introductions. Nevertheless, in our relationship with God we do need recommendations: “My Mother Immaculate, St Joseph, my Father and Lord, my Guardian Angel, intercede for me.” My children, we are in the presence of God our Lord, ready to have our usual conversation with him. I would like you to meditate with me on the scenes Holy Mother Church places before our eyes during the Easter Triduum; because Christ’s life is in a certain sense repeated in each one of us, both in its interior process – its sanctification – and in its external expression.

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 29 March 1959, Easter Sunday

## Running away for twenty centuries {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.9.1}

Haven’t you suffered on seeing our Lord all alone these past few days? In the meditation this morning I reminded your brothers on the General Council of St Peter’s fervent promise: “Even if I must die with you, I will not deny you.”1 He proclaimed it with all his heart and will; but then came his human weakness, his denials and his flight. And how many of the other disciples did the same as Peter! Many had eaten the bread and fish multiplied by Christ; others had received light for their blind eyes. A good number had had life restored to their dead limbs, or were part of the huge crowd which followed him because his doctrine was divine and touched their hearts... But there came a time, after the hosannas which accompanied his triumphant entry into Jerusalem,2 when Christ was left alone, nailed to the plank of wood on which he was to die. Our Lord was all alone, because all his disciples had fled. And who stayed with him? Mary, with a small group of faithful women, and an adolescent. All the others had turned cowards. My children, right now let us – you in your youth and I, who already am starting to grow old – firmly resolve to be humble. And at the same time as we tell our Lord fervently that we will be faithful, that we are ready to make any sacrifice, let us say to him: Jesus, with your grace; Mary, my Mother, I need your help. I am so weak, I commit so many faults, I make so many small mistakes that if you forsake me I feel capable of making much bigger ones! As I speak, pray to our Lord yourself, in the silence of your heart, and go back over your past life. For I am sure that in spite of your youth you already have had some experience of pride, of sensuality, and of the blindness caused by your shortcomings, just as I have even sadder experiences than you because I am older and have more defects. But in spite of everything we dare to tell God

1 Mt 26:35 2 Cf. Mt 21:9

our Lord: “I’m ready for anything, I really want to become what you called me to be when you gave me this vocation: not just alter Christus, another Christ, but ipse Christus, Christ himself. But I’m so weak, so prone to error, that unless you help me I will achieve nothing useful.”

## Approaching Jesus through humility {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.9.2}

Now is the time, my child, to draw close to Jesus like Mary of Bethany, the woman in whose house Jesus sought peace and repose.3 She was moved to throw herself on her knees and kiss his feet.4 My children, see how Christ has washed our feet, like the first Twelve. He is who he is, and we are who we are: poor creatures. But listen to what he tells the Apostles at the Last Supper: “If I then, your Lord and Teacher, have washed your feet, you also ought to wash one another’s feet.”5 These words from St John’s Gospel have to lead you to examine how you’re doing, not only as regards good manners – politeness – toward your brothers, but genuine on-going charity, which has to be an ever-present characteristic of your relationships: how do you behave toward your Directors and toward your brothers? Bear in mind that a young person of your age, even though he has embarked on a divine path, can sometimes experience very difficult moments, psychologically speaking, and feel he is to some extent misunderstood. You may have seen it perhaps in friends and acquaintances: some get so blinded by pride welling up from the bottom of their souls that they begin to despise the mentality of their parents, teachers and elders, even though it is good, or at least worthy of respect. My son, examine yourself here and now, hic et nunc: how do you act, both exteriorly and interiorly? Are you truly humble? Are

3 Cf. Lk 10:38-42 4 Cf. Jn 11:2 5 Jn 13:14

you capable of mortifying your self-love for the sake of charity? Are you capable of accepting the humiliations God asks of you, in small matters that don’t jeopardise the truth? Ask our Lord now to grant you humility, because if pride is not taken in hand promptly, with the passage of time it only gets worse. And as well as prayer, you also need to practise giving way in topics of conversation that are a matter of opinion, that don’t affect Christ’s doctrinal and moral teaching. Don’t always try to have the last word. Let it be! Learn to hold your tongue and give way graciously, out of charity. In the last analysis, it’s a question of disappearing, and that means being humble and assimilating the spirit of the Work thoroughly. With the passage of time people sometimes experience solitude in their professional, social and even interior lives – the solitude of Christ on the Cross. But if they realise that they are ipse Christus, Christ himself, they can overcome this feeling of loneliness by seeking help from this Lord of ours who has conquered death and now reigns victorious over all things. And after tasting defeat, they too are victorious and make even more effective progress in their vocation. My children, love Christ and his solitude. And don’t let your mistakes get you down, as long as you try to pick yourself up and start again. For, I repeat, if we feel that we are ipse Christus we will seek help from Christ on the Cross, we will be victorious with him, we will feel part of his marvellous company and we will love the solitude of Christ on the wood. It is a good devotion – you and I may sometimes be moved interiorly to practise it – to spread your arms wide in the form of a cross and go like that to kiss the feet of the Crucified Lord. This devotion is based on humility and shows our love for Christ, for his Cross and for his holy Passion.

## Always victorious {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.9.3}

In the liturgy of today’s Mass we read a marvellous passage: haec est dies quam fecit Dominus: exsultemus et laetemur in ea.6 This is the great day which the Lord has made, the day of his victory. Where are the soldiers the authorities had posted? Where are the seals they had placed on the stone closing the entrance to the sepulchre? Where are the men who crucified Jesus? Today it is Christ’s enemies who are fleeing, for he never loses battles. My children, during these thirty-one years of my vocation in Opus Dei I have often had to ask our Lord for forgiveness… At the same time, however, I can assure you that I have given you the best of myself. I have tried to pass on to you with the utmost fidelity all that God our Lord has given me; and when I didn’t do so properly, I have acknowledged my mistake immediately, I have asked our Lord and those who were with me for forgiveness, and I have returned immediately to the fray. In spite of all this, I am also conscious of all the times Christ has clearly been victorious in my poor flesh. The Work has also had its Cross, but Christ has always come out the winner. You were not aware that we were being persecuted for many years, even by good people. You were not aware of it because the Father forbade anyone to write or speak about it. It was the same sort of persecution as our Lord suffered at the hands of the chief priests: calumnies, lies, deceit, and insults: in the press, in private conversations… We were an object of ridicule for everyone. Everyone thought they had a right to spit on us. And yet we were happy in that solitude. We knew where to find Christ and we knew he was with us. So we kept our mouths shut, and smiled, worked and prayed. I didn’t try to defend myself until I was told to do so by the Holy See.

6 Ps 118:24

That took place not all that long ago. We were insulted from the pulpit and in the confessional. Some civil authorities were prepared to put me in jail. People spread calumnies about us in communities of nuns, and in one of them a book was publicly burned… Some friends turned their backs on us, but there were others who were very loyal: our Lord will have prepared them a very special place in heaven! You were completely unaware of it, thank God! Yes, my children, that’s how the Work was accomplished – with no money and with no virtues. All I had was twenty-six years of age and a good sense of humour. The Work was accomplished through the holy lives of your first brothers, with their constant smile, with their prayer, their work and their silence. That’s how Opus Dei came about; it has had its Cross and Resurrection, soundless but wonderful. And now, like a new Pentecost, you can hear a great variety of tongues, manifesting the Spirit of God, the catholicity of our spirit. My children, love Jesus and his Blessed Mother. For, like Jesus, we have always kept very close to his Mother, Mary, the Mother of God, who has been the Mother of Opus Dei, the Queen of Opus Dei, our beauty… Joined in filial love to the Mother of God, we have never been left without the help of her tender smile in any of the difficult times. You will always be safe and sound if you show just a little bit of good will. You will be safe and sound in the victory of the Resurrection. The key thing is never to allow ourselves to stray from our Lord’s presence. Earthly things are only as important as we make them. If we are immersed in God nothing which happens here below will disturb our interior peace. When our mistakes and shortcomings cause us to give importance to these insignificant events and let them get us down, it’s because we want to. If we stay close to our Lord, however, we feel safe. When we are united to Christ’s Cross, to the glory of the Resurrection, and to the fire of Pentecost, every obstacle can be overcome.

Of course, sometimes sufferings can last somewhat longer: our body can give out on us and fall sick, so we have to drag it around like a tired donkey. Nevertheless you and I have the perfect remedy for that. Not only do we have the sacraments like any other Catholic, but in addition we have all the means of sanctification the Work provides, and among them, the confidential chat. Open your heart really wide! When Sacred Scripture says that when one has found a friend one has found a treasure,7 that is no exaggeration. True friends are few and far between here on earth; but there is no better friend than one’s brother. Open your heart in the fraternal chat, and you will see how much fortitude, peace and serenity you get. You will see things in their proper perspective and will not attach importance to the things of the earth, because they do not have any. This will give you such serenity, even humanly speaking, that your mind will be made clearer and your will strengthened.

## Every day a resurrection {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.9.4}

Haec est dies quam fecit Dominus: exsultemus et laetemur in ea,8 this is the great day which the Lord has made; let us rejoice. In the first Epistle to the Corinthians St Paul writes: expurgate vetus fermentum, ut sitis nova conspersio,9 cleanse out the old leaven that you may become a new batch. And in another passage he invites us to transform ourselves in novitate sensus.10 Now is the time to make a change, my children. What holiness means is being reborn every day, starting anew each and every day. You won’t be discouraged by your mistakes if you have the good will to begin again every time.

7 Cf. Sir 6:14 8 Ps 118:24 9 1 Cor 5:7 10 By the renewal of your mind: Rom 12:2

There was once a poor priest whose defects weighed on him very heavily, perhaps not without reason. Every day in his convent, he had to put the Blessed Sacrament in the monstrance where it was exposed for adoration, but he was constantly assailed by scrupulous thoughts. Until one day his superior called him in and told him with a lot of supernatural sense: “So you have many, many shortcomings, and not just small ones? Then gather them all together in a heap and put them under the monstrance, and you will be raising our Lord that much higher.” My son, try to be faithful your whole life long. And when you feel you’re not, ask God for help. Strive eagerly and cheerfully, in a sporting spirit, to make progress in your supernatural struggle, and you’ll conquer. Take all your shortcomings, all those obstacles blocking your way, and place them under Christ’s feet so he will be raised on high and triumph – and you along with him. Don’t let anything bother you, rectify your intention, try and try again, and in the end, if you yourself are not able, our Lord will come and help you to leap the hurdle, the hurdle of holiness. This is the way to renew ourselves, to conquer ourselves: every day becomes another resurrection, reassuring us that we will make it to the end of the road, where love awaits us. Confitemini Domino, quoniam bonus, quoniam in saeculum misericordia eius,11 praise the Lord for he is good, for his mercy endures for ever. This psalm is very much to the point. Because he is good. Say it softly, on your own: quoniam bonus. How good God is! He has sought us out, he has brought us to his Work, he has initiated us in this holy way of being effective, of loving all mankind as coming from God, and bestowing on them peace and joy! My children, thank Jesus for these things, because he is good. And so he forgives our mistakes, and is grateful for our attempts to rectify our errors, our continuous renewal, and our effort to be like him, to draw close to him.

11 Ps 118:1

## Difficulties and faith {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.9.5}

Today’s Gospel is taken from St Mark: “As soon as the feast of the Sabbath was over, Mary Magdalene and Mary, mother of James, and Salome bought spices to anoint the body of Jesus.”12 What marvellous generosity! They cannot expect anything from our Lord any more. Nevertheless they go to pay him their last respects. By contrast, look at the shoddy example of Peter and the rest. The faith of these women really moves me, and makes me recall many good things about my mother, as I’m sure you also recall many wonderful details about yours… I thank God our Lord for having wanted there to be women in Opus Dei. On the thirteenth of February 1930 I still was thinking that there wouldn’t be any women in Opus Dei; and just a few hours later, on the fourteenth of the same month, the women’s section was born… I repeat that those women did not expect anything. Jesus was a corpse. They had seen him ill-treated and crucified. The violence of the Passion! The unanimity of the condemnation! “May his blood be on us and on our children.”13 Joseph of Arimathea and Nicodemus alone opposed the injustice. I like to say that they had the spirit of the Work, because they did not proclaim themselves as disciples of the Master, nor did they accompany him during his moments of triumph, but they were prepared to defend him publicly before the authorities who condemned him to death. Later, they were courageous and generous enough to retrieve his dead body. Today the Cross crowns the most noble buildings, but then it was just a scaffold. Those in power were against Jesus; likewise, the townspeople. The women knew about the soldiers’ presence. They knew that the tomb was completely sealed, but just the same they spent their money, and at sunrise they went to anoint the dead body

12 Mk 16:1 13 Mt 27:25

of our Lord: et valde mane una sabbatorum, veniunt ad monumentum.14 They were truly courageous! “And they were saying to one another: Who will roll away the stone for us from the door of the tomb?”15 The stone was very large. This often happens. The difficulties are clear to see, but if there is love we are not deterred by them. With audacity, determination and courage, what has to be done is done! Who would remove the stone? They couldn’t do it by themselves. Nevertheless, they kept heading toward the tomb. My son, what about you and me? How are we as regards hesitations? Do we have that same holy determination? Or do we have to confess that we feel ashamed as we contemplate the determination and audacity of these holy women? When they reached the tomb, “they saw that the stone was rolled back.”16 That always happens. When we make up our minds to do what we have to, difficulties are easily overcome. Be obedient! It is not possible to grow in a single isolated virtue. Christian life is like a tapestry in which virtues form a strong weave. It is not a single strand, but a tightly-woven cloth which covers us from head to foot. If one of the capital virtues, such as obedience, is lacking, the cloth gets torn. Sometimes it is difficult to obey, even objectively, and sometimes really hard. But every one of us knows (some more clearly than others) that when we decide to serve God the difficulties disappear. It is the teaching of the Gospel of today’s Mass. When the holy women arrived at the tomb, “looking up, they saw that the stone was rolled back.”17 That is why we have to make a firm resolution: “Lord, grant me your grace to be brave when the

14 And very early on the first day of the week they went to the tomb: Mk 16:2 15 Mk 16:3 16 Mk 16:4 17 Ibid.

time comes for practising all the virtues which are indispensable for serving you.” “And entering the tomb, they saw a young man sitting on the right side, dressed in a white robe; and they were amazed. And he said to them, ‘Do not be amazed; you seek Jesus of Nazareth, who was crucified. He has risen, he is not here; see the place where they laid him. But go, tell his disciples and Peter that he is going before you to Galilee; there you will see him, as he told you.’”18 “Tell his disciples and Peter…” Love Christ in his Vice-Christ, the Roman Pontiff, who for us is the third love of our lives! Our great loves are: Christ, with God the Father and the Holy Spirit; our Lady; and the Pope. That way we will always be victorious in this beautiful, splendid war which always leads to victory and to peace, for us and for all mankind.

18 Mk 16:5-7

# PERSEVERANCE IN LOVE * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.10}

You have taken a few days’ break from your normal work. You have come together to listen to the talks and meditations of your brothers who are priests, and to carry out your own very personal examination of conscience; and so you have renewed your resolve to fight interiorly. Now all that is needed is your perseverance: qui autem perseveraverit usque in finem, hic salvus erit,1 he who endures to the end will be saved.

## Constancy in the spiritual life {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.10.1}

I must speak to you, my children, of something which is not altogether in keeping with my spirit but which nevertheless cannot be lost sight of. I repeat: it does not become the spirit given me by God and which I must pass on to you, because our way is one of love, and we are not motivated by fear, in the sense of dread. This life ends very quickly and afterwards there is reward or punishment, and so I understand perfectly that a soul would wish to be completely purified for its eternal meeting with the Blessed Trinity. For this reason I look upon Purgatory as an expression of God’s mercy. I think that God has gone to that extreme because of the love he has for us. I like to think that our journey will end in heaven, after enjoying half a heaven on earth thanks to the call we have

* Notes of a meditation given at the end of a retreat in Rome, 4 March 1960 1 Mt 10:22

received. But don’t ever forget that this happy ending is only made possible by perseverance. Have you ever seen one of those television programmes where some poor unfortunate is asked a question and given just thirty seconds to answer? My children, what each of you, what I myself have chosen as the purpose of my life this very morning, with all its ins and outs, with all its grandeur and little details, all adds up to just a few seconds. Time flows by, life is ebbing away…! Sacred Scripture tells us that man’s life is like grass: it blooms with the first light of sun and by evening it is already withered.2 If we were to ask a poet he would say that “in the brilliance of a lightning flash we are born, and its glow still lingers when we die…”† Don’t forget that everybody has to die: statutum est hominibus semel mori.3 Everybody, you and I as well. Some die when they are young, like you, and others when they have begun to grow old, like me: children, the aged, and men and women in the prime of life. We can’t afford to waste time! This is what I want to remind you of: time is short, so very, very short. And so let’s ask ourselves: how shall we manage to persevere? By being just as determined and by trying as hard as other people do in human affairs. Have you not seen how fathers or mothers give their lives for the well-being of their families and refuse to let themselves be overwhelmed by difficulties? And other people do the same for what they call their honour, which is sometimes nothing more than their pride. Have you not seen how they struggle, and persist, without losing heart? I remember once giving a clergy retreat in Avila, in the province of Castile, where there is an old palace that belonged to the Duke of Abrantes. I was struck by some words inscribed on the main façade,

2 Cf. Ps 103:15 † Al brillar un relámpago nacemos / y aún dura su fulgor cuando morimos: Gustavo Adolfo Becquer, Spanish poet, 1836-1870 (Tr.) 3 It is appointed for men to die once: Heb 9:27

which I was told were the result of the stubbornness of its illustrious owner. From the palace, through a postern gate in the wall, he could enter and leave the city directly, but the town council forced him to have it closed up. He did so, but he had the following inscription set over a window on the façade: “Where one door is closed, another is opened.” Stubbornness! Perseverance! Are you and I like that in our affairs? When something does not go well for us today, tomorrow it will! My children, be stubborn. Bring your stubbornness onto a supernatural level.

## Means to persevere {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.10.2}

And what are the means to persevere? We have them in the practices of piety which imbue our life. I can assure you that whoever fulfils the norms, whoever struggles to fulfil them, in health and sickness, in youth, maturity and old age, come rain or shine, when it’s easy and when it’s hard, is predestined if they persevere to the end: I am certain of their holiness. Because in the norms of Christian piety we find prayer, presence of God, friendship with the Blessed Trinity, Father, Son and Holy Spirit, and with the Mother of God, Daughter of God and Spouse of God, and with St Joseph, whom I call our Father and Lord and the thought of whom gladdens my heart, and with our Guardian Angel. Why do we take so much care of our plan of life? To carry out our daily work. It is a constant struggle, but peace is to be found in struggle. Peace is a consequence of victory over ourselves! And whenever we suffer a defeat – a little one, because the children of God are normally defeated only in little things – it is an opportunity to humble ourselves and continue fighting: militia est vita hominis super terram,4 man’s life on earth is a battle. Don’t be under any illusions: when you get old you’ll have to fight just the same as you do now. And I’m not talking only about temptations of the flesh: a worse enemy is to be found in pride, vanity, stubbornness, and not

4 Job 7:1

wanting to bear Christ’s yoke on one’s shoulders, even though he has said: iugum enim meum suave est, et onus meum leve,5 my yoke is easy, and my burden is light. And what other essential means do I recommend to assure fidelity? How can we be faithful and persevering in our vocation? By being sincere. Firstly, sincere with God. This can be hard, because human beings tend toward anonymity. People in influential positions in society receive tons of anonymous correspondence; anything I get I just throw in the waste-paper basket. But… many men and women prefer to remain anonymous before God. I have seen communist mobs organise atrocities and many good people going along with it. I have seen churches burned, convents set alight, innocent people murdered with hardly anyone realising what was happening: all done by an anonymous crowd. How pitiable if, instead of living face to face with God, we prefer to live in anonymity! I love vocal prayer, and I have often recommended it to you. But what about mental prayer, where we are face to face with God without set forms of words? Without anonymity, Lord, I come to take my place at your side. Take good care of your mental prayer and your continual presence of God. Be sincere with yourselves as well. That’s even more difficult. You’ll have heard it said that the best business on earth would be to buy people up for the price they are worth, and sell them for what they think they are worth. It’s very difficult to be truly sincere. There is always a reason not to be. And finally, sincere with our Directors. Why have we come to Opus Dei, my children? We have come for something very specific: to be saints. It is like when someone goes to hospital for treatment. If, when the time comes to take their clothes off and be examined, they say “no way”, and if, when asked to describe their symptoms, they refuse to talk, then instead of being admitted to hospital they should be locked up in an asylum. We have come to Opus Dei to be

5 Mt 11:30

saints. And so we have to overcome our fear of letting ourselves be known, because if we don’t we can’t be cured. Your Directors and the priests need to know you well. Don’t ever try to hide anything! Without that sincerity with God, with yourself, and with those in charge, I see your perseverance as being in jeopardy. On the other hand, if you are always simple with God, with yourself, and with the Directors, you will persevere with ease. It should also be said that being reluctant to say what has happened to us is the height of foolishness. What some people try to hide, others have no problem about proclaiming publicly, sometimes even exaggerating it, over a cup of coffee. But when serving God some people are overcome by an extraordinary sense of embarrassment… Let me recall what my mother used to say when I was a small boy and wouldn’t come out to meet her visitors: “Josemaría, only be ashamed to sin.” Is that clear? We should only be ashamed to offend God! If you have offended him, then go straight to the doctor to show him the injury, to describe the symptoms of the disease. If you don’t, you can’t be cured. But if you speak out, on the other hand, it will always be easy to persevere. Among your brothers who joined me before the Spanish Civil War there was one whom I was very fond of. Afterwards things got complicated. Years later he asked for the Admission as a Supernumerary. And when he speaks now to your younger brothers he reminds them to tell the truth always if they want to be faithful. See what wonderful and appropriate means we have to be healthy, pure and reliable, so we can live like strong Christian warriors in the world and always be victorious! God does not lose battles!

## Enemies of perseverance {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.10.3}

Discouragement, my children, is an enemy of perseverance. The soul can encounter moments of struggle, both at your age, and also

in middle age and even later – it happens to me too; moments when the soul feels moved to cry out, with St Paul, those words I find so consoling: “I see in my members another law at war with the law of my mind and making me captive to the law of sin which dwells in my members. Wretched man that I am! Who will deliver me from this body of death?”6 One feels then the inroads of pride and sensuality and a longing, not for freedom, but for licentiousness. And if we are not forewarned, that is when discouragement makes its appearance. My children, why does this happen? Because of your inadequacies and mine? Because of your defeats, which are sometimes continual? Because of a really big pitfall you weren’t expecting? Open your heart, be simple and get up again, with renewed affection, joy, trust and strength, a strength that comes from God. Dominus illuminatio mea et salus mea, quem timebo? Si consistant adversum me castra, non timebit cor meum.7 Don’t think I’m exaggerating when I say that in Opus Dei we are contemplative souls. It is my own experience, and it has to be yours too: in dialogue with God at every moment. Do you want an example? How about this? You catch your finger in the door and let out an expletive, but then you say: “Lord, it’s nothing at all. I could well deserve to go to hell. I’ll offer it up…” And when you experience humiliation, when the earth seems about to swallow you up, remember that Christ of ours, scourged and crowned with thorns; and then, without any outward show, unite yourself to him. Cry out silently: Dominus illuminatio mea et salus mea, quem timebo?8 Remember that “omnia in bonum”,9 and be firmly convinced that when we love God, anything that happens to us has been willed by him for our good.

6 Rom 7:23-24 7 The Lord is my light and my salvation; whom shall I fear? Though a host encamp against me, yet I will be confident: Ps 27:1,3 8 The Lord is my light and my salvation; whom shall I fear?: Ps 27:1 9 All things are for the best: cf. Rom 8:28

Pray to God that I never cease to dialogue with him. May this Father of yours become a good son of God, and be forever in his company: may I be a contemplative. It is to him that our last thought at night should go up, peacefully; and to him too our first thought of the day, with a serviam! I will serve! This isn’t fanaticism, it’s love, because you and I have fallen in love. A further enemy of perseverance is lukewarmness. Let me read you some words from the Apocalypse: scio opera tua… “I know your works; you are neither cold nor hot. Would that you were cold or hot! So, because you are lukewarm, and neither cold nor hot, I will spew you out of my mouth.”10 Don’t be annoyed if I tell you I have known whole nations like that, neither cold nor hot… The result is that everything is submerged in the most dreadful lukewarmness, everything seems fine to them. Utinam frgidus esses aut calidus! 11 My dearest children, don’t ever be lukewarm. Lukewarm individuals have hearts of clay, hearts of wretched flesh. From time to time I have encountered hard hearts – with apologies to the doctors: I was going to say that I have actually touched them – hearts that are hard and cold, but noble; on coming into contact with the warmth of the Heart of Christ they melt like bronze into tears of love and atonement and are set afire! And there are other hearts which are made of clay: these crack and crumble into dust. Disgusting, my children! O Jesus, keep lukewarmness well away from us! Anything but lukewarmness! Shall I warn you of something else? There is a stupid little enemy, but a very effective one nonetheless, namely, a lack of diligence in examination. The examinations of conscience have been included among our Norms for a very good reason. If you ever get scrupulous about your examination of conscience you should try the following very short one. “What have I done wrong? Forgive

10 Rev 3:15-16 11 Would that you were cold or hot!: ibid.

me, Lord. What have I done well? Thank you, Lord. What could I have done better?” – and a resolution. It just takes two minutes, or half a minute. Then there’s the particular examination, that specific area where you have been bringing the enemy to battle, just to make sure he doesn’t bring you to battle on a point that would be harder for you. Never omit it! A fourth enemy of perseverance is lack of love. My dearest children, if there is no love in our lives, then our celibacy is meaningless. If we don’t fall in love, then instead of being saints we’ll just be repulsive old bachelors or spinsters. Build up your relationship with Christ! When people came to see me in the early days I often got them to read books on our Lord’s Passion. Fall in love with Jesus! Be another of the people with him; allow your heart to delight in him! You will find yourself coming closer to him and will fall in love with him. In spite of all your passions and weaknesses, you will be faithful, you will persevere.

## Confidence in God {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.10.4}

We have spoken about the means to keep going, and the enemies of perseverance. I would like us to consider two more items. First, how God rewards loyalty. Second, the things I wrote about it – in response to God’s will – in our particular law. We open the New Testament at chapter fifteen of St Matthew’s Gospel, and we hear these words from God’s mouth: “I have compassion on the crowd, because they have been with me now three days, and have nothing to eat.”12 He had just cured the dumb, the blind, the lame, the sick, whole multitudes of people who came to him. But Christ’s heart is overflowing with love and he has compassion on all those people who have been following him for three whole days!

12 Mt 15:32

Lord, you’re listening to us now! I seek you in the centre of my soul, as these sons of mine do too. My God! Those men followed you for three days, chasing after you as if you were an acrobat or a conjuror, and some of them just wanting to take what you said and twist it,13 and after three days you rewarded them with the great miracle of the multiplication of the bread. Then what will you not do for us? Turn to Jesus in all confidence, my children, especially when you’re under pressure: tell him your problems in all sincerity. Don’t you see how you have won him over with the sincerity of your response? Don’t you realise that it is he who has sought you out, who has brought you to the Work? Tell him: “Lord, we’ve been following you for more than three days, with love and sacrifice.” In chapter eleven of St Luke’s Gospel we find these other words of our Lord: “Which of you who has a friend will go to him at midnight and say to him, ‘Friend, lend me three loaves; for a friend of mine has arrived on a journey, and I have nothing to set before him’; and he will answer from within, ‘Do not bother me; the door is now shut, and my children are with me in bed; I cannot get up and give you anything’? I tell you, though he will not get up and give him anything because he is his friend, yet because of his importunity he will rise and give him whatever he needs. And I tell you, Ask, and it will be given you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you. For every one who asks receives, and he who seeks finds, and to him who knocks it will be opened.”14 What more could you want? Heaven and earth will pass away before a single word spoken by Jesus Christ fails to be fulfilled! This promise of our Lord’s endows us with fortitude. This is the reward he gives. Ask, seek, call out. Prayer, prayer, prayer! This is the way.

13 Cf. Lk 20:20 14 Lk 11:5-10

Some of my older sons will have heard me comment on the text of Scripture I have written down here. It is a very beautiful passage from the Acts of the Apostles.15 Peter has been thrown into prison. You all know the story. The whole Church is praying for him. An angel wakes him up, removes his chains, and leads him out. He makes for the house of a disciple where his brethren in the faith are all gathered together in prayer. He knocks on the door, and keeps at it until a servant girl comes out to answer it – and she gets scared when she sees him. How very natural! Here is her name – many illustrious women would have given all their fortune to be mentioned in Holy Scripture, but instead, the one who does appear is this servant girl – Rhoda, the woman who got scared and wouldn’t open the door. Some said, “‘Angelus eius est! It must be his angel!’ But Peter continued knocking; and when they opened, they saw him and were amazed.”16 “Ask, and it will be given you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you. For everyone who asks receives, and he who seeks finds, and to him who knocks it will be opened.”17 We have no alternative but to persevere. Ask, ask, ask. Don’t you see what I do? What I try to do is the following. When I want something, I make all my children pray for it, and I ask them to offer their communions, their rosaries, and very many mortifications and aspirations, thousands of them! If each of us personally perseveres, God our Lord will give us everything we need to be effective in spreading his kingdom on earth. “If you, evil as you are, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will your heavenly Father give the good

15 Cf. Acts 12:1 ff 16 Acts 12:15-16 17 Lk 11:9-10

Spirit to those who ask him?”18 Do you see how God treats those who persevere? Knock, knock and it will be opened to you!

## Assuring fidelity {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.10.5}

In chapter two of the Acts of the Apostles we read this phrase, which has been inscribed in some of our oratories. Erant autem perseverantes in doctrina apostolorum et communicatione fractionis panis et orationibus.19 In doctrina apostolorum: Faith. In communicatione fractionis panis: the Bread. Et orationibus: the Word. Faith, Bread, Word! We’ll persevere, we’ll be victorious, and after a life of happiness on earth we’ll have all that love awaiting us in heaven. There is another passage of the Gospel I want to read to you so we can consider it together: “Which of you, desiring to build a tower, does not first sit down and count the cost, whether he has enough to complete it? Otherwise, when he has laid a foundation, and is not able to finish, all who see it begin to mock him, saying, This man began to build, and was not able to finish.”20 It seems that everyone in the Gospel is pointing at that unfortunate man and laughing at him: he “began to build, and was not able to finish.” What sort of person doesn’t stop to think whether he’ll be able to put the roof on the building and not leave it half done? But we can! We have the means! My children, “He who endures to the end will be saved.”21 The words are Jesus’ own. With supernatural and human clarity I have written that our perseverance in the Work is totally voluntary. You are here because you want to be, which seems to me to be the most supernatural of all reasons. A human being receives God’s grace,

18 Lk 11:13 19 And they devoted themselves to the apostles’ teaching and fellowship, to the breaking of bread and the prayers: Acts 2:42 20 Lk 14:28-30 21 Mt 10:22

feels its strength and responds: ecce ego, quia vocasti me,22 here I am, because you have called me. Sometimes you will have no alternative but to bring the gospel parable to life and go out in search of the lost sheep that has got separated from the flock.23 Don’t ever feel afraid of a sheep that’s got the mange: it would be criminal to allow it to be lost through lack of firmness and fortitude, through not using the means at the shepherd’s disposal: the slingshot, which strikes the predatory wolf and puts it to flight, the staff, the dog which coaxes the sheep back to the flock, and the loving whistle. I beseech you, make whatever sacrifices are necessary to bring it back, always respecting its freedom, because anything else would not be pleasing to God our Lord. In that case, anyone who lags behind, entangled in the briars along the way, does so because he chooses to, because he wants to be unhappy, because he doesn’t want to love Christ, because he’s not prepared to use his freedom properly. In Opus Dei we don’t browbeat anybody. Perseverance depends on each of us individually. Everybody, absolutely everybody, helps you, for we are all members of the same body and are obliged to give one another strength and support by injecting virtuous acts into its bloodstream. If you’re going through a difficult patch your brothers or sisters will reach out their hand to help you. But if you insist on letting go of that hand, you’ll be lost. And when that happens the Father suffers: his heart fills with tears. To finish, let me remind you of another important point, one which has been written into our particular law. There are three things which ensure happiness on earth and an eternal reward in heaven: a firm, virginal, joyful and unquestioning fidelity to faith, to purity and to one’s vocation or calling. God wants our fidelity to be virginal; he wants our purity to blossom in such a fashion – if we have followed other paths – that it

22 1 Sam 3:6 23 Cf. Lk 15:4-7

can be described as virginal; and the same applies to vocation. These three things are untouchable; they are not to be pawed over. Is there a single person who would allow his mother’s integrity to be questioned? My faith, my purity, my calling: untouchable, firm, virginal, joyful, and unquestioning. That is how to persevere to the end. As Jesus has said, “He who endures to the end will be saved.”24 Some of you, my sons, will be leaving Rome this year. I don’t know if I’ll see you again. I don’t like making statements of that sort, but they slip out now and then. You will be faithful if you are humble, if you don’t forget that you will always have weaknesses. Engrave this very deeply on your hearts: our strength is our weakness. A feeble child takes refuge in its mother’s embrace. Though we are strong adults and have been sent forth along all the ways of the earth, in spite of our strength – which we should never lack – we have to see ourselves as little children, weak and in need of everything. And then, with those glances of affection, those Marian Norms and Customs that are at the heart of our spirit, we have to throw ourselves into the arms of our heavenly Mother.

24 Mt 10:22

# THE GOOD SHEPHERD * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.11}

A day of recollection is an occasion when God grants us special graces to reflect upon the purpose of our lives, which is to become saints ourselves and sanctify other people. But today I would like to explain to you once again what our spirit is in regard to a wonderful means of sanctification, one which has been instituted by Jesus Christ, namely, the sacrament of confession. Starting from that divine institution, I also want to make some points about something else which is also an expression of the maternal solicitude of the Work – spiritual direction with the Director, the fraternal chat. As usual I’ve brought with me some books, notes and papers. It sometimes happens that afterwards I don’t use them and develop other ideas in the meditation. But this book I always make use of, namely, the Gospel, because I only want to speak words of life, the words of our Lord Jesus Christ.

## In Christ’s sheepfold {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.11.1}

Let us open St John’s Gospel at chapter twelve: Amen, amen dico vobis, qui non intrat per ostium in ovile ovium, sed ascendit aliunde, ille fur est et latro,1 truly, truly, I say to you, he who does not enter the sheepfold by the door but climbs in by another way, that man is a thief and a robber.

* Notes of a meditation given during a recollection in Rome, 12 March 1961 1 Jn 10:1

My children, what peace there is here for your hearts and mine! We are not thieves or robbers, because we have entered per ostium, by the door, into this sheepfold of Opus Dei. We have come here per Iesum Christum Dominum nostrum, for the love of Christ, to our Lord’s little nook where his sheep dwell, and only his sheep – a place of peace and goodness. But let us continue reading St John: qui autem intrat per ostium, pastor est ovium, the one who enters by the door is the shepherd of the sheep. “To him the gatekeeper opens; the sheep hear his voice, and he calls his own sheep by name and leads them out.”2 Jesus the Good Shepherd opens his sheepfold, the sheep hear his voice, and he knows them all, every single one. How ancient the scene appears, doesn’t it? But don’t imagine it is so ancient that it doesn’t happen nowadays too. Quite the opposite, in fact: it is completely contemporary. I remember once, travelling along a road in Castile, we saw some men driving heavy stakes into the ground. Afterwards they stretched netting on the stakes to form a circle, leaving one part of it open. Finally one of them began to shout loudly in a tone that was in some indefinable way affectionate. And the sheep came along and began to enter. He called them one by one, and had a special greeting for one, and a caress for another. He knew them all. What an up-to-date scene! My children! My dearest children! Don’t forget that each of you has entered through the gate, through the love of Christ. You are all sheep of the same sheepfold, and at the same time you each have to be, in a certain sense, a good shepherd to the other sheep. And just as you should let yourselves be led and should answer when you are called by name, you have an equally serious duty to contribute to the holiness and the perseverance of your brothers and sisters. If I ever were to see a son of mine weaken, and weaken to the point of losing his earthly happiness, and maybe his eternal happiness too, I would not excuse from sin those who lived with

2 Jn 10:2-3

him, for having failed to provide him with the means to persevere, means he had a right to. None of you is ever alone, no one is just an isolated verse: we are all verses of the same divine, epic poem. And none of us ever want to see any fissures appearing in this unity. We all want to see harmony reign in Christ’s sheepfold. We want to be like a great army, oves et milites Christi [sheep and soldiers of Christ], on the road to holiness.

## Having recourse to the Good Shepherd {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.11.2}

Et cum proprias oves emiserit, ante eas vadit, et oves illum sequuntur, quia sciunt vocem eius.3 When the shepherd has brought out his sheep, he goes before them all, and the sheep follow him, because they know his voice. We ought to follow those who fulfil the function of good shepherds. And your brother ought to listen to each of you too, when you make a fraternal correction, sometimes with a glance, sometimes saying what the circumstances require. On other occasions you should call to mind the “compelle intrare” of the Gospel.4 If our Lord wanted strangers to be compelled to come to the banquet, how much more will he want you to employ a “holy compulsion”, the blessed compulsion of charity, of love, on your brothers, sheep of Christ’s same sheepfold! This beautiful compulsion of charity, far from depriving your brother of his freedom, gently helps him to use it properly. Don’t forget that. I am no longer a young man. I don’t say that for the pleasure of calling myself old, but because I feel duty bound to pass on to you the following idea, which seems unimportant and nevertheless is crucial. Take careful note of what I’m telling you and engrave it on your hearts. The person speaking to you is not just a priest, but the Founder, and there is only one of those. You will see many Popes (I myself have met a number); Cardinals, scores; bishops, even more

3 Jn 10:4 4 Lk 14:23

… but there is only one Founder of Opus Dei, even though he has very little foundation: just one! And God will call you to account if you don’t pay attention to what I say. Through my mouth Christ speaks to you very especially, because in his name I am in a special way the Good Shepherd. And I must stress that each one of you in turn is also a good shepherd. Alienum autem non sequuntur,5 the sheep will not follow an unknown shepherd. What this means is that, if someone forsakes Jesus’ teaching, they start to stray and end up losing their joy and peace, and possibly even their soul. Because sometimes, instead of fleeing from the stranger – alienum autem non sequuntur – they could distance themselves from their Directors, from their brothers, and go instead to someone who is so ignorant, imprudent, or unprepared, that he is capable of leading them to their doom. My children, you need to make the firm resolution to not make that mistake. Our Lord himself, through St John, explains to us that we shouldn’t seek advice from outside, because that would mean going voluntarily to the edge of the precipice. You have to flee from the stranger: “they flee from him.”6 You should only listen to the voice of the good shepherd! You know, don’t you, who is the good shepherd for my sheep? The one who has received the mission from me. And normally I give it to the Directors and the priests of the Work. Those who don’t know anything about Opus Dei are not in a position to act as shepherds of my sheep, even though they may be good shepherds of other sheep, and even though they may be saints. For my children, though, they are not the good shepherd Christ talks about. Is that clear? Sed fugiunt ab eo.7 Follow the Master’s advice: flee. Why should we pay attention to someone who doesn’t know anything about the spirit of the Work? We have to listen to the voice of the

5 Jn 10:5 6 Ibid. 7 They flee from him: ibid.

good shepherd, of those who have received the mission to feed the sheep of Opus Dei. Anybody else is not a shepherd with this specific mission.

## The healing doctor {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.11.3}

My children, I now want to consider what is laid down in our particular law. I have told you thousands of times that I am a great friend of freedom, and I also know that my children have a lot of common sense. I can’t accept that any local Director, whose job it is to open the doors of Opus Dei to these sheep of Christ, could be so stupid as to allow anyone to enter who doesn’t behave as I will now proceed to explain in detail. In the Work we all ought to go to the sacrament of confession at least once a week. You should go to confession to the priests appointed. You can in fact go to any priest who has faculties from the local bishop. In that way I defend freedom, but with common sense. All my children are completely free to go to confession to any priest approved by the bishop, and nobody is obliged to tell the Directors of the Work about it. Is it a sin to do that? No! Is it good spirit? No! That soul has put himself or herself in danger of listening to the voice of the bad shepherd. Admittedly, since most of the members of Opus Dei live in their own homes in all sorts of places, they can’t always go to priests of the Work, and sometimes they have to go to confession to other priests. When that happens, when they open their hearts they will give forth “the smell of a field which the Lord has blessed”,8 the fragrance of a life fully dedicated to God and adorned with a sensitive conscience. But if it were to happen otherwise, they should place themselves in the hands of their brother the good shepherd, even when it means having to take steps which are out of the ordinary.

8 Gen 27:27

If in special circumstances someone needs more careful medication, as it were, that is, if they need specific and prompt advice, more intense spiritual direction, they oughtn’t look for it outside the Work. If they were to behave differently, they would be voluntarily leaving the right path and heading for the precipice; without a doubt, they would have lost their good spirit. What does a sick person do when he wants to be cured? He goes to a doctor who knows him. “Examine me thoroughly,” he tells him, “carry out tests, take my blood pressure and temperature…” The doctor examines him, listens to his chest, has him X-rayed, and so forth. If the doctor does his job properly, he will ensure that the patient, through weakness or forgetfulness, doesn’t omit to tell him anything that could be relevant. And if the patient isn’t a madman, he will be quick to tell the doctor all the symptoms and circumstances he thinks might help the diagnosis, even seemingly unimportant ones. He won’t go to the first doctor he finds, and afterwards to a second and a third, to get a prescription for an aspirin, but he’ll go to the one who knows him well. You will go to priests who are your brothers, as I do. And you open your hearts wide – with its rottenness, if that’s the case! – sincerely, really wanting to be cured; otherwise the rottenness will never be made whole. And we do the same in spiritual direction, with the Director or whoever hears our fraternal chat. If we went to someone who could only cure our injury superficially… it would be because we were cowards, because we were not good sheep, because we would be hiding the truth to our own detriment. And as well as doing harm to ourselves, by going to a chance doctor who could not give us more than a few seconds of his time, who could not apply the scalpel and cauterise the wound, we would also be harming the Work. If you were to do this, you would have the wrong spirit; you would be unhappy. You would not sin because of it, but alas for you! You would have begun to err, to make a mistake. By not being ready to be cured, by not being willing to

behave properly, you would have begun to listen to the voice of the bad shepherd. And you would also be doing harm to everybody else. The confessor would of course safeguard the sacramental seal: all priests always do so scrupulously. But if another soul were to come to him seeking advice, and tell him they were thinking of joining Opus Dei, that confessor would perhaps tell them to forget about it, because he would think: “What, go to the same place as that other unfortunate, that cancer patient who didn’t want to be cured?” You know the doctrine of the Mystical Body, the Communion of Saints. Well, you would be doing harm to your brothers, and those who will come afterwards, and to yourself, and to the whole body of the Work. Because after all, that bad shepherd didn’t come looking for you; you yourself were the only one to blame. The one who is not the good shepherd, since he doesn’t have the right remedies, non venit nisi ut furetur et mactet et perdat,9 can only come to steal and kill and destroy. We need to follow the very specific, clear spirit God has willed us to have. Our spirit is very clear; our ascetical and mystical doctrine is very clear indeed. And anything that deforms that spirit is to steal and to kill. Resolutions! Clear ideas! We can and we cannot. Do I sin? No. Do I have to tell the Director? No. But let me repeat: alas for you! You poor unfortunate soul! Omnes quotquot venerunt fures sunt et latrones.10 Those who are not the good shepherd turn out to be thieves and robbers. The only good shepherd is the one who himself knows, and is following, the spirit which you live by; the one who has received the mission from the person with the power to give it to him: “To him the gatekeeper opens; the sheep hear his voice, and he calls his own sheep by name and leads them out. When he has brought out all his own, he goes before them, and the sheep follow

9 Jn 10:10 10 Jn 10:8

him, for they know his voice.”11 Consequently the members of Opus Dei, if they really want to be faithful, “will not follow a stranger, but they will flee from him, for they do not know the voice of strangers.”12 And can other shepherds not seek out my sheep and feed them well? No. Our Lord says so categorically: qui non intrat per ostium in ovile ovium, sed ascendit aliunde, ille fur est et latro,13 he who does not enter the sheepfold by the door but climbs in by another way, that man is a thief and a robber. Surely, couldn’t some wellintentioned person come along to lend a hand, take a little flock of sheep, lead them to good pasture, and bring them back to the fold? No. No way! It is not I who say so, but our Lord himself. Anyone who hasn’t received the mission from the Directors is not a good shepherd, even though he performs miracles. Because when a priest hears confessions he doesn’t act just as judge, but also as teacher, doctor and father: as a pastor, a shepherd. How could someone exercise those functions if he didn’t know what God wants of us, in accordance with the vocation he has given us? How could he possibly do so, if he doesn’t possess our spirit, if he doesn’t have a legitimate mandate and consequently the special grace to exercise his mission properly? Ego sum pastor bonus. Bonus Pastor anima suam dat pro ovibus suis.14 I am the good shepherd. The good shepherd sacrifices his life for the sheep. My children, I don’t mind telling you this next bit. It happened many years ago. You know that the institutions raised up by God all suffer from misunderstanding, especially at the beginning, and our Lord allows them to experience a lot of opposition… Sometimes it is “good people” who give rise to this persecution. Objectively it is the work of the devil; subjectively we cannot really judge.

11 Jn 10:3-4 12 Jn 10:5 13 Jn 10:1 14 Jn 10:11

Well, years ago, at a particularly difficult moment, that son of mine who was aware of all these trials, erected in the Father’s office, beside the door leading to the balcony of the oratory of the Blessed Trinity, a travertine marble plaque with a reproduction of the Good Shepherd from the catacombs, along with these lines of a poem by Juan del Enzina: “Such a good little flock, and especially in such a valley my pleasure is to guard it. An oath I have sworn never to forsake it but always to protect it.”† From the very beginning, from the second of October 1928, I have felt impelled by God to love you, and to love everything in your lives, with the love of a father and a mother. Nothing that concerns you is alien to me, and the same is true of those thousands of daughters and sons I have never met. Your brother acted very well in those dangerous circumstances. We were warned about them by Cardinal Schuster of Milan, who behaved wonderfully. He was a saint, and maybe some of you will see him canonised. Two of my sons, the Director and the priest of the Centre in Milan, went to see him. The Cardinal asked them: “And how is the Father? Has he had any crosses lately?” They replied: “Well, we don’t know of any in particular, but if he has he’ll be very happy, because he’s always telling us that if we find the Cross it’s a sign that we’re close to Christ…” The Cardinal then said: “Tell him to watch out. Tell him to remember what happened to his fellow-countryman St Joseph of Calasanz, and to get moving.” And indeed, your Father, a poor man, but one who wants to be a good shepherd, went to… But let’s not talk about that now, and keep what I’ve told you in your hearts. A good shepherd. But also good sheep. Good sheep, you say? Yes, my children, yes indeed: good sheep. I haven’t the slightest doubt that you will all be always good sheep.

† Tan buen ganadico, / y más en tal valle, / placer es guardalle. / Y tengo jurado / de nunca dejalle, / mas siempre guardalle

## Opening one’s heart sincerely {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.11.4}

Spiritual direction. In the Catechism of the Work you will have studied how spiritual direction is the responsibility in the first place of the local Directors – lay-people, mind you! It is also given by the appointed priest in the exercise of his ministry. But none of them create a little coterie of followers. We cannot tolerate any sort of division; nobody can say: “I belong to Paul, or I belong to Apollos, or I belong to Cephas, or I belong to Christ. Is Christ divided?”15 So-and-so is not a spiritual director, because in the Work, spiritual direction takes place only in actu; in other words, the lay Director exercises spiritual direction only during the fraternal chat or when consulted about something; and the priest when hearing confessions. Each of you also has the duty to give prudent, yet heroic, spiritual guidance to your brothers around you by means of fraternal correction. You are all the good shepherd. All of us, by the fact of being in Opus Dei, have a mission, which is a duty and a sacred right, to help others strive for holiness. Ego sum pastor bonus. Bonus Pastor animam suam dat pro ovibus suis.16 I am the good shepherd. The good shepherd sacrifices his life for the sheep. He makes whatever sacrifice is necessary. And you too ought to be ready to do the same. And the first one is very clear. Don’t exercise that right – because it is a right that we all have

– if you can avoid doing so, and it can be avoided always or nearly always. A firm resolution: the first sacrifice consists in not ever forgetting what the people of Castile express very graphically: don’t wash your dirty linen in public. The first expression of your selfgiving is not to be so cowardly as to take the dirty linen to be washed outside the Work, that is, if you really want to be saints; and if you don’t, you’re not wanted here.

15 1 Cor 1:12-13 16 Jn 10:11

Whenever I’m not feeling too well… You know that from time to time I haven’t been well: you will have noticed that so far this year I have scarcely been able to come to see you. Today, as soon as I heard you were having a recollection I called the Rector, because I was keen, really keen to spend some time with you… As I was saying, whenever I don’t feel too well I go to the doctor more frequently; and I let him examine me, and he feels me wherever he wishes, and I answer all his questions; if not, I would be acting like a madman. Well, do the same thing in the spiritual life. “The good shepherd lays down his life for the sheep. He who is a hireling and not a shepherd, whose own the sheep are not, sees the wolf coming and leaves the sheep and flees; and the wolf snatches them and scatters them. He flees because he is a hireling and cares nothing for the sheep.”17 There you have a precise portrait of the man who doesn’t have the mission to feed the flock. If he is a good priest he does just what he has to, he gives some generic advice: “Try to improve, say a Hail Mary…” What sort of mission is that for a teacher, doctor, father or judge? And so you see the unhappy end of the person who imprudently seeks the advice of an unknown shepherd. My children, open your hearts! Your first brothers set you a wonderful example. I didn’t wish to hear their confessions. Now I go to confession to one of your brothers, and when I get up, he kneels down for me to hear his confession; we’ve been doing that for many years now. At the beginning I didn’t normally hear my sons’ confessions, because I didn’t think it sensible to tie my hands with the sacramental seal. They voluntarily told me everything, absolutely everything, outside confession. In that way spiritual direction forged ahead splendidly and souls were sanctified. I am concerned about the formation of the younger people; I’m worried that they could become a little bourgeois. In the early days we lacked everything, or practically everything; we were ill-treated,

17 Jn 10:11-13

slandered… and always happy, always smiling, always effective. Your brothers had to go to university and give classes and work to earn their living. I am pleased with you, my children: I know that you are studious and cheerful. But pray that we get it right, so that all my children earn their own living and learn the value of money right from their youth. That way they won’t become bourgeois. Your brothers, as I said, used to open their hearts to me outside confession in all simplicity and sincerity, as everybody continues to do now in the fraternal conversation with the Director. My children, don’t be ashamed whenever you experience your limitations. Don’t be shocked because you have the fomes peccati [the “tinder of sin”] in your hearts. Don’t be surprised by anything. Be truly faithful! Sincere! Sincere! If the Father, because he is father and mother, leaves things very much open, then you, because you are steady, reliable sheep, should have enough common sense and supernatural sense to appreciate that, in order to let the Good Shepherd work, you should waive certain rights. In exchange you will experience greater effectiveness in the work of your sanctification and in the sanctification of the whole of Opus Dei, in the sanctification of your brothers and sisters, of many souls, and of the Church herself. Holy Mary, you who are refuge of sinners and our Mother, present these resolutions before God’s throne, and through your powerful intercession make them effective.

# THE GOOD SEED * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.12}

Let us continue deepening in our reading and meditation of chapter thirteen of St Matthew’s Gospel, which contains the parables of the kingdom of heaven. But first of all, bear in mind that the corporate mission of the Work, as well as our own personal mission, can be reduced to this: instruction in the faith, planting the seed of good doctrine, God’s light, in the minds and hearts of all mankind. We must give, not only witness, but also instruction, because Opus Dei is nothing other than a great world-wide catechesis.

## Sowing good doctrine {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.12.1}

You have often heard me say, and you yourselves have repeated it to your brothers in Circles and meditations, and in the course of spiritual direction, that the greatest enemy of Christ and the Church is ignorance. That is why we have to work at our formation, to grasp the doctrine of the faith well and afterwards pass it on without disfiguring it, in spite of our own mistakes. In Opus Dei our Lord gives us abundant spiritual food to distribute generously to those around us – people who very often may be “poor”, even though materially well off, if they lack divine grace or don’t even know about God.

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 4 February 1962, 5th Sunday after the Epiphany

God wants us to sow seed and distribute bread by means of the apostolate of the renewal of Christian life. Apostolate is the fruit of charity and the measure of our love for God. All our undertakings are animated by the love, faith and hope God has placed in our hearts. We never attack anybody: ours is a supernatural enterprise which doesn’t tolerate violence, but rather promotes understanding, so we may live in peace with all mankind and do good to all. The awareness of your own insignificance and wretchedness, my children, is not a reason for not carrying out a constant apostolate, deeply humble, and all the more effective for all that. Remember the woman Jesus met at the well of Sichar: Numquid ipse est Christus?1 Is this man not the Christ? she asks her neighbours. The sinner becomes an apostle, and runs to call her neighbours to tell them the Good News. Never be inhibited by the mistaken thought that it is hypocrisy to preach about good when you are so aware of your own weakness. After all, the fact that Jesus himself had reminded the Samaritan woman that she was a sinner did not cause her to have the false humility of failing to pass on the good news. And the fruits of your apostolate will grow and be effective, like the fruits of the persuasiveness of that unfortunate woman who hadn’t followed the way of the Lord up till then. Rest assured that our heavenly Father will turn us into a burning coal, to set fire to those around us, to give light and heat, forgiving all, understanding all, living peacefully with all, not wishing ill to anybody.

## The gift of tongues {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.12.2}

Don’t forget that, precisely because God “desires all men to be saved”,2 “it pleased [him] through the folly of what we preach to save those who believe.”3 In his most lovable will he has chosen to

1 Jn 4:29 2 1 Tim 2:4 3 1 Cor 1:21

need our preaching, our word, our poor and deficient knowledge, to lead mankind to the truth, to the path of faith, through humility and doctrine. Absolutely everybody! He also seeks out those who are far away: St Paul tells us that “Christ Jesus came into the world to save sinners. And I am the foremost of sinners.”4 Do you see then, my dearest children? Witness and good example aren’t enough. We have to stir people up by announcing the Good News. That is the charge Jesus has laid upon us with his continual preaching, which was a ceaseless dialogue with his listeners, teaching, instructing, and as I have often told you, using the divine teaching method of parables, to communicate with even the humblest and least gifted of his hearers. For many years now you have heard me say that in order to teach and spread doctrine we need the “gift of tongues”, which isn’t a question of speaking many languages, but something much deeper, more spiritual, more psychological. It means being able to adapt ourselves to the capacity of our listeners. It’s not a matter of speaking in platitudes to the masses to make ourselves understood, but of speaking wisely, as a Christian, using words everyone can grasp. I ask our Lord and his blessed Mother that all my children may have this gift of tongues. From reading the Gospel you will realise why I want you to have the gift of tongues. Consider Jesus our Lord: see how he instructs his listeners. Sometimes he speaks to them from the boat while they are seated on the shore; other times on the mountain, so that the whole of the crowd can hear him; sometimes amid the noise of a banquet, in the quiet of a house, walking through the fields, or seated under the olive trees. The Gospel accounts, as I said, are strewn with parables: examples that materialise doctrine and clarify spiritual truths without debasing or cheapening the word of God. For everyone,

4 1 Tim 1:15

both the learned and the less so, these human images make it easier to grasp and understand divine truths. Thus Christ addresses each person in a way he or she can understand, and he makes use of parables, because, as the Evangelist explains, “he did not speak to them without a parable.”5 He talks about nets and fish, to the fisher folk;6 seeds and vines, to the farm labourers;7 he tells the housewives about the lost coin8 or the leaven mixed with three measures of flour to make the weekly batch of bread;9 he talks about a wedding feast, or a public celebration,10 to engrave on his listeners’ minds the characteristics of the kingdom he has come to found. Now too the divine Master’s lips utter those teachings, addressed to the people of all time, which were first heard in the districts of Palestine. With the “gift of tongues”, they were attuned to the mentality of those who heard him then, without changing the content of the message. Nowadays they fit our present time equally well, and will continue to do so for future generations, always leaving the deposit of faith unchanged.

## In Peter’s boat {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.12.3}

One of your brothers was speaking to us this morning about boats and the sea, about Peter’s little boat moored at the shore of Lake Genesareth, and he invited us to consider how our Lord stepped into the boat and commanded them to draw off a little from the shore. I thought about you and me. I have often told you that we are with Jesus in Peter’s boat, but a bit apart from the crowd, in order to be heard more easily and not be trampled on.

5 Mk 4:34 6 Cf. Mt 13:47-50 7 Cf. Mt 13:31-32; Mk 12:1-12; Lk 13:6-9 8 Cf. Lk 15:9 9 Cf. Lk 13:20-21 10 Cf. Mt 22:2-14; Mt 25:1-13; Lk 12:36

This we achieve with our interior life. So long as we fulfil the Norms, we can be in the middle of the world and, at the same time, in some way separated yet very closely united. So much so that we wish to be fully immersed in the bloodstream of society, in all upright human undertakings, sanctifying them and making them supernatural. Is it in fact the case that you are immersed in that bloodstream, in Christ’s boat? If because of our particular circumstances, on some occasion we don’t find ourselves physically rubbing shoulders with other people, we should certainly always be doing so spiritually. Is that happening to you? Do souls actually hurt you? Are you concerned for all souls, do you feel for them with a great sense of understanding and apostolic zeal? Do you also take care of your own soul? This latter in a different sort of way, because whereas concern for others should be overflowing with charity and understanding, concern for oneself should be strict and severe. Do you feel in the world and at the same time separated from it, immersed in Christ? This is something you can achieve through continuous mortification, which won’t kill you, through piety, and through devotion to the Blessed Trinity and to the Mother of God. Rejoice in addressing her as Daughter of God the Father, Mother of God the Son, Spouse of God the Holy Spirit. You can achieve it through these universal ideas of piety which, with the passage of the years, become few in number but intensely strong, because they encompass every aspect of our lives. For many years now I have often spoken to you about the boat: it’s an image I have also used in some of my letters. The boat is this: to feel immersed in God – although it’s more than just a feeling, it is to know that one is immersed in God. This is the boat of the fisher of men. But men are floundering in a great sea, a dark ocean, and we have to stay in the boat because otherwise we cannot catch them. Are you safely ensconced in the boat? Do you act with the spirit of Opus Dei in everything you do, from the tiniest details to things

of greatest importance? You already know that little things are very important if they are looked after and carried out for love… This is an excellent opportunity now for me to ask God for forgiveness for all the vanities of my life! Ne respicias peccata mea!11 It is also an excellent opportunity to consider that I am a son of God, a brother of Christ and a son of the same Mother… What are you and I doing, my son, to increase the catch, to draw many shimmering fish into the boat? You have been chosen to lead souls to holiness, to bring them to our Lord and unite them to him, and thus to extend the kingdom of God to all hearts. You must be like that net ex omni genere piscium congreganti,12 which is thrown into the sea and gathers fish of every kind. Ut eatis,13 our Lord tells you. My children have to learn that they can’t remain hidden away at home, but have to fan out into every environment. Our first and principal duty is to seek out souls where they are to be found and bring them back to the boat, wounded by love, by sorrow, by selfgiving, or at least by the desire for self-giving.

## The world for inheritance {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.12.4}

But let us turn to today’s Gospel, where Jesus our Lord offers us a parable. Simile factum est regnum caelorum homini qui seminavit bonum semen in agro suo,14 the kingdom of heaven is like a man who sowed good seed in his field – in agro suo. And what is your field, my son? The world, the whole wide world. When I was small I used to be spell-bound by the history of my country, seeing how those great lords – mighty warriors, powerful kings – used to divide up their kingdoms: one son would get one district and another one another… Nevertheless I also realised that, no matter how much land they had, what they ruled over was just a

11 Look not on my sins: Roman Missal of Pius V, Ordinary of the Mass 12 Mt 13:47 13 Go forth: Jn 15:16 14 Mt 13:24

little patch of ground. Afterwards I realised that God has given you and me the whole world for our inheritance: postula a me, et dabo tibi gentes hereditatem tuam.15 Consider what this means in terms of responsibility and greatness. Let us continue. The man in the parable started well. He sowed good seed in his field, he set to work. But there are other tasks to be undertaken first: sowing is neither the first nor the last of them. First the farmer gathers the harvest and, having taken what he needs to make bread for his sustenance, he sets aside a portion of the grain for next year’s crop. Then, when the time has come, he prepares the ground: he ploughs it well, so the air can enter the furrows and ventilate the ground; he lets it rest a while, he fertilises it, and uproots any weeds that may have sprung up… Finally, he scatters the seed from the previous harvest into the waiting soil, so it can decompose and germinate new life. Without this act of generosity there would be no future harvest. But sowing is not the end of his labours. While the grain of seed is germinating and becoming an ear of corn, see all the cares and anxieties that await him!
