He has to tackle all the pests, worry about drought when the grain is starting to ripen, or the danger of storms when the reaping is about to begin… He has to wait a lot, to work hard and suffer a lot, before the grain is safely stored in his barn. My children, I’m talking now about the seed of doctrine, the “good seed”16 the Gospel refers to. Holy Mother Church stores it in her barn – the deposit of faith which she protects jealously and hands on from one generation to the next – so it can nourish and be given as food to many other souls. I am not unaware of the difficulties you may encounter. It is certainly true, and I have often pointed it out, that in this world you come from and in which it is your divine vocation to remain, there

15 Ask of me, and I will make the nations your heritage: Ps 2:8 16 Mt 13:24

are many good things, the results of God’s indescribable goodness. But men have also sown weeds, as in the Gospel parable, and have spread false doctrines that poison men’s minds and goad them to rebel, sometimes frenetically, against Christ and his Holy Church. On the basis of our own littleness, we ask God to make us into good seed, from which to make bread both for the altar and for the table: may we learn to understand, to forgive, to live in harmony with others, and to pardon.

## While men were asleep {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.12.5}

Let us continue with the parable. Cum autem dormirent homines… “While men were sleeping, his enemy came and sowed weeds among the wheat, and went away.”17 This is very striking! We can’t miss a single word of the things our Lord tells us. Because isn’t it true that in our lives it is sleep, the wrong kind of sleepiness, that makes us waste the good seed sown in our minds and hearts? Tell our Lord that you want to do everything you can. And when you see that you haven’t succeeded, that you have fallen asleep – a sad business, that sleep! – then it’s time to make an act of contrition: Domine, tu omnia nosti, tu scis quia amo te,18 Lord, you know everything, you know that I love you. I let myself go all too often! But our collapse is due, not to lack of love, but to the limitations of nature. And so we should tell him at once: from now on, quia tu es, Deus, fortitudo mea,19 because you are my strength, I will be strong with you. The defeats are mine, the victories yours. I don’t wish to allow evil to thrive in the world! And the field will be ploughed and receive the farmer’s generous sowing and care. And then, with God’s blessing, the harvest will come: a harvest of souls for God. There is another sowing, the Eucharist, which gives us daily bread

17 Mt 13:25 18 Jn 21:17 19 Ps 43:2

and divine food, food to nourish souls which can become divine seed in turn. It’s quite clear: the field is fertile and the seed is good: the owner of the field has sown the seed at the right moment, with consummate skill; moreover, he has stationed watchmen to protect the newly-sown seed. If afterwards weeds appear, it is because men

– and we Christians especially – haven’t done our duty but have fallen asleep, and have given the enemy an opportunity. So we have to be watchful, and not fall asleep. A few days ago, listening to the Holy Father, I seemed to hear the cry of Sacred Scripture: Custos, quid de nocte? Custos, quid de nocte? 20 which I have used in some old meditations. Sentry, be watchful! We have to be alert. We must listen to that cry. My children, you must always be on the lookout and stay alert. You are well able to teach those other souls who are with you in Opus Dei what being watchful means, because you know that we are vigilant by the mere fact of fulfilling our Norms. I’ve said from the beginning that I am utterly convinced of the salvation – the holiness

– of anyone who fulfils the Norms of life of Opus Dei. Fight to fulfil them, even though sometimes you don’t feel like it, even though sometimes upright souls may have to go against the grain, feeling that their life is a bit of a farce, and they have to make an act of faith and keep praying and working until the last stone of the day has been set in place. Don’t fall asleep! Because if you do, in the midst of good fortune evil will come upon you. “So when the plants came up and bore grain, then the weeds appeared also.”21 What a divine teaching method the parables are! They are clear and luminous to simple souls, but unintelligible to people who are complicated and rebellious: that’s why the Pharisees didn’t understand them. The sower, the field, the enemy, the weeds… Draw closer to Christ, my son, and ask him to explain the

20 Is 21:11 21 Mt 13:26

parable to you – edissere nobis parabolam!22 – as the first Twelve asked him, in the intimacy of your prayer. I want you to consider how evil has prospered. Within this field of God, which is the world, Christ’s legacy, we find weeds. I want you to consider it now, so you are never deceived by the myth of constant and irreversible progress. Understand me: progress, properly oriented, is something good, and God wills it to happen. I am referring to that other progress which blinds so many people, because sometimes they don’t see that the human race, in some of its steps, actually goes backwards and loses what it had previously acquired. Evil has prospered! Right from the start, from the cradle of the Church, even during the Apostles’ lifetime, there were persecutions and heresies: persecutions by the pagans, and all those heresies, in the early centuries of Christianity… And Islam… and Protestantism… and now Communism. Within the field God has fashioned on the earth, in Christ’s legacy, we find weeds: and not just weeds, but lots of them!23

## The sower of weeds {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.12.6}

“And the servants of the householder came and said to him, ‘Sir, did you not sow good seed in your field? How then has it weeds?’ He said to them, ‘An enemy has done this.’”24 What a great pity! It really hurts me to point it out, but do you not see how the devil’s seed thrives everywhere? Satan and those

22 Mt 13:36 23 From the point of view of the faith, our Father explains that the various currents of thought which differ from the authentic teaching of Christ are the historical expression of the weeds the Gospel speaks about. But this judgment never applies to individuals, who may not be to blame for their situation. Saint Josemaría always had his heart and his arms wide open to non-Catholics and non-Christians, for whom he had the greatest respect and affection, so much so that he requested and obtained from the Holy See permission for them to be received in Opus Dei as Cooperators, if they freely want to be. 24 Mt 13:27-28

who serve him don’t take holidays in their endeavour to lead men into evil, while we Christians are all too often unconcerned about guiding the affairs of this world of ours aright. The bad sleep, the lethargy of those who ought to be watchful, has given the enemy the opportunity to sow weeds abundantly. Lay people are particularly at fault, because it is they who are more directly responsible for the temporal affairs of this world, which they ought to direct to God. Many people don’t want to acknowledge that this is the case, because nowadays people don’t dare talk about hell. They are afraid to speak about the interventions of God’s enemy, Satan. He can’t even be mentioned. And I tell you that we have to acknowledge that the devil is active, and I don’t mean in any strange sort of way. It gives me great devotion to recite at the foot of the altar: Sancte Michaël Archangele, defende nos in proelio: contra nequitiam et insidias diaboli esto praesidium! [Holy Michael Archangel, defend us in the battle. Be our safeguard against the wickedness and snares of the devil.] May St Michael free us from the devil’s influence in so many things in our own lives and our neighbours’: our own bad temper, sensuality, or pride, all of which the devil takes advantage of. All this can be overcome by being sincere, and learning how to be immersed in God, to be recollected. As long as we stay in our Lord’s boat the violence of the waves cannot reach us, because Christ is the captain of the ship, and he does not lose battles. From childhood I have often reflected on the fact that many good people are called on to suffer all sorts of misfortunes on this earth: financial setbacks, family problems, having to renounce even causes for legitimate pride… At the same time I saw other people, who didn’t seem to be good – I’m not saying they weren’t, because we shouldn’t judge anybody – for whom everything worked out very well, as it does now: wealth, fame, human success, sensual satisfaction… And many rulers, as we see constantly throughout history, who for years and years ride roughshod over the rights of entire nations without anybody putting a stop to their abuses. Until one day the thought occurred to me that even bad people also do

good things, though not for supernatural reasons; and I understood that God has to reward them in this life, since he cannot do so in the next. I was reminded of the phrase: “Thus is the ox fattened for slaughter.” But also, my children, what does it matter if we experience sufferings, when Christ repays us even here on earth with serenity and peace! When we are faithful to him we don’t suffer all that much. As long as we don’t exaggerate, it’s all little enough. As long as we remain faithful, within the boat, we experience a great sense of peace and tranquillity deep in our hearts. Ask our Lord, therefore, for the grace to make your self-giving complete, never to let yourself be vanquished by opposition, whenever it appears. If we ever suffer more than we should, it is a sign that we haven’t learned to rest in God’s hands.

## The final victory {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.12.7}

It is clear that there are weeds in the midst of the wheat, and this will always be the case because – let me repeat it so you don’t forget it – the enemy of souls never rests: he never takes holidays. On learning this sad fact, the servants of the parable asked the owner of the field: “‘Then do you want us to go and gather them?’ But he said, ‘No; lest in gathering the weeds you root up the wheat along with them. Let both grow together until the harvest; and at harvest time I will tell the reapers, Gather the weeds first and bind them in bundles to be burned, but gather the wheat into my barn.’”25 My children, do you understand all this? We Christians, especially those totally dedicated to the Lord’s service, cannot forget that regnum meum non est de hoc mundo,26 the kingdom of Jesus Christ is not of this world, and that’s why it happens so often that God’s enemy apparently triumphs here below. But this thought shouldn’t prevent us from toiling away, because Christ is

25 Mt 13:28-30 26 Jn 18:36

triumphing too, here in this world. The more evil we encounter, the greater should be our sense of responsibility; the more obstacles we meet, the greater are the obligations it imposes on us. We ought to work with this hope and this sense of responsibility – a responsibility that doesn’t leave us paralysed with anxiety, but which leads us to confront the issues more decisively. And do it for love, do everything for love. With faith like that we can work away happily. The final victory will be God’s, even on this earth: “Gather the weeds first and bind them in bundles to be burned, but gather the wheat into my barn.”27 What a pity! We want to turn the weeds into good seed, we want souls to be saved. Let us ask Jesus to make the fruit of his redemption abundant, ever more abundant, divinely abundant! And to achieve this, we ask him to make us good children of his blessed Mother.

27 Mt 13:30

# CHRIST’S YOKE * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.13}

“The lot fell on Matthias; and he was enrolled with the eleven apostles.”1 He took the place left vacant by the traitor Judas. As we were saying yesterday, my children, on you and on me there has fallen a divine lot, God’s choice. We remarked on it in passing while talking about something else, but we did say it. Twice I reminded you of those words of Isaiah: Ego redemi te, et vocavi te nomine tuo: meus es tu,2 I have chosen you and called you by your name: you are mine! This divine choice demands a personal response from each of us, one which has to fill every moment of our existence: both our external behaviour, which is seen by the people around us, and also internally, visible only to God, scrutans corda, 3 who reads our inmost thoughts. The spiritual life is such a very intimate and personal thing! At the same time, our interior life has to be in complete harmony with our external behaviour. Otherwise we would be hypocrites, externally appearing to be dedicated while internally meriting God’s reproach. To put you at your ease I would like to reassure you that, no matter what you find sometimes in your heart, if you are faithful to God and try to please him, then your spiritual life will be in complete harmony with your external behaviour.

* Notes of a meditation given during a retreat in Rome, 24 February 1962, at the time the feast of St Matthias the Apostle 1 Acts 1:26 2 I have redeemed you, I have called you by your name: you are mine: Is 43:1 3 Ps 7:9

Struggle! This is what interior life consists of, battling every day and every moment. My children, it’s not beyond our strength. With God’s grace, as the Apostle says, we can do everything: omnia possum in eo qui me confortat!4 We can do all things in Him who is our strength. Nothing is difficult for a soul in love with Jesus Christ. Nothing is difficult for loyal souls who try to harmonise their interior battle with their external behaviour. Everything becomes easy through devotion to our Blessed Lady. And we will be victorious if we keep very close to Christ: he does not lose battles, and with him we will conquer and be victorious. On this earth of ours, someone who has led an army to victory and performed something out of the ordinary, receives all sorts of acknowledgements, honours and acclamations whenever he appears in public. You work before God, you struggle before him, and so almighty God, the Virgin Mary his Mother, St Joseph, and the angels and saints, have all taken notice of you. All this splendid multitude, with our Lord at its head, will receive and crown you, if you struggle interiorly and are faithful.

## Becoming small through humility {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.13.1}

Let us consider what God says to us in today’s Gospel: Confiteor tibi, Pater, Domine caeli et terrae, quia abscondisti haec a sapientibus et prudentibus, et revelasti ea parvulis.5 How wonderful! We praise you Lord, God of heaven and earth, because you have kept these things hidden from the wise and understanding and you have revealed them to little ones. Who are these little ones? Babies with feeding-bottles? No, my children! He is speaking about you and me, if we become small through humility. Anyone at all, no matter how wise or talented, if he humbles himself before our Lord, as we wish to do here now, is really one of those little ones to whom

4 Phil 4:13 5 Mt 11:25

God reveals great things, at the same time as he gives them the confidence that they will in the end achieve victory. We have to try to become humble. Quia abscondisti haec a sapientibus et prudentibus... [“For you have hidden these things from the wise and understanding...”] In accordance with my wishes, the oratories of my daughters are always presided over by a representation of the Mother of God, because they never had a foundress: so they can love her a lot and have her for their foundress. When Mary is acclaimed by her cousin Elizabeth, she replies with those marvellous words: quia respexit humilitatem ancillae suae, ecce enim ex hoc beatam me dicent omnes generationes,6 because he has looked upon my humility, my lowliness, for that reason he has raised me above all peoples. What a wonderful model for my children and for all Christians! In calling us to Opus Dei God has treated us like favourite children. Without any merit on our part, he has taken possession of us and has elevated us to share intimately in his divine life. Let us not cheat him through laziness, pride or sensuality…! We have to fight against all that, but don’t forget that through that struggle we will win, and that victory brings peace, which we will never lose if we are humble. Precisely through our effort to become little, our sincere desire to be humble, God will endow us with the knowledge of great things.

## Having recourse to Jesus {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.13.2}

From Jesus’ lips we have yet another wonderful statement: venite ad me omnes qui laboratis et onerati estis, et ego reficiam vos,7 come to me, all who labour and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest. It may sometimes happen that we find ourselves bereft of physical energy, and indeed sometimes too even our moral resources may seem exhausted. This can happen for a number of

6 Lk 1:48 7 Mt 11:28

reasons: through the passage of the years and the onset of old age, or perhaps because we are physically exhausted after a hard day’s work, or we feel besieged by a temptation we haven’t been able to shake off decisively; or we simply happen to be ill. Whenever this happens, let us listen to our Lord’s words: “Come to me…” We have to have turn to Christ, we have to talk to him, because holiness consists in embracing our Lord. Yesterday I told you to go and dwell in the house of Bethany with Lazarus, Martha and Mary; or in the house of Nazareth with Jesus, Mary and Joseph. Let us try to do that especially today: you will see how easy the struggle becomes, and how everything, absolutely everything, even the very thing which seemed sheer weakness, becomes a source of strength. “Come to me, all who labour and are heavy laden…” When you find yourself under stress, do you immediately have recourse to God? Do you often go to him in the course of the day? Do you ask him for help in the midst of your work? You don’t have to take a break to spend time with God; it shouldn’t ever be necessary. Our oratory, at that moment, is precisely whatever task we are involved in. And the same is true whenever we are listening to a conversation or taking part in one. Who can deprive us of the power of lifting up our hearts to God, silently, to tell him: “My Jesus, this business is getting me down,” or “this other thing worries me”? But without exaggerating, because some people – don’t get upset if I tell you this

– really love to exaggerate. Have recourse to God with objectivity, and he will enlighten you enough to open your heart in spiritual direction, in the fraternal chat and in sacramental confession; and at that moment he will give you the grace you need to build yourself up again. He will unburden you. You will feel, along with your poor human strength, all the power of God’s grace. And you will continue on your way willingly, joyfully and happily.

## Taking up the Cross {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.13.3}

Tollite iugum meum super vos...8 How wonderful! See how much God trusts us. From time to time have you ever asked yourself why it is we carry all this weight of Christian responsibility, why we work so much, why we have each left our own family, why we have forsaken our possessions, however great or little they were, and why we have even renounced doing our own will? Well, we have done so in order to take up God’s burden, to follow Christ. And how do we follow Christ? He himself has told us in the Gospel: “If any man would come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross and follow me. For whoever would save his life will lose it, and whoever loses his life for my sake will find it.”9 We have been denying ourselves from the moment we came to the Work ready to obey, internally and externally, and that is how we follow Christ. Carrying the cross… What cross do you have? If I were to speak with each of you individually, in confidence, I would help you see that if the cross feels so heavy that it seems enormous, it’s because it is yours, and not Christ’s. It’s heavy because it is the result of pride. I would make you see that it’s subjective and doesn’t really exist: you’ve created it yourself through your sensuality, pride, and laziness. When we look at things objectively they are reduced to half a hundredth part. The rest, I repeat, is of your own making. How are we supposed to carry the yoke Jesus wants to place on our shoulders? Joyfully, my children. The Cross always presents itself as something very holy, a source of happiness, a sign that one is close to Christ. The Cross, Christ’s Cross, is not heavy: iugum enim meum suave est, et onus meum leve,10 for my yoke is easy and my burden light. How often have you meditated on this? Have you really thought about it? How often, just how often?

8 Take my yoke upon you...: Mt 11:29 9 Mt 16:24-25 10 Mt 11:30

Let us decide to make a practical resolution: if you ever feel blind – it’s not something that happens to everybody, but it can happen occasionally – and seem to be burdened with a mountain, speak out immediately in spiritual direction, talk about it simply and frankly and in a refined way. You will experience a great sense of relief, on listening to the person you chat with, or the priest, giving you good, salutary advice to overcome the situation.

## Openness and docility {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.13.4}

It’s very much a fact that no doctor can treat himself, no matter how much he knows about medicine: he goes to someone else and tells them what is the matter with him, with all the symptoms, even the most insignificant. Well, if this happens with the body, the same is true of the soul: we need the help of someone else who, apart from having a special grace from God to advise us, is in a better position to evaluate our situation. Father, do you need to be helped too? I do indeed. Accompanying me I always have two of my sons, the Custodes, who are strictly obliged to tell me everything I should be told, in both spiritual and material matters. I do the confidential chat, just like you. They also give me fraternal correction, although in my case it’s not called that; and I listen to what they say just as you do, and am grateful for it and try to put it into practice. And you too have the obligation to welcome fraternal correction and the things you are told in spiritual direction. Do you know why I included all this in our particular law? Because it is important for the Father to have the same means of sanctification as his children. I too need to be supported, because I cannot judge the state of my own soul: I would get it all wrong. And the same is true for you, my child: you cannot judge your own situation because you would always get it wrong. Someone who is scrupulous would err by excess, and someone who is easy-going, by defect; the scrupulous one by regarding himself or herself as very

bad, and the easy-going one because he or she was too complacent, and in both cases they would be mistaken. What have you got to do to avoid both these dangers? I’ll tell you again: since nobody is a good judge in their own case, we should go in all humility to the judge and the doctor, to spiritual direction, to confession and the fraternal chat, and there open our hearts clearly and with refinement, accept the advice readily, and strive to put it into practice. At that moment our own judgment is of no importance: what counts then is the judgment of the person listening to us. That’s the solution Jesus offers us in today’s Gospel: “and you will find rest for your souls.”11 Yesterday I mentioned three things which, when they are absent, take away our peace of mind and make us unhappy here on earth; and on the other hand, if they are present they guarantee our getting to heaven: fidelity in faith, purity, and vocation. If we remain firm in faith, in chastity, and in our calling, we will be happy no matter what happens. Our Lord grants us the gaudium cum pace, the joy with peace, that we ask for every day in the Preces, he gives us light to see ourselves interiorly, or at least so that others can, to help us and give us a hand. Because sometimes we need a guide, just like blind people, to direct our steps. Nobody will deny that sometimes all a blind person needs in order to walk confidently is a stick, but only in places he is familiar with, even though he cannot see them, because he has often been there before. On the supernatural path of our vocation, on the other hand, very often the same things don’t happen twice. We need the help of a guide, someone who can see, to show us the way: this way, that way… so we don’t bump into things. We have to let ourselves be guided gently, and led by the hand, and be grateful for it. You understand what I’m trying to say, don’t you?

11 Mt 11:29

My child, ask our Lord to grant you that sort of docility, both now and for times like that. During this retreat ask him to give you a very clear idea in your mind, so that you love the holy sacrament of Penance and spiritual direction very much, and accept the advice you get very fruitfully. That way you will be forever more saintly, a better child of God; and you will achieve peace, more and more of it every day.

## A commitment of love {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.13.5}

This morning at Mass you heard in the Epistle a passage from the Acts of the Apostles. You were astonished at how effortlessly they solved the problem of how to fill the vacancy in the Apostolic College left by Judas. In my case, what impresses me most about it is the prayer they recited. They said: “Lord, who knows the hearts of all men” – it was a choice between Joseph and Matthias, because they were both very worthy men – “show which one of these two you have chosen to take the place in this ministry and apostleship.”12 My children, I’m not suggesting that something like that has happened to each of us, but I know that you do pray, as an expression of your love for God. I’m sure that not a day passes but you offer some little thing for the apostolate, so God may send the Work lots of vocations. I am certain that you have each come to the Work as the result of the effectiveness of my prayer and mortification, and the prayer and mortification of your sisters and brothers. You are here, therefore, because God has chosen you. And in doing so, he has given you everything you need to be faithful to our vocation and achieve holiness. And so if you don’t become a saint, if you don’t behave faithfully, the fault is yours.

12 Acts 1:24-25

But the Father isn’t trying to leave you with the bitter sensation that you’re not going to be faithful. You do want to be, and these days you’re trying to renew your resolution, your desire to be faithful. Faithful, because every day you try to do your work more perfectly; because you try to fulfil the Norms better; because you strive to tackle all the events of your life for love. We are in the Work because the love of God has drawn us to it, because we want to stand beside the Cross with Christ, like his Mother Mary, who is our Mother too; with the faithful disciple John, and with the holy women who stayed with Jesus amid the general cowardice when all the rest fled. Lord, we want to be faithful, we want to place ourselves beside you on the Cross and be apostles of yours. We have come at a very good time, my children: after twenty centuries the world scarcely knows about Christ. Millions of souls either haven’t heard about him at all or only know of him incompletely and erroneously. Reflect on the commitment we have before God and the whole human race. We mustn’t hinder the grace Jesus has showered upon us from the moment he called us. Say to him: “Lord, I want to be faithful.” What you have to do is recall these sincere resolutions in times of difficulty, whenever you feel tempted to be unfaithful in some way. I mentioned yesterday, as I always do when preaching anywhere, both to my children and others, that whenever I do my prayer aloud, those listening to me have to make the effort to speak personally with our Lord, with his Blessed Mother, with the Holy Angels, and tell him their own story. Because when the priest preaches, it will remind you of things in your own life, past and present. Some of them you will be ashamed of, and you will ask for forgiveness; others will make you happy, on seeing how much God has helped you, and you will thank him for it. You will finish your work more perfectly, whatever it is, always for supernatural reasons, for love,

resolved to remain with Jesus on the Cross, to lead all mankind to love him. That is the good path of our holiness. I have to go. In the five minutes of meditation that are left, you can each turn to our Blessed Lady and make acts of love and atonement; you can raise your hearts to God in thanksgiving and make some resolution to get a lot of benefit from this retreat.

# OBEDIENCE AND LOVE * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.14}

These days we have been looking at the role of virtues in the Christian life. Now we are going to talk about obedience, and as always we try to grasp what it entails and see how to apply it to our specific situation. And the starting-point, let me remind you once again, is the life of Christ, who is our model. Once again we are struck by how essential obedience is, as we can see from two wonderful but very brief biographies Jesus had written about himself. One of them says: erat subditus illis,1 he obeyed Mary and Joseph. The other describes Jesus as obediens usque ad mortem,2 obedient unto death. With a model like this, my children, how could we fail to love the holy virtue of obedience? I would like the spirit of obedience to imbue every last recess of our souls and our apostolate, every last fibre of our being. Because I must remind you that when one virtue is weakened, it affects the whole of the interior life. Especially when one isn’t struggling to be chaste, when the sine qua non, the indispensable element of holy purity is not functioning properly, then as a consequence one also begins to falter in the area of docility.

* Notes of a meditation given during a retreat in Rome, 9 March 1962 1 Lk 2:51 2 Phil 2:8

## The vine and the branches {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.14.1}

My children, you are doing your own prayer. Don’t forget that I, or that brother of yours who sits here and says the things he feels he ought, to facilitate everybody’s prayer – both he and I are only trying to provide some assistance. For it to be fruitful, you yourselves have to make an effort, with the help of grace, and muster all the resources at your disposal: your memory, to recall the appropriate images; your understanding, to work on God’s light; your will, to take decisions and move your hearts with sorrow, love, repentance and thanksgiving. When Jesus taught his disciples he used to speak in parables, which is an enchanting way of instructing and teaching, a marvellous way of communicating with all sorts of people, from the highest to the least educated. Sometimes the disciples used to ask him, when they didn’t understand: “Explain to us the parable.”3 Today I would like us to listen to the parable we find in chapter fifteen of St John’s Gospel as if hearing it from Jesus’ own lips: Ego sum vitis, vos palmites,4 I am the vine, you are the branches. You have the whole parable in your mind and imagination. You see that a branch separated from the stem, from the vine, is useless and can’t yield fruit; it’s a dry faggot to be thrown into the fire or used to beat animals, a worthless stick that men tread on contemptuously. My son, you are the branch. Draw conclusions from that fact. They are as follows: you must be united to the people whom God has placed in positions of government, who are the stem, the vine to which the rest must be closely united. If not, you won’t yield me any fruit, or your fruit will be vanity, or perhaps completely rotten; and instead of nourishing souls, instead of possessing and fostering virtue, you will spoil everything and be a source of corruption and malice.

3 Mt 13:36 4 Jn 15:5

## Fruitfulness of obedience {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.14.2}

Consider now that other passage of the Gospel, St Luke’s account of the Apostles on the lake of Genesareth. Our Lord came to them and said: duc in altum, et laxate retia vestra in capturam,5 put out into the deep and let down your nets for a catch. And Peter’s reply: Praeceptor, per totam noctem laborantes nihil cepimus, Master, we have toiled all night and caught nothing; sed in verbo tuo laxabo rete;6 nevertheless, at your word, I, who have caught no fish, I, who am someone who has borne no fruit, will begin to work again on what seemed so sterile, and I will draw in the net full of fish. That is what they did and they brought in piscium multitudinem copiosam,7 a great shoal of fish. See the power of obedience! See how fruitful it is and the joy it bestows! And moreover, as a result of having put Christ’s command into practice, those men were filled with charity. When they saw the marvellous catch they called the others, and there was enough to go around. The catch, the effectiveness, and God’s blessing were shared among them all. Don’t you see? Consider in contrast the sterility of those who don’t want to obey, who say they can’t obey. Perhaps they justify their position by giving reasons, but the truth of the matter is that they don’t want to be pure, they don’t want to employ the means to receive grace and so carry out a fruitful apostolate with other people. To each of you, and to me, our Lord says: duc in altum,8 put out into the deep! If only we really wanted to…! I think about myself, and I have so many things to say to our Lord. You think about yourself.

5 Lk 5:4 6 Lk 5:5 7 Lk 5:6 8 Lk 5:4

Perhaps you will say, “Father, I can’t…” You can! It’s all a matter of humbly approaching Jesus. This is what St Luke shows us when he narrates a somewhat striking incident in our Lord’s life. “While he was in one of the cities, there came a man full of leprosy; and when he saw Jesus, he fell on his face.”9 The people draw back from him in fear and loathing, but Jesus doesn’t. He always receives people mercifully, because he has come on earth to do good. The sick man asks him: Domine, si vis, potes me mundare,10 if you want to, you can make me clean. He was a leper, completely covered in leprosy! But how trustingly he approaches Jesus, and how unassumingly he speaks! Prostrate at the Master’s feet, the poor leper describes his problem in all simplicity, without trying to conceal the seriousness of his condition, because, as the evangelist explains, he was “full of leprosy.” After contemplating this moving conversation, won’t you and I also draw close to Christ, sure that he awaits us with open arms? Won’t we say that marvellous prayer: Domine, si vis, potes me mundare,11 Lord, if you want to, you can make me clean? Isn’t it true that you have often felt deep in your heart the need to recite Peter’s words of burning faith after the miraculous catch of fish: exi a me, quia homo peccator sum, Domine,12 depart from me, Lord, for I am a poor sinner? In the depths of your heart you well know that you need it. My son, don’t we have reasons to do the same? Without theatrics, alone in your room, on your knees or even prostrate, kiss your crucifix and say: “I am a poor sinner. But come to me! Here I am: hide me in your most loving Heart, and purify me, because I want to be clean!”

9 Lk 5:12 10 Ibid. 11 Ibid. 12 Lk 5:8

You are now well into the retreat. I am convinced that these days are aimed at only one thing: renewing with our whole heart many acts of contrition and repentance. If each of you and I, right now, make an act of true love for God, of true repentance, even if our faults have been minor ones, how well we will have profited from this retreat! Like Peter, ad genua Iesu,13 kneeling before Jesus, we say: exi a me, quia homo peccator sum, Domine,14 depart from me, Lord, for I am a sinner. But straight after that we implore him to pour out his mercy on us, not to abandon us. A sick person needs a doctor, and I am sick. A sheep that has strayed needs the shepherd to seek it out. My children, let us tell it all to Jesus. We each have our own little tragedies and we each have to formulate our own acts of love and reparation. Then how well we will have made use of these days of retreat!

## Union with the Directors {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.14.3}

Qui manet in me, et ego in eo, hic fert fructum multum,15 whoever is united with me, and I with him, will bear much fruit. Take note. We return to the parable of the vine and the branches. Listen to Jesus telling you: sine me nihil potestis facere,16 without me you can do nothing. You have to be convinced that if you don’t learn to obey you will never be effective. By obeying, you will be united to our Lord. And then there will rise from your heart St Paul’s cry: omnia possum in eo qui me confortat!17 I can do all things in him who strengthens me. My children, let us be very united to the vine, closely joined to our vine who is Jesus Christ, through unconditional obedience to our Directors. The Directors never ask us to do anything that goes against the aim of Opus Dei, which is the sanctification of its members and

13 Ibid. 14 Ibid. 15 Jn 15:5 16 Ibid. 17 Phil 4:13

apostolate. And so let us meditate on the advice of Holy Scripture: obedite prepositis vestris, non tantum bonis et modestis, sed etiam dyscolis,18 obey your superiors – not only the kind and gentle ones, those you get on well with, but also those you find more difficult, those who humanly speaking are very different from yourselves. We always have to see God in them, both at the higher levels and also our own immediate Directors. And so in the Work we always want to obey, such that whenever two of us are together, one of the two, according to precedence, acts as the Director, always respecting one’s dependence on one’s own local Director. You can read about it in the Catechism of the Work, where we have explained it fairly well. There are some people who rather ingenuously expect the Directors not to have any defects, and that’s quite impossible: all of us are saddled with them! I have often told you how consoling I find it to read in the Gospel that the Apostles had plenty of defects, and big ones too. All the saints we venerate on the altars were still full of defects when they died – if they hadn’t they would have been proud and wouldn’t have been saints – but they compensated for them with love and fidelity. Two days after the death of St Louise de Marillac, the co-founder of the Sisters of Charity, St Vincent de Paul said about her: “She was an extraordinary woman. It’s true she had a fiery temper, but she was a very good person.” So don’t worry, my children, we all have defects, and maybe even big ones. But we have to try to get rid of them, because they won’t go away by themselves. They don’t matter as long as our lives are filled with love and our intentions are pure; and as long as we are trying to get rid of them. By waging this marvellous, blessed interior warfare we will be good children of God. What a great pity it would be if I had to say to any of my children: sic non potuistis una hora vigilare mecum?19 Is it possible

18 1 Pet 2:18 19 Mt 26:40

that you couldn’t watch with me for even one hour? Are you not able to work uprightly, like a good son, helping our Lord? Jesus needs you… for an hour! And why haven’t you managed to respond? Because what is our earthly life but an hour? Remember how we’ve often described it, as brief as the twinkling of a star in the sky. Well, the duration of our earthly existence is even less than that. As the poet says: “In the brilliance of a lightning flash we are born, and its glow still lingers when we die. So short is human life!”† My son, with the help of grace, you can now make the act of contrition I mentioned earlier, and begin again wherever you have to; with God’s help you can get rid of the “dumb devil” that we sometimes have inside without realising it, and open your soul completely in spiritual direction to let God’s light enter abundantly. And then, if you begin again with fervour, if you speak clearly, your soul will be cleansed. With this total sincerity, when you are united to your Directors by obedience, no obstacle will be able to hinder your apostolate, and you will be a good son of God in Opus Dei: vibrant, effective, hard-working, normal. And you will bear fruit, fruit for making the wine which inebriates, and the wine which the priest changes into the Blood of our Lord Jesus Christ.

## Obeying out of love {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.14.4}

Let us return to the example of Christ our Model. The whole spectrum of his earthly life, from his entrance into the world at the Incarnation to his death on the Cross, is marked by the sign of obedience. He is born in a humble stable, in a cave, because that was the will of the Blessed Trinity, to set us an example of many virtues. Afterwards, erat subditus illis,20 he is subject to Joseph and Mary, he leaves himself at the mercy of his creatures. And when the

† Al brillar un relámpago nacemos / y aún dura su fulgor cuando morimos. / ¡Tan corto es el vivir! Gustavo Adolfo Becquer, Spanish poet, 1836-1870 (Tr.) 20 Lk 2:51

moment comes to begin his public life, established by God from eternity, he doesn’t hesitate to leave the home of the Mother he loved so much, and set forth to the fields, cities and villages to announce the Gospel. Earlier on, his fellow-townsmen were so struck by his closeness to Joseph in his home life and professional work, that when they thought about him they immediately associated him with the holy Patriarch, and attributed to him the same trade as his father. When he started to preach in public and perform miracles, people remarked: “Is not this the carpenter’s son?”21 Like that, from one act of obedience to another, flows the earthly life of Jesus, who is presented to us all, in those very graphic words of St Paul, as obediens usque ad mortem, mortem autem crucis,22 obeying unto the Cross. What is the source, the power that explains Jesus’ obedience? It was the charity which totally filled his human will: his loving zeal for God’s honour, and his love for the salvation of the world. This double love in the human heart of the Word Incarnate, within the inexpressible mysteries of the Blessed Trinity and the Incarnation, reveals God’s infinite love, which lies at the origin of the history of the salvation of the human race: Christ the Saviour, Christ the Redeemer, obeys with the desire to restore to God the Father the glory which had been injured, and to mankind the salvation it had lost. For the sake of this Love Christ lived a life of obedience. Our Lord chose to retrace the path of man’s rebellion in the opposite direction. At the devil’s instigation, human beings had fallen into sin through mean-spiritedness, through the poverty of their love for God and through misplaced self-love, and that had created a chasm between mankind and the Creator. Jesus, the Word Incarnate, becomes the bridge, the divine aqueduct of obedience, whereby

21 Mt 13:55 22 Phil 2:8

God’s love will flow once more into the soul of every human being. And if they respond they will be brought back to life again by charity, they will find that God’s will is sweet and wholesome, and they will learn to obey once again, following Christ. Let us meditate a lot on the mystery of Christ’s obedience: it is a historical fact that lives on forever, hidden deep in the Church’s heart. Christians who live in the light of faith and are moved by the Holy Spirit, desire to be intimately united to Christ’s obedience and to activate in themselves something of the mystery of the obedient Jesus, thus contributing to the glory of God and the salvation of mankind. My son, do everything for love. Internal responsibilities in the Work should never be carried out passively: love always leads us to find solutions, to propose and to accept suggestions, to find new forms of apostolate, with the self-same spirit, to extend God’s kingdom, always respecting the personal freedom of others. If Love is present there is no room for being over-sensitive when the Directors – always for apostolic reasons and for the good of souls, including our own – ask us to take on new tasks, perhaps different from the sort we like. Among us there is no such thing as promotion or demotion, because the fact of changing jobs is merely a matter of its suitability for our own sanctification or the effectiveness of the apostolate.

## Obedience in Opus Dei {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.14.5}

In what sort of things are we supposed to obey? In big things or in little things? I would like to remind you, as always, that there are no little things or big things. The smallest thing can become very great through love. But let us take a look at what it says in our particular law – briefly, because there are only a few minutes left. Whom ought we to obey? In the first place, God and the Church’s authority. Our obedience, in the first place, is a strong and

docile bond of union with the Holy Father and the bishops in communion with the Holy See, in everything that has to do with the Church’s teaching and discipline. That’s how it has always been. We have a great veneration for the diocesan bishops, in accordance with the norms of our law, and we try to get Catholics to respect and obey them. Next we should fulfil the dispositions and recommendations coming from the Father, either directly, or indirectly though the Directors, in everything referring to the spiritual life and the apostolate; and also obey our confessor in the intimate personal things of our soul. What should our obedience be like? In the first place it should be supernatural: we have to obey the person in charge of a small group just as readily as we do the Father, since we always see God in our Directors. Moreover, bear in mind that I’m not interested in a corpse-like obedience. I don’t have any use for corpses: I bury them piously. Obedience therefore should be voluntary, the obedience of living people with intelligence, love for God and a sense of responsibility. It should be universal, encompassing everything. There are no specialists when it comes to docility: in this matter all of us are specialists in everything. It should be prompt. I often compare it to the synchronised parts of a watch. If one of the parts isn’t working properly, the watch goes slow. Think of all the harm and ineffectiveness we cause by delaying our obedience. It must be cheerful: hilarem enim datorem diligit Deus,23 God loves the one who gives cheerfully, and obedience means giving our all, giving our very selves. Our obedience should be simple, without any complications. And it should be silent. There are foolish people who talk too much. Everyone knows what they are going to do before they have obeyed. That’s not the way. A robust obedience:

23 2 Cor 9:7

sometimes, although it’s not the normal thing, our Lord asks for an obedience that, in addition to all these qualities, has to be strong, heroic. I remember the consolation of a soul who had to carry out something that was beyond human strength, and deep in his heart he heard the words: Inter medium montium pertransibunt aquae!24 Don’t worry: the waters will go through the mountains. And always, an effective obedience: we must act on what we hear, responding with deeds, not just words. To finish I want to remind you once again that our obedience has to be like Christ’s, who, moved by the freedom of love, was obediens usque ad mortem, mortem autem crucis,25 obedient unto death, and death on a Cross. Our obedience is the exercise of personal freedom. If I am determined to be damned, nobody can save me, because God’s grace respects my freedom. As St Augustine puts it, “He who made you without your own self, will not save you without yourself.”26 It’s the same idea, because the best use of one’s freedom – the most complete act of freedom – consists in giving oneself to God through love. We have freely undertaken certain commitments. We have given ourselves to God because we chose to respond to his call, which is the most supernatural of reasons, and we have bowed our heads most willingly to accept the yoke, a yoke which, as Christ himself says, is easy and light: iugum enim meum suave est, et onus meum leve.27 It only becomes unpleasant when we don’t accept it, when we give free rein to our sensuality, which drives us to live like animals and not like human beings who are doubly indebted to God

– because he created us, and because we are Christians. The same happens if we allow pride, which is a worse scourge than sensuality, to raise the standard of rebellion in the soul, or if we don’t fight to subject our passions to right reason, with the help of grace.

24 Ps 104:10 25 Phil 2:8 26 St Augustine, Sermon 169, 13 27 For my yoke is easy, and my burden is light: Mt 11:30

Let us seek the help of our Mother the Blessed Virgin, whose own obedience was so marvellous, so humble and so natural. When the Angel Gabriel announces to her the mystery of the Incarnation, our Lady, troubled and surprised at the greatness of the message revealed to her, asks in all simplicity: “How shall this be, since I have no husband?”28 When the Archangel explains that the fruit of her blessed womb will be the work of the Holy Spirit, because nothing is impossible to God, Mary replies: “Behold, I am the handmaid of the Lord; let it be done to me according to your word.”29 At the enchantment of these virginal words, as the fruit of this act of identification with the divine will, “the Word became flesh and dwelt among us”,30 the work of our redemption began.

28 Lk 1:34 29 Lk 1:38 30 Jn 1:14

# LEAVEN * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.15}

I have here some old notes I made in 1935 and in February 1936 for some retreats I gave your brothers, because what I want to say to you now at the start of this retreat is the same as I said to those sons of mine all those years ago. “Send out your light and your truth.”1 We are gathered here together to try to see that light, to sanctify ourselves, precisely because we are continually dealing with holy things. I don’t mean physical objects like chalices, ciboria and monstrances, not at all, but souls. Precisely because of that, we may perhaps become accustomed to seeing the wonders of God and the deficiencies of human beings. That can perhaps make it a little more difficult to withdraw and consider the state of our own soul. Today you and I have come here to consider our own individual situation.

## A glance at the world {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.15.1}

Emitte lucem tuam et veritatem tuam,2 send out your light and your truth. These days are going to be an excellent opportunity, because God our Lord wants you and me to be saints, so that we can sanctify other people. And so you need to look at yourself, to look at God our Lord, and then to look at the world and see how it is.

* Notes of a meditation given at the start of a retreat in Rome, 27 March 1962 1 Ps 43:3 2 Ps 43:3

In that other retreat all those years ago I invited your brothers to observe the situation of mankind. See that red stain, I said to them, spreading rapidly across the earth, blotting out everything, trying to wipe out the slightest expression of anything supernatural. And wherever one doesn’t see that stain, one sees every day the progressive advance of a great wave of sensuality, or should I say of bestiality, because men are tending more and more to live like animals. And one sees yet another colour advancing steadily, especially in Latin countries, and more hypocritically in other places: an anticlerical atmosphere – of the bad sort – which seeks to confine God and the Church completely to the realm of conscience. Although that’s not quite the right description. Let’s put it more clearly. It seeks to confine God and the Church to the private sphere, so that one’s faith has no expression in public life. I don’t think I’m exaggerating: those three dangers are constant, evident, and aggressive. That was how things were then. Have they changed much since? I don’t think so, unfortunately. And what are you and I going to do about it? We have to implant the cleanliness of purity in the life of mankind, in human society, and, echoing the Gospel phrase, to season everything with the light and salt of Christ. That is why Opus Dei came into the world, as I reminded the people who were doing the retreat back in 1935: the sanctification of work; apostolate; abundant reparation, in desire and in reality. Our goals can be stated clearly and concisely as follows: holiness and apostolate. This is the purpose of our vocation. We have taken a glance at the world. We cannot shut our eyes to what we see: it would be completely unacceptable. Neither should we be overcome by a passive, inert pessimism, but on the contrary we should be set on fire and filled with the same holy impatience as Christ, who strode ahead of his disciples with hurried steps –

praecedebat illos Iesus 3 – as he headed up to Jerusalem for the last time, to be baptised with the baptism to which his spirit constantly beckoned him.4

## Making good use of the retreat {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.15.2}

What do I recommend to do the retreat well? The same as I recommended to my children many years ago. In the first place, interior recollection. Don’t turn your senses outwards; mortify your memory and imagination; avoid dissipation. And here I want to repeat something I’ve mentioned dozens of times. It is a story about a gang of thieves, one used by many classical spiritual authors. These authors recount how the bandits of their time, big, powerful men, used to take a scrawny little boy along with them on their raids. When they came to a house with its doors securely bolted, but with a tiny window open on an upper floor, apparently inaccessible, they would form a human tower: the little fellow would climb up and enter the small opening, then steal down the stairs and open the front door so the whole gang could enter to rob and plunder. My children, this often happens to souls. It is a strategy of the devil, who does whatever he can to keep us from encountering our Lord, and especially to keep us from benefiting from a retreat. He uses the silliest distraction to make us lose our concentration, and through that tiny window all the concerns we left behind come back in; we lose the recollection we need and fail to gain all the benefits God wants to give us. Along with interior recollection, exterior silence. For a soul dedicated to God the night-time silence is truly necessary. Once nightfall comes I feel a deep need for recollection. My children, silence is a divine blessing; during these days it is eminently

3 Mk 10:32 4 Cf. Lk 12:50

advisable and in many ways, a necessity. If I want you to have more personal latitude than usual during this retreat, it is because I know that each of you will make better use of the time by carrying out privately those acts of piety that we normally do together. You will try to be recollected, to keep silence, and that way you will be better able to hear what God wants to tell you. The divine Sower will come, and his seed has to fall on good soil: not beside the path, nor on rocky ground, nor among thorns. We don’t have to say to him: edissere nobis parabolam,5 because you and I have understood him perfectly. Omnis ergo qui audit verba mea haec et facit ea assimilabitur viro sapienti, qui aedificavit domum suam supra petram,6 therefore, anyone who listens to my instructions and fulfils them will be like a wise man who built his house on rock. This is forever our duty: to listen to God’s words and put them into practice, especially during the retreats and in the daily periods of recollection. Then God seems as if he were ours alone, as if the only people on earth were just the two of us: he and I. We have to offer ourselves unreservedly: you, so that God can make use of you, and everything that is yours, just as he pleases; and I the same. In listing the conditions for a good retreat, another one stands out: trust. Do you remember the Good Shepherd? How his love makes him follow the sheep that runs off, how he goes after it and seeks it out?7 Therefore we can be confident. God will give us light to see that he is everything, that outside of him nothing has any value at all. Christ invites us to follow him closely: si quis vult post me venire, abneget semetipsum,8 if anyone wants to come after me, let him deny himself; and he will give us the strength to persevere. This

5 Explain to us the parable: Mt 13:36 6 Mt 7:24 7 Cf. Lk 15:4-7 8 Mt 16:24

nevertheless demands of us a great spiritual docility, something which is indispensable for the interior life, so we can pay attention to the words the Holy Spirit breathes in our inmost soul. One needs a very sensitive ear to hear our Lord speaking. This is more easily understood by means of a comparison I have used thousands of times. If a spot of dust or a tiny insect alights on the delicate skin of a small child, the reaction is instantaneous. If it lands on the rough, callused hand of a labourer who has spent years tilling the soil, it won’t cause any reaction at all. If you don’t hear God’s voice during these days of retreat, you have to rid your heart of that hard crust. This can be achieved in two ways: mortification and contrition – with love. I next have a series of reasons I offered my hearers as to why they should be grateful to God: they are equally valid for us now. We should be particularly grateful to him for these days of retreat: ecce quam bonum et quam iucundum habitare fratres in unum! 9 Behold, how good and pleasant it is when brothers dwell in unity! Consider how we are gathered here like the Apostles in the Upper Room, I reminded them, and then consider, with wonder, how we are the first ones in the Work. The point I made then, nearly thirty years ago, is still true: we are very grateful to you, Lord, for these days of retreat in “our” house. The word “our” is underlined. I was referring to the Residence we had in Ferraz Street, where we had the first tabernacle of the Work. I say the same to you now: give thanks to God for this retreat which we are doing “in our house”, in this Centre here in Rome. This circumstance has its significance, because if God is willing to give his light to all souls, and particularly those dedicated to him, he is most especially willing to illuminate us who are charged with governing Opus Dei.

9 Ps 133:1

## The leaven of a few {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.15.3}

God our Lord, in the midst of great desertions, has always retained a faithful remnant who act on the multitude like leaven. “A remnant will return, the remnant of Jacob, to the mighty God. For though your people Israel be as the sand of the sea, only a remnant of them will return.”10 The unplucked fruit remains on the olive when it is shaken, “as at the gleaning”,11 as the prophets said. And as St Paul wrote to the Romans, “so too at the present time there is a remnant, chosen by grace.”12 Jesus used just a few as leaven: that group of saintly men and women who cooperated with the first disciples, in whose hearts he had sown a marvellous seed. I have here a note of mine written in 1935. Though it may no longer be applicable to the Work as a whole, it continues to be relevant to the situation in many regions. My daughters and sons who are beginning the Work all over the world will find joy and peace in this thought. Written on this card are the words: Our mission is divine, a mission of effectiveness and victory. But there are few of us. They didn’t need to do a retreat to discover that there were very few of us. And then I added: Do we want there to be more? That was a rhetorical question: Then let us be better! My dearest children, if we have that sense of responsibility, trying to be better every day, we will obtain from God, more abundantly every day, the grace of a vocation to the Work for many people, so that they too may be better and be more, and no longer just a few. Today we are, by the grace of God, a multitude, and we can see the effective action of the leaven: those few people of the first hour who had faith in God and in this poor sinner, and who have been,

10 Is 10:21 11 Is 24:13 12 Rom 11:5

just like you, an effective leaven, thanks to the power of their supernatural life, their work and their willing spirit of sacrifice. Next come some phrases which for many years used to greatly enkindle my love for God. They are the words in which Jesus expressed his desire to set the world ablaze with his fire. I found myself unable to repress the impetuous fervour they aroused within me, and use to shout them aloud: ignem veni mittere in terram, et quid volo nisi ut accendatur?13 I came to cast fire upon the earth; and would that it were already kindled! And the response: Ecce ego, quia vocasti me! 14 Here I am, for you have called me! All my children should feel the same generous desire to do everything in their power, making whatever sacrifices are necessary, to mobilise in God’s service all the stiffened, benumbed energies of humankind, making our own our Lord’s cry: misereor super turbam,15 I am sorry for the crowd. Nobody in Opus Dei can be at ease and not be deeply moved at the sight of the anonymous masses: a flock, a drove, a herd, as I once wrote.16 How many noble passions lie buried in their apparent indifference, how many possibilities! We have to serve them all, we have to lay our hands on each of them, like Jesus: singulis manus imponens,17 to bring them back to life, to heal them, to enlighten their minds and strengthen their wills, so they can be of use! And we will turn the flock into an army, the drove into a levy, and from the herd we will draw, purified, those who no longer wish to be unclean. But let us continue. St Paul says to the Corinthians: modicum fermentum totam massam corrumpit,18 a little leaven leavens the

13 Lk 12:49 14 1 Sam 3:6 15 Mk 8:2 16 Cf. The Way, 914 17 He laid his hands on every one of them: Lk 4:40 18 1 Cor 5:6

whole lump. The following note is dated 23 February 1936. I was inviting your brothers to consider this fact: There are still few of us. And with total confidence I added: All the better! So we are facing a multitude? We, on the one hand, are united in love; whereas they, although apparently united, in fact are fragmented, because they are only united in hatred: the hatred which was nourished by the situation in Spain in 1936 and which has always existed: the hatred darkness feels for light,19 the eternal struggle of evil against good, and which nowadays God our Lord has allowed to crystallise, at least in part, in Communism. We are by vocation a leaven of Christian life in the human race. Leaven doesn’t produce its effect all at once. Tell this to your brothers from regions where they’re still beginning, where they’re finding the going hard because there still are so few of them. No, it doesn’t produce its effect violently or partially, but acts on the whole of the dough through its own inner power, silently and effortlessly. Leaven makes the dough light and tender, makes it rise and become good to eat. Without it, the flour and water would yield only a heavy, indigestible, unwholesome lump. Christ our Lord has always used the leaven of a small number of people, and this notwithstanding the fact that he wants to save not just a minority but all mankind: omnes homines vult salvos fieri.20 Look at the leaven of Bethlehem… Look at the leaven of Nazareth – follow me with your imagination and your memory – and Thabor and the Upper Room. Look at the leaven of Calvary! And then? Then comes Pentecost, the gift of tongues, the mass conversions.

## Doing and disappearing {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.15.4}

What is the great mass of mankind like? I asked those sons of mine in those far-off days. On asking the same question again now, we are forced to admit that it is in great need of a leaven capable of

19 Cf. Jn 1:5 20 He desires all men to be saved: 1 Tim 2:4

divinising it; and, by divinising it, making it truly human. Even many of those who call themselves disciples, even those who are officially devout, have need of this leaven. The world will always stand in need of leaven. But in order to work as leaven, we have to overcome the concupiscence of the flesh, and be detached from earthly honours, and spurn riches. We have to stifle the triple concupiscence St John reminds us of: omne quod est in mundo concupiscentia carnis est et concupiscentia oculorum et superbia vitae,21 all that is in the world is affected by the lust of the flesh and the lust of the eyes and the pride of life. Many people don’t want to be leaven, because to be effective, it has to disappear and go unnoticed, without receiving appreciation or human esteem. Being leaven takes a lot of supernatural life and a great spirit of sacrifice. I can tell you now: if you make good use of all God’s graces, more than you have up till now, you will be able to recite that verse of the psalm: non moriar – because grace is something that nobody’s going to take away from me – sed vivam, et narrabo opera Domini,22 I shall not die, but I shall live, and recount the deeds of the Lord. And in this retreat, my dearest children, you and I have to make firm, specific resolutions, because that’s the only way we can become Christ’s good leaven. Let me read for you what I said to your brothers back then, so you can see once again that the spirit hasn’t changed: A good leaven for Christ, apostles in the middle of the world, with a persevering apostolate, working little by little, but intensely, knowing how to wait, gaining ground every day. Without any press releases or fanfare, because our work can never depend for its growth on external agencies but on the intimate, intrinsic power of the Holy

21 1 Jn 2:16 22 Ps 118:17

Spirit, who acts in our souls and makes St Paul’s cry come true: “It is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me.”23 I don’t want to continue any further. Now it’s up to you to work on all I’ve just told you for yourself, making a little summary of it. You have all you need to perceive God’s voice inviting you and me to be leaven: after all, we’re so completely hidden here, my sons! We seem to be in contact with nobody, and yet you see how from here in Rome there goes forth strength, grace and enlightenment, a supernatural current, to the entire Work. Let’s not place obstacles in the path of grace. We must be convinced that to be leaven we have to be saints. We should tell our Lord we’re sorry for our evil ways and really implore his help, and make clear and specific resolutions to remedy our past faults, and prepare very humbly to be the leaven God wants. Like in Bethlehem, like on Calvary: few in comparison to the multitude, but filled with love for the Cross of Jesus. We have the custom of invoking our Blessed Lady at the end of our meditation, and no day could be more appropriate for that than today: may she enlighten us, may she sustain us along the way, so we may emerge enflamed, and our lives may become lives of love, for God, for the Church, and for souls. Let us say, as the fruit of our renewed and improved Christian self-giving: vivo autem, iam non ego; vivit vero in me Christus.24

23 Gal 2:20 24 It is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me: ibid.

# A SUIT MADE TO MEASURE * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.16}

Hodie, si vocem eius audieritis, nolite obdurare corda vestra.1 Very often indeed, my dearest children, have I meditated on these words which we priests recite every day, and helped others to do so too. Today, if you hear his voice, don’t harden your hearts. And some of you may ask: “Father, do you think that here among your sons there could exist someone with such a hardened heart that he is not prepared to open it wide and reveal it clearly, so that God’s grace can pierce it, by doing all he can to be docile and respond to God’s inspirations?” I would say that one thing is bare-faced hardness (which would make it impossible to persevere in Opus Dei) and another is the fact of having some corner of our soul which is not properly disposed. It is possible that deep in our heart there could be something not quite right, which could indeed be described as hardened. I know a soul that in its deepest recesses felt the divine cry of those words in St Luke’s Gospel: ignem veni mittere in terram, et quid volo nisi ut accendatur? 2 I came to cast fire upon the earth; and would that it were already kindled! This is the fire of God, that burns, illuminates and cauterises. But, my children, although fire can melt bronze, it causes earthenware to harden. And if a heart contains too much of the latter, if it retains things it shouldn’t and is

* Notes of a meditation given during a retreat in Rome, 25 February 1963 1 O that today you would hearken to his voice! Harden not your hearts: Ps 95:7-8 2 Lk 12:49

unwilling to reject them, it becomes hardened by contact with the divine fire. But, you’ll say, we’re talking about little things! Very true, my children, they are indeed little. But let me remind you that for souls in love with God, big things and little things are all very relative. Something quite insignificant can become a very big obstacle to intimacy with our Love.

## An ancient practice in the Church {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.16.1}

Hodie, si vocem eius audieritis, nolite obdurare corda vestra.3 The other day, when speaking to a group of your brothers in the Pentecost Oratory, the thought occurred to me that every human being is in some way special, so much so that in any given group each one of us needs to be treated differently. I will explain. Have you noticed how people generally, you and I included, have a sort of innate tendency to label things according to our particular background? When a doctor sees someone in the street, he says to himself without realising it: “That person has liver trouble.” And if instead of a doctor he’s a tailor, he says: “What awful clothes,” or maybe, “What a well-dressed individual!” And if he’s a shoemaker he notices the person’s footwear, and so on. You and I are God’s children, dedicated to serving our fellow men for love of him. So whenever we meet people we have to see them as souls, and think to ourselves: here is a soul whom I have to help, whom I have to understand, whom I have to befriend, whom I have to help save. Anyone who does not hunger to help all souls does not have a vocation to Opus Dei. Let me continue with the thread of my idea. From one and the same cloth, suits of different sizes and cuts are made according to each person’s tastes and needs. One and the same medicine serves to cure many different illnesses, according to the circumstances of

3 Ps 95:7-8

each patient. Likewise, during the retreats we all receive the same doctrine, the same for all, the doctrine of the Church and the good spirit of the Work, and according to our personal situation we each strive to adapt it to our own needs and fashion a made-to-measure suit, with the help we get in personal spiritual direction. Yesterday you had the preparatory meditation of the retreat. And now I want to make use of this opportunity to explain what exactly a retreat is supposed to be. Is it old or new? What century does it date from? My children, it has its origins in Christian antiquity! The first Christians already did retreats. After Christ’s Ascension into heaven we find the Apostles and a large group of the faithful gathered in the Upper Room, together with our Blessed Lady, awaiting the outpouring of the Paraclete which Jesus had promised. And there the Holy Spirit finds them, perseverantes unanimiter in oratione,4 deep in prayer. The same is true of those members of the early communities who, without withdrawing from everyday life, dedicated themselves to God in their own homes; and the anchorites who went off into the desert to dedicate themselves in solitude to the contemplation of God… and also to work! They worked in order to eat, and the ancient traditions tell us that when they didn’t have a pressing need to do so, to avoid idleness they used to make and unmake baskets. It is always necessary to work, because that is why God created man: ut operaretur.5 Afterwards came the monks, in bigger or smaller communities, who set aside some periods of time for more intense prayer. Later still the great religious orders – Franciscans, Dominicans and others

– continued the same tradition; the orders of clerics regular, particularly numerous in Italy; the religious congregations, the societies of common life… All Christians who have really been

4 Acts 1:14 5 Gen 2:15

concerned about the state of their souls have done some form of retreat, because it’s a Christian custom. And we, the children of God in Opus Dei, like those first Christians, do exactly the same, although we are not religious, nor do we abandon the world in order to dedicate ourselves to God, but we do need to exercise our souls in an intense relationship with our Lord. Because to perform exercises, as the word itself suggests, means repeating a series of actions. One speaks of physical exercises, and military exercises… It was military life that inspired the title of the work by Abbot Ximénez de Cisneros, Exercises of the Spiritual Life, written in the ancient abbey of Montserrat. It was there that St Ignatius Loyola, whom I really love, withdrew after his conversion. Afterwards, with the help of God’s grace and all the good antecedents available to him, he composed the Spiritual Exercises, which are one way, among many, of carrying out that “exercise” which is so necessary for a healthy interior life.

## Avoiding anonymity {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.16.2}

Withdrawing for a few days to pray is, therefore, a Christian custom. We only have to open the Gospel to find many examples of our Lord spending time in prayer. Christ, my children, teaches us to pray by his example. He would often withdraw from his followers’ company, on one occasion for as long as forty days together,6 and call upon his Father. And he always acted like that: before the great miracles,7 and during his intensive journeying from village to village;8 before undergoing his Passion, and throughout the torments of his death.9 See how he makes sure the evangelists tell us about his prayer to God the Father.

6 Cf. Mt 4:1-2; Mk 1:12-13; Lk 4:1-2 7 Cf. Jn 11:41-42 8 Cf. Lk 11:1 9 Cf. Lk 22:41-42

You and I want to reach holiness: that’s why we’ve come to Opus Dei. To achieve it we must identify ourselves with Christ, we must clothe ourselves with Christ: induimini Dominum Iesum Christum.10 We must all be ipse Christus, my beloved sons, Christ himself. However, we each have to work out how to put on the garment that the Apostle speaks about. We each have to have our personal conversation with our Lord. Jesus was praying in a remote place, before dawn had broken, and Peter went to look for him because many people were waiting for him.11 What must Jesus’ prayer have been like? I am deeply moved by our Lord’s great need – St Mark records that it was long before daybreak12 – to speak with his Eternal Father and entrust to him all the apostolate with souls he was going to do that day. We have no other option. At the start of each day’s labour at Christ’s side, ministering to the souls who seek him, the one thing we must do is turn to our Lord in prayer: only in prayer will we learn to serve others. So then, what are you and I going to do during this retreat? We’re going to converse with our Lord, look for him, like Peter, to have an intimate conversation with him. Note that I say “conversation”, that is, a one-to-one dialogue, face to face, without taking refuge in anonymity. We need this personal prayer, this intimacy, this direct relationship with God our Lord. I find it strange that even people who want to serve God act as if they are afraid to talk to him personally. Many people are quite happy to mix with the crowd and, surrounded by the noisy clamour of the multitude, address very beautiful prayers to God: the Our Father, which we have from the lips of Christ himself; the Hail Mary, the Archangel’s greeting to our Lady… all wonderful prayers! But if you speak to them about emerging from the

10 Put on the Lord Jesus Christ: Rom 13:14 11 Cf. Mk 1:36-37 12 Cf. Mk 1:35

anonymity of the crowd in order to have a personal dialogue with God, many of them recoil at the thought. They’re not ready for it. I am reminded of the sneaky behaviour of those who, seeing people worked up in a street demonstration, mingle with the mob and start shouting and throwing stones themselves… Face to face they would never dare. They are cowards. My children, anybody at all, no matter how unimportant he is, will expect you to make an appointment in order to see him. Then you may have to wait a while when you arrive, spend time in a waiting-room, not get impatient and perhaps come back another day… and so on until you get to meet the individual himself, important or otherwise. You will tell me that this is quite reasonable, and indeed it is, because everybody has the right to have people respect their work or their personal timetable. The fact remains that you have to ask for an appointment and wait. On the other hand – consider this, my son, and think about it very often throughout your life – when we want to see God our Lord, to speak intimately with him, everything is so easy… Jesus doesn’t make us wait for him, he doesn’t have a waiting-room: he is the one who waits for us. You only have to say: “Lord! I want to pray, I want to meet you,” and you are already in God’s presence, speaking to him. What’s more, he doesn’t set a time limit: he leaves it up to you. He’s available, not just for ten minutes or a quarter of an hour. No! For hours, for the whole day! And he is who he is: the Almighty, the most Wise. My children, if you meditate on these things your souls will overflow with joy. You can and should talk to God our Lord: he waits for you, he gives you all the time you want, and he gives you inspirations. He also works through your Directors, whenever a doubt assails you, or when you want reassurance about some idea or supernatural inspiration.

## Bound by love {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.16.3}

Let us continue with the theme of the meditation. We’re trying to tease out the objectives of the retreat. And we have excluded quite a few already, because we already know that making the suit of clothes, or taking the medicine, cannot itself be the goal. The goal is very specific and personal in each case. I will try to help you determine what it is and to do so I will provide you with the cloth. Everybody gets the same cloth, but you each have to tailor your own suit. In the first place, think about your dedication to God. I’m going to read you something that will help. Your dedication presupposes a great act of love, a self-giving which as far as you are concerned cannot be any more complete than it is, because you give yourself completely: your thoughts, your work, your mind, your memory, your senses, your soul and your body.
