What you and I have done, with the grace of God our Lord, is truly amazing! Ascetically, our dedication to God in Opus Dei is, from the start, a life-long commitment; it is with this firm desire that we knocked on the door of the Work. But afterwards each one has to be proved to be suitable; and so, juridically, we have established a particular day in the year, the feast of St Joseph, when those who want to continue renew their dedication, until such time as they are incorporated definitively. Is that sufficient? Does one have to do anything else? No. If you renew your dedication to God every day, that’s wonderful! Forty times day? Fantastic! Juridically it doesn’t mean anything, but ascetically your love will grow more and more fervent, and God our Lord will help you to keep going, improving and sorting out your life. When I speak of the purpose of the retreat, I would like it to help you to reflect on your dedication, to look into the depths of your soul to see what is not going well, the earthenware – the fruit of our

weakness – that cracks in the fire of God’s love instead of melting like burnished metal. Your dedication to God, my son, is linked to your incorporation to the Work, which includes a contract of love. God fills you with his grace. The Work gives us the supernatural and human means, doctrine and formation, and we have to use them willingly and freely to respond to the grace of our vocation. And then comes your own determination to make good use of these gifts. See how all the different elements – juridical, theological, and ascetical – are intertwined with the grace of God, which summons us and at the same time leaves us free, and with our feeble human freedom which replies: “Here I am!” I remember a poor man who for years heard in the depth of his soul, without knowing what it was, the phrase: ignem veni mittere in terram, et quid volo nisi ut accendatur? 13 In the midst of his human weakness he replied: ecce ego, quia vocasti me!14 Lord, here I am, because you have called me…, not knowing what he was being called to. You and I now know perfectly well what God is calling us to: he is calling us to be saints. What a good moment this retreat is to resolve to struggle for the specific supernatural goal you see you need most! What a good preparation if you and I say, silently, in a conversation like that of a son with his father, a lover with his beloved, a soul with God: ecce ego, quia vocasti me…!15 Lord, here I am; Lord, give me light; Lord, let me see. Lord, I want to: cleanse me, make me a good instrument of yours, because I gave myself to you with a commitment of love. Between the Work and each one of you there is a contract, a bond which is the vocational expression of a commitment of love of God, of self-giving, and which is expressed in your desire to practise the Christian virtues that we want to flower in our lives. I

13 I came to cast fire on the earth: and would that it were already kindled! Lk 22:49 14 1 Sam 3:6 15 Ibid.

will now mention a number of these points, in divine and human terms.

## To each one his own suit {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.16.4}

Let us consider briefly three virtues which unite us especially to Christ, like the three nails which fastened his Body to the Cross. They are not the most important virtues – that place is reserved for charity – nor the only ones, because we have to practise all the virtues mentioned in the Catechism of the Work, which is a summary of our particular law: diligence, simplicity, loyalty, cheerfulness… Poverty. The following is a very common spiritual observation, but perfectly applicable to us all: we have to imitate the poverty of Christ our Lord, who “though he was rich,” he was almighty, “yet for your sake he became poor”,16 to the point of having nowhere to lay his head.17 There is another virtue, chastity, about which I once said something I afterwards wasn’t all that happy about, as many of my children have heard me say; but here it is anyway: I said that chastity makes men and women become like angels… I don’t think it’s really true, because the members of Opus Dei are not angels, but normal men and women, with feelings, passions, and defects that they try to elevate to God. What I was trying to express with that comparison, which I’m now not so happy with, is that no apostolic undertaking can be successful if we are not pure and chaste, even though we experience the weight of the flesh. This weight has also been experienced even by canonised saints. It doesn’t matter if sometimes you have to cry out with St Paul: video autem aliam legem in membris meis repugnantem legi mentis

16 2 Cor 8:9 17 Cf. Mt 8:20; Lk 9:58

meae et captivantem me in lege peccati, quae est in membris meis,18 I see in my members another law at war with the law of my mind and making me captive to the law of sin which dwells in my members. Keep going! With the help of God, my children, possumus!19 We will always be victorious, even though sometimes we may be vanquished, if we get up at once and renew the struggle once more. And obedience? It’s enough for now to remind you that it has to be like Christ’s, who “became obedient unto death, even death on a cross.”20 I’ve already mentioned that in the Catechism of the Work you study all our particular law. If there is any detail not spelled out there you learn it in practice by seeing how your Directors govern, with an exquisite respect for the personal freedom of all their brothers or sisters. The time will come when we can publish a book developing some points of our spirit to be studied in the Centres of Studies. For now, that is enough: in the Catechism of the Work (which you learn off by heart – it’s like having the Code of Canon Law in your hands, only more so) and in our life, we find everything we need in order to identify ourselves with the spirit God has given us, and be effective.

## True piety {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.16.5}

As I was saying, the Work demands many other very important virtues of us: diligence, simplicity, order, cheerfulness… And it provides us with the means to practise them, scattered among the norms of piety for every day, every week, every month, and every year. The means are prayer, mortification and work. Our prayer should be so intense and so uninterrupted that it leads us to be contemplatives in the middle of the world. Our mortification should cause us to love the Cross. We can’t be soft

18 Rom 7:23 19 We can! Cf. Mt 20:22 20 Phil 2:8

and comfort-seeking Christians. Suffering and the Cross are inevitable on this earth. We love the Cross. But when it’s ours, it is a cross written with a small “c”, and it is heavy. We are like someone standing with his nose right up against a wall: it can appear insurmountable. But if at that painful moment we act as contemplative souls and children of God, and from the littleness of our life we lift up our eyes and hearts to heaven, we will see that the obstacle is nothing at all, that we can vault over it as a bull-fighter vaults over the barriers in the bull-ring, as they say in my part of the world. When we accept the inevitable sorrows of this world – the small ones and the big ones, they’re all the same – considering that the Cross is Christ’s, then it isn’t heavy and we carry it willingly. “And, Father, do we still suffer?” Yes, my son, and with tears too. But we don’t lose our cheerfulness. Try not to make a show of yourself with tears. I have cried a lot during my life, and I’m not ashamed to say so. “Father, have you been very unhappy?” No, I have been very happy, and I still am. Only the thought that God could let go my hand – though he won’t – only that fleeting fear can sometimes assail my heart and take my cheerfulness away. Will I persevere, will I really persevere? We will indeed persevere, with God’s grace. All that’s needed is to be faithful in little things, because God will hold us by the hand. Prayer, mortification, work. Our lives are filled with sincere piety. Our lives are contemplative, hard-working and unafraid of pain, because we know that if we meet the Cross, it means that we are following the same path as Christ himself. I have to stop here: I don’t like going over the half-hour. My children, invoke our Lady with all your hearts, trustingly. She has been our greatest protector, our greatest recourse, ever since the second of October 1928, and even before then. She is reassurance. She is hope. She is the Mother of Fair Love. She is the beginning and the seat of wisdom. And she, the Virgin Mother and Mediatrix 200 WHILE HE SPOKE TO US ON THE WAY of all graces, is the one who will lead us by the hand to her Son Jesus.

# THE KING’S BANQUET * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.17}

“My Lord and my God, I firmly believe that you are here, that you see me, that you hear me” – you can’t imagine how much I personally needed to include those words in the opening prayer. “I adore you with profound reverence…” Throughout the year we have been briefly considering some ideas to help those who have a mission of government or formation in Opus Dei. And now, as we review our situation today during this recollection, and I try to express your prayer and mine in words, it makes sense for me to look for a way to adapt this dialogue with God to the specific needs of us who fulfil that function. All Christians, when they pause to consider things in prayer, have to make their own the goal proposed by St Augustine: noverim me, noverim te,1 to know God, to know oneself, and draw the obvious conclusions. Moreover, anybody who, for whatever reason, assumes responsibility for the care of souls, has to feel the duty of teasing out in his prayer, not only personal conclusions, but also all those things which will make the fulfilment of his task more effective, with regard to the holiness of those entrusted to his care, their apostolic effectiveness, their eternal happiness and indeed their earthly happiness too. We are obliged to look after them, to care for them, to be concerned for them, with supernatural outlook certainly,

* Notes of a meditation given during a recollection in Rome, 13 October 1963, at the time the 19th Sunday after Pentecost 1 St Augustine, Soliloquies, 2, 1

but also like a mother. And to do that, we can make use of the Gospel of today’s Mass, the nineteenth Sunday after Pentecost.

## A divine invitation to everybody {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.17.1}

Although the priest is called through his ministry to consecrate the Bread of heaven, it is not only to him that these words are addressed: “Blessed is he who shall eat bread in the kingdom of God!”2 All men and women without exception are invited through Baptism to share in the banquet of the great King, Jesus Christ. Each of them has to be told: amice, ascende superius,3 seek to share in the banquet, don’t forget your dignity as a son or daughter of God, look at Christ, whom you ought to imitate,4 who proclaims to all souls: ecce prandium meum paravi, my banquet is ready; venite ad nuptias.5 This is the scene the Church offers us today, from St Matthew’s Gospel. We read: “The kingdom of heaven may be compared to a king who gave a marriage feast for his son, and sent his servants to call those who were invited to the marriage feast; but they would not come.”6 The great King has come and invited many people to his banquet. Here on earth all souls are called to the great King’s wedding feast, but many do not want to go, so they decline the invitation and the hall remains empty. I too know something of those refusals, my children. More than once I have spoken to you about those lonely early times, those years when I practically repeated the words of our Lord: ecce prandium meum paravi, tauri mei at altilia occisa sunt, et omnia parata: venite ad nuptias.7 I have made ready the banquet. I have had the calves and the other fattened animals killed, and everything

2 Lk 14:15 3 Friend, go up higher: Lk 14:10 4 Cf. 1 Cor 4:16; 11:1 5 Come to the marriage feast: Mt 22:4 6 Mt 22:2-3 7 Mt 22:4

is ready. God has prepared for us the fullness of Love, without treachery or weariness, with all goodness and all beauty. “But they made light of it and went off, one to his farm” – to his selfishness, to his comfort. “And another to his business”8 – to his work, which he did not know how to turn into a divine path on earth, perhaps because no one had taught him. We have to cry out as loudly and insistently as necessary to make sure that the events of the parable are not replayed, so that nobody remains engrossed in their ordinary work while failing to turn it into a principal element of their divine Christian vocation, because it is precisely in our job or occupation that God wants us to sanctify ourselves. We have to teach all mankind that to be a Christian is something really extraordinary. We have to teach them that the soul of the believer is God’s holy temple,9 the dwelling place of the Blessed Trinity;10 but that if one wishes to achieve Christian perfection, one has to wage the battles of the interior life resolutely, because the kingdom of God can only be obtained by great effort: regnum caelorum vim patitur, et violenti rapiunt illud.11 My children, you know well – I have been preaching it since the beginning of the Work – that God is no respecter of persons.12 He calls everybody to the same holiness: men and women, priests and laity, the sick and the healthy, the learned and the ignorant. Those marvellous words of St Paul: “He chose us in him before the foundation of the world, that we should be holy and blameless before him,”13 are directed to everyone individually.

8 Mt 22:5 9 Cf. 1 Cor 3:17 10 Cf. Jn 14:23 11 The kingdom of heaven has suffered violence, and men of violence take it by force: Mt 11:12 12 Cf. 2 Chron 19:7 13 Eph 1:4-5

The Apostle also extends a clear invitation to the Christians of Thessalonika: haec est enim voluntas Dei, sanctificatio vestra,14 this is the will of God, your sanctification, because God has not called us to impurity, but to holiness: non enim vocavit nos Deus in immunditiam, sed in sanctificationem.15 Jesus, therefore, invites everybody to holiness: “You, therefore, must be perfect, as your heavenly Father is perfect.”16 And our heavenly Father has also predestined us “to be conformed to the image of his Son, in order that he might be the first-born among many brethren”,17 because “to all who received him, who believed in his name, he gave power to become children of God.”18 God invites us all to have in our hearts the same sentiments as Christ had in his,19 shaping our lives entirely according to the one Model of Christian perfection.20 We are all equally invited, and equally expected, in all circumstances, including in the middle of the world, to be contemplative souls and identify ourselves with Christ.21 In that way we become his followers and “clothe ourselves in him.”22

## Forgetting about self {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.17.2}

But let us return to the Gospel scene. Some of the guests asked to be excused, whereas others reacted more violently: “the rest seized his servants, treated them shamefully, and killed them.”23

14 1 Thess 4:3 15 1 Thess 4:7 16 Mt 5:48 17 Rom 8:29 18 Jn 1:12 19 Cf. Phil 2:5 20 Cf. Rom 10:4; Col 1:28 21 Cf. Gal 2:20 22 Cf. Gal 3:27 23 Mt 22:6

We’ve also had some experience of that in Opus Dei, because for many years we have suffered opposition, gossip and calumny. We have experienced in our own flesh the painful fact of how some people from outside have tried to neutralise our apostolate and those who carried it out. Do you see, my children, how the Gospel is always applicable? How, when we follow our Lord and live his life

– because each of us has to be not only alter Christus, another Christ, but ipse Christus, Christ himself – the sufferings of the Master are in a certain sense repeated in the disciple?24 To identify ourselves with Christ we have to put to death the selfishness of the old man.25 God wants our hearts to be so full of apostolic zeal that we forget about ourselves and instead, with willing self-sacrifice, dedicate ourselves to the whole human race. I think that all my children’s personal problems are solved if, when they come to do their examination of conscience, they can truly say: “Jesus, I haven’t been worrying about myself, I haven’t been thinking about myself at all.” Just think of all the disappointments and anxiety this would spare us! “Lord, I have been totally taken up with my brothers and sisters, with others, for you, for your Love, for your Glory.” And I remind all the sons and daughters of mine who can say that, and there are now very many of them, about St Paul’s words: vivo autem, iam non ego, vivit vero in me Christus.26 If you behave like that, you are a contemplative soul, you are ipse Christus. The solution to all personal problems, let me repeat, is forgetting about oneself and thinking about others, for God’s sake. Thus one travels along the ways of the earth, constructing God’s paths. It is a wonderfully effective medicine. Listen, some time or other the thought will suddenly strike you: “What on earth happened to the afternoon prayer...?” And when you stop to think about it you

24 Cf. Mt 10:24 25 Cf. Rom 6:6 26 It is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me: Gal 2:20

suddenly realise that you were praying all afternoon, and in fact all day! Be sure to keep taking this medicine, this constant conversation with God. My dearest children, lately God our Lord has given me more than ever feelings of great tenderness in my heart. He moves me to love you with an enthusiasm, an energy and a desire which lead me to help you and – why not admit it – to put myself out, to sacrifice myself, for you and for God. And this paternal love does not take away anybody’s freedom, intelligence, or responsibility. You, who have received from me the mission to look after your brothers, ought also have paternal and maternal feelings for them, in which fortitude is fused with charity. If some time you feel that this is the way you live your life, which is the life of Christ – and I’m sure you have felt it, or are feeling it now – then thank God for it.

## Making atonement for others {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.17.3}

When the king learned that his servants had been killed, he “was angry, and he sent his troops and destroyed those murderers and burned their city.”27 God, who is goodness itself, becomes angry. In the Old Testament especially one finds many passages which illustrate God’s anger, his powerful hand chastising sinful humanity. It was fearsome to behold! But that hand once again became gentle, loving and maternal as soon as the hearts and minds of that wayward people returned to him in repentance and atonement. Let us ask our Lord to have pity on all mankind, to continue to seek us out, to make us fall in love, with the eagerness of that good King. Miserere mei, Deus, secundum magnam misericordiam tuam.28 My children, let us ask God for forgiveness for our own faults, and for the faults of those who constitute our pusillus grex [our “little

27 Mt 22:7 28 Have mercy on me, O God, according to your steadfast love: Ps 51:1

flock”], saying to him with our minds, and with the beating of our poor human hearts, attende, Domine, et miserere, quia peccavimus tibi.29 We are required to be ipse Christus, and he is Agnus Dei, qui tollit peccata mundi,30 the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world. With our life of self-surrender we have to blot out from God’s sight our own faults and also those of others. In the same way, we have to try gently to heal the injuries our sheep have suffered, so that the flock is absolutely healthy. I remember – I wrote this for you years ago – as a child in the Pyrenees, on freezing winter days when everything was covered in snow, seeing shepherds making their way along the valleys of my native land, wrapped in their sheepskin jackets. The shepherd would be accompanied by his ever-faithful dogs, and a donkey laden with all his utensils. On top of it all the donkey carried a big pot in which the master cooked his food and prepared the dressings he put on the animals’ injuries. If one of the sheep had “taken a knock”, as they say there, if it had hurt a leg, I have seen the shepherd bring the Gospel parable to life and carry the injured animal on his shoulders. Other times – though they were rustic types who didn’t seem to have much in the way of tenderness – I have seen one carrying a new-born lamb lovingly in his arms. My children, this is beautiful! This is what we too must do: ask our Lord to forgive us and to forgive others; to be understanding and take on our shoulders the one who is going astray or who has “taken a knock.” And if that sheep has lost its sense of direction, then the ones at the back have to be sent forward to take the lead. We should pick up and look after those who are new-born in the Work, giving them the warmth of our embrace.

29 Pay heed, O Lord, and have mercy, because we have sinned against you: Roman Missal, Ash Wednesday, Responsory 30 Jn 1:29

I have spoken to you in parables, like our Lord in the Gospel. But the idea that you, and I too, are ipse Christus, is no parable. We are the good shepherd, who can’t do without a single one of his sheep!

## Going to the thoroughfares {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.17.4}

But God’s affairs always go ahead, even if we are not willing. “Then he said to his servants, ‘The wedding is ready, but those invited were not worthy. Go therefore to the thoroughfares, and invite to the marriage feast as many as you find.’”31 Aren’t you moved, my children? God calls everyone. You and I are part of this great crowd of people that he has sought out along all the highways and byways. And we have come like the guests in the parable: the lame, the blind, and the deaf.32 We can’t turn anybody away, not even people who don’t accept God’s invitation. If God has been so patient with mankind, can we behave any differently? Perhaps you find yourselves dealing with people who are incapable of serving, who are unwilling to lower themselves, who cannot bend down and kiss the floor truly with that cry which issues from our souls every day: serviam! I will serve! What can we do about it? Put into practice Christ’s command which the Evangelist gives us: “Go therefore to the thoroughfares, and invite to the marriage feast as many as you find.”33 We have the duty to do apostolate! We have the obligation not to allow anyone remain passive! My sons and daughters, your brothers and sisters, like the servants in the parable, have to work at the cross-roads of all the upright undertakings of mankind, to arouse in many people the divine vocation to Opus Dei. Wherever there are souls capable of serving God, there we have to be present to bring them to Christ. We have to make known to them the great King’s invitation:

31 Mt 22:8-9 32 Cf. Lk 14:21 33 Mt 22:9

‘everything is ready, come to the banquet.’ It is our duty to call many people to work in God’s service. The spirit of our Work is universal, open to everybody. I have often reminded you that our place is in the street, at the cross-roads of mankind… No closed doors or windows: the more people we are in contact with the better. As members of Opus Dei we should know lots of people, because we are interested in all souls. The work of sowing is something we should all be totally committed to. We can’t remain indoors. God hasn’t wanted his Work to be a residence, or a corporate apostolate: he has created it for the sanctification of souls. Like the servants in the parable, we have to go looking for them and not wait for them to come to us. In order to do that, we have to encounter Jesus in the Bread and in the Word, in the host and in prayer. We are souls who have fallen in love, and who are a little bit “unbalanced.” Many years ago people said I was crazy. A priest friend of mine told me: “they’re saying you’re crazy.” “Please pray, then,” I replied, “so that I become even more so.” It was true, my children. I was indeed crazy, as you are too, with the same craziness: crazy with the love of God. And what should you and I do if, because of our present circumstances, we cannot look for vocations for the Work ourselves directly, except in exceptional cases? The very same: prayer, mortification, work offered to God, with all these we can help – as indeed we do help – those who are working away throughout the world, fighting in the front line of our battle of love and peace. In the year 1934 I wrote: “We are like an intravenous injection in the bloodstream of society.”34 God has chosen us to be messengers of his saving doctrine: therefore, in all the various circumstances in which my children find themselves, they have to be really apostolic, completely certain that when we work for him, nothing we do, no matter how insignificant it appears, is ineffective. God in his

34 Instruction, 19 March 1934, 42

Providence wants to make mankind participate in his Redemption, and he has called us by a special vocation to be especially coredeemers. Last summer in Spain I had many opportunities to speak with your sisters and brothers. I urged them to win new apostles, but I also told them not to be naïve, because it’s not all that easy to determine in advance how exactly it should be done. In fact, I myself have never tried to lay down any precise guidelines on the matter. This zeal for souls is an expression – the best expression – of apostolic zeal, and is directed to the whole population of the world without exception: bringing them to God, setting them alight, purifying them, filling them with love… But after that, precisely because we are interested in getting to all souls, that great throng of people has to be approached systematically. For the zeal of our charity to be the charity of Christ it has to follow the right order, and to spread out in concentric circles. First your brothers and sisters, my sons and daughters, who already belong to our supernatural family and who form part of the flock. This is our first duty. After that we have to aim at the members of our own families – parents, brothers and sisters – because bringing them closer to the Work means bringing them closer to God. After that come all Catholics, and after that again those who call themselves Christians, even though they often don’t know who Christ is. Last of all, everybody else without exception. All souls, every single last one of them! For a son or daughter of God in Opus Dei there is no human being whom we’re not interested in: ite ad exitus viarum, et quocumque inveneritis, vocate ad nuptias.35 Go forth! I have said it to you repeatedly, because it’s not our style to sit quietly at home and not go forth to the highways seeking souls wherever they are to be found, to bring them afterwards to God, wounded by love, by

35 Go therefore to the thoroughfares, and invite to the marriage feast as many as you find: Mt 22:9

understanding, by self-giving, or at least by the desire for selfgiving. For thirty years I have made use of a comparison that makes people laugh. I don’t set out to make them laugh, but graphic illustrations do help to impress spiritual ideas more firmly on our imaginations and lives. Our Lord uses the same technique in his parables. Have you ever seen a woman trying to get a size 40 foot into a size 35 shoe with a shoe-horn? She keeps at it until eventually she succeeds. That’s what we have to do in the apostolate: keep at it, with blessed stubbornness. Exi in vias et saepes – this time it is St Luke writing – et compelle intrare, ut impleatur domus mea,36 go out to the highways and hedges, and compel people to come in, so that my house may be filled. Compel them to enter, induce them, bring them to me: all of that is contained in the “compelle intrare” of the Gospel, which is perfectly compatible with the greatest respect for the freedom of souls, and completely contrary to passivity, laziness or any sort of human respects. My children, you have to approach your colleagues and friends and summon them in God’s name: compelle intrare! If nobody had gone looking for you, you probably wouldn’t be here now, serving God in the Work. You’ve got to tell many people that Christ is seeking them too, as he sought the first Twelve, as he sought the Samaritan woman, and Zacchaeus, and the paralysed man. Each in their own workplace, and wherever they live, should feel compelled to invite many people to fill the banquet hall. You will ask me: “But Father, aren’t you always telling us that a principal characteristic of our spirit is love for freedom? How are we going to force souls to comply?” There’s no contradiction involved. It’s an invitation, a help in making up one’s mind, and not

36 Lk 14:23

in any sense a form of pressure. Precisely because a principal characteristic of our spirit is respect for people’s freedom, the “compelle intrare” in winning new apostles is not a physical push but rather a radiance of light and doctrine; the spiritual stimulus of your work, replete with the genuine witness of your doctrine; the sum total of all the sacrifices you offer; the smile that lights up your face because you are children of God. Plus, although you may not experience what people call tangible “human consolations”, the awareness of your divine filiation, endowing you with a serene happiness that others see and envy, even though sometimes you meet plenty of difficulties on your way. Add to all that your elegance and human warmth, and you have the content of the “compelle intrare”. That is how to be truly present at all the crossroads of the world, that is how to call all souls. I ask all my children to examine their consciences very carefully in their prayer to see what they have done to date to bring souls to Opus Dei. An examination which should give rise to a very firm resolution: to do what they can to obtain many vocations, because we need them. God needs them, the Church needs them, to make our service to mankind even more effective.

## Called and chosen {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.17.5}

“Those servants went out into the streets and gathered all whom they found, both bad and good; so the wedding hall was filled with guests.”37 We need to break through the crust of laziness which sometimes holds us back. We can’t remain passive. Each of us has to get involved in the lives of others, just as Christ did in your lives and in mine. If God hadn’t done so, what would have happened to me? Christ didn’t ask permission to make use of me as his instrument. With divine lordship he came and took up residence in the centre of

37 Mt 22:10

my soul: “do this and that for me”; and I obeyed like a little donkey. He is King of all mankind and we are his messengers. You know how the parable goes. At this point I only want to draw your attention to the words at the end of it: multi enim sunt vocati, pauci vero electi,38 many are called, but few are chosen. I am quite certain that all are called, but that many don’t wish to enter the banquet hall, or arrive without the wedding-garment provided by the master.39 We have been called and chosen through his grace. Every day at Mass I extend my hands over the offerings, the bread and wine which, through the consecration, are going to become our Lord’s Body and Blood, and there I place you all, my daughters and sons: in electorum tuorum iubeas grege numerari,40 and count us among those you have chosen. We don’t think of ourselves as an elite, except in the sense of being a leaven to enkindle others. We are the same as the other men and women around us, but God’s choice has given us a new light and has made us glow red hot. What great good fortune, my children! We have won a supernatural lottery. That’s why I remind you that, with God’s grace and with our Lady’s maternal protection, fighting and conquering, sometimes being conquered but always starting anew, we will continue to be called and chosen. Ordinarily the defeats will be in little things; but even if it happens to be something bigger or even horrendous, we continue to be called and chosen if we rectify and ask for forgiveness. Remember what I told you at the start: how even a poor wretch who is repentant can allay God’s anger. When I think of God’s special love for us I get ashamed, and sometimes – I don’t mind letting you know – I want to weep, and the tears burn my eyes because I haven’t managed to correspond to God’s grace as I ought.

38 Mt 22:14 39 Cf. Mt 22:11 40 Roman Missal, Eucharistic Prayer 1

On considering these things we end up making the resolution always to work and think for Love. Amidst all our faults we feel a great and irrepressible desire to bring others to share our joy also. Don’t you see, my children? Winning new apostles arises spontaneously, it is like the beating of our heart, a hunger to pass on the madness of God’s love to many other souls. What we have reflected on today is indeed good doctrine, and must necessarily do us a lot of good, because it gives us serenity and fortitude and enables us to help others. Even when we see very clearly the abyss between God and ourselves, and he lets us see the full extent of our own insignificance and inadequacy, we also see all his majesty, greatness, beauty, and power… Our deficiencies never take our peace away as long as we always see ourselves in the hands of our heavenly Father. This borrowed serenity, along with the specific grace of our Christian vocation to the Work and to the task we have been entrusted with, enable us to tap into all God’s effectiveness, so we can recover the injured sheep, treat it, and lead it back to the sheepfold. Moreover, my children, we also have to have the courage to acknowledge before God that very often it is the shepherds who are at fault, nearly always in fact – except when a sheep goes crazy and throws itself over a precipice. Then one suffers a lot. We have to remain serene, searching every gully, but avoiding the dangers of the precipices, to ensure, with properly ordered charity, each in his own sheepfold, that nobody is lost. Sancta Maria, Spes nostra, Virgo fidelis, ora pro nobis! [Holy Mary, our hope, Virgin most faithful, pray for us!]

# SOWERS OF PEACE AND JOY * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.18}

I will remind you, as on other occasions, that what I am trying to do is to formulate my own prayer aloud. When we priests give a meditation we are not putting on a show or giving a performance: we are praying to God personally. It is something that derives from the very essence of our contemplative life, while at the same time we try to help others have a trusting, filial dialogue with God, our Father and Lord. Last night I had difficulty getting to sleep, and since I habitually keep a writing pad on the bedside table, I made a few notes to use as an outline for this meditation. And what did I think about, my children? I thought about you. I don’t tend to think about myself. Sometimes yes, my selfishness takes over and I do think about myself. And believe me, when that happens I suffer a lot. But ordinarily it is you who are the subjectmatter of my life: your loyalty and your happiness. I offer you this recipe which you will find useful to avoid worries, to be faithful, to avoid temptations, to be effective: forget about yourselves, and that way Christ our Lord will live in you.

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 29 February 1964, during a workshop on the apostolate of public opinion

## The sower went forth {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.18.1}

Jesus came into the world because omnes homines vult salvos fieri,1 to save everyone. Since he was the Life, the Truth and the Way,2 he wanted to reveal the way, the truth and the life to all men of all times. And, with the gift of tongues, he adapted his message to the mentality of people of all backgrounds and historical periods. He used parables. This is one of the notes I made last night: Exiit qui seminat seminare semen suum,3 the sower went out to sow his seed: words taken from the Gospel of St Luke, that wonderful parable which is so full of lessons, and so relevant today. Isn’t that what you do through your work, through your lives, all over the world? And as for me, why did God take me and start to prepare me right from childhood? My God, how patient you have been with me! Ut iumentum factus sum apud te.4 Sometimes you have had to drive me along a bit like a beast of burden, with blows, because, as you know well, I wasn’t willing. Now I am willing; for many years now I have been willing, with all the weakness that is part of my life, with all my inadequacy, but with all the love of my heart… Exiit qui seminat seminare semen suum, the sower went out to scatter the seed, just as you and I have gone out, serving our Mother the Work, which God has willed to raise up in such a beautiful fashion, so full of wonderful human and divine freedom, so it can adapt to all the circumstances of place and time, so that the word of God can take root and yield a harvest. As we begin to meditate on this parable, I want to ask Jesus something which his disciples often asked him, something which is quite reasonable for me to ask him, as it was for those who followed

1 He wishes all men to be saved: 1 Tim 2:4 2 Cf. Jn 14:6 3 Lk 8:5 4 I was like a donkey before you: Ps 73:22

him closely, eager to assimilate the nourishment of his teaching. I don’t now wish to highlight the exalted virtues that led them to seal their faith with martyrdom. I’ll speak instead about their defects, because that’s the aspect that poor people like us find particularly encouraging. And what was it they asked Jesus? Edissere nobis parabolam!5 Explain the parable to us! O Jesus, in this period of prayer this morning, give these sons of mine, and me too, a clear insight which we will never lose, so we can pass it on to others. Edissere nobis parabolam! Explain to us well, really thoroughly, this idea of working for your glory, sowing your seed everywhere, including the use of the media for spreading ideas and news which have such a powerful influence on public opinion. It is true that we do have many apostolates: those we call personal, and among them the apostolate of public opinion, with the thousand forms it has to have; and also the collective apostolates. But this whole spectrum of apostolates, which will always be expressed in a variety of ways, in accordance with the needs and circumstances of individuals and occasions, all this, I repeat, is nothing more than a way of teaching doctrine. Tell me: isn’t it the case that you try to teach doctrine, not only to a small group of people at a conference centre, in a Brief Circle or a Study Circle, in a talk or a conference, but also, as our Lord did, continually, and in the open air?

## In the boat with Christ {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.18.2}

Have you noticed how our Lord, when he wanted to speak to the people around him, would often draw apart from them somewhat? While being completely one of their number, being of the people and for the people, he gets into the boat and tells them to put out a little from the shore. This is our life, my children. Being in the

5 Mt 13:36

world and of the world, working in the world, addressing ourselves to the world, we have to be in the boat of the Work, somewhat separated from the crowd. Separated by what, my children? By something that doesn’t separate but unites. Neither did the sea, the little sea of Galilee, separate Jesus from the crowd. The water united them. Similarly, our contemplative life, our loving dedication to the Love of loves, helps us to forget about ourselves and serve all souls generously. It unites us to the crowd. I am telling you this because we need to be reminded of it. Our life of prayer, the spiritual means we have in the Norms and customs and spirit of the Work, help us to open our arms wide like Christ, for we are Christ. Then all souls without exception will fit in our hearts. This is our boat! Let’s make a firm and manly resolution. A resolution based on our own experience, the abundant grace of God, the protection of our Mother, our good will and our own defects, and the struggle we are resolved to wage! That is contemplative life. That is what it means to be in the boat. Everything we do in Opus Dei, then, is done to teach doctrine. But, just as our Lord coepit facere et docere,6 we cannot lead a double life; we cannot teach what we do not practise. We have at least to try to practise what we teach. Primum, facere: first comes doing. We need to begin our apostolate with the witness of example: a constant, humble and persevering example, the example of people who are well-versed in sacrificing themselves in small things, so that, in the phrase of Holy Scripture, we can hunt down the foxes that eat up the vineyard.7 We have to fulfil the little duty of every moment, carrying out our professional and social undertakings, imbuing our relationships with charity, so as to mollify hatred, fratricidal strife and division, the waves of the angry storm that rages in this poor world of ours. It

6 Began to do and to teach: Acts 1:1 7 Cf. Song 2:15

is, indeed, our world, for it is God’s handiwork, and he has given it to us as an inheritance. So first comes our example and afterwards the word: the spoken word, the written word and the word as image.

## Sowers of love and peace {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.18.3}

Last night I took note of a passage in St Paul’s Epistle to the Romans: “Every one who calls upon the name of the Lord will be saved.”8 We want to lead people to Christ. We want all human beings to love him. You know it and you teach it, although there are some who regard themselves as geniuses and who nonetheless are so ignorant and uneducated that they don’t even know the things that are necessary as means to our salvation… They don’t understand thing things that concern our Lord, and they regard them as foolish and puerile, and they don’t realise that in the sight of God, who is eternal, we creatures are truly nothing. That is why we have to call upon our Lord, as we used to call our mother in times of need, or as we used to take refuge in the strong arms of our father, the one who engendered us with a holy love which I bless so many times a day. We have to invoke our Lord, we have to call upon him with filial piety! Quomodo ergo invocabunt in quem non crediderunt?9 How are they going to call him, if they don’t believe in him? Aut quomodo credent ei quem non audierunt?10 How are they going to believe in him if they have never heard of him? Quomodo autem audient sine praedicante?11 How are they going to hear if there is no one to tell them, no one to preach to them? Quomodo vero praedicabunt nisi mittantur?12 How will they speak to them, if there is no one to send them? You yourselves have the mission of going

8 Rom 10:13 9 Rom 10:14 10 Ibid. 11 Ibid. 12 Rom 10:15

to all peoples, just as I do, because Jesus Christ has given it to us, as he did to the first Twelve, and the Church has ratified it. It is our mission to sow the doctrine of Christ throughout the world, to spread this light of God, to wage this marvellous war of peace and love, to bring it to all mankind without exception of race, language or social background: sicut scriptum est: quam speciosi pedes evangelizantium pacem, evangelizantium bona!13 How beautiful are the feet of those who announce the gospel of peace, who announce true riches! I want to make a little digression. You know that I am not very keen on ceremonies, but something was needed for the moment of incorporation to the Work, something graphic and expressive. You also know that normally I don’t attend the Oblation or the Fidelity of any of my children. I do this deliberately – you’ll find a note to that effect on file – because I’m all in favour of naturalness and simplicity. And so, although I’ve never been very keen on ceremonies, at the beginning of the Work there was a moment in the ceremony when I used to bend down and kiss the feet of your brothers who were doing the Fidelity. I was reminded of those words of the Apostle: “How beautiful are the feet of those who preach the good news.”14 Later I came to the conclusion that it was too affected and not in keeping with the simplicity of our lifestyle; and so, just as a bad architect has to do, I took an eraser and eliminated that custom. By that gesture I was trying to express the idea that we are engaged in a beautiful war of love and peace: in hoc pulcherrimo caritatis bello! We are trying to bring Christ’s charity to all mankind, without any distinction of language, nationality, or social background. In wartime everything is greatly simplified: the Church herself, for example, greatly facilitates the celebration of marriage in wartime. Well we, who are always at war, have to be always fully

13 Ibid. 14 Ibid.

mobilised and combat-ready. It’s urgent, we must hurry! We have to be watchful and alert!

## A greater fruitfulness {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.18.4}

Sed non omnes obediunt Evangelio,15 but not everyone obeys the Gospel. We know that this does happen, that lamentably even many Catholics are not faithful. Sometimes the little waves of filth that spatter the Work are generated by Catholics, and even by priests or religious. Over at the Nile they are constructing a huge dam with a view to regulating the waters of the river. I don’t really know about these things, but it used to be beneficial for the river to overflow its banks from time to time so it could enrich the fields with the silt brought down by its waters! And so it is, my children, that the waves of filth which others want to throw at us fill us with spiritual and apostolic fruitfulness, although the dirty work of those who churn them up pains us. We feel so sorry for their poor souls! Because indeed, non omnes obediunt Evangelio.16 People who call themselves Catholics, but who don’t show it with their deeds, are not good Catholics. Operibus credite,17 our Lord said in the Gospel: believe the works. And St John explains: non diligamus verbo neque lingua, sed opere et veritate,18 let us not love in word or speech but in deeds and in truth. Isaias enim dicit, Domine, quis credidit auditui nostro?19 Lord, who will believe what I say? Do you see that sometimes they don’t believe? There is no theological malice in what I am going to say. If it were a lack of charity I wouldn’t say it. But there are people in whom the old adage finds fulfilment: quidquid recipitur ad modum

15 Rom 10:16 16 Ibid. 17 Jn 10:38 18 1 Jn 3:18 19 For Isaiah says, Lord, who has believed what he has heard from us? Rom 10:16

recipientis recipitur, ideas are received according to the condition of the recipient. They do not believe, because they are to a large extent corrupted. If you were to serve them the wine from the wedding feast of Cana, which bore witness to Jesus’ first miracle and the first manifestation of his divinity, on mingling with their warped consciences it would turn to vinegar. My children, let us for our part carry on serving good wine, always telling the truth, because fides ex auditu, auditus autem per verbum Christi.20 In the end the faith will take root in their souls too and, with God’s grace, they will believe our words. The faith has to do with things that are not seen, things that cannot be touched physically. It’s true that I often say, with forgivable theological inaccuracy, that I “don’t need” faith, because I often find myself “touching” God. My Jesus, I touch you, I see you, I feel you, within my poor wretchedness, granting effectiveness to my uselessness, strength to my weakness, optimism to my failures!

## All-round improvement {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.18.5}

This workshop which finishes today, my children, has been an opportunity for learning to sow more extensively, with a supernatural outlook, because you are Christ and you have gone forth to scatter Christ’s seed, to teach doctrine. But, my dearest children, for that sowing to be effective you need to strengthen a number of different aspects – spiritual, psychological and professional. I will try to explain what I mean, making use of both mind and will. You carry on praying in silence and I, as I said, will be doing my personal prayer aloud. The first thing that needs to be reinforced is the spiritual aspect; and that is achieved by constantly developing our interior life. We

20 Faith comes from what is heard, and what is heard comes by the preaching of Christ: Rom 10:17

need to be effective in doing this apostolate of teaching doctrine, and I don’t have any other recipe for effectiveness than the one the first Christians had. There isn’t any other, my children. The world has progressed greatly in many respects... It’s wonderful to see how God helps the human mind in all its scientific research, which necessarily has to lead to God, because if it is authentic that’s where it leads. We now have means which our parents at the turn of the century didn’t have, nor our grandparents in the last century either... When I consider that there are three generations, more or less, in every century, I realise that all my ancestors, from the time of our Lord until now, are only sixty generations; they would easily fit in this oratory. In such a short space of time we have progressed from bows and arrows to nuclear energy, and the same has happened in the rest of man’s activities also. What changes have taken place from then to now! And nevertheless, in the spiritual life we have the same means as they did: in this at least there is no scope for further progress. The same recipe: personal holiness! There isn’t any other. Ascetical struggle! Striving to put into practice what some people regard as theory to be written about and not life of our life. Living by faith in Christ. Hope! Hope for effectiveness in spite of our failings, and hope in the love which awaits us. And finally, love moves us too! The theological virtues are all closely intertwined and act effectively on the contemplative lives of the children of God in his Opus Dei, and on me, this poor priest. So you have to enrich your spiritual response with personal holiness. You also have to reinforce the psychological aspect, that part of the soul which is also spiritual. That helps to improve your sense of serenity, which is a very secular way of describing another marvellous, splendid reality: the cardinal virtues.

Serenity is, in the first place, the fruit of fortitude. Dominus illuminatio mea et salus mea, quem timebo? 21 The Lord is my light and my salvation; whom shall I fear? Not even God, because he is my Father! God is our Father. Think about that a lot, my children. It’s a thought that will give you a lot of strength and peace. Serenity is also the fruit of prudence. Don’t ever be slow to change your mind. If, when tackling something about which you already have arrived at one conclusion, you get some new information or you are told you made a mistake, it’s only natural for you to change your mind. In those cases, rectifying is not in any sense humiliating. Only people who are obtuse – and we have no place for such people in Opus Dei – are incapable of rectifying. Serenity also implies temperance. You have a divine vocation which leads you to live in the middle of society. The street is where we live. And there too is where we have to remain, like everybody else: at work, in family life, in professional and social affairs… Just the same as everybody else, but with an interior light which our friends, whoever they are, necessarily discern, analyse and admire. The light is the psychological aspect of this spiritual serenity. Finally, strive to improve the professional aspect, your technical training and development, so as to carry out your work more effectively. In this aspect I have no doubt that you will get better every day. Are you drawing your own conclusions, my son, or are you sitting there half asleep and not making any effort of your own? That would be a pity.

## Obstacles to sowing {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.18.6}

When teaching clear doctrine it’s not unusual to encounter obstacles. Christ came to bring light to the world twenty centuries ago, and it is still in darkness. Most of the world is not Christian. Many of those who regard themselves as Christian are not united to

21 Ps 27:1

the vine: they are separated branches that cannot produce fruit.22 And those who are in fact united to the vine – as you can see, we continue speaking in parables, like Jesus – and have the glory of possessing the fullness of the faith and who call themselves Catholics, very often don’t behave as such! In many cases they lead a double life. What a failure on Christ’s part, some might say! But no, it’s not a failure. I’ll tell you what I’ve told you very often before: God is so good that he wants us to serve him freely, not like animals or inert objects such as stones. All irrational creatures fulfil their end necessarily. The Creator has endowed man, on the other hand, with a special privilege: he harbours in himself a spark of the divine intellect – understanding and free will. Man is the only creature who can say to God: “I don’t want to!” Therefore for me the most supernatural reason is: “because I want to, Lord. You stretch out your hand lovingly, and with your grace I strive to grasp it: because I want to, because I love you!” Our Lord is so good that he left us free. He gave us a wonderful capacity for all the virtues, but also the sad capacity – the reverse side of our created condition – for every failing, every aberration, and the loss of the freedom he won for us as God’s children. Opposed to God’s marvellous plan stands man, who since the fall has been pronus ad peccatum – inclined to evil. All human passions rise up, along with the activity of hell. Many people don’t want to hear about hell or the devil. You will meet difficulties, then. Jesus did too. People didn’t listen to him, they didn’t understand him. Do you remember how it was? The multitudes followed him because he wrought great and wonderful signs; very often they regarded him as some sort of juggler doing magic tricks. Others followed him out of self-interest, because he gave them food or because he cured the sick. Others through a noble desire to learn: he spoke of very learned things and he taught them in such a way that, with a little bit of good will, they

22 Cf. Jn 15:1-8

could really come to understand. Others yet again, ut caperent eum in sermone,23 to take hold of something he said and twist it… Don’t be surprised if you come across people who twist what you say or write, and in what was nothing except peace and sweetness they claim to discover strife and evil. If it happened to our Lord, it’s only natural that it will happen to us too.24 The instigators of the difficulties can be classified in three groups. The first are those who are fanatically opposed to the Church of God. Since we serve the Church, they will attack us, although we love them. The second are those who have a one-party mentality in temporal affairs: they too are fanatics. The third are those who have a one-party mentality in the religious sphere, in things which are free. There’s very little one can do for them, because their fanaticism leads them to advocate a single apostolic grouping, of which they are the permanent overseers. These are the most fanatical of all, they don’t want anybody other than themselves doing anything, and they are totally opposed to the legitimate freedom of consciences. They seem to be determined to see souls lost. They’re like the dog in the manger, who can’t eat the fodder himself and won’t let anyone else do so either: he would rather see it rot! Anything good, if carried out by any agency other than theirs, they regard as evil. What a great number of souls are lost through all this fanaticism! You and I cannot be fanatics. Lord, may my children never be fanatics! May we always work with a heart which is large and understanding: a heart made to the measure of your own.

## The truth in charity {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.18.7}

I will finish by telling you that there is a special prescription, a medicine to cure all illnesses, a force capable of assaulting all ramparts and of conquering all difficulties. It is what St Paul wrote

23 Mt 22:15 24 Cf. Mt 10:24-25

of to the Corinthians: caritas mea cum omnibus vobis in Christo,25 my love will always be with you in Christ Jesus. We, who are bearers of Christ, who want to be ipse Christus, Christ himself, have to speak the truth kindly and positively, without hurting people: veritatem facientes in caritate.26 In spreading the Church’s teaching, in making Opus Dei known, I would like you to bear in mind what Christ does to defend what is practically indefensible. “The scribes and the Pharisees brought a woman who had been caught in adultery, and placing her in the midst they said to him, ‘Teacher, this woman has been caught in the act of adultery. Now in the law Moses commanded us to stone such. What do you say about her?’ This they said to test him, that they might have some charge to bring against him. Jesus bent down and wrote with his finger on the ground. And as they continued to ask him, he stood up and said to them, ‘Let him who is without sin among you be the first to throw a stone at her.’”27 Everybody left and Jesus turned to the woman: “Woman, where are they? Has no one condemned you?” They had been wanting to stone her. “She said, ‘No one, Lord.’ And Jesus said, ‘Neither do I condemn you; go, and do not sin again.’”28 Jesus does this to defend a lost cause. My children, if ever a wave is stirred up by scribes and Pharisees, by certain priests of the New Law, by certain little groups that call themselves Catholic, remember what I’ve told you: caritas mea cum omnibus vobis in Christo.29 And who are they trying to stone? Some unfortunate woman? No, not at all: this Mother of ours, who is holy and good! Jesus, will you forgive me for saying that I’ve been only once to the Capitoline Museum, but I recall that they have there a statue of Venus portraying such a sense of purity that her nudity does not

25 1 Cor 16:24 26 Speaking the truth in love: Eph 4:15 27 Jn 8:3-7 28 Jn 8:10-11 29 My love be with you all in Christ Jesus: 1 Cor 16:24

arouse any evil desire. As regards the Work, the more naked she appears, the more truly she is seen, the more she has to be loved. Make her known without any sense of fear. Consequently we are especially obliged to defend this good Mother! Our Lord has set us an example in the way he defended an unhappy woman. We for our part have to care for the Work, which is good, so very good, holy, divine, and beautiful, very beautiful! And she cannot defend herself: she is a minor, my children. I will add that the same thing happens to me: now that I am getting older, I feel I have the right to expect you to defend me, because it would only take a small push to send me flying. My children, understand that we have the duty to defend the truth. You have to aim for spiritual, psychological, and professional perfection. Say truthfully to all mankind: caritas mea cum omnibus vobis in Christo.30 Because our spirit consists of being bearers of Christ, and because our dedication to God in the middle of society leads us to be ipse Christus, Christ himself. And don’t forget, always have recourse to our Lady. She is our assurance, Seat of Wisdom, Mediatrix of all graces, who leads us by the hand to proclaim the saving doctrine of our Lord Jesus Christ everywhere.

30 Ibid.

# LOVE FOR THE CROSS * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.19}

Today I feel obliged to do the prayer aloud, for a number of reasons: I’ll mention two of them. The first is to celebrate a day which is very significant for us: the feast of the Holy Cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, whom we love with all our heart. The second is because I don’t have the gift of bi-location – nor do I have the slightest desire for it – and since I won’t be able to join you later for the veneration of the Lignum Crucis, I want to say a few words to you now.

## Accepting the Cross joyfully {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.19.1}

Let us contemplate the event of today’s feast, the finding of the Holy Cross. St Helena was the person who discovered the wood on which our Lord died. And immediately a question comes to my mind: who goes looking for the Cross nowadays? Very few people. You know how the world in general reacts to the Cross, including many so-called Catholics for whom it is a scandal or foolishness, as St Paul wrote: iudaeis quidem scandalum, gentibus autem stultitiam.1 Lord, after so many centuries this aberration still is the case, even among people who love you and follow you. There are three possible attitudes to the Cross. One is to avoid it, which is what most people do. The second is to seek it out rashly,

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 3 May 1964, at the time the feast of the Finding of the Holy Cross 1 A stumbling block to Jews and folly to Gentiles: 1 Cor 1:23

looking for great trials and undergoing extraordinary penances: if this impulse doesn’t come from God I wouldn’t regard it as a good thing either, because it could be the result of hidden pride. The third attitude is to receive it joyfully when God sends it: to my mind, this is the best way to approach the Cross. When I think of St Helena going in search of the wood of the Cross, it amazes me that for three hundred years it hadn’t occurred to any Christian to try to find the wood which had been Jesus’ triumphal throne. My first reaction is one of astonishment; but afterwards I don’t feel so astonished, because I realise that people don’t look for mortification, they flee from pain, they avoid worries… The world, Lord – I say so in your sacramental presence

– doesn’t want saints: saints are uncomfortable. Neither does it want martyrs: martyrs are uncomfortable too. Certainly, my children, I never tire of reminding you often that you have to try to be saints; but not the sort of saint that needs two other saints to put up with him. I say this because we are just ordinary mortals, poor sinners, who have decided, within our limitations, to follow Jesus; as a result, without wanting to, we have brought many crosses into the lives of those close to us and thereby contributed to their sanctification too. The saint is never just a man on his own: he is always surrounded by a group of other saints. Some of them are afterwards recognised as such by history; and others go unnoticed, but have a very high place in heaven. I have spoken to you very often about this Opus Dei which God willed to found in the year 1928! Thirty-six years have passed, and I myself am just a poor man, and God, obviously, wants me to be a saint, just like you. But I think I have contributed to others becoming saints. I haven’t worked any miracles, but I have created saints. There are other people who have worked miracles and are also very holy. On other occasions I have told you about how God, who is both fatherly and motherly, in his loving desire to sanctify me – may I

say it, Lord? – had to strike me, and did so, to fashion from this poor man the first stone of his building, Opus Dei. But for every blow he struck on the nail he struck a hundred on the horse-shoe, namely, the people close to me. I loved them deeply, and God wished to sanctify them too. Only God is responsible for the horseshoe, not I. But I want to take advantage of this great and wonderful feast of the Finding of the Holy Cross to pray for the whole Work and for your brothers and sisters, so that they all become real saints. You for your part ought to pray for me, so that I don’t end up a failure, an “insuccesso”, as they say here in this beloved Italy; so that God doesn’t fail in me, but rather is successful. On the other hand, I am so convinced of my own unworthiness, and of my sorrow and love, that I rely for everything on him, on my Lord Jesus Christ. My children, you can see I’m not exaggerating. The Cross still is a symbol of death, instead of being a sign of life. People still flee from the Cross as if it were a gallows, when it is a throne of glory. Christians still reject the Cross and identify it with suffering, instead of identifying it with love. You and I have to go out in search of the Cross, to seek it out (you understand me), in order to love it, to receive it with our arms wide open, whenever it comes – that is the third solution I mentioned earlier; that is our way. A Cross from which he reigns: oportet illum regnare! 2 From which he manifests his greatness, his immense greatness, eternal, majestic, all-conquering. But we must be small: illum oportet crescere, me autem minui.3 My children, you know from your own experience that the Father does not deceive you. I have so often told you and written for you, what I have experienced in this poor life of mine: that suffering is not suffering when we embrace it in union with Jesus. The Cross is not something to run away from, but something we have to receive with love. And then the Cross is

2 He must reign: 1 Cor 15:25 3 He must increase, but I must decrease: Jn 3:30

Christ’s. He carries it, and therefore it isn’t heavy. That’s why I get a bit indignant when people speak so much about the cross, and crosses. You’ll tell me it’s the habitual way of speaking in ascetical works, and it’s true. But I’m not convinced. No, Lord, no! In the spirit of Opus Dei, the Cross and crosses are joy, honour, improvement, perfection, holiness. Joy here and victory in the next world! That was the idea I wanted to obtain as the fruit of this morning’s prayer: not only for you, but for all my children whom God calls to this supernatural enterprise. Thirty-six years of failure in my own poor life, and every day I find myself worse off! Where would I be if I didn’t love the Cross? Where would I be if I didn’t live upon the Cross? The Cross is not just a symbol, but the expression of triumph: the throne of my God, the throne of a victorious king.

## A building crowned with the Cross {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.19.2}

It was the year 1943. God, ludens coram eo omni tempore, ludens in orbe terrarum,4 was playing with this sinner as with a child, and performed one of his really spectacular “stunts”. This poor sinner doesn’t normally talk about anything other than things of God’s ordinary providence, because I have no desire for anything unusual. Sometimes, nonetheless, because I am ordered to do so by someone who is in a position to be obeyed, I have no option but to mention things which fall outside the scope of ordinary providence. Rest assured then that I do so full of gratitude to God, just as I am equally grateful for the ordinary operation of his law in the universe and in souls. That is something that always seems to me an extraordinary miracle. That’s why in Opus Dei we are not seekers after miracles, we don’t look for extraordinary things; we see, hear, feel and touch God in ordinary everyday things, where Christ is awaiting us with loving eyes, and with the Cross…

4 Prov 8:30-31

It was the year 1943. This poor man was saddled with the task of preparing the ordination of the first priests. He experienced a bittersweet taste which was a product of his lay mentality and his love for the priesthood. It was obvious that I needed to have sons who were priests. The sacramental wall! My God, I bless you for your sacraments! We needed to have souls equipped with the ministerial priesthood and with the specific spirituality of Opus Dei, to serve all mankind – like all priests, neither more nor less than they – but dedicated especially to their brothers and sisters, giving meaning to that phrase I will never tire of repeating: placing one’s heart on the floor as a soft carpet for the others to tread on. And Lord, I couldn’t find a suitable solution. I remember my conversations with Alvaro, all that delving into canon law, my sessions with the worthy bishop of Madrid, Don Leopoldo… It wasn’t I who was the cause of them, it was Don Leopoldo who took the initiative, and he came up with the most amazing solutions! One of them was to endow a chaplaincy for each priest. Imagine what that would have meant for the Work, which is poor and always will be, and has and always will have poverty as its treasure: it would have required an enormous capital investment! But as well as that, it didn’t solve the problem of complete internal autonomy which would leave the priests free to go wherever the Directors wanted – which is where God wants – in the service of their brothers and sisters, and other souls. The idea, and I say so with all the respect and affection which I had and still have for Don Leopoldo, was completely and utterly daft. And so, with that bitter-sweet taste in my soul, I began to celebrate Mass on 14 February 1943. And there our Lord produced something which left me completely overwhelmed… My God, I’ve never looked for anything extraordinary, nor have I ever felt the need of it. I want to be good and faithful, and all of you here have to help me to be so: good, faithful and cheerful. But God does things his way when he wants to; and it was then, intra Missam [during

Mass] when the Priestly Society of the Holy Cross came into the world. He even gave me the seal of the Work… Where was I, my children? It was God’s will! A lay character, certainly, a lay mentality and a priestly soul. A society, not in the sense of a group, but an ordinary everyday sense, because in our life everything unfolds in an ordinary fashion, common in the good sense of the word. Our world is a world of ordinary things. Society. A society of individuals who have a priestly soul – priestly zeal and love for the priesthood! The last stones in the juridical path of this great supernatural edifice are still being laid, because it’s not finished yet. Excuse the analogy, my children, but it really does involve giving birth, which is painful, but also joyful! The Church too celebrates the joy of the Sorrows of the Mother of God. How well we understand that, don’t we? The liturgy is a good source of ascetical theology.

## Holy concerns {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.19.3}

At this point I must go off at a bit of a tangent. You know, my children, how keenly I am currently following the work of the Second Vatican Council, and how much I have supported it with my prayer and mortification, and on occasions with my own contribution, and how much I have asked you to pray for it and to get others to do the same. I have often told you that I want to be faithful to the decisions of the Church’s hierarchy in the smallest details – and you too – not as subject to an authority but with the piety of sons and daughters, with the love of people who are and who feel themselves to be members of the Mystical Body of Christ. I haven’t concealed from you either how much it has hurt me to see people – I don’t wish to judge their intentions – who have taken advantage of this assembly in the life of the Church to give free rein to their own ideas, which are sometimes diametrically opposed to what the Magisterium has been teaching for centuries, and thus cause harm to the Church. We beseech you, Lord, that the Holy

Spirit, the Paraclete, who comes as the fruit of the Cross, may draw from it many favourable consequences for mankind. But enough is enough: Christian people are being led into sin, and there are plenty of individuals who are spreading serious errors under cover of the Council. Enough is enough, I say! I am reminded of St Catherine of Siena, whom I admire so much for the way she loved the Roman Pontiff and the Church, and for the forthright way she spoke out. I don’t have, Catherine, the freedom of action you had. What would have happened if I hadn’t kept quiet for the past thirty five years; and what is more difficult, if I hadn’t kept those young sons of mine quiet, all the more heroic in their case, because they couldn’t be expected to act with the same sense of prudence and responsibility as weighed upon me? But we can always talk about it with you, my Jesus, our Jesus, with you, Eternal Father, with you, Holy Spirit Paraclete, O Blessed Trinity, and with the Mother of God… Among all the good things the Council has given the Church, I would now like to mention one: honouring our Blessed Lady with the title Mater Ecclesiae, which we bear so deeply engraved on our hearts. And another, which may appear small enough: including St Joseph, our Lady’s Spouse and Jesus’ foster-father, in the Canon of the Mass. But I beseech you, Lord, to crown this assembly of the Church with the Holy Cross, as you willed to be crowned with that other crown, made of thorns, which was your crown of victory: just as Christian kings have been crowned since the time of Constantine, and churches too. O Cross of Christ, be for us a cross of life! A cross of life, a sign of victory. Blessed are you, throne of the Redeemer and throne of us who wish to be other Christs in spite of our own shortcomings, poverty, mistakes, and sins, and through it to love all mankind. My children, it’s true, saints are uncomfortable people. It’s obvious. Some people don’t seem to want even to mention them. I was really upset on reading a biography of St Teresa of Avila in

which the authors claim she was neurotic. You and I are sinners, we want to achieve holiness, but we are not neurotic. The words of the Apostle are once more fulfilled: animalis homo non percipit ea quae sunt Spiritus Dei,5 the unspiritual man does not understand the things of God. These people give the impression that they reason like that “unspiritual man”: they are incapable of understanding the psychology and physiology of souls fully dedicated to God. And they have the nerve to call it neurosis! It’s clear – allow me to make another digression – that St Teresa didn’t seek any sort of extraordinary supernatural experience, nor has any saintly soul ever done so, of that I am quite certain. These things cause one to suffer a lot. But our Lord, who after his resurrection chose to disguise himself as a gardener,6 is well able to tend souls as if they were flowers, and he protects them from nighttime chill or the noonday heat that could destroy them. So when necessary he announces: “Here I am!” And what happens then, from the point of view of the animalis homo [the unspiritual man], appears to be hysteria, whereas in fact it is perfectly normal, supernaturally normal, with phenomena which can be perceived in the souls and in the bodies of the recipients, children of God, and which to them are totally normal. St Teresa herself, for many years of her life, when these divine caresses weren’t necessary, had no ecstatic experiences, neither external nor internal, of phenomena which were out of the ordinary.

## Our Lady’s gentle hand {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.19.4}

But let us return to the Cross, this blessed Cross which scandalises so many people. Nowadays some people seem to regard zeal for souls as a crime! And we read ghastly statistics of defections. It is the first fruit, not of the Council, nor of the Holy Spirit, but of human frailty. Conversions are scarce or non-existent.

5 1 Cor 2:14 6 Cf. Jn 20:15

Catholics can be ill-treated for years, as we can see in all those countries which have fallen under the Communist yoke, but one cannot lay a finger on Jews or Protestants without the whole world protesting – which I agree with entirely, but why are not those who are persecuted for the faith defended just as vigorously? Lord, look after the Holy Father, whoever he happens to be. I will always love him. I love the present Pope very especially, because he has started to defend his children and to make his paternal protest heard on behalf of all those Catholics who are suffering in jails and concentration camps, experiencing the Cross and undergoing martyrdom. The torture they are subjected to may not be physical, but is most certainly mental and psychological, using modern techniques, and they experience the pain and sorrow of the destruction of their faith. Lord, look after your persecuted Church. I know personally what it is like to be within a hair’s breadth of martyrdom. But I have never experienced the pain of the indifference of those who are duty bound to care for the flock, and for all the other sheep of God’s pasture. Lord, don’t allow your Church’s saints and martyrs to be forgotten! On the other hand, if they felt loved and cared for, if they felt highly regarded, perhaps they wouldn’t be so holy, nor would they achieve the martyrdom of such desolation. Lord, you know what you’re doing, and I love all of them: those who suffer silently and those who weep openly, those who hunger and thirst for you and for love of their fellow men, and who find themselves alone in their chains. I can say that when one experiences that anguish – because I have experienced it – one does not feel unhappy, but one does feel afraid. Lord, I have felt afraid, but I have never felt the coldness of indifference which leaves the soul frozen. Those poor people! Lord, give them strength, look after them, you who are Goodness itself, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. O Mother most holy, Mother of the Church, Mother of the children of God, Mother of all men: sweeten the suffering of these

brothers and sisters of ours, let them feel your gentle hand. Which of you does not recall, when you were ill with a fever, the touch of your mother’s hand on your forehead? How pleasant, how soothing, how peaceful, how beneficial, was that motherly gesture! Lord, through the intercession of your Mother, touch the martyred foreheads of all those children of yours, children of the One, Holy, Catholic, Apostolic and Roman Church, who suffer and weep in silence. And for my children in Opus Dei, increase their love for the Cross, and also their sense of gratitude for your having crowned this supernatural edifice with that marvellous symbol of triumph. My God, my Jesus, you know that everything in Opus Dei is yours, the Cross and the name, yours and your children’s. Let us contemplate it with love, may we be truly grateful for this sign of your goodness and dominion, of your royalty. Upon it you have placed the seal of the things that belong to you: the Holy Cross. Place upon our foreheads and upon our hearts, these poor half-rotten hearts, the hand of your Holy Mother Mary, to restore us to health and fill us with love.

# PUBLICANS AND SINNERS * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.20}

There is a saying in my country that “you have to dance to whatever tune is played.” It’s something that applies very well to the children of God in Opus Dei, who have to be present in all environments everywhere, making a divine melody of our existence. The words and the music can be anything at all, but the overall effect has to be divine poetry. I say so because a few moments ago, reading the Gospel of today’s Mass, I came across the verse of St Luke which says: erant autem appropinquantes ei publicani et peccatores ut audirent illum,1 the tax collectors and sinners would draw near to Jesus to hear him. Let us meditate on it now in the presence of our Jesus who sees us and hears us from the tabernacle where he is really present.
