My God, I behold you there, and I wish to make an explicit act of faith in your real presence, with the firm resolution to engrave deeply on the hearts of my children that our tabernacles always have to be very dignified, in spite of the fact that we ourselves are not, because there you are present, Body and Blood, Soul and Divinity.

## Life and doctrine {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.20.1}

“The tax collectors and sinners were all drawing near to Jesus.” On reading this passage of the Gospel it immediately occurred to me

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 7 June 1964, at the time the 3rd Sunday after Pentecost 1 Lk 15:1

that in their apostolate the children of God have to be on the same wavelength as the publicans and sinners, all those people – friends, colleagues and perhaps relatives – who are very distant from God, so they can lead them back to him once more. So we have to live among them like leaven in the dough, without any sense of repugnance, understanding them, making allowances for them insofar as possible, so we can then warm them up and lead them to the Love of Christ Jesus. Our Lord wants us to have the same spirit as he had. Notice how he always wants to be with people. Don’t you love the way he never rejects anybody? He always has a word for everybody, he opens his sweet lips to all. He teaches, he instructs, he brings them news of joy and hope, through the wonderful, unique fact of being God living among men. I contemplate you, Lord, surrounded by publicans and sinners. You said that it is not the healthy but the sick who need a doctor.2 And there are so many souls in this world who are sick! Beginning with ourselves, who often are incapable of responding fully to what you ask. But you have appointed us to go out to all the thoroughfares and bring in whoever we find: the blind, the lame, the crippled,3 so you can restore them to health. We find ourselves surrounded by indifference, people who are very often opposed to our own way of life: ignorant men and women who profess to be Catholics but who don’t live like Catholics; surrounded, Lord, by Pharisees: small-minded Catholics who think they possess your teaching but who very often don’t put it into practice. We try to restore their spiritual health by leading them to the warmth of your Heart. But as we know well, we cannot do this unless we are closely united to you, Lord, who are our health and our strength. Each of your children in Opus Dei truly tries to be

2 Cf. Mk 2:17 3 Cf. Lk 14:21-23

ipse Christus, Christ himself, so that you yourself may act through those poor instruments of yours. Am I claiming then that we have all the answers? We certainly seek to have them, Lord, and the Work doesn’t spare any effort or sacrifice to bring it about. I now beseech you especially for a more robust and constant formation for your daughters and sons in Opus Dei. So, learning and instruction, then? Most definitely. Help us, Lord. I ask the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit to make our formation in Opus Dei ever more effective and constant: may it last all our life long, and be solid. May we always have a “Catholic nose”, may we flee from anything that stinks, and always walk along safe paths! We don’t lack sound teaching, thank God. But do we try to give witness to it by being other Christs? I am prepared to state, Lord, that I am certain that your children in Opus Dei, and also this poor sinner who is now doing his prayer aloud, really and truly want to bear witness to you in everything they do, in unity of life. And so through your goodness – through your power, and grace, and love, and the intercession of our Lady – I ask you for insights, graces and new strength. Like the kingdom of God itself, which suffers violence,4 we are prepared to do violence to ourselves to make our behaviour conform perfectly to the demands of the faith, in unity of life, let me remind you once again, and thereby proclaim our teaching to others in perfect harmony. That is what our Lord did, as the Evangelist tells us on summing up his earthly life: coepit Iesus facere et docere,5 Jesus began to do and teach. First life, then instruction, the enlightenment of souls.

## Aware of our limitations {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.20.2}

I generally celebrate Mass with a chalice of no great value. It is just gilded brass: only the cup, on the inside, is silver. When I raise it up after the consecration, I see it as a symbol of my own life – the

4 Cf. Mt 11:12 5 Acts 1:1

struggles, the victories, and the defeats. The victories are his, Christ’s; the defeats are mine. It looks as if it’s made of gold, but when you take it apart you can see where it says, simply and clearly, “brass.” It doesn’t try to deceive anybody by pretending it is gold, but loudly proclaims: “brass”! It is a sincere chalice. My children, my dearest children! Now is a good moment to ask our Lord to help us, because we’re made of clay just like the rest of mankind. We are rational animals, and all those animal instincts are lurking in the bottom of our hearts. But we have to be like gods, because our Lord has made us share in his divine life and has laid upon us the duty to co-redeem – to apply to souls the fruits of the Redemption he won for us on the Cross. So it is natural that Opus Dei, right from the start, has wanted to associate with publicans and sinners, people who are far removed from Christ, in order to lead them to him. Let us contemplate our Lord’s example. Jesus receives everybody. He accepts all their invitations; and when they don’t invite him, he sometimes invites himself: “Zacchaeus, make haste and come down; for I must stay at your house today”,6 he says to the publican who had climbed up a tree to see him go by. And we, my children, have to do the same, because we are moved by the charity of Christ himself: caritas Christi urget nos.7 The Work of God was born to spread throughout the world the message of love and peace our Lord left us. And so our attitude to souls is summed up in that expression of the Apostle which is almost a cry: caritas mea cum omnibus vobis in Christo Iesu,8 my affection for all of you, in Christ Jesus. Charity makes us sowers of peace and joy in the world, loving and defending the freedom of souls, the freedom which Christ respects and won for us.9

6 Lk 19:5 7 The love of Christ compels us: 2 Cor 5:14 8 1 Cor 16:24 9 Cf. Gal 4:31

The meaning of our life as children of God in Opus Dei is expressed in apostolate. From this comes the desire to live peacefully with all mankind, to overcome all barriers in the charity of Christ. From this too comes our desire to struggle to eliminate all forms of intolerance, compulsion and violence in man’s behaviour toward his fellow man. God wants to be served in freedom, and so an apostolate that didn’t respect the freedom of consciences would not be right. And so each of you, my children, has to aim for a boundless charity at all times: understanding everybody, making allowances whenever necessary; with a great zeal for souls, certainly, but the attractive kind of zeal, without any rough manners or off-putting behaviour. We cannot place error on the same level as truth, but, while always respecting the right order of charity, we ought to welcome those who are in error with a great sense of understanding. I always like to remind you, so you appreciate it very clearly, that the Church’s teaching is not compatible with errors against the faith. But can we not be firm friends with people who profess these errors? If we ourselves are solidly grounded in our behaviour and our doctrine, can we not pull the same cart as them in many areas of life? Our Lord wants us to be present on all the paths of the earth, sowing the seed of understanding, charity and forgiveness in hoc pulcherrimo caritatis bello, in this beautiful war of love, forgiveness and peace. Don’t think that this is just a good idea, or something to be recommended. It is much more than that: it is an imperative command from Christ, the mandatum novum10 I speak about so much, which obliges us to love all souls, to understand other people’s circumstances, to forgive them if they do anything that needs forgiving. Our charity has to be such that it makes up for all the deficiencies of human weakness, veritatem facientes in

10 The new commandment: cf. Jn 13:34

caritate,11 treating those in error with love, but not making any concessions in matters of faith.

## Unafraid of our environment {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.20.3}

What consequences flow from all this? The first is that we shouldn’t ever be afraid of our environment. There have been times when I have been afraid, and nevertheless I keep telling you that I’m not afraid of anything or anybody; all I want to do is fulfil God’s will. Perhaps our Lord wanted me to experience fear on one or two occasions so I would be clear that our strength is on loan. We can make all the ways of the earth divine, and live together with sinners and understand them and make allowances for them, if we don’t lose sight of the fact that with God we can do everything: omnia possum in eo qui me confortat.12 I have never liked the example sometimes used to describe what happens in Christian life, namely, the good apples which are spoiled by a single bad apple. I don’t deny the possibility, but we, my children, shouldn’t be afraid of rubbing shoulders with those who don’t profess or don’t practise Christ’s teaching. While taking the appropriate precautions, we shouldn’t avoid anybody, because we have all the spiritual, ascetical and intellectual resources to avoid being harmed: we children of God in the Work cannot allow ourselves to be affected by our environment, but rather it is we who have to set the tone for the people around us: our tone, the tone of Jesus our Lord, who associated with sinners and mixed with them.13 In a word, we ought to be in constant dialogue with our colleagues, friends, and every soul we meet. This is the holy tolerance I have spoken to you about so often. We can certainly call

11 Speaking the truth in love: Eph 4:15 12 I can do all things in him who strengthens me: Phil 4:13 13 Cf. Lk 15:2

it tolerance; but tolerance seems to me a very weak expression, because it’s not just a matter of accepting, as a lesser or unavoidable evil, the fact that others see things differently from us. It also implies giving in, giving way in everything personal, in matters of opinion, in everything – excluding essentials – which could be a source of conflict. In the last analysis, it is a matter of filing down any rough edges wherever it can be done, so as to create a common ground of understanding which can provide light for those in error.

## A special apostolate {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.20.4}

But let us continue reading the Gospel of St Luke. Jesus opens his divine mouth and instructs us with a parable which illustrates his compassion for all those who are in error. “What man of you, having a hundred sheep, if he has lost one of them, does not leave the ninety-nine in the wilderness, and go after the one which is lost, until he finds it?”14 Do you not see our own lives described here? Following after the sheep who has gone off or who wants to get lost, and leading it back to Christ’s fold. For us this is a very special apostolate. We have to pray first of all for all the members of the supernatural family we form part of, as we priests do at the altar. Afterwards, we have to pray for the shortcomings and mistakes of our fellow Catholics, and for all mankind. And go out to look for the lost sheep, searching trails and gullies, leaving no stone unturned until we find it. From the beginning of Opus Dei we have had special regard for those who are far removed from God. The doors of our Centres are wide open to them, and we invite them to cooperate with us in any upright human undertaking they feel attracted to. Like Christ himself, of whom the Pharisees said quia peccatores recipit et manducat cum illis,15 this man receives sinners and eats with them, we don’t turn anybody away. Let those modern-day publicans and

14 Lk 15:4 15 Lk 15:2

Gentiles come and work side by side with us! We behave like Jesus: we receive them, we eat together, as genuine, loyal friends, precisely because we are – we try to be – ipse Christus, by God’s grace. You will see how this attitude of yours will lead them to the faith that they either never had, or happened to lose. When they do return to the faith, you have to love them even more: you have to continue journeying through life arm in arm with them, conversing like true friends, foreseeing their possible difficulties so as to confirm them on the right path; improving your own grasp of the faith, because in these matters any attempt at dialogue is doomed to failure, and even counter-productive, without doctrine and the gift of tongues. This is one more reason to give even greater priority to acquiring a solid, continuous, and deep formation based on sound principles. With that sort of preparation you have no reason to be afraid of contact with people who are in error. A thing I have found particularly upsetting is to hear people say, referring to someone who had embraced our faith after many years, perhaps an entire lifetime, without seeing the Light: “Be careful, he’s a convert.” We have to try to love them even more, without suspicion, with joy, because “there will be more joy in heaven over one sinner who repents than over ninety-nine righteous people who need no repentance.”16 But we also have to be careful not to betray their desire to follow Christ by pretending something is good when it isn’t, just in case, because of their insecurity – they are like newborn babies in the faith – or their impulsive reactions, they might go astray from the true path they had started to follow. We have to pray a lot for the unity of life of all the members of the Work! If my children assimilate the formation they receive properly, they won’t be overcome by a false sense of inadequacy when they discover they are made of clay, when they see the poor

16 Lk 15:7

quality of the material they are made of. They will be able to ask for forgiveness, to be sincere and to begin once again, convinced that our vocation leads us to portare Iesum Christum,17 to bear Christ in our lives, in spite of our personal shortcomings. And so, with our Lady’s help, we will achieve our principal objective, the sanctification of ordinary work, which for us is a sacred duty. By doing so, while fully conscious of all our own weakness and all the strength of God, with whom we are identified, and being optimistic in spite of our weakness, we will make all the paths of the earth divine. We will live together “with publicans and sinners”, with all those people who perhaps have never even thought about life from a spiritual point of view. We will approach them in all humility, fully convinced that if they had received all the graces we have, they would be much better than us. We will stand beside those men and women who are so bereft of doctrine or who, if they do possess it, do not reflect it in their behaviour, to try to get them become, like us, bearers of Christ.

## Drowning evil in an abundance of good {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.20.5}

Even that does not exhaust our charity: we also have to live with those who are opposed to Christ, because otherwise we couldn’t reveal to them the good they need to know. Don’t be seduced, however, by false tactics in the apostolate. You will find people who are blinded by their very desires to win souls, and with the excuse of going in search of the lost sheep they end up falling into the quicksand of the error they aim to combat, deceived by imprudent agreements or concessions. We want to treat everybody well: both those who love Christ and also those who perhaps hate him. But we also feel very sorry for the latter, and so we try to treat them kindly, and help them discover the faith – drowning evil in an abundance of good. We shouldn’t ever

17 Cf. 1 Cor 6:20

regard anybody as an enemy: if, through bad faith, they are militantly opposed to the Church, our own good behaviour, friendly and unwavering, is the only way they have, with God’s grace, to discover the truth or at least to respect it. If their attacks are caused by ignorance, our own doctrine, backed up by good example, may manage to get the veil to fall from their eyes. We will always defend the sacred rights of the Church, but we will try to do so without hurting or humiliating anybody, or arousing any hostility or resentment. Whom are we opposed to? Nobody. I cannot love the devil, but anybody who is not the devil, no matter how evil they are or appear to be, I wish them well. I don’t feel antagonistic toward anybody, nor have I ever done so. I reject any ideas that are opposed to Christ’s dogmatic or moral teaching, but at the same time I am duty bound in all Christian charity to be open toward those who hold those ideas. On the other hand, my children, very often the attitude of these poor people is a consequence of bad formation: in not a few cases they have had nobody to teach them the truth. I think, therefore, that on the day of judgment many souls will reply to God in the words of the paralytic of Bethsaida: hominem non habeo,18 I had no one to help me; or like the unemployed labourers to the owner of the vineyard: nemo nos conduxit,19 no one has called us to work. Although the faults of these unhappy souls may be culpable and their perseverance in evil deliberate, they are also the victims of a deep ignorance which only God can measure. Lord, the need to help them is like a dagger in my heart. O Good Shepherd, go in search of them yourself, put them on your shoulders, bringing to life that beautiful scene illustrated in the catacombs. When the shepherd finds the lost sheep, “he lays it on

18 Jn 5:7 19 Mt 20:7

his shoulders, rejoicing. And when he comes home, he calls together his friends and his neighbours, saying to them, ‘Rejoice with me, for I have found my sheep which was lost.’”20 We will lead them to your fold, O Lord, in the nets of our norms of piety, our specific means of sanctification, where they will be safely at your side. We will sing them beautiful couplets, couplets of love, like that sower, you yourself, Lord, who went out in the morning to scatter the good seed. We will sing them songs of love, like those labourers returning exhausted from their toil in the fields. We will teach them to sing songs of human love in a divine key. Do you see? We live together with publicans and sinners, inspired by an indescribable charity. We have learned it from Jesus himself. My God, how beautiful our vocation is! No external display, in no way distinguished from our fellow-men, because we are totally the same as them, but with a divine light burning in our souls. I am reminded of a folk-song of my country: “To love without recompense / is the most beautiful of loves. / I love you and seek for naught / ’Tis true I love you well.”† We have to be ready to slay our pride, and then disappear and not feature further. Because some people, sadly, want to be leaven, but without becoming mixed with the dough.21 They are incapable of passing unnoticed. How can the grain of wheat be fruitful unless it is buried and dies? 22 A great sign of pride is the desire not to disappear, as if our name were meant to count for something on this earth, when in fact it doesn’t: the only thing that counts is God, his glory, and the salvation of souls. Let us ask God for greater insight and the grace to struggle, and so we will achieve perfect unity of life. If you behave like that, it will give rise to wonderful human and supernatural consequences. The Work of God has a great

20 Lk 15:5-6 † El querer sin esperanza, / es el más lindo querer. / Yo te quiero y nada espero, / ¡mira si te quiero bien! 21 Cf. Mt 13:33 22 Cf. Jn 12:24

contribution to make to the happiness of mankind, if we are faithful; and we will be faithful because “the Lord is faithful; he will strengthen you and guard you from evil.”23 I see the Work centuries hence, always young, elegant, beautiful and fruitful, defending the peace of Christ, so that the whole world may possess it. We will contribute to the recognition of the rights of the human person, of the family, and of the Church, in society generally. Our work will help to diminish fratricidal hatred and hostility among nations, and my daughters and sons, fortes in fide,24 firm in the faith, will anoint all injuries with the gentle balm of Christ’s charity. Does it not make you very happy to realise that God has wanted our supernatural undertaking to have this spirit which is so tangible in the Gospel but which seems to be so forgotten in this world of ours? Thank Jesus for it, thank our Lady, and renew your desires for coredemption and apostolate. What a great enterprise awaits us! Because he who has begun his Work in us will bring it to completion.25

23 2 Thess 3:3 24 1 Pet 5:9 25 Cf. Phil 1:6

# A LOVE THAT IS STRONG * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.21}

Mary Magdalene. I have often felt that she has been slandered a lot. Certainly she had been guilty of many indiscretions; but when she meets the Master she repents deeply and reveals a marvellous capacity for pure love. Therefore I’ve often considered that perhaps many have gone too far in exaggerating her errant lifestyle. At any rate, it is very consoling to see that our Lord doesn’t separate himself from us when we don’t separate ourselves from him, even though our lives may not have been very exemplary and may even include serious offences. God pardons and lovingly embraces his repentant children who want to begin again. It is very consoling, I was saying, to see how our Lord brings to life the figure of the Good Shepherd about whom he preached so lovingly.1 All his sheep are very important to him; and he never shuts the door on any who are injured or diseased, when they return desiring to be cured.

## The repentant sinner {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.21.1}

I want to remind you once again of another statement by Jesus in the Gospel: it is the sick who need a doctor.2 Don’t feel humiliated when you discover your wretchedness – all of us are in the same boat! Our Lord doesn’t cease to love us on that account. But we do

* Notes of a meditation given in Elorrio (Spain), 22 July 1964, feast of St Mary Magdalene 1 Cf. Lk 15:4-7 2 Cf. Mt 9:12

have to be sincere. Be very sincere: lose your sense of fear, your shame at letting yourselves be seen as you are. Only like that can you receive the appropriate treatment, so as to be cured and work more effectively, and come to realise just how much our Lord loves us, since he is capable of forgiving our greatest weaknesses. But let us return to the Gospel scene we are contemplating today.3 The Pharisee, who understands little or nothing about true love, thinks badly of our Lord because of the previous behaviour of that woman who approaches him and bathes his feet with precious ointment. The Pharisee, in his pride, thinks badly of Jesus and of Mary Magdalene: hic si esset propheta, sciret utique quae et qualis est mulier quae tangit eum, quia peccatrix est,4 if this man were a prophet, he would certainly know who and what sort of woman it is who is touching him, for she is a sinner. That is the typical reasoning of those whose hearts are stained by pride; and usually their pride conceals a whole host of other sins. They judge others badly, they criticise, they’re incapable of speaking plainly, and then they don’t understand that if they open their hearts they can be helped to amend their lives. They may come to realise that they were entirely mistaken, and that what they viewed as a defect in another is in reality a generous or even heroic attempt to behave well. My children, when you think that something important, or something that seems important, is not going well, say so clearly and respectfully. Avoid the easy option of remaining silent, because by your silence you can become accomplices in evil, or you can lack charity by making an erroneous judgment. Go to the Director and say what’s bothering you: “There’s something I have to tell you…” And speak very clearly, without beating around the bush, always

3 Although scholars distinguish between Mary Magdalene and the anonymous sinner who throws herself at Jesus’ feet in the house of Simon the leper, the liturgy represents them as one and the same person. In developing his meditation along the lines of the liturgical texts of the feast, our Father follows the latter interpretation. 4 Lk 7:39

telling the truth, come what may. If you are right, they will thank you; and if you’re mistaken, they will explain the matter to you. Reading this Gospel passage once again, I am moved by this woman’s decisiveness and how she is not deterred by human respect. In the world at large, my children, it is very common for people to worry about what others think; and perhaps even more so among those who should live with their eyes set on God, disregarding the opinions of men. What a sad thing it would be to prefer to “look good” in people’s eyes, and less good or even bad in God’s! In his infinite mercy, and his infinite justice as well, our Lord lets himself be won over by sincere love, by Mary’s love filled with sorrow, and he doesn’t deny her his forgiveness.

## In the house at Bethany {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.21.2}

But let us turn now to that other Mary, the sister of Lazarus, who always lives with her mind occupied with God. Let us place ourselves in the house in Bethany, a resting place whose doors were always open to Jesus.5 There our Lord had a home, as I have often pointed out to you, just like the one he should find in our hearts and our Centres, in our tabernacles, because we treat him well and strive to welcome and love him better each day. Bethany moves me deeply; it has always done so. That house was our Lord’s home. He knew that at any time of the day he would be received there with affection and gratitude. The whole family was forever waiting for his arrival, and missed him when he wasn’t there. They never got used to the Master’s visits. It is easy to imagine how eager they were to hear the voice of their great Friend Jesus, to listen to his advice, helping them to bear their sorrows and setbacks. And we can also imagine how happy our Lord was to share in their family joys. That house, I repeat, was a home to Jesus.

5 Cf. Lk 10:38

There he was loved and cared for; there he could rest after days spent working and preaching. I would like each of us to understand very clearly that when we work, when we pray – these two activities amount to one – when we take care of our brothers or sisters, when we go to look for souls, we can play the part of Martha and Mary. Whatever we do, wherever we are, we can always attend to Jesus, take care of him and listen to him. So it is very important that we give our life a sense of eternity, that we never fall into routine or habit, because behind every task our Lord is waiting for us. You have to fight against tiredness and a lack of willingness, which can and almost certainly will happen to you at some point or other, by striving to do things better every day, perhaps the same things done at the same time. If we work for love they will never be the same things. They will acquire a very distinctive flavour! Our life of contemplation, our effort to stay close to God always and everywhere, in every circumstance, turns our earthly pursuits into divine treasures which God accepts and multiplies. Let us renew our resolution to show with deeds, in the tasks entrusted to us, that we are contemplatives. May our only concern be to live for God; and then we will live for the world too, in order to sanctify it. Capturing the warmth of that family scene where Jesus rests, the Evangelist writes that Martha had a sister named Mary who listened with great attention to Jesus’ words.6 It is easy to imagine Mary totally enthralled by the power of those words, by the charity they convey, by their demands. Mary listens with great attention to the divine word, and it seems quite right to us that she does so. Then how is it that you and I fail to pay close attention to our Lord, who speaks to us too, in a thousand different ways? Cast off the excuses that make you a coward in the face of God’s demands. He is asking you to be more pious, more punctual, more affectionate, more apostolic, more hard-working, more prayerful… He addresses his

6 Cf. Lk 10:39

words to you, and wants you too to choose the better part,7 to make him your only concern. Let us return to Mary Magdalene, who teaches us to love our Lord ardently. I repeat that I have often felt she has been slandered a lot. Certainly, the Gospel states that our Lord had expelled seven devils from her,8 but she wasn’t an abandoned woman, in the sense that expression has nowadays. I am sure this is so, because otherwise our Lord, without rejecting her, wouldn’t have allowed that gesture of trust and love. I say this because Christ is the Good Shepherd who goes out searching for the lost sheep and doesn’t return until he finds it. But at the same time he shows by his behaviour that he admits of no half-measures. His purity, his clean life, is so resplendent that that when the disciples see him speaking with a Samaritan woman they are surprised. Do you recall the scene? Mirabantur quia cum muliere loquebatur! 9 Our Lord also speaks with the adulterous woman, but he admonishes her, charitably and clearly, to amend her life and to sin no more.10 My children, learn from the Teacher’s example. We aren’t silly creatures who do strange things. We act naturally, but also with supernatural sense. We flee from occasions of sin, which requires courage. We avoid relationships that would lead us to waste our time or even separate us from God. Don’t be foolhardy: we should never set foot in a place where we would find it impossible to see Jesus Christ. That’s why I am convinced that Mary Magdalene was a woman with weaknesses, but not an abandoned woman. Today is a very good opportunity for a deeper, more sincere conversion, one that will lead us to detach ourselves decisively from everything that may still bind us here below, in order to belong more completely to God. In a word, we need to be quite determined

7 Cf. Lk 10:42 8 Cf. Lk 8:2 9 They marvelled that he was talking with a woman: Jn 4:27 10 Cf. Jn 8:11

to be more fully souls of prayer. To be a soul of prayer, my children, means to be very close to our Lord; it means love, dedication, an upright life, purity.

## Always close to Jesus {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.21.3}

My children, may each tabernacle truly be Bethany for us, and each Centre as well. This will be the case if my daughters in charge of the household administration, unseen and unheard, also keep up that truly contemplative conversation in their own clean souls. May they always combine the prayer of Mary and the work of Martha, taking care of Jesus so he manages to rest and recover his strength to continue fulfilling the Will of his heavenly Father. This idea is not foreign to my daughters, since they already are doing all that. I have to remind you again that for many people it was indeed strange; they didn’t think it was possible to seek Christian perfection while remaining in the middle of the world without changing one’s state. After much effort and with the help of divine grace, I managed to get those ecclesiastics to understand. And it is precisely this work of my daughters, the apostolate of apostolates, that is praised so highly by the Holy See. I never tire of repeating this in speech and in writing. As we pray now we are very united to the continuous prayer of my daughters and sons throughout the whole world. Let us ask our Lord, who is presiding over us in the Tabernacle, to grant us his grace, so that we manage to get him to stay with us like Martha and Mary, like the disciples at Emmaus.11 Lord, we need you. Never abandon us. May we never forsake you. My children, by the laws of nature I will die before you do. I don’t know when it will happen, but I accept death wherever God wants it, whenever he wants it and however he wants it. I behold the eventide of my life drawing near, and both for eternity and for each

11 Cf. Lk 24:29

day I remain in this world, I ask for myself and for you: ‘Lord, stay with us! Stay with us in that Bethany of all our Centres! Stay in our hearts! And if we ever have the misfortune of separating ourselves from you, come out to meet us yourself, and remind us that only you have the words of eternal Life, of peace, of tranquillity; of a peace and joy the world cannot give. And help my daughters who look after our homes, who look after the whole Work, who look after you in doing those jobs because they spend all their time praying and working with you. Help them turn their souls into a Bethany where you can rest.’

## Trusting in God {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.21.4}

Interior life is always necessary. When we try to develop our relationship with God, our friendship, our intimacy – because we have to seek more than just friendship with our Lord – we become convinced, with all the strength that comes from that conviction, that we are children of our heavenly Father: of him! Of that God who is all-powerful, eternal, all-wise, and who can never fail us. And we will always be victorious, not through our own ability, because we are worth nothing, but because we can count on God, who does not lose battles. Our own shortcomings shouldn’t get us down as long as we are prepared to overcome them. They will be with us as long as we live, but they won’t separate us from God, if we trust in him and if we have confident recourse to our Blessed Lady. It is precisely in these things that we will find the opportunity to get closer to Jesus, to feel his strength, because with his grace we can conquer in the little skirmishes, as well as in the big battles that can also arise. When you stumble across these obstacles, either the ordinary little ones or the more significant ones, when you discover these impediments which tend to slow down the soul’s progress, to derail your efforts to persevere in your chosen path, lift your hearts to God. Don’t get depressed, don’t let yourselves be overcome by

gloominess or discouragement: God still loves you, and he allows those difficulties to happen so we can grow more and more in love, faith and hope. God never abandons those who give themselves to him! He asks us not to give in; he asks us to be supernaturally and humanly stubborn and root out those tendencies. You have to reject, therefore, the false excuses that come from the comfortable attitude of thinking that we are weak. I repeat, God doesn’t abandon us, he gives each of us his grace abundantly to help us get rid of the pebbles that hinder our walking. I assure you that as long as we struggle like that, we will be able to approach Jesus as confidently as Martha and Mary; we will have a child’s right to beseech him to work the miracle of snatching us from death, and he will do it! Fight, therefore, and be sincere, very sincere! Open your souls and let the light of grace enter, so you can get good advice, so you can get the help of others’ prayer and mortification, with affection, and also with fortitude if necessary, with the strong hand of a father and the gentleness of a mother. God is awaiting us. We can never respond properly to our God’s thoughtfulness and patience. He doesn’t need us in any way, and yet he awaits us. Where? In the things of every day, in ordinary life, which in his company acquires an unsuspected wealth of detail and value. Don’t look for extraordinary things. Our way is to be content with the great miracle of ordinary everyday things that don’t attract attention: there, in those apparently unimportant things, God’s ordinary providence is concealed. That is the asceticism of Opus Dei: making everyday tasks great through love. This idea is expressed in the popular phrase “still waters run deep.” While we are occupied in the same tasks as our friends, relatives and colleagues, we can tell our Lord that we love him, that we love him with all our heart, that we give him everything we have. I have never been able to say which human occupation is the most important. Whenever I’m asked I always give the same answer: the one that is done with most love. Yes indeed, my

children, relationship with God is love, love, love! Only that way does the soul not shrink from sacrifice, but finds all the demands of self-giving very natural. What’s more, we don’t regard them as sacrifices, because all we see are different ways of loving. Do you remember how our mothers used to look after us, especially when we were very small? They didn’t make sacrifices, they simply loved us! They spent all their time looking after us. No matter what happens, never lose your trust. Everything will be fine if we rely on God. This is not the thoughtlessness of indifference: sometimes we can suffer, we can go through a bad patch, there can be something we don’t understand… but God allows all that, and from him only good can come. Moreover, we should remember that he has given himself for each of us, so we can be happy, so we can rejoice eternally in his presence. When we find something difficult, when the temptation arises to be unfaithful in things of little importance, or things which matter, remember that Jesus died for you and for me, so that we would be faithful to him.

## The resurrection of Lazarus {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.21.5}

I have always been moved by the Gospel passage describing the resurrection of Lazarus.12 It is a dialogue of friendship and sincere love inviting us to tell the Master everything that happens to us: ordinary and extraordinary, joys and sorrows; our plans and projects; our interests and worries. Tell him everything, because he is interested in it all: there is no aspect of our lives Jesus doesn’t care about. Do you remember? The two sisters, Martha and Mary, who have learned so much from the Teacher, who have rejoiced at the miracles he worked during his journeying through their land, in their moment of trouble look for help from the only person who can give it. They send Jesus a message which moves him: “Lord, he whom you love is ill.”13 But Jesus doesn’t set out at once, he makes

12 Cf. Jn 11:1-46 13 Jn 11:3

it clear that he is not in any hurry, and stays in the same place two days longer. Once again we can meditate on something which is very much part of our asceticism: holiness in everyday life, including when our best hopes are frustrated, or when we experience the harshness of violent opposition. I would say that especially in this latter case God is looking for our fidelity. He wants us not to despair and to keep doing everything we can. He may be slow in coming, but he will come, and we will experience the power of his love to remedy situations which are hopeless, humanly speaking. Jesus stays in the place he was in when the message arrived. He doesn’t set out for Bethany at once, although his thoughts and his heart must surely have turned frequently to that little house where he had so often found understanding and affection when other places, including his own people, his own town, didn’t offer him any. After two days he sets off in haste for the village, but the love and confidence of the two sisters cannot wait. First Martha, and then Mary, go out to meet him. Both of them address Jesus with the same respectful complaint, moved by a deep sense of affection: “Lord, if you had been here, my brother would not have died.”14 Engulfed in sorrow, they don’t realise that by reacting in that way they are setting limits to God’s power, because God knows best: he knows what each of us needs. He can draw good out of evil; and from great evil, great good. My children, even if circumstances appear to be particularly difficult, let us never impose limitations or conditions on Christ’s love; we have to pray with hope and confidence. In my work of preaching I have repeated very often that Jesus, with his most merciful heart, a heart of flesh, like ours, understands us and teaches us how to love! I for my part find it very easy to enter into that Gospel scene and contemplate the Master,

14 Jn 11:21,32

heartbroken over the death of his friend Lazarus. Let us dwell on our Lord’s words and actions carefully. He has listened to what Martha and Mary have struggled to say, between sobs, and in reply he asks them to have faith: “I am the resurrection and the life; he who believes in me, though he die, yet shall he live.”15 My children, only if we count on God can we be victorious; only if we have recourse to him can we bring Life to our lives. The two sisters persevere – how human and how supernatural it all is – and persist in their petition. Jesus, accompanied by a large crowd of people, goes to Lazarus’ tomb and – how heartening this is

– on arriving there, can no longer contain himself: he weeps brokenhearted for Lazarus: et lacrimatus est Iesus.16 Yes indeed, we really have to love each other! We have to be distressed – without losing our supernatural outlook – by each others’ sufferings! We have to be deeply interested in the people around us, with real family affection, always passing through the Most Merciful Heart of Jesus and the Immaculate Heart of Mary! That will ensure it is always pure, supernatural and human. Jesus then proceeds with authority to ask for the stone closing the tomb of Lazarus to be rolled back. Iam foetet!17 There will be a stench, a number of them remark in amazement. Jesus had deliberately allowed those days to pass so that we would have palpable proof of his omnipotence, so that we can be certain that if we trust him everything can be solved, absolutely everything, my children! Nothing, no matter how rotten, stinking or corrupt, can hinder the healing action of God’s grace. In various cathedrals I have seen very graphic representations of this scene, and it is very instructive. All the spectators are shown holding handkerchiefs to their noses to ward off the stench of death. In these situations, when even those who regard themselves as friends cannot stand it and

15 Jn 11:25 16 Jesus wept: Jn 11:35 17 Jn 11:39

draw back, or remain at a prudent distance, we hear the Creator’s mighty voice – because we shouldn’t forget that, with the Son, the Father and the Holy Spirit are always present – we hear the imperious voice and see the miracle that astonishes all those who are there with Jesus. The Master orders: Lazare, veni foras!18 Lazarus, come out! Lazarus’ body immediately comes to life, and he returns once more to live with his dear ones, in the midst of general rejoicing and astonishment. But I would like you to notice not just the extraordinary miracle that has been wrought. Notice too how Jesus finishes off every detail. He, who did all things well,19 is not content with just working the miracle; he descends to the smallest details and tells them to remove the bindings which had been used to wrap the corpse.20 See how thoughtful God is. Aren’t you impressed by the greatness of his love? I have continually emphasised, from my own paltry experience, that God always pays well: when we give him anything, little or much, we get it back a hundred-fold, either then or later. His promises never fail. But there is more. On thinking about the joy of that family, the witnesses of the miracle, and Jesus’ own joy, his heart overflowing with joy for the happiness of others – just as he had wept before, on seeing Martha’s and Mary’s tears – I have been reminded of the aspiration we say so often: omnia in bonum! 21– everything is for good. And that includes suffering, as long as we don’t try to maintain it stupidly, or to invent it with complications of our own imagination. Whatever happens to us, if we abandon ourselves in our Lord’s hands we will find peace and strength, because divine grace will make us into effective instruments.

18 Jn 11:43 19 Cf. Mk 7:37 20 Cf. Jn 11:44 21 Cf. Rom 8:28

My children, I ask you once again now, have loving recourse to God in this Bethany we have in our Centres. From your work, from your get-togethers, go and look for him, spend time with him, tell him you love him. Whenever you can go physically to the Tabernacle take advantage of the opportunity; if that’s not possible, do so in your heart, and assure him that you love him, that you long to be with him. If we are closely united to God, we will necessarily be united to all our brothers and sisters throughout the world, without any form of discrimination. With the Heart of Jesus, our hunger for souls will grow daily, as well as our zeal to co-redeem, to spread everywhere the happiness we have found. We can and we ought to be very apostolic and transmit the effectiveness of our self-giving and fidelity to the apostolate being carried out all over the world. In the divine economy nothing is ever wasted, not even that task which seems insignificant humanly speaking; all of it will produce delicious fruit in due season, sooner or later, because everything is made use of. Ask our Lord to send the Work lots of vocations. You can ask still more insistently, and you will if you enter into Bethany, because that way you will have the omnipotent strength of Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament.

## The love of the holy women {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.21.6}

I would like now, on the occasion of today’s feast, to bring to your minds another Gospel scene. Jesus is dead, and has died for all mankind – for you and for me – on the ignominy of the Cross, on that scaffold which at the time was felt to be as shameful as the gallows or the guillotine. Our Redeemer’s love and suffering were so intense, so infinite, that that instrument of torture and death has become a source of life and peace. “O death, where is your victory?”22 Death was powerless against Life; and in our case,

22 1 Cor 15:55

mortification, death for the love of God, obtains for us Life and blessings. But let us return to the scene. Jesus is dead. Mary Magdalene, Mary the Mother of James, Salome, and other women, have brought spices to embalm the body of Jesus.23 After the tragedy of the Cross all the disciples have fled, every last one of them! All that remain are a few women and an adolescent, John, whose fidelity was rewarded with the Redeemer’s words: “Behold, your mother!”24 In response to the fidelity of a few short years, our Lord responded by entrusting him with what he loved most on earth, and also giving him himself. With our eyes on this little band of women, strong in the hour of sorrow, loyal in spite of everything, let us renew the resolution never to forsake God any more. And if on some occasion we have in fact fled, we promise him it won’t happen again, at the same time as we ask him to grant us all the strength to persevere.

## At the empty tomb {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.21.7}

The tomb is empty! Mary Magdalene weeps, her face bathed in tears. She needs the Master. She has gone there to be consoled a little by being near him, to keep him company, because without him nothing is worthwhile. She perseveres in prayer; she looks for him everywhere; she has no other thought but for him. My children, God lets himself be won over by such fidelity, so that you and I may draw the right conclusion; so that we learn to hope and love truly. The Evangelist gives us a lengthy account, with a marvellous wealth of details.25 Mary continues sobbing inconsolably. Nothing in this world can relieve her sadness. Therefore she continues to look for the Master, with holy stubbornness, even after the others return to the city. When her tears threaten to overwhelm her, two angels dressed in white draw near and ask: “Woman, why are you

23 Cf. Mk 16:1 24 Jn 19:27 25 Cf. Jn 20:1-18

weeping?”26 Mary has never seen them before, but she answers them simply: “Because they have taken away my Lord, and I do not know where they have laid him.”27 She opens her heart and tells them her great concern, convinced that anyone who belongs to her world will understand her perfectly. The same should be true of us, who are of the world but without being worldly. And if we see that the world is straying from God, that it is unaware of God’s love, we will tell others our experience clearly and forcefully, like this woman, in order to lead them once again to the Creator. And right away Jesus goes out to meet Mary Magdalene, who sees him but still doesn’t recognise him, so great is her sorrow. She thinks he is the gardener and unburdens herself to him too, asking him forthrightly: “Tell me where you have laid him, and I will take him away.”28 These few words show the measure of her love. She well knew that the Pharisees, the Jews and the Roman soldiers had organised a careful search for Jesus’ body and were determined to punish those who had taken it, even when it was clear that Jesus had risen. Well, despite the dangers and threats of that campaign of intimidation, she doesn’t renounce our Lord. My children, I repeat that God is expecting a lot from our love, from our fidelity. He expects us to be determined to fulfil our tasks with great care, to overcome all obstacles. He expects us not to shrink from difficulties, which will not be lacking. He expects us always to choose his Love, with all its blessed risks, over comfort, over the cowardice of doing nothing or remaining anonymous. Finally, he expects us to seek him out stubbornly, come what may. “Mary!” Jesus exclaims. And her inconsolable grief suddenly vanishes. “Rabboni! Teacher!” Nothing else matters now. She has found him and doesn’t want to leave him. She knows, even before

26 Jn 20:13 27 Ibid. 28 Jn 20:15

the disciples do, that he is alive and reveals himself to his own. From that moment she experiences an unbounded joy. Once again our Lord shows us that he relies on those who follow him faithfully. He tells her to inform all his disciples that she has seen him, that he has risen, that he will be with them and will never abandon them.29 Let’s ask our Lord, our God, that we may all persevere faithfully; that we may never stray from the path; that, moved by contrition for our sins and mistakes, we may increase our love and our desire to make reparation, since he has forgiven us and has entrusted us with the task of telling those around us that he lives for us, that he is our true peace and happiness. We finish by invoking our Mother to show us the path to follow and keep us very united to her Son. Mary teaches us that there is only one answer to the continuous requests that God makes of each of us: to say to him, in all humility, a sincere fiat! Be it done!

29 Jn 20:17-18

# LIKE YEAST IN THE DOUGH * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.22}

“The kingdom of heaven is like leaven which a woman took and hid in three measures of flour, till it was all leavened.”1 This passage from the Gospel of today’s Mass can serve us as a reference point for some short considerations which may be useful for people who, like yourselves, want to spread God’s saving word everywhere in society. “The leaven leavens the dough when it comes close to the flour, and not simply close, but so as to be actually mixed with it – for he said not simply ‘put’ but ‘hid’.”2 Our life should be like that: all very ordinary, without any fuss or noise, but with the power given by God to those who want to do his will. What is the Christian vocation but a universal call to holiness? Holiness does not consist of doing more difficult things every day, but of finishing them off ever more lovingly. God may call some people to a type of life and activity which could seem very spectacular humanly speaking, as part of his extraordinary providence, but the majority of us who believe in him and love him are led along the simple path of his ordinary providence.

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 15 November 1964, celebrated that year as the 6th Sunday after the Epiphany 1 Mt 13:33 2 St John Chrysostom, Homilies on St Matthew’s Gospel, 46, 2

## Naturalness {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.22.1}

And so we are faced with a paradox: our Christian faith, which fills the whole of our supernatural life, is expressed in a very natural way. What does “natural” mean here? It does not mean leaving aside the theological virtues of faith, hope and charity, which are the heart of our Christian existence, infused by God in our soul. If we are not continually nourished by the fruits of these virtues we cannot follow Christ. Naturalness means that the supernatural, the divine life present within us, reveals itself in very simple and ordinary things, in everyday life. Behaving naturally is a virtue which requires effort, assiduous practice, and application. As human beings we are attracted to unusual things, to great exploits. We dream about opportunities which rarely occur in life, and we tend to despise the supernatural value of the more commonplace things, as if God weren’t to be found in them. We have to acquire the supernatural virtue of naturalness. Isn’t that a contradiction in terms? How can the natural be supernatural? Today, in God’s presence, I would like us to try to understand these things, guided by the Master’s hand. After listening to his words and meditating on them, we will understand that we don’t have to wait for unusual and extraordinary occasions to be truly Christian. We will learn to measure the most ordinary events with the yardstick of God’s love, and we will be able to reveal to others the hand of our heavenly Father in the commonplace events which go to make up our lives.

## Salt and light {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.22.2}

The leaven is buried in the dough, not just to lie there, but to enable it all to be leavened. God does not call us to lead an anonymous existence, but to a simple and natural presence as Christians in the world we live in and to which we belong. “You are

the salt of the earth; but if salt has lost its taste, how shall its saltness be restored? It is no longer good for anything except to be thrown out and trodden under foot by men. You are the light of the world. A city set on a hill cannot be hid. Nor do men light a lamp and put it under a bushel, but on a stand, and it gives light to all in the house. Let your light so shine before men, that they may see your good works and give glory to your Father who is in heaven.”3 So we Christians have to give light, we have to be present. We cannot hide away in the catacombs of shyness or inhibition. We cannot be ashamed of doing good, or of speaking about God. Salt has to season, because if it doesn’t it becomes insipid and useless. As Christians we possess the light of God: it is not our own, but something we have received and have to pass on. “What I tell you in the dark, utter in the light; and what you hear whispered, proclaim upon the housetops.”4 When Jesus our Lord was accused before Caiaphas, he replied: “I have spoken openly to the world; I have always taught in synagogues and in the temple, where all Jews come together; I have said nothing secretly. Why do you ask me? Ask those who have heard me, what I said to them; they know what I said.”5 If we now take a look at chapter six of St Matthew’s Gospel, we will find another series of teachings which seem to contradict what we have just read. “Beware of practising your piety before men in order to be seen by them; for then you will have no reward from your Father who is in heaven.”6 Immediately afterwards the Divine Master opens his lips to explain to us, with great skill as a teacher, what he expects us to do. Give alms, not to the sound of trumpets, but without your left hand knowing what your right hand is doing. Pray, not

3 Mt 5:13-16 4 Mt 10:27 5 Jn 18:20-21 6 Mt 6:1

spectacularly, at the street corners, but in your room, with the door closed. Fast, not with a woebegone, gaunt expression, but with washed face and perfumed head; and your Father who sees in secret will reward you.7 What have we got here? Weren’t we told it was necessary to preach publicly, and that our works should shine before men? Why are we now being told to do good, to pray and to fast without being seen, except in the presence of our heavenly Father? Let us ask our Lord to explain his teachings to us, because we want to follow him, we ardently want to do what he asks, without taking refuge in obscure meanings or contradictions, which his teachings cannot contain. His words are words of eternal life: heaven and earth will pass away,8 but his words will not pass away.9

## Preaching without practising {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.22.3}

While reading and meditating on the holy Gospel we have admired a quality our Lord always esteems greatly: simplicity, sincerity. How wonderfully he praises the publican, in contrast to the hypocrisy of the Pharisee! How tenderly he embraces little children! Their parents brought them to him for him to bless them, but his disciples tried to stop them. Jesus indigne tulit,10 he took exception to it “and said to them, ‘Let the children come to me, do not hinder them; for to such belongs the kingdom of God. Truly, I say to you, whoever does not receive the kingdom of God like a child shall not enter it.’ And he took them in his arms and blessed them, laying his hands upon them.”11 The hypocrite cannot understand that spirit of simplicity. What is his sin? That he doesn’t practise what he preaches.12 Hypocrites

7 Cf. Mt 6:2-18 8 Cf. Jn 6:69 9 Cf. Mt 24:35 10 Mk 10:14 11 Mk 10:14-16 12 Cf. Mt 23:3

tend to be show-offs: they parade a virtue they don’t actually possess, they use sacred things for their petty personal advantage, and make goodness unattractive by using it as a cloak for evildoing. At the root of hypocrisy one finds a lie, as a permanent attitude: anything that is straightforward has to be twisted out of shape if it is to be understood by that warped mentality. Hypocrisy is the daughter of the devil, who is the father of lies and lover of ostentation: “[The devil] said to him, ‘If you are the Son of God, throw yourself down; for it is written, He will give his angels charge of you, and on their hands they will bear you up, lest you strike your foot against a stone.’ Jesus said to him, ‘Again it is written, you shall not tempt the Lord your God.’”13 It is tempting God to expect him to give us extraordinary things, to try to forget that it is in the ordinary life of every day that God’s love and tenderness are poured forth. See how Jesus never consents to ostentatious display, carried out for the wrong intentions and religious only in name. He preached publicly; we have heard his teachings from his own lips. But on one occasion some of his relatives, who don’t believe in him,14 say to him: “Leave here and go to Judea, that your disciples may see the works you are doing. For no man works in secret if he seeks to be known openly. If you do these things, show yourself to the world.”15 And Jesus, who knows why they say this, “said to them (…) ‘Go to the feast yourselves; I am not going up to this feast, for my time has not yet fully come.’ But after his brothers had gone up to the feast, then he also went up, not publicly but in private.”16 He has never spoken in secret, but now he goes up to Jerusalem incognito. He doesn’t want his mission of peace and love, of

13 Mt 4:6-7 14 Cf. Jn 7:5 15 Jn 7:3-4 16 Jn 7:8.10

redemption, to become the plaything of those who try to present him as a curious novelty, something to help pass an afternoon. On another occasion Jesus is disgusted by the ostentatious scene of people putting large amounts of money in the Temple treasury; but he is deeply moved by the action of a poor widow – whom he alone noticed – who gives everything, the two pence she possessed.17 Lord, I entreat you today to engrave on our souls your own way of acting, your own sensitivity: your naturally supernatural attitude. Thus we will learn that we shouldn’t do good in order to be seen by others, and at the same time that it shouldn’t bother us if we are noticed when, with a right intention, for love of you, we are trying to imitate you but others interpret our deeds either as secretive or as ostentatious.

## The mustard seed and the wheat {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.22.4}

Let us continue considering the words of this lovable Teacher to see what Christian naturalness should be like. He teaches us with very ordinary examples, things we can all relate to. “The kingdom of heaven is like a grain of mustard seed which a man took and sowed in his field; it is the smallest of all seeds, but when it has grown it is the greatest of shrubs and becomes a tree, so that the birds of the air come and make nests in its branches.”18 That is how the supernatural life begins to grow in us, like a divine seed which the Holy Spirit deposits, with Baptism, in our souls, in the clay we are made of. Perhaps at the beginning nobody notices anything, because our Christian life still is fragile and almost defenceless. But if we don’t set up barriers to grace, the supernatural life starts to grow in us, we put forth roots, then branches and leaves, and finally flowers and

17 Cf. Mk 12:41-44 18 Mt 13:31-32

fruits. Through God’s goodness, his work will shine before men, and they will glorify, not the work, but its First Cause. “The kingdom of God is as if a man should scatter seed upon the ground, and should sleep and rise night and day, and the seed should sprout and grow, he knows not how. The earth produces of itself, first the blade, then the ear, then the full grain in the ear.”19 This is the naturalness God asks of us: to follow step by step in our souls what he is cultivating, to reveal, without any spectacle and without hiding it, the deeds he works in us. Let us not think nor vainly assert as ours what is due only to God; but neither let us hide what he has done, so that all may see it and glorify our Father who is in heaven. This is the naturalness of the supernatural, which flourishes among simple ordinary things. But simple things are not always easy to grasp. There is in man a desire for extraordinary and complicated and mysterious things that impedes his progress toward religion: he confuses what is natural with what is easy. Sometimes Christianity is thought of as an arcane teaching for the initiated, something which is meant only for a handful of people.

## Naturalness is not secrecy {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.22.5}

I’ve been repeating these same things for many years now, in speech and in writing, lending my voice and hand to something that our Lord taught continuously. This Gospel teaching has now reached millions of people, and, with God’s grace, they each go toward holiness along their own path, turning the little events of every day into opportunities to sanctify themselves and other people. Nevertheless I don’t wish to pass over in silence something which has occasionally pained me, although it has never worried me, namely, that this spirit of simplicity and naturalness has been

19 Mk 4:26

mistaken for secrecy. Many years ago, when Opus Dei was a little mustard seed, some people, possibly with the best of good wills, invented complicated stories of mystery and strangeness, which existed only in their own imagination. People who are accustomed to conceive of only one legitimate form of religious expression, marked by badges, placards, and parades, and by a not always very clear confessionalism, failed to grasp the freedom of those who wanted to express their faith with deeds and not with slogans. It is a mentality which seeks above all to put labels on things, to have everything encapsulated as soon as possible in the box of its own prejudices and summary judgments; or that other mentality which regards as secretive whatever transcends the limits of its own ignorance. It was useless to try to explain, charitably and politely, that something should be judged by its fruits, that they should look at things dispassionately, that they should admit in practice what they claimed to support in theory, namely, the freedom of every human being, and every Christian, to follow his or her own path in all honesty, always in accordance with the law of the land. Some people still are tied to these weird expressions of prejudice, as serfs were tied to the land in olden days. What they say in effect is: if the faith is the most important thing in life for you, please wear a placard on your back to say so, so that we’ll know who we are dealing with. I am a priest of God and for many years I have always dressed as a priest in public, because Catholics and non-Catholics alike have the right to recognise me as God’s minister, one who follows him and who aspires, with his grace, to manifest that fact in word and deed. But the lay members of Opus Dei – men and women, young and old, celibate and married, of all social backgrounds, engaged in hundreds of ordinary civil undertakings – don’t need any type of badge or emblem. They are on an equal footing with their fellowcitizens, with the same rights and the same duties. They have no

wish to live off the fact of being Catholics – in fact they find the very idea repulsive. They live in spite of being Catholics in places where people who practise their faith are discriminated against, because nowadays being a Catholic doesn’t bestow any advantages, as it did in former times, but only problems and misunderstandings. Our Lord, as we have seen, was neither secretive nor ostentatious. How can genuine Christians be recognised? By their fruits, by their deeds. Members of Opus Dei are people who, by that very fact, are trying to live a life of true piety, composed of prayer and frequent reception of the sacraments; who are constantly trying to improve their doctrinal and spiritual formation; who are trying to do all in their power to ensure that nobody around them can say that they didn’t speak to them about God. But all that doesn’t separate them from the world they live in; it doesn’t make them the odd man out, detached from the lives and the affairs of all upright people who earn their living through daily work. When Catholics try to live in accordance with the faith, to express in practice what they confess in their heart and with their lips, anyone who gets to know them will notice very naturally this determination not to betray the faith of their baptism. Perhaps someone might think: but this is not very natural or common; that sort of behaviour can or will clash with the environment they live in. More than thirty years ago, faced with a similar issue, I wrote that if in some case the upright behaviour of a Christian contrasts with the environment, the natural and ordinary thing is that the contrast should be noticed.20 And these cases, without drama or spectacle, happen all the time. Does that mean that our vocation is something secret? It is an open secret, the lawful modesty of people who don’t wish to have the intimacy of their consciences become a topic of conversation in salons and cafés. It is a fact that is public, but not a matter for

20 Cf. The Way, 380

publicity: it is making oneself known through one’s way of life and one’s work.

## Children of the light {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.22.6}

“Praise God and give thanks to him; exalt him and give thanks to him in the presence of all the living for what he has done for you. It is good to praise God and to exalt his name, worthily declaring the works of God. Do not be slow to give him thanks. It is good to guard the secret of a king, but gloriously to reveal the works of God.”21 How are we to proclaim the Lord’s mercy to each of us? He has given us life, both natural and supernatural, he nourishes us with the treasure of his teaching and with his own Body and Blood. St Peter gives us the answer: “like a lamp shining in a dark place, until the day dawns and the morning star rises in your hearts.”22 Both in our own personal behaviour and also in the customs of the society we live in, sometimes the shadows are very deep and we see darkness everywhere. If we have recourse to our Lord we discover “the true light that enlightens every man, coming into the world.”23 When we are united to the Light, we ourselves give light too – clearly, simply and sincerely. This apostolate of naturalness and simplicity is not at all easy. “For every one who does evil hates the light, and does not come to the light, lest his deeds should be exposed.”24 When people ask “where are the Christians who can serve as a good example?” very often the reply is readily to hand in the lives of people around them. But they prefer not to notice. If they were to realise that genuine Christian life is something simple, heroic, supernatural and serene, they would perhaps reject it, because they seek what is complicated,

21 Tob 12:6-7 22 2 Pet 1:19 23 Jn 1:9 24 Jn 3:20

a strange medley of unusual things which they can then easily dismiss as “inhuman.” “For you are all sons of light and sons of the day; we are not of the night or of darkness.”25 This is the nature of the supernatural life: transcending our own mistakes and shortcomings to irradiate something of the light of Christ: “I have come as light into the world, that whoever believes in me may not remain in darkness.”26 How can we give light, how can we reflect even a little of the light we receive from God? We need to be deeply humble. By trying not to be a party lantern, gaudy but ephemeral in its tinsel. It shines proudly at the top of a pole, amid the hubbub of the celebration, but the following morning it will be cast to the ground, swept into a corner by the wind, and forgotten. We must not try to be a party lantern, then, but a simple, useful light, that shines and warms, that guides like a beacon in a dark place.27 The naturalness our Lord asks of us is not some form of human achievement, something we can attain on our own. If we were to think that the light were ours, it would slowly die out, it would be a will-o’-the-wisp whose light is deceptive. “What I do know of myself, I know from your enlightening of me; and what I do not know of myself, I will continue not to know until the time when my darkness is as the noonday in your sight.”28 Naturalness and simplicity are not simply techniques for apostolic activity. To set out to be natural as a policy only leads to complication. Naturalness is a consequence of sincerity of life, selfknowledge, humility, trust in God, and the daily effort to ensure that a good tree brings forth good fruits.29

25 1 Thess 5:5 26 Jn 12:46 27 Cf. 2 Pet 1:19 28 St Augustine, Confessions, 10, 5 29 Cf. Lk 6:43

## Allowing the Holy Spirit to act {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.22.7}

Christian asceticism offers us a clear and simple way to grow in interior and exterior naturalness, in sincerity of soul, so that we let ourselves be seen as we are. It is to develop our relationship with the Holy Spirit. He comes to us to wash what is dirty, to water what is arid, to heal what is wounded, to bend what is rigid, to straighten what is crooked, to warm what is frozen.30 The Divine Paraclete comes to us as a true friend: to save, to heal, to teach, to console. “And as a man who being previously in darkness then suddenly beholds the sun, is enlightened in his bodily sight, and sees plainly things which he did not see, so likewise he to whom the Holy Spirit is granted, is enlightened in his soul.”31 When God’s light comes to us it doesn’t make us strange or different from other people. Its radiance is like a divine sun which enables us to distinguish between the insignificance of our own contribution and how much God gives us. In that contrast we learn how to be Christians in all simplicity, to do good unostentatiously, to rectify our mistakes as often as we make them, and to give freely to others what we have received freely ourselves.32 Have you ever seen one of those documentaries which use photographic techniques to show the whole life-cycle of a flower in just a few seconds, from the moment it starts to bud, through every stage of development until it finally begins to wither? Christian life is not like that. It develops little by little, usually very naturally and without any miracles. If God wants to, he can allow us to experience extraordinary expressions of his providence, but what I admire particularly is his ordinary providence. The gentle slow transformation of the flower, day by day, from the first appearance of the bud on the stalk to the final ripe fruit, is a sort of image of the

30 Cf. Roman Missal, Pentecost Sunday, Sequence Veni, Sancte Spiritus 31 St Cyril of Jerusalem, Catechesis, 16, 16 32 Cf. Mt 10:8

serene natural process of holiness in the soul, without the soul itself being aware of it. It is not just one miracle but many miracles, all of them simple, ordinary, daily ones. That is how our life has to be: full of naturalness in the faithful fulfilment of our daily work, in our relationships with our loved ones, in our dialogue with our friends and colleagues, and in the especially intense moments of our relationship with God – prayer and the sacraments. That is how to behave with simple heroism, without the desire for extraordinary events which is often an excuse to avoid fulfilling our little daily duties. And if anyone should ask us what is the reason for our hope,33 we will speak to them about God, whom we meet every day, just as we would speak to a friend who is genuinely interested in anything we are keen on or excited about. “In my Father’s house are many rooms; if it were not so, would I have told you that I go to prepare a place for you?”34 God does not lead everybody along the same path, and every Christian has to learn to respect and love those who walk along paths which, since they come from God, must lead to God. In the history of the Church there have been, and continue to be, many striking cases of involuntarily spectacular asceticism, great wonder-workers, and private revelations. But I can see that God calls most or nearly all of us along the path of naturalness, to fight in ordinary little things. If it were ever to occur to someone that this isn’t very much, that one needs to accomplish great feats, to hold great rallies with hundreds of thousands of participants singing and waving flags, then our love for the freedom of every individual, and the freedom Christ has won for us,35 should lead us to respect those opinions and those feelings. And in turn I expect them to respect mine – this path of daily prose which Christ himself has walked, even during his

33 Cf. 1 Pet 3:15 34 Jn 14:2 35 Cf. Gal 4:31

triumphal entry into Jerusalem trotting on a simple donkey. The same path as the one our Lady followed during her earthly life: though she was the Mother of God, she revealed the mystery to nobody and went unnoticed, as just another of the women of her village. Let us learn from her how to behave simply and naturally.

# FRUITFULNESS * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.23}

Today is a particularly joyful occasion, because it is the feast of our Lady of Ransom. Many years have passed since I first went to her basilica in Barcelona to entrust to her everything I had to do in Rome. That was in 1946. We had to open a new pathway in the Church, and the obstacles seemed insurmountable. Your brother Alvaro, whom I had sent to the Eternal City, found himself facing a blank wall and wrote to me saying: “Father, you will have to come, because I can’t do anything more.” We were told that we had come a century too soon. I embarked on that journey trusting in God and in the Blessed Virgin: ecce nos reliquimus omnia et secuti sumus te! – that was my prayer at the time. Quid ergo erit nobis?1 See, we have left everything – family, reputation, everything in life, each of us doing what God asked of us in our own place – for love of you, and we have followed you: what will become of us? I have often reminded you that God heard our prayer – he never refuses to listen to his Mother’s requests – and in those early years in Rome he wrote a wonderful page in the history of the Work. Venite, adoremus Deum, et procidamus ante Dominum!2 How appropriate these words from the Introit of today’s Mass are. God

* Notes of a meditation given in Pamplona, 24 September 1966, feast of our Lady of Ransom, which that year fell on Ember Saturday in September 1 Mt 19:27 2 O come, let us adore God, let us kneel before the Lord!: Roman Missal of St Pius V, Ember Saturday in September, Introit (cf. Ps 95:6)

and his Mother are so good to us that they always listen to us. I can assure you, with joy and gratitude to God, that the intention we have been praying for for many years now has been resolved. God has used the recent Council, which a number of your brothers were involved in, to open up in the Church the path along which we will be able to travel as we should. It is the fruit of thousands and thousands of Masses offered for that intention, hundreds of thousands of hours of prayer, millions and millions of hours of work offered to God, countless aspirations, pain and sorrow without reckoning, the sufferings of people who have left this world with a smile of their lips and peace in their souls, very united to the Father’s constant prayer. But we have to keep praying as if it hadn’t yet been resolved, until I tell you to stop; and afterwards we will keep praying in thanksgiving. And always, “Let us worship and bow down, let us kneel before the Lord, our Maker! Let us sing to the Lord; let us make a joyful noise to the rock of our salvation!”3

## Love means deeds {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.23.1}

The Gospel of today’s Mass recounts two lessons our Lord teaches us, one in words and the other in deeds, both of which are very appropriate for us, his children in Opus Dei, who are called by God, by virtue of our specific vocation in the Church, to be very fruitful: Ego elegi vos et posui vos, ut eatis et fructum afferatis, et fructus vester maneat.4 St Luke tells us that Jesus told this parable to the multitudes: “A man had a fig tree planted in his vineyard; and he came seeking fruit on it and found none. And he said to the vinedresser, ‘Lo, these three years I have come seeking fruit on this fig tree, and I find none. Cut it down; why should it use up the ground?’ And he

3 Ps 95:6,1 4 I chose you and appointed you that you should go and bear fruit and that your fruit should abide: Jn 15:16

answered him, ‘Let it alone, sir, this year also, till I dig about it and put on manure. And if it bears fruit next year, well and good; but if not, you can cut it down.’”5 See how patient God is! The fig tree was growing in fertile soil, it received all proper attention, and nevertheless it yielded no fruit. He could have uprooted it immediately, but he didn’t, he redoubled his efforts to make it produce: he dug around the trunk to aerate the soil, he applied manure… Think of your own situation; I think of mine. God our Lord, as well as having given us life, has also granted us the gift of faith, and has given us the gift of belonging to his Work, planted in the good soil which is the Church. He looks after us with paternal and maternal care, and very often we don’t produce the fruit he has the right to expect. How much he puts up with from us!
