My son, we should be very conscious of our duty to be saints; to produce supernatural fruit in our lives, always and everywhere, because we are contemplative souls. St Paul says so clearly: unusquisque, in qua vocatione vocatus est, in ea permaneat,6 every one should remain in the state he was in when God called him. We are not just like the rest of the ordinary citizens we meet, we are the same as them, because the world is the place where we are sanctified, where God has sought us out and where he has placed us, so that by his grace we can purify it of the dross of sin and give it back to him. But he asks us to be fruitful, producing the fruit of a love which necessarily expresses itself in deeds. I am already nearly sixty-five years old – though I don’t feel old

– and I have known very many souls. Years ago, when I was younger, I knew a priest who, while giving Holy Communion to a community of enclosed religious, was saying affectionate words within his contemplative heart to Jesus hidden in the Sacred Host.

5 Lk 13:6-9 6 1 Cor 7:20

While he was distributing the Body of Christ to those good nuns who were “buried to the world”, he dared to say to him, not aloud but interiorly, “Lord, I love you more than these do.” And the Lord replied in a silent locution, “Love means deeds and not sweet words.” I say the same to you now. God is looking for your fruits, for an effectively holy life; so that you can draw other souls to him, set them afire, purify them, lead them along the path of Love, and make them holy. He looks for fruit from you as he did from the fig tree in the parable which, in spite of all its leaves and all the care lavished on it, was still barren.7 And you won’t produce fruits of holiness and apostolate for me if you don’t have genuine, authentic piety. On the other hand, if you have piety and doctrine you will be apostles.

## Sanctifying work {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.23.2}

The vocation we get from God makes our work wonderfully effective. Many years ago, I wrote that the spirit of Opus Dei contains the wonderful fact that all worthy and upright human undertakings can be turned into divine ones. This is God’s will. Work is at the very core of our way of seeking holiness. This is what the Church expects of her daughters and sons in Opus Dei, this is what the Blessed Trinity and our Lady of Ransom expect. There is no honest human work which cannot always be sanctified and made into an instrument of sanctification. In the service of God there’s no such thing as unimportant jobs. They are all very important. The farmer who tills the soil carries out a noble task, as does the university lecturer who unites learning and faith; or the craftsman, working at home; or the financier who tries to make the world of finance serve the needs of society. I will never tire of reminding you that the value of a job depends on the personal conditions of the person carrying it out, the thoroughness with

7 Cf. Lk 13:6-9

which it is done, and the love of God expressed in it. Any work, provided it is honest and upright, can and ought to be offered to God. To sanctify our work, as you know very well, we have to carry it out as perfectly as possible. This applies to everybody! To street sweepers as much as to writers, because the quality of the work depends on the love whereby it is carried out. Human perfection is the foundation of Christian perfection; if something is not humanly perfect it can never be so spiritually. Consequently, in Opus Dei there is no room for shoddy workers, unless they change. In the spiritual life, my dearest children, nobody is perfectly healthy, we are all to some extent shoddy workers… otherwise we would be perfect already, and as long as we live we are wayfarers, we’re on our way, and every single one of us is imperfect! Consequently, for God’s children in Opus Dei to achieve Christian perfection in their profession or job, they need to be trained to administer their freedom well. They need to live in God’s presence, to cultivate sincere piety, to acquire and perfect the doctrine that enables them to assimilate Christ’s dogmatic and moral teaching in accordance with their individual circumstances. From the moment we join the Work we are constantly being formed, both in personal spiritual direction – sacramental confession, the fraternal chat and fraternal correction – and in collective spiritual direction: meditations, circles, recollections, annual courses… The process of being formed never ends; we never regard ourselves as beyond it all. So we are always happy to go to the annual course, which is a period of relaxation and improvement, as well as to the other means of formation established by our particular law. We are very keen to make good use of them to improve humanly and supernaturally, and to grow in union with God. Let us return to the sterile fig tree. It seemed to be doing something, it was growing leaves… but it wasn’t producing any

fruit at all! Lack of response is the only thing that makes us ineffective; as far as God is concerned everything has been arranged. Let yourselves be formed. Open your hearts frankly, don’t leave any corner hidden. That way the fruits that God and souls expect of us will be all the tastier.

## Our gaze fixed on heaven {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.23.3}

Let us continue reading today’s Gospel. After the parable of the fig tree St Luke tells us that Jesus travelled from one place to another, teaching on the Sabbath in the Jewish synagogues. On one of those occasions our Lord found among his listeners a poor ill woman “who had had a spirit of infirmity for eighteen years; she was bent over and could not fully straighten herself.”8 That woman, fettered by Satan, was unable to raise her eyes to heaven. My son, is not this a very clear image of the soul which lives far from God? Very many people find themselves in this sad situation. Friends and acquaintances of yours in whom there is not yet any sign of supernatural life, because they are enslaved by sin. I have often told you that the Redemption wrought by Christ on Calvary in a certain sense still has to be carried out: it has to be applied to souls one by one. And God has sought us out to be his instruments. We have to be faithful. It is very important that you and I live as we ought! All Christians in the Church constitute a single body, the Mystical Body of Christ, which is composed of different members. What would become of a body if it were all head? Without a heart, without a stomach, it couldn’t survive. Or if it were all hands or feet? It needs the wonderful diversity which God has provided for the good of the whole body. Opus Dei, within the Church, is a perfectly balanced body. We each have to sanctify our occupation or job, sanctify ourselves in

8 Lk 13:11

that job, and also be instruments of holiness. This is the hinge, I repeat, on which Christian perfection in the Work turns. Holiness in the middle of society. I have a great love for the Religious Orders. The religious lead lives of evangelical perfection by means of vows received by the Church, and that’s how they sanctify themselves. We are not interested in vows, either public or private. We are interested in virtues. Some people speak about “the three evangelical counsels” as if there were no more than three, and that’s not so: the Gospel is full of counsels of Christ, wonderful counsels which are valid for all Christians. We have to follow them all. And over and above all those counsels, there is an imperative command – charity. You know that from the beginning I had a plaque placed in all our Centres with the words of the Lord’s “new commandment”: mandatum novum do vobis, ut diligatis invicem sicut et ego dilexi vos.9 It continues to be new because people don’t obey it. You, my children, should obey it always, joyfully bearing the defects of the people you live with. Don’t behave like the dung beetle, which rolls a piece of dung into a ball with its legs and then carries it around the place. Behave like the bee, which goes from flower to flower seeking the goodness hidden within them, and then turns it into the sweetest honey, a delicious food, which in our brothers and sisters is made manifest as the good aroma of holiness. In a word, love one another, love one another a lot! Opus Dei is a family composed of more than forty thousand members scattered throughout the world, breathing the life of the first Christians. They were cor unum et anima una,10 of one heart and one soul, and the same happens in the Work. God has willed it so. Thank you, Lord! I don’t understand it: he has chosen to make use of you and me to carry out a divine and human task of peace,

9 A new commandment I give to you, that you love one another, even as I have loved you: Jn 13:34 10 Acts 4:32

understanding, forgiveness and a holy sense of understanding in the world … I don’t know about you, but I am just a poor man. God has been too generous, because we can see that we are inadequate instruments. It’s as if someone had taken the leg of a table and used it to create a beautiful illuminated manuscript with graceful calligraphy. It all seems like a dream. He has chosen you too, my children, and as long as I live you are co-founders.

## Opening one’s heart {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.23.4}

When he meets that crippled woman, “Jesus (…) called her.”11 Our Lord looked upon her, as he looks upon us always. In these post-conciliar times, when so many heresies are being propagated, let us make an explicit act of faith in the Real Presence of Jesus in the Blessed Eucharist: O Lord, who preside over us in the tabernacle, I believe that you are really, truly, substantially present in the Sacred Host: Body, Blood, Soul and Divinity. I believe that it is you, the Son of Mary ever-Virgin: I love you, I reverence you, I adore you. Jesus looked with loving eyes, the eyes of a father and a mother, upon that poor sick woman, he had compassion on her and called her… He has also chosen you and me, moved by mercy. Did we deserve it? No, I certainly didn’t, and you neither. Perhaps you were walking along with your eyes fixed on the ground, on earthly things, when he crossed your path deliberately, and invited you: “Look up, look at the sky!” How good Jesus is! Mulier, dimissa es ab infirmitate tua,12 woman, you are freed from your infirmity. “And he laid his hands upon her, and immediately she was made straight, and she praised God.”13 Do you see how relevant the Gospel is? What did Jesus say to her? “Woman, you are freed from your infirmity.” She could now raise

11 Lk 13:12 12 Lk 13:13 13 Ibid.

her gaze and look at the sky, at heaven. Just like us: we are cured, but we are inclined to evil. We tend toward earthly things, and if God doesn’t help us, we can commit the greatest atrocities, myself first of all. We are capable of perpetrating all the horrors and evils which the most unfortunate individual has ever committed. I reminded you earlier that, of all the counsels Jesus gives us in the Gospel, the first and most important is charity – love. That is why we have to take note of other people’s good qualities, not look at their defects, and much less exaggerate them. Because if we are sincere we will discover lots of defects in ourselves. I have them and I have spent forty years fighting against them; I am the first one who was called to Opus Dei. My daughters and sons serve God, the Church and all souls joyfully, because God is not pleased by something done reluctantly. If someone does something for me with a long face I don’t appreciate it. Servite Domino in laetitia!14 Not an external superficial gaiety, but an interior joy which proceeds from a deep union with God and which brings with it peace, the gaudium cum pace which is characteristic of the Christian vocation. One loses this good tranquillity, through pride, when one wanders off the road, when one is not faithful to the faith, when one is careless about purity. But here below everything can be solved, everything except death, and for us death is Life, if we go contritely to God our Father. So if you ever lose that supernatural joy, run straight to confession, to personal spiritual direction, and open your heart wide. I referred earlier to charity, and now I add that you have to be very sincere and seek advice from the person who can give it to you in God’s name. We shouldn’t conceal in our hearts, like a dirty puddle, the shortcomings and the things we are ashamed of. In the last analysis, there is very little difference between one woman and another, or between one man and another.

14 Serve the Lord with gladness: Ps 100:2

For my part I see myself capable of every conceivable crime, so nothing scandalises me: I understand everything, I forgive everything. Speak immediately, because if you leave the puddle there, the uncleanness will spread, you won’t be at peace, and you will reach the unfortunate stage of living hypocritically, and even sacrilegiously… Speak out, conquer your sense of shame. My mother, when her friends called at the house – I was very small at the time, because I was able to hide under the bed – used to say to me: “My son, come on out of there and greet these ladies. Only be ashamed to sin. Go on, give them a kiss.” I used to reply that I didn’t have any kisses ready… My very dearest children, God has called us to overcome our embarrassment and open our hearts in spiritual direction and in the sacrament of Penance, allowing the light of grace to enter the deepest recesses of the soul, like the light through that window, to purify, burn, enkindle, illuminate and sanctify. Make the resolution to be sincere and open. Lift up your eyes to heaven, don’t look just at yourselves. Accept the advice you get as coming from Christ our Lord himself. The secret of fidelity is to forget about oneself, to think about others, to begin again with humility. It’s time, therefore, for clear resolutions. One last point: after charity and sincerity, work and industriousness. In the Work we all work like donkeys. We earn our own living and support our Centres. Opus Dei doesn’t need any money, but the corporate undertakings, which do so much good to so many souls, most certainly do. And so we have to ask many other people to help too. I will end now by turning our good Mother, Our Lady of Ransom. We haven’t been able to celebrate the Mass of her feast because the liturgical regulations impede it, but it is even better to obey the Church. I beseech her, Mother of God and our Mother: keep these daughters and sons of mine healthy in body and soul. May they all love you madly. May they set fire to everything around

them. May many more people join the Work so we can multiply. There are already very many of us throughout the world, but there have to be even more of us, because we are needed in all corners of the world. We all have to be conscious of that responsibility. You are brothers or sisters, and in a way – quite a big way – fathers or mothers of those who come after you, because they will be your children: children of your prayer, your sacrifice and your selfgiving. Moreover, they will make progress thanks to your good example. That way you will send the Mystical Body of the Church, and the Work, good arterial blood: with acts of contrition, with constant sacrifices, contributing to the unity and the holiness of all, to the joy of being children of God.

# DYING AND REIGNING * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.24}

Today’s liturgy reminds us that there is a kingdom of heaven here on earth, and that all earthly realities must be subject to its King. For Jesus Christ has been given “all authority in heaven and on earth.”1 He has been constituted “King of kings and Lord of lords.”2 In him are fulfilled the words of the Psalm: “Ask of me, and I will make the nations your heritage, and the ends of the earth your possession.”3 Today it seems appropriate, my children, to begin our prayer where we sometimes finish, using words the Church asks us priests to recite: “We give thanks to the Father, who has qualified us to share in the inheritance of the saints in light. He has delivered us from the dominion of darkness and transferred us to the kingdom of his beloved Son.”4 This is the song of love we will sing eternally in the kingdom of heaven; but we can begin it now on earth if we subject ourselves to Christ’s kingdom: “A kingdom of truth and life, a kingdom of holiness and grace, a kingdom of justice, love and peace.”5

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 29 October 1967, Solemnity of Christ the King 1 Mt 28:18 2 Rev 19:16 3 Ps 2:8 4 Col 1:12-13 5 Roman Missal, Solemnity of Christ the King, Preface

## Dying like a grain of wheat {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.24.1}

At this time, when kings are gradually disappearing from the public life of nations, to be replaced by other people who enjoy more authority than kings ever dreamed of, I have just recently received news that the persecution against our Catholic brothers and sisters in Eastern Europe has intensified. And no one is protesting: political expediency leads people to keep silent. It is prudence according to the flesh.6 But I cannot keep quiet, I cannot stop praying: Lord, this is part of your kingdom too! Lord, you drew light from darkness, draw forth truth from error now! Silence the slanderers! Hold back the swords of those bent on cruelly martyring your faithful children who form part of your earthly kingdom! But God can reply with the words of the Apocalypse proclaimed in today’s liturgy: “Worthy is the Lamb who was slain, to receive power and wealth and wisdom and might and honour and glory and blessing.”7 Yes, my children, we must die in order to reign with Christ. But the death Jesus asks of us is not a violent, bloody one. He asks you and me to die by mortification and penance, made sweet and lovable by our intimacy with God, by the Bread and the Word, by our prayerful encounter with the Lamb immolated on the Cross and in the daily Sacrament of the Altar. Ours is to be a death like that of the grain of wheat that decays beneath the soil.8 But from its disappearance stems fruitfulness, victory, resurrection. And in the meantime, as long as we don’t lose our supernatural outlook, joy and peace as well. During the years I’ve spent working for Christ I can hardly recall a single day when I didn’t have to suffer for his sake and for his Work. Ours has to be a mortification done for love, my children.

6 Cf. Rom 8:6 7 Rev 5:12 8 Cf. Jn 12:24

Dignus est Agnus, qui occisus est... 9 Lord, in union with your death, may we learn to die a little every day. Lord, you cross the paths of these children of mine with your Cross. They know that, because they love you and seek you; but sometimes they don’t realise that you cross my path too and give me your Cross. And so I beseech you, Lord, to carry the Cross with me. That way this peace and this joy will be filled with your power and strength, and will no longer be human but will have become divine. This peace and strength of yours will make us worthy to receive the Holy Spirit, who is the fruit of the Cross. And together with the Holy Spirit, the gift of wisdom, to be able to govern, to contribute to your glory and to your endless reign.

## Co-redeeming with Christ {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.24.2}

How will Christ reign in the world? By first reigning in each of us. And how will he reign in each of us? By reigning in all our actions, thoughts and desires. Do you want to continue the prayer on your own…? From the depths of your souls, cry out like St Paul to the Colossians: gratias agimus Deo Patri,10 we give thanks to God the Father. The Three Divine Persons, Father, Son and Holy Spirit, have already made their appearance. Listen to the voice of the Son: “Sacrifices and offerings you have not desired, but a body you prepared for me; in burnt offerings and sin offerings you have taken no pleasure. Then I said, Lo, I have come to do your will, O God”:11 to be clothed in flesh, and, through my Passion, to save those unfortunate children and redeem them. The Son himself, with the Father and the Holy Spirit, has sought us out to help him co-redeem. Eripuit nos de potestate

9 Worthy is the Lamb who was slain...: Rev 5:12 10 Cf. Col 1:12 11 Heb 10:5-7

tenebrarum,12 he has freed us from the power of darkness, per sanguinem crucis eius,13 by the Blood he shed on the Cross, et vocavit nos vocatione sua sancta,14 and he called us with his holy calling. Haven’t I told you many times that we should have a “superiority complex”? But Father, wouldn’t that be a sign of pride? No, my children! It is a consequence of humility, a humility which leads me to say: Lord, you are he who is. I am nothing. You have every perfection: power, strength, love, glory, wisdom, dominion, honour. If I get close to you, like a child in his father’s strong arms or in his mother’s wonderful lap, I’ll sense the warmth of your divinity and the light of your Wisdom, and I’ll feel your strength coursing through my veins. With St Paul, let us once again “give thanks to the Father who has qualified us to share in the inheritance of the saints in light.”15 Holy light, light from God! This is what St Paul means. For you, my son, this light is your lay mentality and your priestly soul. How many people fail to understand our way in the Work because they don’t have this light! They don’t understand, Jesus, they don’t understand! They don’t know what it means to serve, to work for the Church without self-seeking, without compromising her by creating human factions. Gratias agimus Deo Patri… Let us give thanks to God, “who has delivered us from the dominion of darkness and transferred us to the kingdom of his beloved Son.”16 We thank him for the light we have received. At the same time, my children, be convinced that the best devotion is to make many acts of contrition for our sins, which have cost the blood of the divine Lamb. So many mistakes and shortcomings, past, present, and future, rise up before our eyes!

12 Col 1:13 13 Col 1:20 14 2 Tim 1:9 15 Col 1:12 16 Col 1:13

But God our Lord once again draws us into Christ’s Heart to receive his affection, understanding, and forgiveness. Domine, tu omnia nosti, tu scis quia amo te.17 Lord, you know my problems, my blindness, my wavering; you know my sensuality and foolishness. Yet despite everything you’ve chosen me ante mundi consitutionem:18 before the heavens and earth came to be, you already had each of us in mind and loved us with special love. O Lord, Most Sweet Heart of my Jesus: thank you for being father and brother, thank you for the Holy Spirit residing in my soul, enabling me to carry out human deeds that are also divine. By his blood, St Paul continues, “we have redemption, the forgiveness of sins. He is the image of the invisible God, the first-born of all creation.”19 He who is our King, the first-born of every creature, and our God, is also our forgiveness. And being the first-born, he is also our brother, because he is the son of Mary our Mother.

## Christ at the summit {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.24.3}

Quoniam in ipso condita sunt universa… “For in him all things were created, in heaven and on earth, visible and invisible.”20 What is Opus Dei but a reflection of all this? From the events of daily life we have to extract this heavenly light, this infinite treasure of graces hidden in God. But we have to recognise them, to discover the “quid divinum”, the divine element, as you have heard me say hundreds of times, present in each and every one of the ordinary incidents of our lives. In Christ all things are founded, “visible and invisible, whether thrones or dominions or principalities or authorities”,21 everything, absolutely everything! Omnia per ipsum et in ipso creata sunt,22 all

17 Lord, you know everything; you know that I love you: Jn 21:17 18 Eph 1:4 19 Col 1:14-15 20 Col 1:16 21 Ibid. 22 Ibid.

things were created through him and for him. It is his love that has placed us on this earth; he has given us the great gift of life; he has wanted us to be in the world and love the world, which is another of his great gifts; he has given us the vocation to Opus Dei, which is such a wonderful treasure. Words cannot express the great beauty of our calling. That is why it has been so ill-treated and so misunderstood these past forty years! Et ipse est ante omnes, et omnia in ipso constant,23 so he is before all things, and all things subsist through him. Tell me if these words cannot be applied also to this Work of God, which has come to fulfil the apostolic mandate to place Christ at the summit and in the marrow of all human activities: et ego, si exaltatus fuero a terra, omnia traham ad meipsum.24 Because no upright activity is excluded from the field of our apostolate, which becomes an expression of Christ’s redemptive love. We, through the grace of our specific Christian divine vocation, are God’s instruments to contribute to sanctifying the world from the very centre of civil society, to fulfil what St Paul spoke about: instaurare omnia in Christo.25 Et ipse est caput corporis Ecclesiae; “he is the head of the body, the Church; he is the beginning, the first-born from the dead, that in everything he might be pre-eminent.”26 See, my children, how this leads us to sanctify our lives, to try to finish everything for love, not to seek ourselves but to seek him who is the hidden treasure, in each and every thing, both great and small, in things which stand out and in things which don’t, in joy and in sadness! In sadness which shouldn’t last more than an instant in our hearts: all it takes to banish it is sincerity.

23 Col 1:17 24 And I, when I am lifted up from the earth, will draw all things to myself: Jn 12:32 (Vg) 25 To gather up all things in Christ: Eph 1:10 26 Col 1:18

All the things of this earth, then, including material creatures, including the earthly temporal activities of mankind, have to be brought to God – and now, after sin, have to be redeemed and reconciled – each according to its own nature, according to the immediate end established for it by God, but seeing also its ultimate supernatural destiny in Jesus Christ. “For in him all the fullness of God was pleased to dwell, and through him to reconcile to himself all things, whether on earth or in heaven, making peace by the blood of his cross.”27 We have to place Christ at the summit of all human activities. Alas for those who reject the blood of Christ! They are not men of peace but of strife, gossip and slander. We should have a lot of pity for them. Let us beseech the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit, through our Lady’s intercession, to keep us from falling into such errors, to make us good sons and daughters – which doesn’t mean that we won’t have defects – and to help us not to disobey the person we are supposed to obey. Per eum reconciliare omnia in ipsum;28 this reconciling of all things in him is what God expects, from the work his children do. Pacificans per sanguinem crucis eius:29 sowing peace, mark you, sive quae in terris sive quae in caelis sunt,30 on earth and in heaven. My children, you already get a considerable part of heaven here on earth! And the fullness of heaven awaits you hereafter.

## God is all-powerful {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.24.4}

How often, during these past forty years, have I felt, as it were, suspended by a thread between heaven and earth! But is it possible, Lord, that you would abandon me like this? I acknowledge my sins,

27 Col 1:19-20 28 Col 1:20 29 Making peace by the blood of his cross: ibid. 30 Ibid.

my mistakes, my lack of correspondence to grace. But tu es, Deus, fortitudo mea,31 you are my God and my strength. There comes to mind once again our “superiority complex”, for I am in God’s hands, and he is my Father, “holding dominion from sea to sea, and from the river to the ends of the earth.”32 Everything, absolutely everything, is subject to him! Et adorabunt eum omnes reges terrae; omnes gentes servient ei,33 all kings will adore him, all nations will serve him. Even Satan serves him. Even creatures who are damned for all eternity render service to God. So we should have a “superiority complex”, because potestas eius, potestas aeterna, quae non auferetur: et regnum eius, quod non corrumpetur,34 his power is eternal power, which will not be taken from him, and his kingdom is a kingdom that will not be destroyed. We should never forget that you, Lord, can do all things. And we need to rectify our intention, as a ship’s course is rectified on the high seas: by always looking at the star, looking at Mary. Then I’ll always be sure of reaching port. And I’ll point out where the reefs lie. I will lay them bare unashamedly. Sometimes they are little snares, sometimes flagrant ignorance or hateful excuses. And at other times they are expressions of the inadequacy of men who can’t stand your granting fruitfulness to anyone else. “You are my Father, my God, and the Rock of my salvation”,35 reads the Psalm; “I will establish his line for ever.”36 And we, my children, are part of God’s lineage, because we want to journey along all the paths of the earth, which still hold the print of Christ’s footsteps. What a joy to belong to God, to be children of all the love

31 Ps 43:2 32 Ps 72:8 33 Ps 72:11 34 Dan 7:14 35 Ps 89:26 36 Ps 89:29

and all the power of God’s majesty! What a joy to be able to offer him our whole heart! The liturgy also leads us to recall – and I’ll underline this for you

– that a disciple is not above his teacher.37 The texts of the liturgy show us how to imitate the Son of God. Contemplate him as he is being interrogated by Pilate: “Are you the King of the Jews?”38 And our Lord’s reply: “Do you say this of your own accord, or did others say it to you about me?”39 Jesus defends himself in a logical fashion. He first shows that the charge is a calumny. He then explains: “My kingship is not of this world; if my kingship were of this world, my servants would fight, that I might not be handed over to the Jews; but my kingship is not from the world.”40 My children, we have not here a lasting city.41 We are not only citizens of this world, which never ceases to be passing and contingent, but we are also citizens of the kingdom of heaven! How I like to quote that phrase of St Teresa, that this world is “a bad night in a bad inn.”42 Let us return to the Gospel of today’s Mass. “Pilate said to him, So you are a king?”43 And Jesus replied: “You say that I am a king. For this I was born, and for this I have come into the world.”44 Wonderful! What marvellous humility! Our Lord did not keep silent. It is true that on other occasions the Gospel shows us Christ’s silence, but it is also logical and wonderful, human and divine at the same time, that he should speak out: “For this I have come into the world, to bear witness to the truth. Every one who is of the truth hears my voice.”45 But how many people in the world are really seeking the truth seriously? There are, on the other hand, many who

37 Cf. Mt 10:24 38 Jn 18:33 39 Jn 18:34 40 Jn 18:36 41 Cf. Heb 13:14 42 St Teresa of Avila, The Way of Perfection, 40, 9 43 Jn 18:37 44 Ibid. 45 Ibid.

are determined to remain in error, because that makes it easier for them to indulge their senses and live a brutish, animal life, in keeping with their sensual inclinations. My children, we have a lot of apostolate to do. And it shouldn’t ever surprise us that, in spite of being surrounded by people who love us a lot – as well as God, who loves us with special love – we continually discover errors, shortcomings and mistakes in every life. We have to ensure that our soul is magnanimous, that our understanding attains immense heights. Don’t dwell on the amount of darkness there is; gather up, on the other hand, the bright lights of the things that are going well in the lives of your brothers or sisters, and in work, and in family life. Our whole existence is summed up by service, it’s a wonderful service to God our Lord! Nothing in this life, as we have seen from those passages from the letter to the Colossians, the Apocalypse and the Gospel of St John, should lead us away from the presence of God. We have to act always with a great sense of peace, joy and serenity, confident that Christ never abandons us. If you want Christ to reign in your hearts – to reign in mine – be sincere. In this way you and I will always live with the confidence that comes from being faithful and happy, “that we who now glory in fighting under the banner of Christ the King, may likewise reign with him in our heavenly home.”46 Let us entrust these hopes to Mary, Regina mundi, Queen of the World, who, by remaining faithfully beside Jesus when he was raised on the Cross,47 contributed to her Son’s great triumph.

46 Roman Missal of St Pius V, Mass of Christ the King, prayer after Communion 47 Cf. Jn 19:27

# OUR LADY OF SORROWS * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.25}

Holy Mother Church used to celebrate the feast of Our Lady of Sorrows twice a year. There was no fixed date for the first feast, since it depended on the date of Easter. It was Friday in Passion Week, which was called the feast of the Seven Sorrows of the Blessed Virgin Mary: a feast which has disappeared with the liturgical reform. But the one we commemorate today continues, the day after the feast of the Exaltation of the Holy Cross. Our Mother the Church wants the faithful, who are children of Mary, to accompany the Blessed Virgin in her sorrow. She wants us to try to comprehend the suffering her soul underwent throughout Christ’s life, but especially during his passion and death.

## For the sins of all mankind {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.25.1}

I remember something that happened many years ago. I was giving a retreat, and in one of the meditations I spoke about our Lord’s Passion. I emphasised that it was not only original sin that caused our Lord’s terrible sufferings, but our personal sins as well: my failings and those of my hearers. I was moved by the consideration, and the people present were moved too. But when I finished, one of those people who don’t even know the catechism came up to me and said: “Why suffer so much, and why make others suffer over Christ’s Passion? Why talk so much about our

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 15 September 1970, feast of Our Lady of Sorrows

sins being the cause of the sufferings of Christ? Why talk about the ‘sufferings’ of Christ, since God cannot suffer?” I placed myself on the same level as him as a “theologian”, tried to make myself as small as I could, smiled, and told him to recall what he had studied in the catechism: that all things are present to God, that for him there is no past or future; and therefore Christ, while respecting our freedom, was aware of all our betrayals during his Passion and crucifixion. He suffered for all that gentleman’s betrayals, and for mine too, and for those of everyone who will ever be born. Look what the liturgy says about the Blessed Virgin, Mother of God: O quot undis lacrimarum / quo dolore volvitur, / luctuosa de cruento / dum revulsum stipite / cernit ulnis incubantem / Virgo Mater Filium!1 The Virgin Mother, in her sorrow, weeps floods of tears as she contemplates, broken on the blood-stained wood, the Son she cradled in her arms. The Mother suffers because her Son suffers; and her Son, don’t forget, suffers for you and me. There are possibly very few places in the world where these things still are considered in this way. Therefore, with our eyes closed and our souls open to contemplation and to the light of historical and divine facts, let us make an act of contrition, of compunction. Let us ask our Lord’s forgiveness for the lashes and thorns, for the Cross, for the nails that fasten him to the wood, for the lance which will pierce his side, for the gall and vinegar they offer him as consolation. Let’s ask his forgiveness for the mockery, the abuse, the jeers.

## Mockery and jeering {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.25.2}

The chief priests, scribes and elders taunt him: “If you are the Son of God, come down from the Cross.”2 But he doesn’t want to

1 Breviary of St Pius V, Hymn from the office of Matins 2 Mt 27:40

come down. He has gone to the Cross in the spirit of an Eternal Priest, to immolate himself for our sins, as both Priest and Victim. My children, let us be filled with repentance. Without Christ’s sufferings for our sins, there is no point in considering the sorrows of our Lady, his Mother and our Mother. Tradition tells us that St Joseph died before the Crucifixion: Christian art normally depicts him as dying accompanied by Jesus and Mary, and so he is the patron of a happy death. On the other hand, we know nothing about our Lady’s parents. I think about them now because it seems very natural to do so, very much a family thing: if any of you suffer, so do I, and when I cause you to suffer then it hurts me even more. Something similar happened on Calvary. Consider that very supernatural family gathered around Jesus: the Blessed Virgin, the Apostles, the holy women. Who knows whether Joachim and Anna may have been there too, very old indeed, since they were already aged, according to one tradition, when our Blessed Lady was born. I like to consider things that way, from the divine and the human point of view; and realise that all of them suffered because of Jesus’ passion. Especially his Mother, who knew that her Son’s suffering was in a certain sense necessary, because it was what he had come into the world for: to reveal his divinity and to redeem all peoples, including the Jewish race. When you do the Stations of the Cross, which is a wonderful devotion, pay careful attention to those terrible moments when they are leading Jesus like a lamb to the slaughter.3 Imagine if it were one of us. Almost none of my own family is still alive. But you are young and have many relations still living: how they would suffer to see you dishonoured! For what they did to Jesus above all was to dishonour him: they said he wasn’t the Son of God, that he was a liar, a deceiver; they jeered at him because he was the son of a

3 Cf. Is 53:7

carpenter. They forgot that he was a descendant of David. Then, after the dishonour, comes the physical suffering: they scourge him, they mock him, they clamour for his death, which is granted to them. My children, how happy we should be if we encounter opposition, if people mix a bit of the truth with a lot of lies, if they treat us badly, if they pass over things they would regard as good and publicise what they regard as bad: and it may be that this latter is not only not bad, but is in fact the holy pride of our self-giving to God.

## Accompanying Mary {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.25.3}

My children, let us never leave Jesus alone. Let us have a great devotion to Christ crucified, and a tender, filial devotion to Mary, Mother of God and our Mother, who stands upright and firm beside the Cross, alone or practically alone, her soul pierced with grief. My children, for your part, say something to our Lord, and also to his Mother. Tell her what we would tell our own mother if we saw her offended and mistreated in this way, the object of malicious stares. She endures all of this out of love for her Son. She lets herself be crucified in desire, accepting all the shame and humiliation. Accompanying Mary like this is a good way, I repeat, to have presence of God, especially since we can be sure that today very few people here on earth are doing so: they attack her, they insult her as they insulted her Son, they rend the garments of her great privileges, they offend her in every way. But not you and I! Mother of God and our Mother! We believe everything the Church has taught down the centuries. And it saddens us to see that some have abandoned you through lack of piety. I could have put it more strongly: through lack of respect, or

even scorn for your love, which is such an important and wonderful part of Christian interior life. St Thomas Aquinas said that his book was the Crucifix. His book! In those days a book was something of immense value, it was a treasure, because there were so few of them. Now, in contrast, there are many books that destroy man’s faith and dignity. May our book be Jesus crucified. May our book be Mary at the foot of the Cross: strong, upright, but a woman and a Mother, her heart engulfed by sorrow, in need of an affectionate word and consolation. Above all she needs love for her Son, who is nailed to the wood with such inhuman cruelty! If we also bear in mind that this cruelty was the punishment inflicted on someone who had committed crimes against humanity, think of what Jesus’ suffering must have been like. Even though he possessed all that infinite divine love and all that immeasurable capacity for suffering, all that too would necessarily have been an affliction: the dishonour, one thief at his right and another at his left as companions in victory, although only one would be victorious: the one who repented and was sorry, who acknowledged that our Lord bore no guilt at all for the punishment he had received, and who was moved finally by charity. My children, let us be filled with repentance. Make an affective prayer now, placing yourself at the foot of the Cross amidst all that hubbub and loneliness, that shouting and silence. See how heaven’s light grows dim, and listen amazed as the centurion exclaims: “Truly this man was the Son of God!”4 Eia, Mater, obsecramus / per tuas has lacrimas, / Filiique triste funus / vulnerumque purpuram, / hunc tui cordis dolorem / conde nostris cordibus.5

4 Mk 15:39 5 Breviary of St Pius V, Hymn from the office of Matins

Mother, by these tears of yours, and by the bitter death and the blood of the wounds of your Son, we beseech you to fill our hearts with the sorrow you felt in your own most lovable Heart.

## A sword through her heart {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.25.4}

Our Lord suffers through my fault! And so does God’s Mother. Nowadays some false “theologians”, who are devoid of piety and so ignorant that they don’t even know the catechism, want to change the panorama of the redemption with their fanciful theories. They say that it is really impossible to commit sin, that it is impossible for man to offend God. Well, if Adam didn’t sin, then there was no need for Christ to redeem us. The whole structure of dogma collapses! It would make nonsense of that wonderful expression of St Paul’s: “I decided to know nothing among you except Jesus Christ and him crucified.”6 But what does the Church’s Tradition teach us? The breviary, which is a wonderful library, contains this passage from one of St Bernard’s sermons: “Our Lady’s martyrdom is expressed both in the prophecy of Simeon and in the account of our Lord’s passion. ‘This child that you see’, said the venerable old man, referring to Jesus, ‘is destined to be a sign of contradiction’; and addressing his Mother, ‘which will be for you a sword that will pierce your heart’ (Lk 2:34).”7 I would say that we already have ample evidence of that ourselves. Here, in my beloved Italy, was born the devotion to our Lady of Sorrows, represented with seven daggers through her heart. The piety of the faithful needed to express it in some such graphic way. It was the Servites who started the devotion and spread it to practically all Catholic countries. Although there was a man once, an ignoramus and a bad writer to boot, who wrote in a Roman

6 1 Cor 2:2 7 St Bernard, Sermon on the Twelve Stars, on the Sunday within the Octave of the Assumption, 14

newspaper that this representation of the Blessed Virgin with daggers through her heart was a Spanish devotion, macabre and fanatical. In fact it is Italian, very Italian… although that’s neither here nor there. Personally, I think it fully represents what St Bernard goes on to explain: “Truly, O Blessed Mother, a sword did pierce your heart. But merely in so doing, it rent your Son’s flesh.” It is the Passion of the Mother and of him who is the fruit of her virginal womb. “Nay more: after your Jesus had given up his spirit, the cruel lance which opened his side could no longer in any way touch his soul, but did most certainly transfix yours (…). The force of that sorrow pierced your spirit to such an extent that you fully merit to be proclaimed more than a martyr, because in you the torment of your soul exceeded bodily pain (…). It should not therefore surprise you, brethren, that Mary is called a martyr in spirit.”8 And he adds a little further on: “Perhaps someone may say: Surely she knew beforehand that he had to die? Without doubt. And did she not expect that he would rise again soon? Most certainly. And in spite of all that she suffered because of the crucifixion? Yes, most exceedingly. On the other hand, my brother, who do you think you are and whence do you forge those subtle distinctions, that cause you to be more surprised by Mary’s compassion than by the sufferings of Mary’s Son?”9 That is the voice of Tradition. My Jesus, hear me! O Mother, I beseech you, by the life, the passion and the death of your Son, have mercy upon us, defend the Church! Jesus, my love, by the love you have for your Mother, have mercy on your Church. Have mercy! I raise up my prayer with confidence, with no other motive than the honour of the Blessed Trinity, Father, Son and Holy Spirit, my love for you my Mother, peace for the Church, love for the Work, and the defence of our vocation.

8 Ibid. 9 Ibid.

We finish with words of praise to the Blessed Trinity: Esto Patri, Filioque, / et coaevo Flamini, / esto summae Trinitati / sempiterna gloria, / et perennis laus honorque / hoc et omni saeculo. Amen.10 All acts of praise to the Blessed Trinity taste to me like honey from the comb: to the Father, and the Son, and the co-eternal Fire, to the entire Trinity be everlasting glory, and eternal praise and honour, now and for ever, Mother of God and my Mother. Let us adore the Father, who created us; the Son, who willed to become incarnate; the Holy Spirit, who is the fruit of the Cross, God with us, who acts in our souls in grace. Let us honour the Blessed Trinity especially on this day, and let us sing to Mary: Daughter of God, Mother of God, Spouse of God! My children, let us resolve to be always at Mary’s side, at the foot of the Cross, in desire and in fact. Let us resolve to console her, and to read from the book that is Christ crucified, in order to be filled with peace, joy and desires for holiness.

10 Breviary of St Pius V, Hymn from the office of Matins

# THE TEMPTATION OF EXTRAORDINARY THINGS * {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.26}

My Lord and my God, I firmly believe that you are here… We have to stress your Eucharistic presence in the tabernacles all over the world where you are reserved. You are very often alone, because people don’t come to keep you company – especially many who ought to be looking after you with special care. We firmly believe that you are here, really present, Body, Blood, Soul and Divinity. We believe at this moment that you see us, that you hear us… I adore you with profound reverence, I ask you for pardon for my sins and grace to make this time of prayer fruitful. My Mother immaculate, St Joseph, my Father and Lord, my Guardian Angel, intercede for me. We are starting a few days’ retreat, which we pray will be very fruitful: all of you and I too ask this of our Lord, of our Lady and of St Joseph, our Father and Lord. We are gathered together in this little oratory where we have set up an altar and a tabernacle that exceeded our financial resources but not our love, the least unfitting we could manage for our Lord. Here in this oratory the whole Work is represented. And we ought each to be convinced that the holiness of the others depends to a large extent on the support we give them. Not only with our work, done for the right intention and with charity and fortitude, but

* Notes of a meditation given in Rome, 3 March 1974, 1st Sunday of Lent, at the start of a retreat

also with our interior life. This doesn’t mean that we should get upset about our past faults, about all the times we haven’t lived up to God’s hopes for us. At the opportune moment we have made use of the famous “staples” (the necessary remedies) and we have sought purification of soul in love and contrition. All of this, my children, should give us peace.

## A diabolical temptation {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.26.1}

This morning, reading St Matthew’s account of Christ’s temptations, the thought occurred to me that we would be crazy if we thought we were incapable of sinning. It is true that our Lord has often given us his strength. But does that mean that it will always be so, and is it thanks to ourselves? It is natural to think that we can indeed count on it, because God is our strength: quia tu es, Deus, fortitudo mea!1 And God’s strength comes to us through Mary. My children, to the end of our days we will experience temptation. Don’t ever forget that! Right to the last moment we will have difficulties, temptations, obstacles, and strife. That’s why we have to understand the circumstances of each one of my children, of each one of your brothers, and make allowance for them in everything that warrants it, and help them with our example and the fact of a sincere, constant interior life, which is expressed by our self-denial and in clear deeds of service. Nowadays the devil is particularly active. I’m not exaggerating. It describes very well, unfortunately, what one sees in ecclesiastical and civil life. We could feel, for example, that we are far removed from the concerns of this world, in the desert, like Christ, but brought there not by the Holy Spirit but by the devil. The Holy Spirit tells us through St Matthew, in the Gospel of this first Sunday of Lent, that after having been baptised by John, “Jesus was led up by the Spirit into the wilderness to be tempted by

1 Ps 43:2

the devil. And he fasted forty days and forty nights, and afterward he was hungry.”2 God allowed the tempter to have access to Jesus. He tries to take advantage of the physiological needs of Christ, perfectus Deus, perfectus homo,3 to divert him from his divine mission. And how does Jesus react to this? The whole dialogue is marvellous. It affects me deeply, because you can see how the devil tries to influence Christ. “If you are the Son of God, command these stones to become loaves of bread”,4 he proposes to him. Some Church Fathers state that the devil wasn’t sure if Christ was the promised Messiah, but only suspected it. “Since the devil had heard the heavenly voice saying ‘This is my beloved Son’, and had also heard John giving such exalted testimony of him, and since on the other hand he now sees him hungry, he is quite puzzled by it all. On the one hand he cannot believe that the one about whom he has heard such things can be just a man, and on the other he cannot credit that the one he now sees hungry can be the Son of God.”5 And so he tries to test him, perhaps in order to find out who exactly he is. On the other hand, though, our Lord was willing to undergo these temptations to reassure us, to teach us how to repel the enemy by his own experience and example. Jesus replies to the tempter’s approach, “It is written, Man shall not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceeds from the mouth of God.”6 See how the devil tries to impose conditions on Christ. “Show me that you are the Son of God!” And he could also challenge us: “Show me that you are a saint! Put yourself in this danger, do something extraordinary for me, don’t be satisfied with ordinary things…”

2 Mt 4:1-2 3 Perfect God and perfect man: Athanasian Creed 4 Mt 4:3 5 St John Chrysostom, Homilies on St Matthew’s Gospel, 13, 2. Cf. also St Hilary, Commentary on St Matthew’s Gospel, 3, 1 6 Mt 4:4

My children, our way is the ordinary way, the prose of every day. This could be one of the temptations my children face, now and throughout the ages; one of the ways the devil tries to bind them and make them ineffective, as he tried to subvert Christ’s own mission: “Do something spectacular, change these stones into bread…” But as you have seen so often in the Gospel, Jesus never works a miracle for his own benefit. Whenever he does work one, moved by compassion, it is in order to help those who ask him with faith, to provide his listeners with a motive of credibility, and not to avoid effort or problems.

## The extraordinary in the ordinary {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.26.2}

The lesson is very clear, my children. What did you expect to find when you came to the Work? Extraordinary things, first-class miracles? We don’t live by miracles. Our Lord worked them, and he continues to work them now through our apostolic work and in our own personal inner life. But don’t expect any others. In Opus Dei we constantly experience the marvel of ordinary life: miracles that don’t startle anyone, that don’t shine out like stars to attract attention, but go unnoticed. God makes use of ordinary events to draw souls to his love. He makes use of you and me, even when we don’t feel a single spark of life. If we are very aware of our own smallness and limitations, then the devil won’t trap us like that, but we do have to be on our guard: it would be tragic if we were to let ourselves be entangled in that snare. Tell me: the fact that in the Work there are people of all races and nationalities, from all backgrounds – healthy and sick, learned and less so, youngsters who are just embarking on life and old people who are about to leave this world, single and married, each coming from somewhere different – isn’t all that quite extraordinary?

Humanly speaking, there is no reason why we should all be so united: in faith, in works, with a single supernatural goal and a great desire to treat all souls affectionately. This is not what normally happens anywhere. It’s truly extraordinary. And all the more so since we experience it in circumstances which are very often materially uncomfortable, when we are commonly very short of resources to start and develop the apostolate. In this life, look for the extraordinary in ordinary things which go unnoticed. Some people only see God’s action when the heavens open and they hear a voice from on high… No! Daily life, the normal life of a Christian who has faith – when working or relaxing, when praying and sleeping, at every moment – is really supernatural, a life in which God is always present. And don’t expect anything else.

## Don’t impose any conditions {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.26.3}

Having failed the first time, the devil tries again. “Then the devil took him to the holy city, and set him on the pinnacle of the temple, and said to him, ‘If you are the Son of God, throw yourself down.’”7 Imagine trying to tempt God, the Creator of everything that exists, in such an obtuse way! “Throw yourself from the pinnacle of the temple, and the angels will bear you up…” Once again we are faced with the spectacular. And our Lord, who performs great miracles not only when he is asked, but even when he sees the need, because he is moved by pity, pays no heed to the devil. He rejects the temptation to work extraordinary deeds for his own benefit. O Lord, who hear us now, I offer you my anguish for you to see: the anguish I feel on seeing my own life, which has never been as I would have wished it to be; the anguish I experience whenever one of my children starts to waver, or when I contemplate the state of mankind, this world which seems to be a cyclone of contrary forces

7 Mt 4:5-6

in which nothing is ever still, nothing fixed, nothing has weight or volume, nothing is in its place. Let us draw the conclusion: non tentabis Dominum Deum tuum!8 You shall not tempt the Lord your God! Lord, it would be a great pity if my children and I did not learn from this example and these words of yours which St Matthew offers us, but allowed ourselves to be defeated by the devil. My dearest children, don’t ever let it even occur to you to impose conditions on our self-giving in God’s service, in exchange for something extraordinary. Lord, we don’t ask you for anything out of the ordinary: for us everyday things are extraordinary enough; they can be first-class miracles, if we carry them out lovingly. In the last analysis, I am still preaching the words you placed on my lips nearly fifty years ago, and this is the lesson which all my daughters and sons pass on in their turn. You have made my heart thrill with this preaching and teaching, and you have given light to my mind and strength to my will, and filled me with assurance and consolation. Patience, serenity, repose, interior peace, piety, confident awareness of our divine filiation, certainty of the Love which, through the Most Sweet Heart of Mary, flows from the Most Sacred and Merciful Heart of Jesus. O Lord, who are present here in the Eucharist: we would like to recline our heads on your breast, like the adolescent John. And like John, to be faithful at a time of desertion and cowardice, when all are scattered by fear, when all human strength is dissipated, when all human wisdom is reduced to foolishness. All that useless chatter! I think about my things, which are yours, which are his, Christ our Lord’s, because I want to be his, because he has deigned to be mine. Each of you should do the same. That’s the only way to keep

8 Mt 4:7

going, you in your things and I in mine, conscious that they are not ours but his. And all the rest doesn’t bother us at all.

## Anguish and joy {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.26.4}

These days I am experiencing a sort of interior affliction. It doesn’t take away my peace, but it does get me down at times. There is no earthly basis for it, and perhaps God is allowing it to compensate for the great sense of satisfaction I feel when I realise that our new house, God willing, will soon be finished – it should have been finished some time ago.9 By God’s grace, and by your grace too (your human qualities: patience, wisdom, kind words, good deeds) and the assistance of your brothers the architects, that “cava”, that quarry will produce many polished stones, white and gleaming, which will serve as foundation stones for very different apostolic undertakings all over the world, but all with the same spirit: because they will be carried out by souls who won’t hold back in their commitment, who will give themselves completely, conscious that God awaits them every moment and every second: hodie, nunc! Now, at this very moment. There is a special providence of God watching over the Work. These days I have been reminded very forcibly of the time when these buildings were being built. Bruno Buozzi, Villa Sacchetti, Domenico Cirillo. I remember all the excavations. I remember all that badly-set marble – because it’s a fact, it was. I remember having to defend the beauty of the altar against the rough handling of people who tried to get away with sloppy workmanship. I remember dreaming about offering a sumptuous tabernacle to our Lord, in memory of the first tabernacle of the Work, a borrowed one, made of wood – and now here it is. It all seemed so impossible! And now nevertheless I am filled with anguish. Why is it, Lord? Because I don’t love you enough, because with the passage of the

9 Our Father was referring to Cavabianca, the Roman College of the Holy Cross, which was in the process of being finished

years I am less child-like, although old age usually causes men to return to the years of their childhood… I really ought to be more a child. Lord, make me one, so that I only think about little things. That way my love will be greater and I will realise that I am beginning to respond properly to you.

## Spiritual insights {#inedito.while-he-spoke-to-us-on-the-way.26.5}

Every day, when I have you in my heart after receiving you at Mass, having said the prayers which for generations the Church has prescribed for her priests, I say to you: Lord, I believe that it is you. Lord, thank you for having come to this dunghill of my breast. When I say all this, I become aware that I am yours, your brother, because I am the son of your Mother, because I am the son of St Joseph, whom I wish to love more every day: St Joseph, my Father and Lord. Afterwards, I ask you for pardon for my faults, for all this clay I drag around with me, and I consider how it is not only I who offend you, but the whole human race. And I feel a great desire to make reparation. I want to atone. I want to, Lord, but how can I, if I can do nothing, if I am worth nothing, if I possess nothing, if I am nothing? Then I have recourse to your infinite merits, the merits of your Mother, those of the Patriarch St Joseph. And I add: and the gold of my children, which shines the world over! There is some dross there too (they are human after all) but it is gold. And I conclude by saying: and also those flashes of light that shine in my poor life, which is shadow and darkness; those gleams of light which shine in the night of my soul, by your grace and my own poor response to it. That is how I give thanks after Holy Communion: the same every day… with Joseph and Mary I go to you, in the house at Nazareth, which is becoming a reality within me. And I like to make an explicit act of faith in your real presence in the tabernacle. It makes me very happy to shout aloud, making sure that it can be

seen that I mean it, that you are hidden in the sacramental species, Body, Blood, Soul and Divinity! You are there, Jesus, in the tabernacle: and I, Lord, am here, crippled, blind, leprous, dead! Miserere mei, Deus, secundum magnam misericordiam tuam!10 Don’t forsake me, because all that will be really true if you leave me. I am capable of imposing conditions on you, allowing myself to be seduced by that subtle diabolical activity which doesn’t come barefaced but clothed in the trappings of holiness. Haec omnia tibi dabo,11 I will give you all things – all things! – the devil says to Christ. What has he got to offer us? We will lose our peace of soul and our soul itself, if we impose conditions on God, if we don’t give ourselves wholeheartedly, if something holds us back. When I was thirty-two, I asked those who followed me, and I also wrote and published it: “What does it matter whether the thing that binds you is a thread or a chain?”12 The important thing is that you have to be free to move, without conditions or bonds. And for us, for you and especially for me, a silken thread that binds us to things which are not God’s is a chain that enslaves us. We don’t wish to impose conditions on you, Lord! We don’t wish to limit our commitment! And so, when Satan comes to tempt us in all those ways, we will say to him with all our soul: Vade Satana! “Begone, Satan! for it is written, You shall worship the Lord your God and him only shall you serve.”13 Mother of ours, Ancilla Domini,14 handmaid of the Lord, help us to follow your example at all times. May we respond to our Lord’s

10 Have mercy on me, O God, according to your steadfast love: Ps 51:1 11 Mt 4:9 12 Cf. The Way, 170 13 Mt 4:10 14 Lk 1:38 320 WHILE HE SPOKE TO US ON THE WAY demands as you did: fiat mihi secundum verbum tuum,15 may your beloved will always be done in me unconditionally.

15 Ibid.
